# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 30

## Page 14813

Volume Thirty
From the Book of Amicable Settlement to the Book of Endowments

In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful.

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man purchased a house with two doorways, each situated in a dead-end alley. One doorway is blocked up, and the documents attest to both doorways. The blocked doorway is the original entrance, at the head of the alley. He wishes to reopen it. Is he entitled to do so?

He answered:

If he purchased the house together with its associated rights, and the blocked doorway was among those rights, he may reopen it as it originally was, unless this right was excluded from the sale, either expressly or by custom.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Two houses are separated by a street. The owner of one wishes to build an upper room on his house that would obstruct the open space available to the other house. Is this permissible or not?

---

## Page 14814

He answered:

If this harms the neighbour—for example, by allowing him to overlook the neighbour’s property—he must provide a barrier preventing him from overlooking the property below. If it causes the neighbour no harm, either because he builds a barrier preventing such overlooking or because the building does not overlook the neighbour’s property in the first place, he may not be prevented from building.

He was asked:

A man purchased a house overlooking a public thoroughfare and then wished to extend it. He purchased a specified number of cubits of land along the side of the thoroughfare from the representative of the public treasury and erected a wall on the land he had purchased. He also wished to erect a structure spanning the Muslims’ thoroughfare so that he could build a house upon it. Is this permissible or not? Is the sale of the land purchased from the representative of the public treasury, which forms part of the Muslims’ thoroughfare, valid or not? Is the witness who testifies that it belongs to the public treasury guilty of sinful conduct or not? If the second man claims that his building has caused the Muslims no harm, is his claim to be entertained? What constitutes the harm on account of which building over the thoroughfare is prohibited? May a Muslim judge permit him to proceed in the manner described, or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. It is not permissible to sell any part of the Muslims’ public through-road, nor does the representative of the public treasury have the authority to sell it, whether the road is wide or

---

## Page 14815

narrow. The witness has no knowledge beyond what everyone else knows—unless, of course, he testifies that this land belongs to the public treasury because, for example, it once belonged to a man, then passed into the ownership of the public treasury and was unlawfully incorporated into the road. As for his testimony that it belongs to the public treasury merely because it is a road: if he means that the public road is a right belonging to the Muslims, that does not justify selling it. If he means that it is property belonging to the public treasury and may be sold like other treasury property, then this is false testimony, for which he deserves a punishment that will deter him and others like him. The judge may not rule that this sale is valid.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

There are two houses, one east of the other. One is entered by a stairway beneath the other’s gutter, and this arrangement has existed for a long time. May the owner of the house whose stairway and access passage lie beneath the other house’s gutter prevent water from that gutter from flowing onto this stairway because of the harm it causes him, or not?

He answered:

He has no right to prevent the holder of a long-established right from exercising it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14816

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who had erected a building and projecting structures above the entrance to the upper storeys, overlooking his neighbour’s private quarters. He had also obstructed his neighbour’s stairway entrance so that he could neither carry a tray of dough down nor bring a water carrier’s waterskin up.

He answered:

A neighbour may not add anything to a shared passage that has no outlet without the consent of his fellow user or the other co-owners. Nor may he make alterations to his own property that harm his neighbour. If he does so, the co-owner is entitled to have the source of the harm removed, whether before or after the sale. However, if it is removed before the sale, it may not be reinstated; if it is removed after the sale, the purchaser is entitled to rescind the sale because of this diminution.

He was also asked:

A man purchased some shops, including their land and buildings, twenty years ago. Above them is an upper storey. Someone then appeared claiming ownership of that upper storey, but the owner of the shops did not acknowledge the validity of his claim. The claimant erected a new structure on the upper storey. Is he obliged to

---

## Page 14817

demolish the new structure he has erected?

He answered:

If he is in possession of the upper storey and the owner of the lower storey does not claim it as his own, it belongs to the person in possession until someone else establishes proof that it belongs to him. As for the new construction he has added, he has no right to do so unless this falls within the rights attached to his property. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man purchased an upper storey that did not previously project outward. He then renovated it and added a structure projecting over a cul-de-sac shared with his neighbours. He claimed that the alley had a doorway east of al-Zāhiriyya. Is he entitled to add this projecting structure?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. He is not entitled to add a projecting structure over a passage that has no outlet, by agreement of the imams; they have not disputed this. They have, however, differed over whether such a structure may be added over a through passage. That issue involves disagreement and distinctions that are beyond the scope of this discussion.

As for a passage that has no outlet, there is no disagreement concerning it, whether or not it has a doorway leading to a school, for it is not a through passage. If he claims that he has the right to maintain a projecting structure there, his claim is not accepted without proof. He may, however, require the neighbours who dispute his claim to swear an oath denying his entitlement to it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14818

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling concerning a man who owns a house, with a road separating the neighbours’ properties, and has an overhead structure spanning the road and resting on one of their properties, without harming either the neighbour or passers-by, when another neighbour wishes to project out equally far and extend beyond his neighbours [into] (1) the road, causing harm to his neighbour?

He answered:

An overhead structure of this kind, or anything similar, is impermissible if it causes harm, by agreement of the scholars. Likewise, no one may extend any part of a building into a road used by Muslims. Indeed, even plastering the outside of a wall is prohibited unless the wall is set back by the thickness of the plaster.

If, however, such an overhead structure or the like does not interfere with the use of the road, there is a well-known disagreement among the scholars. Some hold that it is permissible, as al-Shāfiʿī maintains. Others hold that it is impermissible, as in one of the two views within the schools of Ahmad and Mālik. Others hold that it is permissible with the ruler’s permission, according to the latter view. Still others hold that if any member of the public objects, it must not be built; this is the position of Abū Hanīfa. And Allah knows best.

## Page 14819

He was asked:

A dead-end alley has several residents, one of whom owns a house there. May he open a doorway other than his original doorway?

He answered:

He may not open a doorway into the dead-end alley closer to its far end than his original doorway, except with the permission of those who share with him the right of passage through that part of the alley. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man built some shops beside a ruined property belonging to someone else. May the owner of the house open an entrance from the ruined property, or not?

He answered:

He may not open an entrance—meaning a doorway into a dead-end alley—without the permission of its residents, unless he has a right of passage through it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14820

He was asked:

A Muslim and a protected non-Muslim jointly own a property which they have demolished completely. May they rebuild it higher than their Muslim neighbour’s property, or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. They may not raise it above the Muslim’s property, for a protected non-Muslim is forbidden to raise his building above a Muslim’s. Whatever must be avoided in order to avoid something forbidden is itself forbidden.

This is analogous to cases in which the unlawful becomes mixed with the lawful, such as when visible impurity becomes mixed with water or other liquids, or when offspring are produced by an animal lawful to eat and one unlawful to eat, such as the simʿ and the ʿisbār (wolf–hyena hybrids), and the mule. The same applies to “cases of indistinguishability,” such as when a man cannot distinguish his sister from an unrelated woman, or a lawfully slaughtered animal from carrion. Since the forbidden cannot be avoided without also avoiding what was originally permissible, both must be avoided. Likewise, whatever action is indispensable to fulfilling an obligation is itself obligatory. This applies only when that action is not a condition for the obligation itself to arise and is within the ability of the person subject to it.

Here, the protected non-Muslim cannot be prevented from raising his building above the Muslim’s unless his partner is also prevented; therefore, both must be prevented. Preventing a Muslim from raising his building above another Muslim’s does not involve raising a non-Muslim’s building above a Muslim’s. This differs from allowing the partner to raise it, for that would entail

---

## Page 14821

a non-Muslim’s building rising above a Muslim’s, which is not permissible. If the two partners raise the building, it must be demolished. A Muslim may not use a Muslim’s standing as a means of elevating a non-Muslim above a Muslim. Whoever enters into partnership with a non-Muslim or employs him, seeking to use the standing of Islam to elevate him above Muslims, has dishonoured Islam and deserves to be humiliated in accordance with Islam. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A jointly owned orchard has been divided, and one of the two partners wishes to build a wall between his property and his partner’s. The other refuses either to let him build or to join him in the construction.

He answered:

He must be compelled to do so. The land needed for the wall is to be taken from each partner’s plot in proportion to his share.

---

## Page 14822

He was asked:

A jointly owned orchard was divided between two partners. One wished to build a wall between his property and his partner’s, but the partner refused to let him build on his land. Who must bear the cost of construction?

He answered:

The partner who refuses must be compelled to build the wall on both partners’ portions of land, if both need a screen between them.

He was also asked:

If, in the same situation, one partner refused to join the other in building, and the other built the wall at his own expense, placing part of its foundation on each partner’s share, may he prevent the partner who did not contribute from using the wall—for example, placing something on it or building upon it?

He answered:

Even if the wall belonged exclusively to one of them, he would not be entitled to prevent his neighbour from making use of it in a way that the neighbour needs and that does not harm the wall’s owner. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14823

He was asked:

A man purchased property from the public treasury in Egypt through a valid transaction under Islamic law and began building. Someone interfered and prevented him from building. Is that person entitled to do so?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah. If a man builds on his own property without infringing his neighbour’s rights, but the neighbour fears that other people will occupy the new building and thereby reduce the rent of the existing building, the neighbour may not prevent him on that account. There is no disagreement among the scholars concerning this.

He was asked:

A man owned a property that was in danger of collapse. He was warned of this but refused to demolish it. It then collapsed onto a child and crushed him. Is the owner liable for compensation or not?

He answered:

According to one of the two scholarly opinions, he is liable for compensation because he was negligent in failing to remove this danger. Liability rests with the legally competent owner if he is present, with his agent if he is absent, or with his guardian if he is under legal interdiction.

---

## Page 14824

And liability for compensation in such a case is the position of Abū Hanīfa and Mālik, one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, and one of the two views within the Shāfiʿī school. The amount due is half the blood money, and assessed compensation for injuries for which no fixed amount is prescribed. Payment falls upon the group collectively responsible for their blood money, if possible; otherwise, it falls upon them personally, according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions.

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

If someone needs to convey his water through another person’s land, and this causes no harm, he may do so. According to another report from him, the landowner (1) may prevent him, just as he may if the person has no need of the water or no need to convey it through that land.

He said:

If a man has a watercourse running through unowned land, and someone whose land adjoins it wishes to divert it, or part of it, towards his own land, causing no harm apart from his benefiting from the water—as he could already benefit from it in its existing course, though this would make it easier for him to do so—I have ruled that this is permissible and that it is unlawful to prevent him. For just as the passage of water through land benefits the owner of the water, thus giving him a right to it, it also benefits the owner of the land, as in the report concerning ʿUmar. Here, he benefits from the other man’s water being conveyed through his land, just as there the other man benefits from his land.

A comparable case is where the owner of a wall has an interest in beams being laid upon it, with

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 14825

no harm being caused by the beams.

The converse of the question of conveying water is this: suppose he wishes to channel the water through his land from one place to another, then discharge it onto unowned land or the land of a consenting neighbour, without harming the owner of the water. Yet he ought to have the right to do so, since he is entitled to occupy an unoccupied place, and likewise to clear an occupied one.

The governing principle is that a neighbour may wish either to establish a new use of his neighbour’s property or to end his neighbour’s use of a place, where ending that use benefits him without harming the other. One of our fundamental principles is that being neighbours confers upon each party rights that someone who is not a neighbour does not possess, and imposes restrictions that do not apply to such a person. Thus, it permits the use of a neighbour’s property when this causes the neighbour no harm, while prohibiting a person’s use of his own property when that use causes harm.

Section

If, according to one of the two reported positions, we hold that he may convey his water, and he needs to convey it through an existing watercourse—for example, to drain water from his roofs and elsewhere into his neighbour’s conduit, or to convey the water of a pool through a channel and then divide it with his neighbour—this is permissible.

---

## Page 14826

Chapter on Legal Interdiction

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man is being harshly pressed for a debt by someone who wishes to have him imprisoned, although he is insolvent. Is his assertion of insolvency accepted, or must he produce evidence of it?

He replied:

If he incurred the debt without receiving anything in exchange, as in the case of a guarantee, and he was not previously known to possess any wealth, his assertion of insolvency is accepted upon his oath. And Allah knows best.

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A Muslim man purchased real estate from a non-Muslim under Muslim protection, then threw himself on his mercy and purchased two shares from him. He undertook, under a legally binding oath, to pay within one month. Is anyone obliged to teach him a stratagem when he is able to pay?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the debtor is able to pay, no one may compel the creditor to cease demanding payment, nor ask him to resort to a device that has no real substance

---

## Page 14827

for that purpose—for example, by receiving payment from him and then returning it to him without any genuine settlement of the debt. If he is insolvent, he must be granted a respite. An unqualified oath is understood to apply when one is able to fulfil it, not when one is unable. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man died leaving a vineyard and a house, with a debt equal to the entire value of that property. His heirs include a wife, a daughter, and the ruler . . . (1) The creditors asked the heirs to sell the property. Are the heirs obliged to sell it, or must the judge do so?

He replied:

If the heirs sell it and pay the debt from the proceeds, that is permissible. If they hand it over to the creditors, who sell it and recover their debts, that is permissible. If they ask the judge to appoint a trustworthy person to handle the matter for them, that is permissible. If they themselves appoint a trustworthy person to handle it, that is permissible. Once the heirs hand the property over to the creditors, they are not obliged to undertake the sale themselves. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 14828

He was asked:

A man traded in cloth, selling it to another person at a specified profit and allowing him to pay the price in instalments. The debtor wishes to travel but has not provided a guarantor. May the creditor prevent him from travelling?

He replied:

If the debt is due and the debtor is able to repay it, the creditor may prevent him from travelling until he has recovered what he is owed. Likewise, if payment is deferred but falls due before the debtor’s return, the creditor may prevent him from travelling until he provides security, either through collateral that safeguards the amount owed or through a guarantor. If the debt does not fall due until after the debtor’s return, the scholars differ on the matter. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14829

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man freed a slave while he was in need, burdened with debts, and without means. May he sell the slave and use the proceeds to repay his debt?

He replied:

If, when he freed him, he was solvent and owed no debt, or owed a debt that he could repay from property other than the slave, then the slave has become free, and the emancipation cannot be revoked. If, however, his debts exhausted his assets when he freed him, the scholars differ concerning the validity of the emancipation. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man brought a claim against his debtor before a judge. The debtor acknowledged the debt, his ability to repay it, and his solvency. The judge then imprisoned him and barred him from disposing of his property. The imprisoned debtor subsequently wished to establish his insolvency before another judge. Is he entitled to do so?

He replied:

His claim of insolvency, after acknowledging his ability to repay and after being barred from disposing of his property, is not accepted unless he explains what caused him to lose his solvency, and that cause is ordinarily plausible—for example, a fire in the house containing his belongings, or something similar. Nor is he entitled to seek a final judgment on this matter.

---

## Page 14830

or to make that claim and establish it before a judge other than the one who imprisoned him and barred him from disposing of his property, without that judge’s permission. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

A man borrowed money from merchants. When repayment was demanded, he refused despite being able to repay, and he was imprisoned under Islamic law. May the judge punish him until he pays what he owes? What is the ruling of Islamic law concerning him?

He replied:

If he refuses to fulfil his obligation to disclose his assets and enable people to receive all that is due to them, and his assets are known but repayment requires some action on his part which he refuses to take, insisting instead on remaining in prison, then, according to the jurists generally, he is to be punished by beating until he fulfils his obligation in this matter.

This has been expressly stated by the followers of Mālik and al-Shāfi'ī, including the Iraqi and Khurāsānī scholars, and by the followers of Imam Ahmad and others. We know of no disagreement concerning this. Indeed, they discuss this issue repeatedly in their books: besides mentioning it in its customary place, they also mention it elsewhere, such as in the chapter on the marriage of non-Muslims. They make it a precedent upon which they base an analogous ruling when a non-Muslim embraces Islam while married to more than four wives and refuses to choose

---

## Page 14831

they state that he is to be beaten until he makes his choice, because he has refused to perform an act obligatory upon him. He is to be beaten until he fulfils it, just as someone who refuses to repay a debt he owes is to be beaten until he repays it.

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said in the hadith recorded in both Sahīh collections:

“Delay in payment by one who can afford to pay is injustice.”

An unjust person deserves punishment. The Sunan collections also report that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Delay in payment by one who has the means permits speaking against him and punishing him.”

Here, “delay” means procrastination in payment, and “one who has the means” means someone able to pay. Thus, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—permitted speaking against someone who delays payment despite being able to pay.

The scholars have agreed that discretionary punishment is prescribed for every sin for which there is neither a prescribed fixed punishment nor an expiation. Sins consist either of neglecting something commanded or committing something forbidden. When punishment is imposed for neglecting an obligation—as in the case of this man and others who neglect their obligations—the offender is punished until he fulfils it.

For this reason, the scholars among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad and others who discuss this issue state that he is to be beaten repeatedly until he pays. Some stipulate thirty-nine lashes on each occasion, while others set no specific number. This depends on whether discretionary punishment has a prescribed limit or not.

The judge may impose a discretionary punishment for his refusal as a penalty for his past conduct. He may also punish him until he personally makes the payment required of him. The judge is not obliged to undertake the sale of his property and repayment of the debt himself.

---

## Page 14832

although the judge is permitted to do so. However, whenever he considers it appropriate to require the debtor himself to sell the property and repay the debt—whether to deter him and others like him from delaying payment, because the judge is occupied with other matters, or because some harm is feared from his undertaking it—the debtor is to be punished by beating until he undertakes it himself.

If he says:

“Selling it now for immediate payment would cause me a loss. Instead, I will sell it on deferred terms and assign you the payment owed by the purchaser,”

and the creditors say:

“We will not accept an assignment, but we agree that it may be sold with payment deferred until that date, and that he should collect the payment and repay us. Any amount lost in dealings with the purchaser is to be borne from his own funds,”

then the creditors’ request is to be granted. The judge may sell the property and appoint someone to collect the payment and repay the creditors, while also punishing the debtor for neglecting his obligation.

The creditors may demand the immediate sale of whatever can be sold for immediate payment, provided it is sold at its fair market price. The debtor must comply with this demand. The judge may carry this out, as explained above, and punish him for neglecting his obligation. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About someone who owed a debt but did not repay it until a claim was brought against him before the judge and others, and travel expenses were incurred: is the debtor liable for those expenses or not?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. If the person who owes the payment is able to pay

---

## Page 14833

and delays payment until the creditor is compelled to lodge a complaint, then whatever expenses the creditor incurs as a result must be borne by the debtor who wrongfully delays payment, provided those expenses are incurred in the customary manner.

He was asked:

About someone imprisoned for debt who has no means of repayment other than property pledged to the creditor: should he be granted time and released so that he can sell it?

He replied:

If he has no means of repayment other than the pledged property, the creditor must grant him time to sell it. If selling it is impossible unless he is released, or selling it while imprisoned would cause him harm, he must be released to sell it. A guarantee should be secured on his behalf, or the creditor or his agent should go to him.

He was asked:

About a man who owes a debt that is due and owns property that provides no surplus beyond what is needed for his own maintenance and that of his dependants. If he wishes to sell it, he can obtain only less than its fair market price. Must he sell it below its fair market price? If he is not required to sell it, should his debt be payable in instalments proportionate to his circumstances, or not?

He replied:

It is not to be sold except at the usual fair market price generally prevailing in that locality, unless

---

## Page 14834

usual market conditions have undergone a lasting change, in which case the fair market price will have fallen, and it should be sold at the newly established fair market price. If selling it is not obligatory, the creditor must grant the debtor respite until his circumstances improve or he has the means to pay. The creditor may demand whatever he can afford at any given time; this is payment by instalments.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a silk-weaver to whom a woman delivered yarn for two lengths of cloth. He fled, and his shop was sealed. The woman complained about her yarn, so the governor ordered the shop opened and permitted everyone to take whatever of their own belongings they found. One length remained on the loom. A man, a mawqiʿ, then came forward, stating that he had left a little yarn with the silk-weaver. He complained to the judge, who authorized him to take the woman’s length of cloth and divide it among those who had entrusted property to the weaver, while deferring consideration of the woman’s case.

He replied:

Property held in trust in a bankrupt person’s shop—such as cloth he is weaving for people, yarn, and other items—belongs to its owners, by the agreement of the Muslims. It must not be given to anyone other than its owner.

If he has received people’s yarn and that very yarn cannot be found, its owner may not take someone else’s property in compensation for his own. Rather, if he made a loan in this connection, it would remain the weaver’s personal liability. The same applies to any wages paid to him for work he has not completed, for that payment is

---

## Page 14835

a debt for which he remains personally liable. Scholars agree that his debts may not be settled using specific items belonging to other people. Anyone who produces evidence that a particular item is his own property may take it. If no one produces evidence, and the man is afraid and knows that what he is weaving belongs not to him but to other people, his debts must not be paid from that property. Nor may he be given some of the yarn merely on his claim, to the exclusion of others; rather, the creditors must be treated equitably.

If someone produces one witness and supports that witness’s testimony with his own oath, judgment is given in his favour. If the only means of identifying each person’s property consists of distinguishing marks, such as each person’s name on his belongings, those marks are to be relied upon. If none of these means is available, lots are drawn among the claimants. Whoever draws the lot for a particular item takes it after swearing an oath. The drawing of lots in such cases is established in an authentic report from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a man who owes a debt but cannot repay it, and who has been shown to have put whatever funds he has obtained towards repayment. His father is wealthy but has paid nothing on his behalf, and now wishes to take him along on Hajj. Will this discharge his obligatory Hajj, in view of

---

## Page 14836

the debt he owes and the fact that he has no means of performing Hajj except through his father?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, if his father takes him on Hajj at his own expense, that is permissible, and it discharges his obligatory Hajj by scholarly consensus.

Scholars disagree, however, over whether Hajj becomes obligatory for him if his father offers him the money. This disagreement is well known. His obligation is discharged whether his father gives him ownership of the money or pays his expenses and provides him with transport without transferring ownership to him.

If he owes a debt and his creditors permit him to travel for Hajj, there is no doubt that he may travel. If they prevent him from travelling so that he remains, works and repays them, they are entitled to do so. Remaining to earn money and repay his creditors would be more appropriate for him and a more binding duty than performing Hajj, and they would be entitled to prevent him from performing Hajj. But if they know that he is insolvent, they may not demand payment from him, nor may they prevent him from performing Hajj.

However, if the creditors say, “We fear that he may go on Hajj and not return, so we want him to provide a surety who will guarantee his appearance,” there are grounds for their demand: their rights remain outstanding, although he is unable to fulfil them.

If he were able to repay and the debt were due, they would undoubtedly be entitled to prevent him from travelling. Likewise, if the debt were deferred but would fall due before his return, they would be entitled to prevent him until he provided security in the form of collateral or a surety; in the former case, until he repaid or provided security. But if the debt would not fall due until after his return and the journey were safe, then concerning their right to prevent him from travelling,

---

## Page 14837

there are two well-known views, both reported from Ahmad.

If the journey is dangerous, as in jihad, they may prevent him from going if it has become personally obligatory for him. If the creditors are able to recover what they are owed, they may let him go, according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. Abū Hanīfa, however, is reported to have held that they may remain constantly at his side. This concerns his remaining in place; if the insolvent debtor wishes to … (1), there is disagreement on the matter. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man owes a debt and has lost his wealth. He has upright witnesses who testify to that loss, but they do not know whether he has acquired any wealth since then. Is his statement that he is insolvent to be accepted upon his oath, or must he produce evidence?

He replied:

If he says, “I have acquired nothing since losing my wealth,” his statement on this matter is accepted upon his oath, according to the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 14838

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked:

A miller holds a written record of a debt owed to him by another man, dating back more than ten years. He has recovered most of it and has stated that less than a hundred remains outstanding. The holder of this document then brought some of an emir’s slave soldiers, who seized two horses for him without any legally valid pledge, without bringing a claim before a judge, and without their owner’s permission. He used the horses for eight months, then claimed that they had been lost. May their owner demand payment for their use during this period, as well as their monetary value? Whose statement is to be accepted concerning their value: his, or their owner’s concerning the hire charge and price?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

If only a hundred remained due to him, and the two horses were worth more than a hundred dirhams, then the man who seized them is liable for the amount exceeding his entitlement. He must also pay for their use because he took possession of them.

The statement accepted concerning their value is that of the person liable—the wrongful seizer—unless he describes them and acknowledges that their value was greater than that, or evidence establishes their value—even the testimony of one witness together with the claimant’s oath. As for the portion corresponding to his entitlement, it is set off against the debt owed to him.

---

## Page 14839

He was also asked:

A man was in debt to another man with whom he had been trading in merchandise for seven years. The amount owed had reached five hundred dirhams, of which he had repaid three hundred. The creditor held a house as security and had brought him before the judge.

The debtor said, “Let him give me eight days, and I will repay him,” but the creditor would not grant him this respite. Is it permissible to imprison him?

He replied:

If a man has merchandise and asks for time to sell it and repay his creditor from the proceeds, he must be allowed the time needed for that. Likewise, if he can arrange to repay his debt by borrowing or some other means, and asks not to be placed under restraint until he has done so, his request must be granted. It is not permissible to prevent him from doing this by imprisoning him. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man owed debts to several people but was unable to pay the amount due. They all agreed to grant him a respite and have him released, on the understanding that he would trade with the money he still had left and repay them. They carried out this arrangement, except for one of them, who refused to do

---

## Page 14840

so after the group had reached an agreement with him. May he recover what he is owed ahead of the others, or not?

He replied:

Having agreed with the others to grant him respite, he may not recover his money immediately to their exclusion, according to the view that an extension granted on a debt already due is binding. This is the view of Mālik and one of Ahmad’s opinions.

As for the view that such an extension is not binding, held by al-Shāfiʿī and by Ahmad in one opinion, or the view that it is binding in transactions involving an exchange but not in gratuitous transactions, held by Abū Hanīfah and by Ahmad in one reported opinion: in the schools of Ahmad, Mālik and others, no distinction is made between his having agreed with them to defer payment until a specified date, to arrange payment in instalments, or to make such arrangements later. He has no right to betray or deceive them. Indeed, even supposing that the extension were not binding, they all share the right to recover payment from the funds remaining in the debtor’s possession.

He was also asked:

A man owes a debt and has been imprisoned for some time. He has no means other than what he earns through his own labour. Is it lawful for his creditors to beat or imprison him, or must they be patient with him and collect payment little by little, according to his earnings?

He replied:

It is not lawful to imprison or beat him under these circumstances. Rather, he must be allowed to work so that he can repay his debt to the extent possible. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14841

He was also asked:

A man owes a debt arising from a guarantee he has given and has no means of paying it except through his work. He wishes to go away as a mu'allam to earn some money, providing someone to guarantee his appearance. Is it permissible to imprison him, or should he be allowed to do this? If the creditor claims that he can meet his obligation under the guarantee, while he claims that he cannot, is the creditor’s statement to be accepted? Must he produce evidence?

He replied:

Rather, he must be allowed to repay the debt by whatever means are available to him, and he may not be imprisoned if he does so. If he claims insolvency but is known to have possessed property, his claim is not accepted without evidence. If he is not known to have possessed property, his statement, supported by his oath, is accepted over that of his creditor. This is the position of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad. Mālik’s position is similar, and a number of scholars have also reported this position from Abū Hanīfah.

---

## Page 14842

He was also asked:

A man owes a debt, and a claim has been brought against him before the judge, who has placed him under guard. He says, “I will remain under guard until I sell my property and repay the debt.” His creditor, however, says, “You must be imprisoned.” Is it permissible to imprison him, or must his creditor remain with him until he sells his property and repays his debt?

He replied:

Rather, if he asks to be allowed to sell enough property to repay his debt, he must be allowed to do so. Imprisonment that would prevent him from doing so is not required. The Muslims are agreed on this. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

An insolvent man has a family to support and fears that his creditor will have him detained, leaving him and his family destitute. He intends to repay the debt if Allah grants him sufficient means. If, at such a moment, he denies the debt and swears an oath to that effect, is he sinful or not?

He replied:

It is not lawful for him to deny his creditor’s right or to swear that he owes him nothing. Rather, he must acknowledge his creditor’s right, explain his insolvency, and seek Allah Most High’s forgiveness.

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him”

“and provide for him from where he does not expect.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14843

He was also asked:

A man owns a slave whom an oppressive man wishes to buy. Fearing that the prospective buyer would not pay him, he said, “Whenever I sell this slave, his price is forbidden to me.” The owner owes a debt. May he sell the slave and use the proceeds to repay it?

He replied:

Yes, he may sell him and pay people what he owes them.

If, by saying, “His price is forbidden to me,” he meant that he would not retain the proceeds but would instead pay his creditors, nothing is required of him. If, however, he intended to declare the proceeds forbidden to himself, one view is that he must offer expiation for an oath, as held by Ahmad and Abū Hanīfa. Another view is that nothing is required of him, as held by Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man owes debts and owns property in al-Qarāfa. He sold half of it under a repurchase arrangement and has testimony establishing this. The purchaser publicized it every month, through his efforts and a half, from the date of the sale. Another creditor lodged a complaint against him and used force to pressure him until he took the remainder of the proceeds of the first sale, while the property remained in the second creditor’s possession. What is the ruling?

---

## Page 14844

He replied:

As for a sale with a repurchase agreement—whereby the parties agree that, whenever the seller brings back the price, the purchaser will restore his property to him, while the purchaser benefits from it by letting it, living in it, or otherwise—such a sale is invalid by scholarly consensus if the condition forms part of the contract.

If the condition was stipulated before the contract and its validity is disputed, the correct view is that the sale is invalid in all cases. The parties’ sole purpose is to engage in usury by advancing dirhams for a fixed term, with the benefit obtained in return for those dirhams constituting the profit. The property sold must be returned to its owner, the seller, and the seller must repay the purchaser what he received from him. However, any money the purchaser received under the name of rent must be deducted from the amount the seller owes him. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

An enslaved man buys and sells for his master, and his master buys and sells in the slave’s name. The master has incurred a debt. Should repayment be sought from the slave or from his owner?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The debt is the master’s responsibility and must be paid from his wealth. Whatever the slave holds belongs to the master and must be used to repay his debt. The slave may also be sold to repay his master’s debt. If the slave conceals any of his master’s wealth that is in his possession, he is to be punished until he discloses it, so that the debt may be repaid from it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14845

He was asked:

A man owed a debt, and his creditor had him imprisoned. The debtor was poor and had no wealth, but had become entitled to the benefits of an orchard through a lawful endowment. Its yield was insufficient to repay the debt during the stated period up to the time of repayment.

He replied:

If he is insolvent, he may neither be imprisoned nor pressed for payment. Rather, he must be granted respite until he has the means to pay.

If he has nothing with which to repay the debt except the benefits of the endowment established for him, the creditor should recover as much as possible from the rental income of the endowed property. If he is found to possess other wealth, the creditor should recover from it whatever is possible. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

If someone owes an obligation but refuses to fulfill it, must his acknowledgment be obtained through punishment?

He replied:

The ruling of Islamic law is that anyone who owes an obligation, is capable of fulfilling it, but refuses to do so is to be punished by beating and imprisonment, repeatedly, until

---

## Page 14846

he fulfills it, whether the obligation concerns a debt he owes, a deposit entrusted to him, property he has usurped, a borrowed item, property belonging to the Muslims, or an act he must perform, such as a wife allowing her husband intimacy with her, or a hired worker providing the services required of him.

This is established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Allah Most High says:

“As for those women whose disobedience you fear, admonish them, forsake them in bed, and strike them.”

Thus Allah, glorified be He, has permitted a man to strike his wife if she refuses to fulfill her binding obligation regarding intimacy and the marital bed. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Delay in payment by one who can afford to pay is injustice.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded this in their Sahīh collections. He also said:

“Delaying payment when one has the means makes it permissible to speak ill of him and punish him.”

This was reported by the compilers of the Sunan collections. Here, delay means postponing payment, and having the means means being able to pay. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus stated that delay in payment by one who can afford to pay is injustice, and that this makes it permissible to speak ill of him and punish him. It is therefore established that punishing someone who delays payment is permissible.

Al-Bukhārī narrated in his Sahīh:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made peace with the people of Khaybar in return for their gold, silver, and weapons. He asked Huyayy ibn Akhtab’s uncle about his treasure. He replied, ‘O Muhammad, expenses have consumed it.’ He then said to al-Zubayr, ‘Take charge of this man.’ Al-Zubayr took him and subjected him to some torture. He then said, ‘I saw him going to these ruins.’ It was in an oxhide.”

When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, learned that this man, who knew the whereabouts of the wealth to which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was entitled,

---

## Page 14847

had concealed it, he ordered al-Zubayr to punish him until he showed them where the wealth was. Someone who conceals his own wealth is even more deserving of punishment. Jurists among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others have discussed this issue, and I know of no disagreement concerning it. They have stated that if someone persists in refusing to discharge an obligation, whether a debt or otherwise, he is to be punished and beaten repeatedly until he fulfills it. The beating is not to be administered only once; rather, it is to be spread over several days until he fulfills his obligation.

The scholars have unanimously agreed that discretionary punishment is prescribed for every sin for which neither a prescribed fixed punishment nor an expiation is stipulated. Sins fall into two categories: neglecting an obligation and committing a prohibited act. Whoever fails to fulfill an obligation despite being able to do so is therefore sinful and deserves punishment and disciplinary measures. Allah, glorified be He, knows best.

He was asked:

A man brought witnesses to his home and said, “Bear witness that my daughter, so-and-so, is of sound judgment, is legally competent to manage her affairs, and is under no legal restriction.” She was married and had children. He made this declaration in the presence of her husband, one of her brothers, and her mother, repeating it several times.

When his witnesses left, her brother told them, “Do not testify that she is of sound judgment.” Then, several days later, her father and brother came, and her father said, “I have withdrawn my declaration that she is of sound judgment.”

---

## Page 14848

Is that, then, valid or not? Is he entitled to withdraw his declaration without any basis in Islamic law?

He replied:

The witnesses must not heed her brother’s words, or anyone else’s, and refrain from testifying. Rather, they must give their testimony for Allah’s sake, as Allah and His Messenger have commanded. Her father has no right to revoke his declaration of her sound judgment. Even if it is established that she subsequently developed imprudence warranting legal restriction, imposing that restriction is not her father’s prerogative, but that of the governing authority. Even if her father had never acknowledged her sound judgment, the restriction upon her ceases once she attains sound judgment, whether or not he declares her to have attained it, and whether or not a judge issues a ruling to that effect. If her sound judgment is disputed and two witnesses testify that she possesses it, their testimony is accepted, and no heed is paid to her father or anyone else.

If she has conducted transactions over a period of time, and a witness testifies that she possessed sound judgment during that period, her transactions are valid, even if her father claims that she was under his legal restriction. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A woman is under legal restriction, and upright witnesses who are not among her unmarriageable relatives have testified to her sound judgment. Is their testimony accepted or not?

He replied:

Yes. If upright witnesses testify to her sound judgment, a ruling recognizing it is issued, even if they are not her relatives. Uprightness, sound judgment, and similar qualities may be known through widespread knowledge, just as Muslims know of the sound judgment of the Mothers of the Believers and other well-known women. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14849

He was asked:

A man has a widowed daughter whose marriage contract he concluded, declaring before the witnesses that she possessed sound judgment. Once his daughter was certain of this, she chose to remain under her father’s legal restriction and declined to be treated as a person of sound judgment. Is her father entitled to revoke the declaration of her sound judgment, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Once she possesses sound judgment, she cannot remain under legal restriction. However, she may choose not to transact with her property except with her father’s permission. If she says, “I will not transact except with my father’s permission,” she may do so, provided that the transaction is not obligatory upon her.

He was asked:

A man died leaving a son and two daughters who had not been declared to possess sound judgment. One of the daughters married and authorized her husband to receive her share of her father’s estate and transact with it on her behalf. Does the aforementioned brother have guardianship over her? May the husband be called to account for what he has received and what he has spent in the orphan girl’s interests?

---

## Page 14850

He replied:

The brother has authority over her with regard to enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong. If she does something unlawful, he must forbid her from doing it. As for placing her under legal restriction if she lacks sound judgment, her appointed guardian may do so, if she has one. Otherwise, the judge places her under legal restriction, and her brother may bring her case before the judge.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man has a seven-year-old son. Another man put the boy on a riding animal without his father’s permission or knowledge. The animal kicked the child, threw him off, and ran away from him. The man then lodged a complaint against the child’s father and had a document drawn up against him alleging wrongful appropriation of an animal of this kind. Is the father liable for anything?

Answer

He replied:

If the father played no part in causing this incident, he bears no liability. Nor does the document drawn up against him under duress impose any obligation upon him, for it was the animal’s owner who instructed the child to ride it, without any involvement on the father’s part.

---

## Page 14851

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Someone purchased something from the public treasury on behalf of an orphan, on terms advantageous to the treasury, but with no apparent advantage to the orphan. Is the purchase valid? Is the appointed guardian liable for the excess paid?

He replied:

If he purchased it for the orphan at the prevailing market price, or at a higher price in the orphan’s interests, that is permissible. But if he paid an excess beyond the customary margin of price variation, he is liable for the excessive amount paid. The advantage to the public treasury has no bearing on the ruling, for this is an outward form with no underlying reality. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man imprisoned in the ruler’s jail feared for his life and was being pressed to repay a debt due under Islamic law. While imprisoned, he had witnesses attest to his acknowledgment that all the real estate he owned belonged to his wife, and she confirmed his statement. Is this acknowledgment valid and effective with respect to all his property, or does it apply only to one-third, with the remaining two-thirds subject to the heirs’ approval, or not? And if he has a young daughter

---

## Page 14852

who is poor, is her maintenance to be paid from the income of this real estate under these circumstances?

He replied:

If he has obligations under Islamic law and gives away his property, leaving those entitled to payment with nothing from which to recover their dues, this transfer is invalid according to one of the two scholarly opinions. This is the position of Mālik and of Imam Ahmad according to one of the two reported opinions from him. The reason is that repaying debts and maintaining one’s children are obligatory. It is therefore forbidden for him to neglect an obligation and devote the funds to something that is not obligatory. He must revoke this transfer and apply the property to his obligations of repaying his debts and maintaining his child.

If, however, the property rightfully belongs to someone else, or includes something to which another person is entitled, it cannot validly be disposed of on that person’s behalf except by virtue of guardianship or agency. If the witnessed declaration concerned his transfer to his wife of what he owned, it does not include anything he did not own.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man has been appointed guardian over the property of orphans, but he is not competent for the task. What is the ruling on his guardianship and his remuneration?

He replied:

No one may be appointed guardian over the property of orphans unless he is capable, knowledgeable about what has been entrusted to him, and trustworthy in handling it.

---

## Page 14853

If the guardian does not possess these qualities, he must be replaced by someone suitable. He is not entitled to the stipulated remuneration. However, if he performs work for the orphans that warrants customary remuneration, it is treated like work performed under other invalid contracts.

He was asked:

A man married his daughter to someone, and she has lived with her husband for years. Her father then came to request something to meet her needs, but the husband said, “I am under legal interdiction, and what is recorded in the document is subject to that interdiction.”

He replied:

His mere assertion that he is under legal interdiction is not accepted. Rather, the presumption is that his transactions are valid and that he is not under interdiction, unless the interdiction is established. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman and had a child by her. Her father wanted to take control of her property and use it for his own benefit, but she prevented him. He then claimed that she was under legal interdiction. Is this claim of his to be accepted when she has displayed no financial imprudence warranting interdiction? May she prevent him from disposing of her property?

---

## Page 14854

He replied:

Her father has no right to manage her property for his own benefit. Indeed, if he does so, this impairs his fitness to act as her guardian, and he must be barred from exercising guardianship over her, including placing her under legal interdiction. If, however, he is fit to act as her guardian and manages her property solely for her benefit, not his own, his guardianship over her is conditional upon her continued financial imprudence. Once she develops sound financial judgment, her interdiction ends regardless of his wishes. If she produces evidence of her sound financial judgment, a ruling must be issued terminating his guardianship over her. She is entitled to require him to swear that he does not know her to possess sound financial judgment, if he seeks this without producing evidence. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A woman claimed that she was under legal interdiction, although her husband had not known this. He then divorced her, and she released him from his financial obligation to her. She subsequently married another man, after which a claim was brought against the first husband for the dowry on the grounds that she was under legal interdiction. Is this claim to be accepted?

He—may Allah have mercy on him—replied:

The claim is not accepted merely on her assertion that she is under legal interdiction. Rather, if she conducts her affairs as a person of sound financial judgment would, then she possesses sound financial judgment and her sales are legally effective, even if she is under legal interdiction. If she produces evidence that she possesses sound financial judgment, her voluntary waiver is fully effective. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14855

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man acknowledged owing money to some orphans and provided a written acknowledgment. When one of the orphans demanded payment, he denied the claim before the judge and swore that he owed him nothing. Later, he asked the orphan to release him from the debt while he was ill. Is such a release valid while he is ill?

He replied:

This release is not valid in reality so long as the defendant continues to deny the rightful claim.

He was asked:

A man entrusted an orphan’s money to an investment manager to purchase fruit under a profit-sharing arrangement, with another man acting as a trusted overseer. The orphan was to receive half the profits, and each of the two men a quarter. The capital was lost, and the investment manager had undertaken the work alone because the other man was unable to participate. The partnership had been established after the fruit had been pollinated. Some issued a ruling that it was invalid and that the investment manager and the orphan’s guardian were liable for the orphan’s money that had been spent. What is the ruling?

He replied:

There is disagreement over the validity of this partnership, but the stronger view is that it is valid. Whether it was valid or invalid, if the orphan’s guardian was negligent in what

---

## Page 14856

he did, he is liable. However, if he acted in a way that appeared to serve the orphan’s interests, he is not liable for wrongdoing by his investment manager. As for the investment manager, if he acted dishonestly or negligently, he is liable; otherwise, he is not. Even if the contract was invalid, whatever entails liability under a valid contract entails liability under an invalid one, and whatever does not entail liability under a valid contract does not entail liability under an invalid one. Each of them must swear an oath denying wrongdoing and negligence.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Some orphans were under lawful guardianship. The Tatars captured them in the year of Shaqhab while they were still children. Some people approached the authorities to instigate the seizure of their property. They had a blood relative entitled to inherit, as well as agnatic heirs. When the heirs learned of this, they had a formal record established confirming that they were the orphans’ heirs in the event of their death. Is it lawful for anyone who knows this to seek to take the property? And is one to wait on account of their absence? Does someone who takes it, knowing this, incur sin?

He replied:

No one other than the heirs is entitled to take this property. However, it is to be used to meet expenses incumbent upon its owner, such as maintenance for his child, and to settle his debts.

If he is judicially declared dead, the property belongs to his heir. Scholars disagree over how long they must wait: some prescribe a fixed period, while others say that they should defer to the judge in this matter.

---

## Page 14857

And any of them who speaks on this matter in order to divert the property to someone not entitled to it is sinful in doing so, by agreement of the imams. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man has an orphan in his care whose property he holds. He has spared the orphan’s property the cost of his upkeep and supports him from his own funds. May he invest the orphan’s property in trade or purchase real estate to increase and develop it without the judge’s permission?

He replied:

Yes, he may do so; indeed, he ought to. He does not need the judge’s permission if he is an appointed guardian. If he is not an appointed guardian, and the person overseeing orphans’ property is a knowledgeable and just judge who safeguards it and directs that it be managed in their best interests, he must seek his permission to do this.

If seeking his permission would lead to the property being lost—for example, if the judge or his deputy is corrupt, ignorant, incapable, or fails to safeguard orphans’ property—then the person holding it is to safeguard it and manage it in the orphan’s best interests without seeking the judge’s permission.

---

## Page 14858

He was asked:

A man died—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—leaving three children and a property. One of his sons was an adult. He demolished part of the property and rebuilt it, married, and had children there, while the other heirs remained inactive. When they requested a division of the estate, he intended to demolish the building. What is the ruling?

He replied:

Their rights to the land remain intact. As for the building, if he built it entirely with his own funds, rather than from the original property, he may remove it. However, he must compensate them for the original building that belonged to them. If he rebuilt it using the original inheritance, it belongs to them.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Regarding dressing boys in silk on festivals and other occasions: may an orphan’s guardian dress him in silk or not? If he does so, is he sinful or not? Likewise, is gilding their caps permissible or not?

---

## Page 14859

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. According to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions, an orphan’s guardian may not dress him in silk, just as he may not give him wine to drink or carrion to eat. Whatever is forbidden to adult men, the guardian must keep boys away from it.

ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb tore some silk that he saw Ibn al-Zubayr wearing and said, “Do not dress them in silk.” The same applies to gold that men are forbidden to wear. As for accusations that the guardian is miserly, these can be countered by dressing the orphan in permissible clothing suitable for looking well-dressed on festivals and other occasions, such as Alexandrian garments and other clothing that provides elegance and adornment and dispels accusations of miserliness without involving anything forbidden.

Anyone to whom the truth has become clear may not turn away from it to something else. No one is obliged to follow anyone other than the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in everything he commands, forbids, permits, and prohibits. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An executor owns properties, and his guardian is in the lands of the Tatars. He has sold his properties to a purchaser on his advice, without a public announcement, witnesses, or a ruling from anyone. Is the sale valid?

He replied:

If he sold before attaining sound judgment, his sale is invalid, especially if he had

---

## Page 14860

sold at a grossly inadequate price. If the purchaser claims that he possessed sound judgment, but evidence establishes his financial imprudence, the sale is to be ruled invalid. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

An emir conducts transactions with people and relies on his accounts. If an amount is overlooked and not recorded, does he remain liable for it, even though he has not established that he owes anything but relies on his agent’s ledger?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If he has exercised due care in appointing his clerk, who is trustworthy, experienced, and diligent in safeguarding people’s funds, he bears no liability. For Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. But if he has been negligent in appointing the clerk, employing someone dishonest or incapable, he is liable for whatever is lost of people’s entitlements through his negligence. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14861

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

What is the ruling concerning agents administering farming villages who are allotted a certain quantity of wheat and other commodities for every faddān of land, whose upkeep is borne by the farmers, and to whom the soldiers also owe a fixed sum in dirhams?

He replied:

If the agent takes for himself only the customary payment for his work, while the holder of the revenue grant takes the excess, then it is the grant-holder who has wronged the farmers. As for the person who takes payment for his work, he has taken what he is entitled to, so it is not unlawful for him.

If the agent has paid the grant-holder, out of the levy, an amount exceeding his own customary wage, and has taken for himself only payment for his work, that is permissible. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14862

Chapter on Agency

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man appointed another man to collect debts owed to him, then dismissed him and demanded from him the outstanding balance. The agent who had handled these affairs then drew up a mutual release between himself and the debtor without the principal’s authorization. Is the discharge valid?

He replied:

If the terms of his appointment do not establish that he was authorized to discharge debts, his discharge of a debt owed to the principal is not valid. If, however, he acknowledges that a discharge was granted, his acknowledgment is accepted concerning matters within the scope of his agency—as when someone appointed to receive payment acknowledges having received it. Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man appoints a broker to purchase an item for him. The broker purchases it for him and takes a fee from the seller for the sale to him at that price. What is the ruling?

---

## Page 14863

He replied:

This is not permissible, because he purchases it for his principal for more than it is worth. When the purchase is based on disclosure of the cost price, the seller adds to the customary profit, thereby deceiving the principal. This applies when there is collusion with the seller, or he is known to do this. If, however, the seller gives him that amount as a gift without his having known about it beforehand, that case is discussed elsewhere.

He was asked:

About an agent who leased out his principal’s land for less than his partnership share?

He replied:

If he leases it out for half the market rent, the agent is liable for the shortfall. Whether the owner may annul the lease is a matter of disagreement among the scholars.

He was asked:

A group of soldiers hired an agent to manage their land grant and instructed him to go out to that land and register leases at its rental value. The agent colluded with his associates and agreed to the farmers’ terms, registering leases below the customary rental value. Are his dealings in matters for which he was not authorized permissible on the strength of the legally valid power of attorney he holds?

---

## Page 14864

He replied:

If he leases the land for less than the market rent and hands it over to them, he is acting wrongfully and exceeding his authority. The landowners may hold him liable for the full market rent, because he handed their land over to others to benefit from it. As for the tenants, if they knew that he was acting wrongfully and giving them preferential terms, the landowners may also hold them liable if they have already benefited from the land. If they have not yet planted it, the owners may prevent them from doing so, for the lease in that case is invalid.

If, however, they were unaware, but the lessor had informed them, may the landowners hold them liable? The scholars hold two views on this. If the owners do hold them liable, may they seek reimbursement from the one who deceived them for amounts they were not contractually bound to pay? There are likewise two views on this. The third view is found within Ahmad’s school and among other scholars.

He was asked—may Allah be pleased with him:

About a woman who appointed her brother as her agent to claim all her rights, bring proceedings on her behalf, and annul her marriage to her husband. This authorization was established before the judge. The agent then brought a claim before that judge against her husband for his principal’s maintenance and clothing. The husband admitted that he was unable to provide these. Some time passed, during which the agent brought him before the judge repeatedly, and he continued to acknowledge his inability. The agent therefore asked the judge to authorize him to annul his principal’s marriage to her husband. The judge authorized him to do so, and the agent then annulled

---

## Page 14865

his principal’s marriage to the aforementioned husband in the husband’s presence, after he had been granted the legally prescribed period of respite before the annulment. Is the annulment valid, and does the marriage end under these circumstances by virtue of the judge’s authorization of the agent to annul his principal’s marriage? Or is a judicial ruling confirming the validity of the annulment required?

He replied:

If the agent authorized to annul the marriage does so after the judge has empowered him to proceed, his annulment is valid. No subsequent judicial ruling confirming its validity is required according to the schools of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others.

However, if the judge himself performs a disputed legal act, whether concluding or annulling a contract—such as concluding a marriage without a guardian or purchasing an item not present on behalf of an orphan—and the matter is then brought before another judge who does not consider that act valid, may the latter overturn it before a ruling has been issued confirming it? Or does the judge’s act itself constitute a judicial ruling? There are two views within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

Annulment on grounds of insolvency is permissible according to the schools of all three imams. Here, the judge is not himself annulling the marriage; rather, he authorizes the annulment and rules that it is permissible. This is comparable to his ruling that a man is entitled to an inheritance and authorizing him to dispose of it; or ruling that a man is a marriage guardian and authorizing him to conclude the marriage contract; or ruling that a purchaser is entitled to rescind a sale because of a defect or a similar reason. Thus, whenever a person has been adjudged entitled to conclude or annul a contract, his doing so is valid, without dispute in cases of this kind. The disagreement concerns only cases in which the judge himself concludes or annuls the contract. Nevertheless,

---

## Page 14866

the correct view is that his conclusion or annulment of a contract does not require a judicial ruling confirming it. All of this would apply if such a matter were brought before a Hanafi judge who does not recognize annulment on grounds of insolvency. If, however, the second judge recognizes it—as would someone who follows the school of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, or Imam Ahmad—he may not overturn this annulment, by agreement of the imams.

The scholars who stipulated that annulment of a marriage on grounds of a defect, insolvency, or similar disputed grounds must be authorized by a judicial ruling distinguished this from a woman’s annulment of her marriage to a slave upon her emancipation. They explained:

“This latter annulment is unanimously accepted and therefore does not require a judge, whereas the former is disputed. Its grounds, too, are subject to juristic judgment, unlike emancipation, which is a clear and ascertainable ground.”

Thus, they required that the annulment be authorized by a judicial ruling; they did not require the judge to rule on its validity after it had taken place. Such a subsequent ruling is not peculiar to these cases. Rather, whenever a judge rules that a disputed legal act is valid, no other judge may overturn it, provided it does not contravene a scriptural text or scholarly consensus.

If what mattered here were a ruling issued after the annulment, there would be no need for a judicial ruling at the outset. Rather, anyone entitled to annul the marriage could do so, and the judge’s subsequent ruling would prevent another judge from invalidating the annulment—just as when someone concludes a contract whose validity is disputed and a judge rules that it is valid. This is clear to anyone familiar with what the jurists have said on this matter. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14867

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man appointed another man as his agent to manage his land grant at Bayt. The agent leased out its land at a fair rate according to local custom, seeking his principal’s benefit and best interests. The cultivators, however, conspired to deceive the agent, since he was a stranger to the district and lacked experience and knowledge of its conditions and characteristics. They obtained leases from him at less than the customary fair rate received by the other grant-holders in the district, whose lands were on an equal footing, without any great disparity or gross inequity. May the grant-holder demand rent from the cultivators at the fair rate, on the same basis as his fellow grant-holders in the district?

He replied:

If he appointed him to lease it out under an unrestricted agency, or told him, “Lease it out on the same terms as comparable land,” the ruling is the same whether the agency was unrestricted or expressly qualified in this way: the agent may lease it out only at the fair rental value applicable to comparable land. If the agent was negligent, leasing it for less than the customary rent and handing the land over to the tenant, the principal may demand that the agent make up the shortfall.

If the person taking the lease told the agent, “This rent is in line with what others pay,” but it subsequently became clear

---

## Page 14868

that he had lied, the full rent may then be claimed from the tenant if he has cultivated the land. Either the agent or the principal may make this claim. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man appointed an agent to sell a house, receive the price, hand over possession, draw up the documents, and have witnesses attest the transaction according to customary procedure. The agent sold the house to someone and received the price. The sale was established, and a judge ruled that it was valid. The house remained in the purchaser’s possession for a time; he then made it an endowment, testimony was given, and a judge ruled that the endowment was valid. The house remained in the purchaser’s possession for three years altogether, before and after its endowment. The selling agent’s principal knew of all this and raised no objection.

After this period, however, the principal claimed that he had dismissed the agent before the sale took place, without the agent knowing. He produced evidence of this in another town, and a judge ruled on that evidence without any claim having been brought against the purchaser, his agent, or anyone holding any of the income from the property in use.

Is this judgment valid, and does it invalidate the sale? Must the purchaser pay the fair rental value, or was his use based on a semblance of entitlement? Must the selling agent refund the price? If the selling agent produces evidence that his principal received the price, does that constitute the principal’s consent? Does the principal commit flagrant sin by claiming to have dismissed the agent after three years, despite having remained silent, misled the purchaser, and received the price?

---

## Page 14869

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

This question depends, first of all, on the dismissal of an agent: does his dismissal take effect before he receives notice of it? Scholars hold two well-known views on this.

The first is that his dismissal does not take effect until he learns of it. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him, as well as a view held by al-Shāfiʿī and one of the two positions in Mālik’s school—indeed, the stronger of the two. According to this view, the agent’s transactions before learning of his dismissal are valid and legally effective. Proof that he was dismissed before the transaction does not undermine a transaction he conducted before learning of that dismissal. Thus, the sale and the endowment, if undertaken in accordance with Islamic law, are valid; neither they nor the judge’s ruling affirming them are invalidated.

The second view is that his dismissal takes effect before he learns of it. This is the well-known position in al-Shāfiʿī’s school, Ahmad’s position according to one of the two reports from him, and Mālik’s position according to one of the two reports from him.

According to this view, the principal’s mere assertion, after the transaction, that he had dismissed the agent is not accepted. If he produces evidence of this in another town and a judge rules on that basis, this constitutes a judgment against an absent party. Such a judgment, if deemed valid, is valid with everyone retaining the right to present their evidence and arguments. The party against whom judgment was given may challenge the judgment and the testimony on any admissible grounds, whether by challenging the witnesses, the judgment, or otherwise. An example would be that the judge who ruled on the dismissal does not consider dismissal effective before the agent learns of it. Nor is he obliged to accept testimony attesting to the dismissal from witnesses who are flagrantly sinful or subject to a suspicion that renders their testimony inadmissible.

Furthermore, if the judge who ruled that the sale and endowment were valid holds that an agent’s dismissal does not take effect before he learns of it, and had been informed of this, then his judgment is legally effective, and it is not permissible to

---

## Page 14870

overturn it under any circumstances; rather, any judgment overturning it must be rejected. If, however, he was unaware of this, or his position is not to uphold validity if the dismissal is established, his judgment has no legal effect.

Likewise, if the second judge was unaware that the dismissal preceded notification, or knew this but does not consider such a dismissal effective, or considers it effective but does not hold that the earlier judgment may be overturned, his judgment has no legal effect.

The principal’s reported knowledge of what his selling agent had done and his silence, the securing of a judicial ruling in the case, and his receipt of the price from the agent ordinarily indicate authorization to buy and sell and the continuation of the agency, unless outweighed by stronger evidence to the contrary.
