Most scholars accept evidence of this kind and use it to reject the principal’s claim that he dismissed the agent, made in order to invalidate the sale—especially given the prevalence of false witnesses.

Even if the endowment were ruled invalid, neither the agent nor the buyer would be liable to compensate for any use enjoyed. If the agent and buyer were misled by the principal through his failure to give notice of the dismissal, the negligence was on his part, and no compensation for that use is owed to him.

If the principal denies receiving the price and no evidence is produced against him establishing receipt, then, if the agent acted without remuneration, his word is accepted against the principal, because he was entrusted by him—just as a depositary’s word is accepted concerning the return of a deposit to its owner. If he acted for remuneration, scholars hold two well-known views on the matter. The agent’s word, however, is not accepted against the buyer. If the sale remains in force,

---

## Page 14871

there is nothing further to discuss. If, however, the sale has been rescinded, they may demand repayment of the price from the agent, who may in turn recover it from the principal.

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An agent sold his principal’s share in a shop on his behalf. The buyer then made that share an endowment. Both the sale and the endowment were established, and the endowment was judicially ruled valid. Subsequently, it was established that the agent had been dismissed before the date of the sale, and his dismissal was judicially upheld. Does this establish that the sale and the endowment were invalid, or are both valid? If they prove invalid, is the principal entitled to claim rent for that share for the period it remained in the possession of the buyer who endowed it, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

There is a well-known disagreement concerning this issue: if an agent’s principal dies or dismisses him, and the agent acts before learning of this, does his agency terminate before he learns of it?

Scholars in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Imam Ahmad, and others hold three views:

The first is that his agency terminates before he learns of it. This is the well-known position in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others, and one view in the school of Mālik. According to this view, the sale proves to have been invalid. Nevertheless, the agent bears no liability, because he was not negligent. As for the buyer, he is

---

## Page 14872

also misled if he was unaware. Whether he is to be held liable is disputed in the school of Ahmad and others; this is also one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two views. The person who misled him bears no liability, nor is either of them held liable, and he has no recourse against the person who misled him, according to the better-known of al-Shāfiʿī’s two views and one report from Imam Ahmad. On this basis, the buyer is liable and has no recourse against anyone.

The second view:

His agency terminates upon the principal’s death, but dismissal does not terminate it until he learns of it. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa, the well-known position in Mālik’s school, and one of the views in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Imam Ahmad. Accordingly, his transactions before learning of his dismissal are valid. Thus, the sale is valid if the man was unaware of his dismissal. If, however, he acted after learning of his dismissal, his transaction is not binding, by agreement of the Muslims. Rather, it is treated as a transaction undertaken by an unauthorized person. Such a transaction is rejected in the school of al-Shāfiʿī and according to the well-known position reported from Imam Ahmad. In the school of Abū Hanīfa and according to the other report from Imam Ahmad, its validity is suspended pending ratification; this has also been related from Mālik. If the entitled party does not ratify it, it is void by unanimous agreement.

The third view:

His agency does not terminate in either case before he learns of it. This accords with a view held by al-Shāfiʿī and Imam Ahmad—may Allah be pleased with them both. The ruling in this case is as stated above. And Allah, glorified and exalted be He, knows best.

---

## Page 14873

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

A man appointed someone to sell some goods on his behalf. The agent sold them on deferred payment, and part of the price became irrecoverable. May the owner demand their value payable immediately, or an amount equal to the deferred price, which is higher?

He answered:

If the owner had not authorized him to sell on deferred payment, the owner may choose between demanding that the seller pay their value immediately in cash and claiming the entire deferred price, with any shortfall borne by the owner of the goods. This is because acting without authorization is like the conduct of a usurper. If property is lost while in a usurper’s possession, leaving a substitute in its place, the owner may choose, in pursuing his claim, between compensation in general—an equivalent item or its value—and the particular substitute received.

This applies where the buyer was unaware of the usurpation, so that he is liable only for the stipulated price. If we adopt the view that contracts remain suspended pending ratification, then, if ratification has not been established but the two parties reach a settlement on the price and mutually agree to it, the liability for the lost property may validly be settled for an amount exceeding its value. This is comparable to their agreeing to fix the dower in a marriage in which it was left unspecified.

---

## Page 14874

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

Amirs obtain the cloth and other goods they need from the markets, taking whatever pleases them. The amir gives the owner a written acknowledgment, or he and his deputies enter it in his ledger. They also borrow dirhams from their associates. All this takes place without formal documents being drawn up or witnesses being called; such is their customary practice. If an amir dies and his administrative staff and his ustādār know of the sums owed to people, may they withhold payment or delay it? Or, in these circumstances, must they pay from his estate the sums they know are owed?

He answered:

Rather, whatever is found in the amir’s handwriting, reported by his scribe, or stated orally by his agent in these matters—such as his scribe or his ustādār—must be acted upon. An agent’s acknowledgment on behalf of his principal concerning matters entrusted to him is accepted, because he is the principal’s trusted representative. The deceased’s handwriting has the same standing as his spoken words in bequests, acknowledgments, and similar matters.

With such evidence, those to whom these sums are owed need not furnish further proof. Requiring them to furnish proof deprives people of their rights and subjects the deceased to torment by leaving them held in bondage to their sins. This wrongs the dead

---

## Page 14875

and the living, particularly in transactions for which calling witnesses is not customary. Requiring proof in such cases departs from recognized standards of justice. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man acts on behalf of an amir in collecting his money. Is he entitled to the established agency fee of one tenth of what he collects—one hundred dirhams for every thousand? May he take this during the amir’s lifetime and after his death, with or without his permission?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If the amir appointed him as his agent in return for one tenth, or appointed him without specifying terms in the customary manner that conventionally entitles him to one tenth, then he is entitled to it. His entitlement to one tenth rests on either an express verbal stipulation or one established by custom.

Such hiring is comparable to leasing land for cultivation in return for a share of its crop. This is the issue known as “qafīz al-tahhān” (the miller’s measure). Anyone who attributes to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the report that “he forbade qafīz al-tahhān” is mistaken. Collecting money in return for an undivided share of it is permissible according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions.

If he worked for him on the understanding that he would receive compensation, but its amount was not specified, he is likewise entitled to the customary fee for comparable work. If anything is due to him from the amir, he may

---

## Page 14876

recover it from his estate without restriction and without his permission. If he has no entitlement to it, however, he may not take anything without his permission. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man appointed another man as his agent with unrestricted authority, on the understanding that he would act only in his best interests and to his advantage. The agent then leased out land whose rental value was ten thousand for five thousand. Is this lease valid or not? If it is valid, is the agent liable for the difference?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The agent has no right to lease it out at such a disadvantageous rate. The owner may hold the negligent agent liable for the loss he has caused him.

As for the validity of the lease, most jurists consider it void. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and, according to one of his two opinions, Ahmad. However, if the tenant was misled and did not know the agent’s true position—for example, if he thought that he was the owner and knew the property’s value—then, according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions, he may recover whatever he becomes liable for from the person who misled him.

His crop is legally protected and may not be cut down without compensation; rather, it is left in place subject to the customary rent, with a disparity beyond what people ordinarily tolerate. Here he is acting wrongfully, and his crop is treated as that of a usurper. May the owner uproot it without compensation? There are two well-known scholarly opinions on this. And may he acquire ownership of it

---

## Page 14877

by paying its cultivation costs? There are two opinions on this as well. The apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that he may acquire it by paying its cultivation costs. As for leaving it in place at the customary rent, he is entitled to do so by agreement. If he alleges that the tenant knew the circumstances, but the tenant denies this, the tenant’s word is accepted upon his oath.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man authorized his slave to lease a shop to someone, and the tenant then sublet it to another person. May the agent accept an increase in the shop’s rent or not? May he demand payment from the second tenant or not? If rent is wrongfully taken from the second tenant, may the tenant recover it from the person who took it or not?

If this slave acts on the principal’s behalf by leasing out his shops, collecting rent, and bringing claims concerning these matters before judges on his principal’s behalf, while his master knows of all this and approves it, is the master’s assertion that he never authorized him to be accepted? If the principal compels the second tenant to enter into a lease different from the first, is this second lease valid or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. In these circumstances, the principal has no right to lease the shop to anyone, whether at a higher rent or otherwise; nor does the first tenant have that right. The principal has no right to demand payment from the second tenant. If he takes the rent from him

---

## Page 14878

wrongfully, the tenant may recover it from him. The principal’s denial that he appointed him as his agent is not accepted when the latter acts on his behalf as agents do, with his knowledge, and is publicly known as his agent. Even supposing that he had not appointed him as his agent under these circumstances, his negligence and his allowing him to act constitute wrongdoing on his part that entails liability. The second lease, which the principal compelled the second tenant to enter into, is invalid. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Some people send others to attend to their affairs and provide them with money for their expenses. Is it lawful for those sent to live on this money and to borrow what they need to cover the remainder of their expenses? Is it permissible to associate with them?

He answered:

If those who sent them provide them with money to spend, that is permissible. Those who sent them must cover their expenses in full as long as they are attending to their affairs. It is permissible to associate with them.

---

## Page 14879

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is the agents’ nawba (crop-guarding arrangement), paid for by the farmers, lawful?

He answered:

If he guards the crop for the landowner and the farmer, he is entitled to payment for doing so. If the amount he charges the farmer corresponds to what the farmer owes him, there is nothing wrong with that. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man authorized another man to make a purchase, but did not authorize him to rescind the sale by mutual agreement. The agent nevertheless did so. Is this rescission valid?

He answered:

If someone appoints an agent to purchase something without authorizing him to rescind the sale by mutual agreement, the agent has no authority to do so. His rescission does not take effect without the principal’s permission, by scholarly consensus. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14880

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man acts as his father’s agent, collecting money owed to him by others. When he approached one debtor, the latter said, “I signed it for your father.” He then said, “If you release me and settle with me for a certain amount, that will settle the matter between us.” They then settled a debt of one thousand dirhams for three hundred dirhams. After the settlement, the debtor acknowledged the debt and took possession of the release. May a claim be brought against him after his acknowledgment, when witnesses attest that the creditor released him, or not?

He answered:

An agent authorized to collect a debt cannot validly release the debtor or settle for only part of the amount owed, even if he has been appointed as an agent for that purpose. Moreover, if the debtor denies the debt until a settlement is reached, the settlement is invalid as regards him, and his liability is not discharged. If the claimant agreed to the settlement only for fear of losing the entire amount owed, he was acting under duress, so his settlement is invalid. He may subsequently demand what he is owed if the debtor acknowledges it or evidence establishes it.

---

## Page 14881

Chapter on Partnerships

Shaykh al-Islām Ibn Taymiyyah was asked:

A group entered into a labor partnership without the consent of some of its members. They performed some work jointly and some separately. Is this partnership valid? What remuneration is each entitled to for the work he has performed? May someone who has done no work receive payment for another’s work without that worker’s consent?

He answered—may Allah be pleased with him:

The labor partnerships concerning which the jurists have differed are of two types.

The first:

Two people form a partnership in the work they jointly undertake as contractual obligations, as do artisans engaged in tailoring, carpentry, weaving, and similar trades, whose remuneration is determined by the work rather than by time. Such a worker is termed a non-exclusive hired worker. The obligation to perform the work rests with one of them in such a way that he may appoint someone else to carry it out. The work constitutes a debt owed by him, like debts consisting of tangible property; he is not obliged to perform it personally

---

## Page 14882

as an exclusive hired worker is. Most jurists, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Ahmad, permit such workers to form partnerships. They regard this as equivalent to a “credit partnership,” in which one partner purchases something for himself and his partner on the strength of his standing, just as a partner undertakes work on behalf of himself and his partner.

They explain that this partnership rests on agency: each partner acts for himself by virtue of ownership and for his partner as an agent. Al-Shāfi‘ī does not permit it, following his principle that a partnership cannot be established by contract; rather, his school recognizes only partnerships consisting of joint ownership. Thus, when two people jointly own property, they share its returns and bear its liabilities. Accordingly, he does not permit an ‘inān partnership when the two capital contributions are of different kinds. He permits it only when the two contributions are mixed together, and he requires profits to be apportioned strictly in proportion to those contributions.

The majority disagree with him on this. They maintain that partnerships are of two types: “partnerships of joint ownership” and “contractual partnerships.” A contractual partnership does not, in principle, require joint ownership, just as joint ownership does not require a contractual partnership, although the two may coexist. There is scholarly consensus that a profit-sharing investment partnership is a contractual partnership, not a partnership of joint ownership, since one party provides the capital and the other the labor. The same applies to orchard-tending and sharecropping partnerships, although some jurists maintain that these fall under hiring contracts and run contrary to legal analogy.

---

## Page 14883

The correct view, however, is that such arrangements have an independent legal basis: they belong to the category of partnerships, not exclusive hiring contracts, and conform to the legal analogy governing partnerships. Since partnerships rest on this principle, the jurists disagreed over partnerships for acquiring unowned resources, depending on whether acting as an agent in such matters is permissible. Ahmad permitted this, whereas Abū Hanīfah prohibited it. Ahmad cited the hadith of Saʿd, ʿAmmār, and Ibn Masʿūd as evidence.

It may be said that this belongs to the second type when the two partners jointly hire out their labor and their two animals under exclusive hiring contracts. Two views have been transmitted concerning such hiring. The view that it is invalid is the position of Abū Hanīfah and a group of Ahmad’s followers, including Abū al-Khattāb and al-Qādī in one of his two opinions. Al-Qādī stated that this follows by analogy from the school’s principles. This rests on the view that liability is not a condition of a labor partnership, as illustrated by partnerships for acquiring unowned resources, such as hunting and gathering firewood. Neither partner is obliged to perform any of the work incumbent upon the other; rather, their arrangement is comparable to sharing in the offspring of their livestock, the inheritance of their orchards, and the like.

Those who permit it say that it resembles a partnership for acquiring unowned resources. In that case, neither partner has an existing obligation to perform work, but the partnership makes whatever either partner does an act on behalf of both himself and his partner. Likewise, here, whatever wages either partner stipulates, or whatever reward is stipulated for him, belongs to both him and his partner. The work is performed on behalf of

---

## Page 14884

himself and his partner. This view is more correct, especially according to those who permit capital partnerships even when the two partners’ funds are not pooled and consist of different kinds of property.

Allah Most High says:

“Fulfil your contracts.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Muslims are bound by their conditions, except a condition that makes the unlawful lawful or the lawful unlawful.”

I believe this is also Mālik’s view.

As for joint arrangements among witnesses, it may be said that they fall under the question of “labor partnerships,” concerning which the jurists disagreed. Testimony cannot be assumed as a contractual liability, nor can it validly be delegated to an agent, such that either partner could act for himself by right of ownership and for his partner by virtue of agency. Remuneration for testimony belongs to the category of reward arrangements, not binding contracts of hire. Thus, this is joint participation in a contract, not a partnership contract.

It is comparable to someone saying to a group: “Build this wall for me, and you shall have ten,” or, “If you build it, you shall have ten,” or, “If you sew this garment, you shall have ten,” or, “If you return my runaway slave, you shall have ten.” Even if he does not specify the reward, while knowing that they work for payment—as with porters working together to carry a merchant’s goods—they are entitled to the customary remuneration for people doing such work, according to the majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, Ahmad, and others. The same entitlement applies to the cook who cooks for pay, the baker who bakes for pay, the weaver who weaves for pay, the fuller who processes cloth for pay, and the bathhouse owner

---

## Page 14885

and shipowner, in keeping with the established practice of paying for the use of their services. All these are entitled to the customary remuneration when no amount has been specified.

Likewise, if someone engages a group to witness his transaction and attest to it in writing, they are entitled to a reward. This is comparable to engaging them for other such tasks. If it is held that they are entitled to a reward, they are entitled to the customary reward for people doing such work, proportionate to the work each performs. If their work and the benefits they provide are equal, they are entitled to equal shares of the reward. The correct position is that this person’s statement is valid if they have not previously formed a partnership. If, however, they have formed a partnership in their earnings from testimony, this is like their partnership in earnings from other reward arrangements and contracts of hire.

Furthermore, the reward for testimony may concern work undertaken as a contractual liability, with the witness entitled to appoint someone in his place to testify for the person offering the reward. In this case, the partnership is valid according to all who recognize labor partnerships—the majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, Ahmad, and others. This is the correct view, supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound reasoning.

If, however, the reward is conditional upon that particular witness giving testimony, the two previously mentioned views apply. Here too, the correct view is that such a partnership is permissible, as is Mālik’s position according to the more correct of the two views. However, neither partner may abandon

---

## Page 14886

the work and demand a share of the other’s earnings; rather, he must perform whatever work the contract requires, whether expressly or by custom.

As for judges compelling them to enter this partnership against their will, this does not constitute unjustified coercion to enter into contracts. For it is the judges who authorize them to earn a livelihood through testimony, and this is conditional upon their being certified as upright. Their situation is unlike that of craftsmen, who earn their livelihood without the ruler’s permission. Since judges have authority in this matter, they may also require them to enter into partnership: at least two must be in attendance, and both must participate in giving testimony, since the testimony of one person does not fulfil its intended purpose.

Accordingly, the requirements of justice between them must be observed. None may refuse to perform work incumbent upon him, nor may any of them claim exclusively any portion of the earnings covered by the partnership, whether they are together or apart. And Allah, glorified be He, knows best.

---

## Page 14887

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

If someone entrusted with an aspect of the Muslims’ affairs follows a school of law that does not permit “labour partnerships,” may he prohibit people from entering into them?

He replied:

He may not prohibit people from this or similar practices in matters open to legitimate juristic interpretation when he has no text from the Quran or Sunnah, no scholarly consensus, or any comparable evidence supporting such a prohibition. This is especially so when most scholars permit such practices and Muslims generally engage in them throughout the lands.

Similarly, a judge may not overturn another judge’s ruling in such matters, nor may a scholar or mufti compel people to follow his position on them. Thus, when al-Rashīd consulted Mālik about requiring people to follow his Muwatta’ in such matters, Mālik advised him against it, saying:

“The Companions of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, dispersed throughout the lands, and each community adopted whatever knowledge had reached it.”

A man compiled a book on scholarly disagreement, and Ahmad said:

“Do not call it ‘The Book of Disagreement’; rather, call it ‘The Book of the Sunnah.’”

---

## Page 14888

For this reason, some scholars used to say:

“Their consensus is conclusive proof, and their disagreement is a vast mercy.”

ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz used to say:

“It would not please me if the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, had never differed. For if they agree on a position and someone opposes them, he is astray; but if they differ, and one person follows one opinion while another follows the other, there is latitude in the matter.”

Likewise, leading scholars other than Mālik said:

“A jurist may not compel people to follow his school of law.”

For this reason, scholars of the Shāfiʿī school and others who wrote on enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong said:

“Practices in such matters open to juristic interpretation are not to be suppressed by force, nor may anyone compel people to follow his opinion concerning them. Rather, these matters should be discussed through scholarly arguments. Whoever comes to recognize the correctness of one of the two opinions should follow it, while someone who relies on the scholars holding the other opinion is not to be censured.”

There are many comparable matters, such as disagreement over selling fresh broad beans in their pods; selling melon and cucumber crops as a single lot; sales concluded by exchange without a verbal offer and acceptance; advance-payment sales with immediate delivery; using a large quantity of water into which an impurity has fallen without altering it; performing ablution after touching the penis or women, after impurities leave the body through somewhere other than the two usual outlets, or after laughing aloud—or not performing ablution on account of these things; reciting “In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful” silently or aloud, or omitting it; regarding the urine and dung of animals whose meat may be eaten as impure, or holding them to be pure; selling specific goods that are not present on the basis of a description, or refraining from such sales; performing dry ablution with one strike or two, wiping as far as the wrists or the elbows; and performing dry ablution for each prayer or

---

## Page 14889

for each prayer time, or making do with a single dry ablution; and accepting testimony given by members of protected non-Muslim communities against one another, or refusing to accept their testimony.

The same applies to partnerships whose capital consists of goods, credit-based partnerships, agreements to tend any kind of tree in return for a share of its yield, and sharecropping on land without trees. These arrangements are of the same kind as labour partnerships. Indeed, more scholars prohibit these forms of partnership than prohibit labour partnerships. Nevertheless, from the time of their Prophet until today, Muslims in every age and region have continued to practise sharecropping and fruit-sharing, without anyone objecting to their doing so. If people were prevented from entering into such transactions, many of their essential interests, without which neither their religious nor their worldly affairs can be properly sustained, would be undermined.

For this reason, Abū Hanīfah would issue fatwas declaring sharecropping impermissible, yet derive further rulings on the basis of the view permitting it, saying:

“People do not follow my opinion prohibiting it.”

This is why his two disciples adopted the view that it is permissible, as did those scholars among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and others who favoured that view.

---

## Page 14890

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man entered into a trading partnership with some people without contributing any capital. Some of the partners have stated that the capital has suffered a loss. Is this man required to bear any of that loss or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If they entered into a partnership on the understanding that some would contribute their labour, like the working partner in a capital-and-labour partnership, while others would contribute their capital, or both their capital and labour, and all or part of the capital was lost without any transgression or negligence by the partner contributing his labour, he is not liable for any of that capital. This applies whether the capital-and-labour partnership was valid or invalid, by scholarly agreement. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man had a large quantity of cloth. A travelling merchant proposed taking that cloth on the understanding that he would purchase an undivided half-share, leaving the other half with its owner, and that they would form a joint-capital partnership in it, each receiving half the profit. The owner informed him of the original cost and added to it, on both sides, an increment which they had agreed

---

## Page 14891

upon. They also agreed that he would travel to Egypt and then return to Damascus, and that if selling in Damascus proved unsuitable, he would travel to Iraq and Persia. A partnership deed was drawn up stipulating that all the assets would be in the purchaser’s hands, to sell and buy, receive and disburse. They recorded that the partnership had been formed with dirhams, although it could only have taken the form described above.

When they arrived in Alexandria, they quarrelled. The owner of the cloth demanded payment from the purchaser and required him to divide the cloth, sell it there at a loss, and pay him the purchase price. Is this sale, which they agreed upon subject to the partnership, valid or invalid? If the purchaser is a partner, may the owner entrust him with receiving the assets, selling and buying, and receiving and disbursing?

If the sale is invalid, the owner is entitled only to his original property. Yet this working partner has worked with it on the understanding that he would receive half the profit. Is he entitled to claim half the profit or not? After this working partner has done the work and the cloth has been transported from Syria to Alexandria, may the owner take back the cloth, leaving the worker’s labour and efforts unrewarded? Or is the worker entitled to claim payment for his work, or a share of the profit customary for comparable work? Please give us a ruling.

He replied—may Allah have mercy on him:

Praise be to Allah.

This transaction is invalid on several grounds.

One is that it combines a sale with a partnership, which is impermissible. The jurists have agreed that it is impermissible to make a sale conditional upon another contract of this kind. Thus, one may not sell to someone on condition that the latter lend to him. Likewise, one may not lease to someone on condition that the latter enter into an agreement with him to tend trees for a share of their fruit; nor may one form a partnership with someone on condition that one borrow from him; nor sell to someone on condition that one buy from him, or enter into similar

---

## Page 14892

arrangements.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A loan combined with a sale is not lawful, nor are two conditions in a sale, nor profit on something for which one has not borne liability, nor selling what one does not have.”

He likewise “forbade two sales in a single transaction.”

This is because, when someone sells or leases something to another in conjunction with a loan, he gives him favourable terms on account of the loan. Yet a loan requires only the repayment of its equivalent; whenever an additional benefit is stipulated, this is impermissible by agreement.

Likewise, sales and partnerships are founded on fairness to both parties. Thus, it is impermissible to stipulate that either party alone receive the profit from a particular commodity, that either receive a fixed amount of the profit, or that either alone bear liability. Whenever someone enters into a sale with another on condition that they form a partnership, one gives the other favourable terms: either in the partnership, by undertaking all the work, or in the sale, by increasing the price, or in some similar way. The contracts thereby depart from the fairness on which they are founded.

Moreover, making the partnership a condition obliges the purchaser to use the property in a particular way and denies him the freedom afforded by an unrestricted contract. Some jurists prohibit such conditions categorically. Others allow them only in cases such as stipulating that a slave being sold be emancipated, or that the property sold be dedicated as an endowment—according to those who permit this—or other conditions involving a benefit unaccompanied by any overriding harm. This differs from a condition that entails abandoning fairness, which is impermissible by agreement.

Another ground is that, in a transaction of this kind, what the two parties ordinarily intend is

---

## Page 14893

to invest the capital under a profit-sharing arrangement, with the profit divided between them. The capital owner may, however, wish to make the working partner liable for half the capital. This is impermissible by agreement, because entitlement to returns accompanies liability. When a sale and a partnership are combined, the partnership is invalid by agreement. A stratagem is therefore employed to achieve this: the working partner sells half the capital. This is why all the capital is placed in his hands. Had the intention been purely to establish a partnership, they would have acted as partners in a joint-capital partnership do, with the capital held by both of them.

This provides a third ground for invalidating the partnership: their agreement that the capital should be held by only one of the two partners. Whether this constitutes a partnership based on non-monetary assets is disputed. However, a false acknowledgment contrary to the facts is forbidden and compromises one’s religious integrity.

That being so, the capital remains the property of its owner. Even if he were a partner, he would not be entitled to leave the conclusion of contracts and receipt of payments exclusively to the other partner. Such an arrangement belongs to a profit-sharing investment partnership, not a joint-capital partnership.

If the sale is invalid, he has no right to demand the stipulated price. If, however, the property itself cannot be returned, its value must be paid instead. If the purchaser, who is also the working partner, has put it to productive use, he is entitled to the customary profit share for someone performing comparable work, drawn from the other partner’s share. The jurists disagree over invalid partnerships, profit-sharing investment arrangements, orchard-tending arrangements and sharecropping arrangements in which the worker has performed the work: is he entitled to the customary wage for comparable work, or to the customary share of the profit?

There are two opinions. The stronger is the second, which is the position of Ibn al-Qāsim. Compensation under invalid contracts corresponds to what is customarily due under their valid counterparts, just as the amount due in

---

## Page 14894

invalid sales and hiring contracts is the customary price and customary remuneration, respectively, while an invalid reward-based contract entails the customary reward.

It is well known that valid partnerships of these kinds entail only a share of the profit, if any, rather than a fixed wage. The same applies to the share he purchased, if it is held that the property itself must be returned despite its increase in value, as some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Imam Ahmad maintain. The working partner who purchased it may claim either remuneration for his work or the customary profit share for someone performing comparable work, according to the two differing opinions.

If, however, it is held that, after taking possession of the property and transacting with it, he is required only to repay its value—as some followers of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik maintain—then the ruling is clear. In any event, under these circumstances, he is not required to repay the excess added above its customary value, by agreement. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man provided capital under a profit-sharing investment arrangement and then died. The working partner continued to trade with it after his death without the heirs’ permission. Is the profit-sharing arrangement terminated? What is the ruling on the profit earned after the capital owner’s death?

---

## Page 14895

He replied:

Yes, the profit-sharing investment arrangement terminates upon the capital owner’s death. If the working partner knows of his death and then transacts without the owner’s permission, whether express or established by custom, and without lawful authority, he is a wrongful possessor.

The scholars have differed concerning the profit earned in this situation. Does it belong solely to the owner, like the natural increase of property? Or solely to the working partner, because he bears liability for the capital? Or must they give it away in charity because it is an unlawful profit? Or is it shared between them?

There are four opinions. The soundest is the fourth: the profit is shared between them according to customary practice in comparable cases. This was the ruling of the Commander of the Faithful, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—concerning the public treasury funds that his sons took and traded with without entitlement. He treated it as a profit-sharing investment, and the jurists relied on his ruling in the chapter on “Profit-Sharing Investment.”

This is because the profit is an increase generated jointly by one party’s labor and the other’s capital. It therefore belongs to both, like any other increase arising from two contributing sources. The right belongs to them alone and extends to no one else. There is no basis for declaring it unlawful for both of them or awarding it exclusively to either one.

Requiring that the working partner receive the customary profit share for someone in his position is sounder than requiring customary remuneration. The capital may yield no profit, and the remuneration for the work may be several times the profit, or vice versa. These partnerships do not have the work itself as their object, such that remuneration would be due for it, nor are they contracts of hire. Rather, they constitute a distinct contractual category: they are partnerships, not contracts of hire, such that whatever invalidates the latter should invalidate the former. Whoever imposes obligations upon them that they do not entail has erred.

If, however, the working partner and the heirs exchanged words that imply, according to

---

## Page 14896

customary practice, that the profit-sharing investment contract should remain in force, he is entitled to his stipulated share of the profit under that arrangement. If he acknowledges a profit, he is bound by his acknowledgment. If he subsequently claims to have made an error of an inexcusable kind, his claim is not accepted. If the error is of an excusable kind, there is a well-known disagreement over whether his claim is accepted.

He may not hand the capital over to another person without the owner’s permission or authorization under Islamic law. If he does so, he is liable for the capital, whether he transferred it under a valid or an invalid contract. Whatever entails liability under a valid contract also entails liability under an invalid one; whatever entails no liability under a valid contract entails none under an invalid one. If, however, the capital was wrongfully appropriated, he is liable in all circumstances. Whenever the working partner is negligent in handling the capital or exceeds his authority, he is liable for it.

Likewise, the second working partner sins if he denies another’s right, conceals money he is obliged to pay, or demands that they undertake to pay him remuneration without lawful justification. The governing authority must ensure that rights are delivered to those entitled to them. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man gave another man capital under a profit-sharing investment arrangement. It later emerged that the recipient owed a debt predating that arrangement. May he give the creditors any of this capital, or not? And if he claims that he received none of the investment capital, or that it was lost, or that there was negligence in handling it without a cause

---

## Page 14897

that is apparent, is his claim to be accepted or not?

He replied:

None of the capital of this profit-sharing investment may be used to repay any debt owed by the working partner unless the owner of the capital so chooses. If he makes a claim contrary to what ordinarily occurs, his word alone is not sufficient for it to be accepted.

He was asked:

A capital owner brought his working partner before the judge and demanded the return of all the capital. The judge ruled that he must repay it. He paid back part of it and requested a respite for the remainder. The owner granted him a respite, with a guarantee for his appearance. The working partner then travelled away from the town for a period. Is the partnership terminated by the owner’s bringing him before the judge, the judge’s ruling that he pay the sum, and the granting of a respite? Does he become personally liable for the capital?

He replied:

The partnership is dissolved by the aforementioned demand. The working partner becomes personally liable for the capital by undertaking the aforementioned journey, since he delayed handing it over beyond the time it became due despite being able to do so.

---

## Page 14898

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

May the working partner in a profit-sharing investment cover his own living expenses from the capital owner’s funds, whether at home or while travelling? If so, may he indulge in fine food and other luxuries at its expense, or must he restrict himself to his customary needs?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If they have agreed on a stipulation concerning living expenses, that is permissible. Likewise, if there is an established custom among them and the contract leaves the matter unspecified, it is interpreted according to that custom.

Without either of these, however, it is not permissible. Some scholars hold that he is entitled to living expenses without qualification, even if he has not stipulated this. This is the view of Abū Hanīfa and Mālik, and one opinion of al-Shāfi‘ī. The well-known view is that he is not entitled to living expenses under any circumstances, even if he stipulates them. Whenever he is entitled to living expenses, these must remain within reasonable and customary bounds. Any indulgence beyond those bounds is charged to his own account.

---

## Page 14899

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Two men entered into a partnership, one contributing an animal and the other some dirhams. They pooled these contributions, agreeing to share whatever profit Allah Most High allotted them, and subsequently made a profit. What is the ruling?

He replied:

The animal’s value is assessed, and that value together with the dirhams constitutes the capital, which they own jointly. According to our view, partnership and division of property are validly effected by verbal agreement, without requiring the two contributions to be physically mingled or separated. Joint ownership is established through a partnership agreement, just as separate ownership is established through an agreement to divide the property and settle the accounts. Whatever profit they make is shared between them. When they divide the property, the animal is sold, and they divide its proceeds together with the remaining funds.

This is clear if we regard a partnership formed with non-monetary assets as valid. Even if we regard it as invalid, an invalid partnership follows the same rules as a valid one regarding liability or its absence, and the validity or invalidity of transactions. They differ only in permissibility and, according to one of the two views, in the amount of profit each receives.

The apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that the profit is distributed as the partners stipulated. According to the other view, the profit follows the respective capital contributions, and the other partner receives the customary wage for comparable work. The sounder view in this case is that he receives the customary share of profit for comparable work. Thus, there are three views.

---

## Page 14900

He was asked:

Two men jointly own a horse. Neither will sell his share to the other or buy the other’s share, nor does either keep the horse by the month, and it is being neglected between them. One of them lent it without his partner’s permission, and it died. Is the partner who lent it liable for his partner’s share or not?

He replied:

If they cannot agree that it should be kept by either of them or by a third party whom they choose, and neither asks the other to settle their joint ownership, the horse must be sold in its entirety and its price divided between them. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Two men jointly own a horse. One permitted the other to walk it so that inactivity would not harm it, but did not permit him to drive it on. The latter allowed someone else to ride it, and it became ill as a result. Is he liable if it dies? Alternatively, must he compensate for its diminished value? Must he pay for any medicine it needs? The partner has been placed under legal restriction by the judge,

---

## Page 14901

although he exercises sound judgment in his dealings; the impediment to this, however, is evidence attesting in his favor. If this is the case, may his partner take its value from his property?

The Answer

He replied:

If the partner has transgressed by doing something authorized neither by Islamic law nor by the owner, whether expressly or by customary understanding, he is liable for any loss caused by his wrongdoing, even if he is under legal restriction. If his wrongdoing has diminished the horse’s value, he must compensate for that diminution. If liability arises from the horse’s death, he is liable for its full value.

He was asked:

Zayd and ʿAmr jointly own a horse. Zayd took the horse and drove it in an unaccustomed manner without ʿAmr’s permission. The horse subsequently became weak and died while in Zayd’s custody.

He replied:

Yes. If the partner mistreated the horse and it died because of his wrongdoing, he is liable for his partner’s share. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14902

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him—about a man who jointly owned a cow with another person. The cow was kept by one of them, who used it and provided its fodder. His partner requested it so that they could settle their respective shares, but he refused, claimed two-thirds of the cow, and prevented the settlement.

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. He must settle their respective shares in the cow. If either partner requests its sale, it must be sold, even against the other’s wishes, and they must divide the proceeds. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, Imam Ahmad, and others.

If the partner takes the milk and its value exactly equals the cost of the fodder, he owes nothing. If the benefit he derives from the cow exceeds the cost of the fodder, he must give his partner his share of the surplus. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14903

He was asked:

About a shepherd who had in his care a flock of sheep belonging to several owners. When funds were needed for their upkeep, he sold some of them and spent the proceeds on the remainder. The sheep sold belonged to some of the owners: some were left with none of their sheep, others with only a few, and others with many. Should they divide the remainder in proportion to their original capital, or should each owner take whatever remains of his own sheep?

He replied—may Allah have mercy on him:

Rather, they should divide the remainder in proportion to their original capital, or the owners of the remaining sheep should reimburse what was spent on their behalf, namely, the value of the sheep he sold.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

About two partners who jointly owned horses. A mare in the keeping of one of them died by Allah’s decree and predestination, and he had a formal record drawn up of its death.

He replied:

If one partner entrusted the mare to the other and it died while in his care, without any negligence or wrongful conduct on his part, he bears no liability.

---

## Page 14904

He was asked:

About a man who jointly owned a mare with another man. With his consent, the mare was in his partner’s keeping. An order from the ruling authorities was imposed on the town, and the mare was seized along with other horses and goods. The other partner now wishes to hold the partner who had custody of it liable. Is he entitled to do so?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he was neither negligent nor guilty of wrongful conduct, he bears no liability for what has been described. In these circumstances, his denial of negligence and wrongful conduct is accepted upon his oath.

He was asked:

About a man who jointly owned an orchard with another man and leased the latter’s share from him under a lease valid under Islamic law. The partner who leased it wrongfully cut down some of the orchard’s fruit-bearing trees that yielded income, without the owner’s permission, although the owner was present. He used some of the remnants and firewood for his own purposes. Is he required to make restitution for what he wrongfully took? May the owner hold to account the assistants who actually cut the wood?

He replied:

He is not entitled to take his partner’s share unless he is entitled to it under a contract of

---

## Page 14905

lease. Whatever he has taken in this way, he must compensate his partner for his share. The owner may demand compensation from those who directly took it, but may not demand it from the partner who instructed them; he may recover his due from whichever of them he chooses. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a group of witnesses who entered into a partnership, with some doing more work than others. Are they entitled to equal shares of the remuneration, or should their shares be proportionate to their work?

He replied:

An unqualified partnership agreement entails equality in work and remuneration. If one of them does additional work as a voluntary contribution, the others receive the same remuneration as he does. If he did not intend it as a voluntary contribution, he may claim from them either the equivalent of his additional work or extra remuneration proportionate to his work. If they agree to stipulate an additional payment for him, that is permissible. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a group of brokers working in partnership to sell goods. Does this impugn their religious integrity? May the ruler—may Allah strengthen him—prohibit them from doing so when no cheating or deception has been found on their part?

---

## Page 14906

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If the merchant who entrusts his goods to a broker knows that he will pass them on to other brokers and consents to this, there is undoubtedly nothing wrong with it, for the broker is the merchant’s agent. By scholarly consensus, an agent, like the principal, may appoint another agent. The scholars disagree only over whether he may do so without the principal’s permission; they hold two well-known views on this. Their disagreement concerning partnerships among brokers rests on this issue: since brokers are agents, they base their ruling on whether an agent may appoint another agent.

If there is an established custom whereby a broker hands the goods over to someone he trusts, that established custom has the force of an expressly stipulated condition. Thus, the majority of leading Muslim scholars—including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad and others—have held “labor partnerships” to be permissible. As Ibn Mas‘ūd said: “Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās, ‘Ammār and I entered into a partnership on the day of Badr. Sa‘d brought back two captives, while ‘Ammār and I brought back nothing.”

“Labor partnerships” serve Muslims’ interests throughout the towns and cities, and many of their affairs cannot be properly managed without them. This applies, for example, to tradespeople working jointly in shops, including brokers and others. One person cannot handle people’s business alone and therefore needs an assistant. Yet the assistant’s remuneration and work cannot be fixed in advance, just as they cannot in profit-sharing investment partnerships and similar arrangements. They therefore need to enter into partnerships.

---

## Page 14907

The majority of scholars regard partnership as an independent contract under Islamic law, conferring upon each partner rights that he would not have without it, as in profit-sharing investment partnerships. Others recognize only joint ownership and the contracts associated with it, and thus prohibit most forms of partnership that people need, such as unequal shares of profit despite equal capital contributions, credit-based partnerships, labor partnerships, and others. The majority’s view, however, is more correct.

When two people enter into a partnership, each acts on his own behalf by virtue of ownership and on his partner’s behalf by virtue of agency. Whatever contracts he concludes, he concludes for himself and his partner; whatever property he takes possession of, he receives for himself and his partner. When people know that they are partners and entrust their property to them, this is regarded as permission for either partner to authorize the other to act.

The ruler has no right to prohibit contracts and acts of taking possession of this kind, which the majority of scholars permit and upon which people’s interests depend. Moreover, prohibiting all of them is untenable under Islamic law, while singling out some for prohibition is arbitrary. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a cost-plus sale in which the seller discloses his purchase price but does not inform the buyer that the original purchase was on deferred payment: is this lawful or unlawful?

---

## Page 14908

He replied:

A sale based on disclosure of the purchase price is permissible, whether at a profit, at a loss, at cost, or by selling a share at its proportionate cost. However, the seller and buyer must have the same knowledge of the price. If the seller purchased the item on deferred payment, the buyer must be informed of that. If he merely states a price without explaining that he purchased it on deferred payment, he is acting unjustly and wrongfully.

It is reported in the two Sahīh collections, from Hakīm ibn Hizām, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The buyer and seller retain the option to cancel the sale until they part. If they are truthful and make matters clear, their transaction will be blessed; but if they lie and conceal information, the blessing of their transaction will be erased.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A merchant in a shop bought a piece of cloth for eleven and a quarter. Afterwards, a man came to him, and the merchant told him that he had purchased it for eleven and a quarter, then sold it to him at a profit of one-half. The buyer took it, and they parted company. An hour later, the buyer returned and sought to force the merchant to take it back. The merchant refused and did not disclose the profit, but the buyer would not relent, and they disputed the profit.

The buyer said, “Take a quarter and an eighth from me.” The merchant said to the buyer, “Sell it to me for eleven and a half.” He replied with the word “Yes.” May he disclose this additional quarter to the original buyer? Is doing so permissible for him in any way?

He replied:

The owner of the merchandise may not state that he purchased it for that amount without

---

## Page 14909

explaining the circumstances. Rather, if he wishes to state that amount, he must explain that the buyer returned it to him on terms that left him half the profit. Whether this was a sale or a cancellation by mutual agreement, people do not regard it as equivalent to someone making a private, unconditional purchase—especially if the buyer compelled him to take it back. The scholars agree that someone who purchases merchandise under compulsion may not state its purchase price without explaining the circumstances, for this is a form of deception.

The scholars have differed concerning someone who sells an item at a profit, then finds it offered for sale in the market and purchases it: must he deduct the earlier profit from the second purchase price, or explain the circumstances, or is neither required of him? There are two views. The first is the view of Abū Hanīfa, Ahmad, and others. If this applies in such a case, what, then, if he offers less than the sale price for it? And what if he is also compelled to do so?

Sales based on disclosure of the purchase price rest upon truthfulness and clarity, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The buyer and seller retain the option to cancel the sale until they part. If they are truthful and make matters clear, their transaction will be blessed; but if they lie and conceal information, the blessing of their transaction will be erased.”

Thus, concealing any circumstance which, if known to the buyer, would prevent him from purchasing at that price is deception. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14910

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man purchased ten pairs of goods in a single lot, then stated the purchase cost of one pair by dividing the total price among the pairs, without adding or subtracting anything. Is this lawful or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he accurately explains the purchase, stating that he bought the pair together with the others and apportioned the price among them all, so that the share attributable to this pair was such-and-such and to that pair such-and-such, then this is truthfulness and clarity in their truest sense. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The buyer and seller retain the option to cancel the sale until they part. If they are truthful and make matters clear, their transaction will be blessed; but if they conceal information and lie, the blessing of their transaction will be erased.”

And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 14911

Chapter on Orchard-Sharing Contracts

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

In my earlier discussion of principles, including principles of jurisprudence, I explained what the Quran and Sunnah teach about people upholding justice, and how this extends to transactions, encompassing both exchange contracts and partnerships. I mentioned that “orchard-sharing, sharecropping, and profit-sharing investment arrangements,” and similar contracts, are forms of partnership. I also explained some of the errors made by those who regard these as exchange contracts, like sales and leases, and consequently apply the rules governing exchange contracts to them.

I explained that sharecropping is permissible whether the seed is supplied by the landowner or the cultivator, as established by the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and by clear legal analogy. I also explained that the hadith narrated by Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj and others prohibiting mukhābara (sharecropping) and the leasing of land refers to their practice of stipulating that the produce of a particular plot should belong to the landowner

---

## Page 14912

as Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj also explained in the two Sahīh collections.

As for calling a sharecropping arrangement muzāra‘a when the seed is supplied by the owner, and mukhābara when it is supplied by the cultivator, this is a claim without evidence. It is like the names these people and their forefathers have devised, for which Allah has sent down no authority. For Sahīh al-Bukhārī records from Ibn ‘Umar:

“Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made an agreement with the people of Khaybar for half of the fruit and crops it produced, on condition that they cultivate it at their own expense.”

The prohibited form of mukhābara did not involve the cultivator supplying the seed.

The point here is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited a partnership—which constitutes leasing land in the broad sense—when it stipulated that the landowner should receive the produce of a particular plot. The matter is as al-Layth ibn Sa‘d said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī: what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited was something that anyone versed in what is lawful and unlawful would recognize as unlawful upon considering it—or words to that effect.

This is because partnerships and contractual dealings require fairness to both parties: they share in gains and losses after each has recovered his original capital. If the produce of a particular plot is stipulated for one of them, it is reserved exclusively for him, while the rest of the crop may not survive. This would wrong one of the partners. It also involves contractual uncertainty and gambling.

The same principle underlies the scholars’ statement, on which I know of no dissent: it is impermissible to stipulate that either party should receive the fruit of a particular tree or a fixed quantity of fruit. Likewise, one may not

---

## Page 14913

stipulate that either party receive the crops from a particular plot or a fixed quantity of the crop’s yield. Likewise, one may not stipulate that either party receive the profit from a particular commodity or a fixed amount of profit. As for stipulating the return of an amount equivalent to the original capital, this is like stipulating the return of the trees and land. The stipulation that an equivalent quantity of seed be returned raises questions that I have discussed elsewhere.

If this is the ruling on assigning either party a particular portion or fixed quantity of the yield—which must instead be shared between them in undivided shares—then the prohibition applies all the more to reserving for either party something outside that yield. Examples include one party stipulating that the other cultivate another piece of land for him, undertake trade in which the profits belong exclusively to the owner of the goods, or irrigate another tree for him, and similar practices common among many people.

Because the worker is in need, the owner may stipulate that he provide some benefit under another arrangement. Thus, he may enter into a profit-sharing investment partnership with him while also entrusting him with goods to trade solely for the owner’s profit; or contract with him to tend trees and cultivate land while also employing him on another piece of land or to help tend his livestock; or stipulate that he be allowed to borrow the worker’s animals, or something similar. Making such stipulations is impermissible, with no disagreement among the scholars that I know of.

This amounts to stipulating an entitlement to a particular portion or fixed amount of the profit. For when one party stipulates the use of the other’s labour or property, he alone receives that benefit, even though there may be no yield, or less than was expected. He will then have wrongfully obtained the benefit provided by the other party and dealt with him on a basis of gambling and usury, for this involves both usury and gambling.

---

## Page 14914

If they agree to this before concluding the contract, it is equivalent to a stipulation within the contract, as we established in Kitāb Butlān al-Tahlīl: a stipulation made before a contract is equivalent to one made at its conclusion.

If one of them voluntarily gives the other a gift—for example, the worker in a profit-sharing investment partnership gives something to the owner, or the farmer gives him sheep, chickens, or something else—this is equivalent to a borrower giving a gift to the lender. The owner may choose to return it, accept it and reciprocate with something of equivalent value, or credit its value to the giver out of the owner’s share of the profit when they divide it, just as a lender deducts its value from the loan principal.

Some people dispute this with us, saying, “He is giving the gift voluntarily.” But that is not so. Rather, he has given it because of their dealings with one another, whether a loan, an exchange, or something similar. This is illustrated by what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith concerning the collector Ibn al-Lutbiyyah of al-Azd. When the collector said, “This is for you, and this was given to me as a gift,” the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Why did he not sit in his father’s and mother’s house and see whether he would be given gifts or not?”

It is established that several Companions, including ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās, Anas, and others, instructed a lender who had accepted a gift to deduct its value from the loan. Sound reasoning also makes this plain: if he accepts the gift before receiving repayment, he has

---

## Page 14915

entered into an arrangement with him to receive both the gift and repayment in exchange for the loan. This is usury itself, for a loan entitles the lender to no more than its equivalent.

If he said to him at the time of the loan, “I will give you its equivalent and this gift,” that would be impermissible by consensus. Thus, if he gives him a gift before repayment because of the loan, on the understanding that he will also repay its equivalent, this amounts to an agreement to receive more than the principal. By contrast, a gift given according to an established custom between them predating the loan would not fall into this category.

This is made clear by the fact that, in accepting the gift, he intends to grant him further time in consideration of it. The arrangement thus becomes an exchange of a hundred plus the gift for a hundred payable at a future date. This is usury itself, unlike exchanging a hundred for another hundred identical in kind and quality, even if a repayment term is stipulated. The latter is a purely gratuitous act, not an exchange transaction: no reasonable person sells something for its exact equivalent in every respect, with payment deferred. He would only do so where their qualities differed, as when exchanging whole coins for broken ones, or one currency for another, with deferred payment. That is impermissible by the consensus of the Muslims.

The same applies to partnerships. If the owner accepts, without compensation, a gift or benefit that the worker provides solely because of their profit-sharing investment partnership or sharecropping arrangement, while also stipulating a share of the profit, then, on this view, such acceptance constitutes an agreement to receive something in addition to his proportionate share. This is like stipulating entitlement to the crop of a particular plot. There may be no profit at all, leaving the worker oppressed and wronged.

For this reason, the worker seeks compensation for his gift and counts its value as something the owner owes him. If the owner does not compensate him for it, he betrays his trust concerning the invested funds—their principal

---

## Page 14916

and profits—as also happens between the farming partner and the cultivator. The cultivator cheats and wrongs his partner because of what he claims the latter appropriates from his property and the like: accepting gifts, for example, and taking food for himself and fodder for his animals from the cultivator’s property over a period of time without right. The cultivator therefore clips off ears of grain before the harvest, leaves grain in the stalks, straw and dust of the threshing floor, steals from his partner, and devises every possible means of theft. His partner, in turn, wrongs him physically by beating him and making him serve him, and financially by demanding expenditure to which he is not entitled, regarding this as compensation for what the cultivator has misappropriated from his property.

The same happens between the owner of the capital and the worker. The worker believes that the owner is taking his labour and wealth: he has to give him gifts and carry goods with him to trade on his behalf, reserving their profits exclusively for the owner because of their profit-sharing investment partnership. He therefore seeks compensation for his labour and wealth by misappropriating capital and profits, lying and concealing information. The owner, meanwhile, believes that the worker is misappropriating capital and profits, diverting some of his funds to expenditure on other property of the worker’s own, gifts to his friends, and similar things whose benefit is unrelated to the investment partnership. He therefore demands gifts and the like from him. Indeed, some workers give gifts only because they know that the owner seeks and desires them, thereby protecting themselves against his harm and oppression.

These dealings lead to disputes, enmity, and violations of persons, honour and property. This results from one party receiving something beyond his proportionate share of the returns, for this departs from the justice required

---

## Page 14917

in partnerships. The Prophet’s statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace),

“Why did he not stay in his father’s and mother’s house and see whether gifts would be given to him or not?”

covers all these situations. When a gift is given for a particular reason, its receipt is governed by that reason, just like anything else received on that basis. An agreement established by customary practice is like an expressly stated agreement. If someone receives a gift on account of a loan or lending, that gift is treated like property received under a loan contract or a profit-sharing investment contract, unless a reciprocal gift is given in return. This is a fundamental principle whose neglect gives rise to immense injustice and corruption.

Section

As we have said concerning property received: until repayment has been made, he may not take any money or benefit from the other party before repayment without providing equivalent compensation, because this entails usury. Giving gifts and lending things for use belong to the same category.

The same applies to profit-sharing investment partnerships and sharecropping. Whenever the owner of the capital takes money or receives a benefit before the proceeds have been fully divided, this is impermissible without equivalent compensation. Examples include requiring the investment worker or cultivator to perform services outside the obligations of the partnership agreement, benefiting from his property, or anything similar—unless he credits him for all of it. Allah, glorified be He, knows best.

For this reason, the jurists differed over the case in which someone gives another person an item of merchandise and says: “Sell it and use its proceeds as capital in a profit-sharing investment partnership.”

---

## Page 14918

One view was: This is impermissible, because the owner alone benefits from the sale before the profit-sharing investment partnership begins. It is therefore like stipulating that the worker sell another item of merchandise.

Another view was: It is permissible, because this sale serves the purpose of the profit-sharing investment partnership and thus resembles a sale made after the contract is concluded. In both cases, the property is held by the worker in trust, and the owner receives no exclusive benefit beyond the purpose of the partnership. The matter calls for further consideration.

He also said—may Allah sanctify his soul:

Section

As for sharecropping, whether the seed is supplied by the worker or the landowner, or one person provides the land, another the seed, and a third the labour, two positions are reported from Ahmad concerning these arrangements. The correct view is that all of them are valid. Indeed, an arrangement in which the worker supplies the seed has an even stronger claim to validity than one in which the owner supplies it.

“The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) entered into an agreement with the people of Khaybar that they would cultivate it at their own expense in return for half its yield of fruit and crops.”

This was narrated by al-Bukhārī and others. The account of the people of Khaybar is the basis for the permissibility of orchard-tending partnerships and sharecropping. They supplied the seed from their own resources; the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not

---

## Page 14919

provide them with seed of his own. The same was true of his successors, such as ʿUmar, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqās, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, and several other Companions: they entered into sharecropping agreements in which the worker supplied the seed.

In numerous answers transmitted by most of his companions, Imam Ahmad expressly stated that it is permissible to lease land in return for a share of its yield. He cited as evidence the account of the people of Khaybar and the Prophet’s agreement with them to cultivate it in return for a share of its yield. This is precisely what leasing land for a share of its yield means when the worker supplies the seed, since it is the tenant who sows the land, and in both arrangements the owner receives a share of the crop.

For this reason, those of his companions who examined this issue closely, such as Abū al-Khattāb and others, said: “This is sharecropping in which the worker supplies the seed.”

Another group of his companions, including al-Qādī and others, said: “Rather, this contract is permissible when concluded in the terms of a lease, but not in the terms of sharecropping.” This is because Ahmad expressly stated elsewhere that, in sharecropping, the owner must supply the seed.

A third group said: “Rather, this is permissible as sharecropping, but not as a lease.” Their reasoning was that a lease is a binding contract, whereas sharecropping, according to one of the two views, is not; and that this resembles the arrangement known as the “miller’s measure.” It is reported from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) “that he forbade the measure of

---

## Page 14920

the miller.” This means hiring someone to grind grain in return for a portion of the flour.

The correct approach is the first: what matters in contracts is their meaning and purpose, not merely their wording. This is the principle followed by Ahmad and the majority of scholars, and it is one of the two views within al-Shāfiʿī’s school. Some of Ahmad’s companions, however, may regard the ruling as changing according to the wording used, as al-Shāfiʿī also sometimes indicates. Examples include an advance-payment sale with immediate delivery expressed in the terms of an ordinary sale, a release from marriage for compensation expressed in the terms of divorce, and a lease expressed in the terms of a sale. These and similar matters are discussed at length elsewhere.

As for those who say that sharecropping requires the owner to supply the seed, they have neither scriptural evidence nor any report from the Companions to support this. Rather, they have drawn an analogy with a profit-sharing investment partnership. They say: “Just as, in a profit-sharing investment partnership, one person provides the labour and another the capital, so too, in orchard-tending and sharecropping partnerships, one person provides the labour and another the capital, with the seed supplied by the capital owner.”

This analogy is unsound. In a profit-sharing investment partnership, the capital returns to its owner, and the two parties divide the profit. Its counterpart is therefore the land or trees, which return to their owner, while the two parties divide the fruit and crops. As for the seed, they do not return it to its owner; rather, it is consumed without replacement, just as the labour of the worker and his oxen is expended without replacement. It is therefore akin to the provision of a benefit rather than a contribution of capital. Requiring the worker to supply it would thus accord more closely with the analogy. This also agrees with what is reported from the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, for some of them entered into sharecropping agreements in which the seed was supplied by

---

## Page 14921

the worker. ʿUmar entered into sharecropping agreements on the terms that, if the owner supplied the seed, his share would be such-and-such, and if the worker supplied it, his share would be such-and-such. Al-Bukhārī mentions this. Thus, ʿUmar permitted this arrangement, and this is the correct view.

As for those who say, “This is not permissible as a contract of hire, because he prohibited ‘the miller’s measure,’” the response is: this hadith is false and baseless. It appears in none of the authoritative hadith collections, nor was it transmitted by any of the leading scholars. In the Prophet’s city, there was neither a miller who ground grain for payment nor a baker who baked for payment. Moreover, during the Prophet’s lifetime, the people of Medina had no measure called the qafīz. This measure only came into use when Iraq was conquered and land tax was imposed on its people; Iraq was not conquered during the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

These and other considerations show that these are not the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, they are the words of certain Iraqis who disallowed such arrangements on the basis of their own independent reasoning. Furthermore, the hadith does not prohibit stipulating a proportional share of the flour, but rather a specified quantity—namely, a qafīz. The same applies to sharecropping: if either party were allotted the crops of a particular plot or a fixed quantity, the sharecropping agreement would be invalid. This is the kind of sharecropping that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited in

---

## Page 14922

the report of Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj, recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:
