“They used to stipulate that the landowner would receive the crops of a particular plot, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade this.”

These matters have been discussed at length elsewhere, and it has been shown that sharecropping is more lawful than leasing for a specified rent. Muslims have differed over all these arrangements. Sharecropping is founded on fairness: if anything is produced, both parties share it; if nothing is produced, both go without. In leasing, however, the lessor receives the rent while the lessee bears the risk: he may or may not obtain what he seeks. Sharecropping therefore involves less risk than leasing. Nor is sharecropping a contract of hire for a specified task, requiring that the work be performed for a wage. Rather, it is a form of partnership, like a capital-and-labour partnership and similar arrangements. Ahmad regards this category of arrangements as consistent with legal analogy.

He permits an owner to hand over horses, mules, donkeys, or camels to someone who hires them out, with the proceeds shared between the owner and the worker. Reports concerning this appear in Sunan Abī Dāwūd and elsewhere.

He also permits an owner to hand over hunting equipment and animals—falcons, nets, beasts, and the like—to someone who hunts with them, with whatever is obtained shared between the two. He permits handing wheat to someone who grinds it in return for a third or a quarter of it. The same applies to handing flour to someone who makes it into dough, yarn to someone who weaves it, or cloth to someone who sews it, in each case in return for a share of what is produced. The same applies to handing hides to someone who makes them into sandals, although a contrary view has also been reported from him on this matter. Likewise, he permits—according to the stronger of

---

## Page 14923

the two reports from him—entrusting livestock to someone who tends them in return for a share of their milk and offspring, and giving silkworms and leaves to someone who feeds and tends them in return for a share of the silk.

As for those who distinguish between sharecropping and leasing on the grounds that leasing is a binding contract whereas sharecropping is not, the response is: this distinction cannot be maintained. If someone enters into a sharecropping agreement for a specified year, it is binding, just as it is binding when concluded using the wording of a lease. Conversely, a lease may not be binding, as when someone says, “I lease you this house for two dirhams per month.” Such a lease is valid according to the apparent position of Ahmad and others, yet whenever a new month begins, he may terminate it. A contract offering a reward for accomplishing a task is akin to a contract of hire, yet it is not binding. Thus, an open-ended contract with no specified term is not binding, whereas a fixed-term contract may be binding.

Section

As for leasing land in return for foodstuffs of the same kind as those produced on it—for example, leasing land to someone who grows wheat or barley on it in return for a specified quantity of wheat or barley—this too is permissible according to the stronger of the two reports from Ahmad. This is also the position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfi‘ī. According to the other report, he prohibits it, in agreement with Mālik’s position.

---

## Page 14924

They argue: “What is sought through the lease is the food, so this is tantamount to selling food for food of the same kind.”

They also argue: “This is a form of mukhābara, which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade. It is also tantamount to muzābana, since it amounts to selling something for something of the same kind by estimate.”

The correct position, however, is that of the majority, because what the lease entitles the tenant to is the use of the land. Hence, if he is able to cultivate it but does not do so, he must still pay the rent. The food is produced only through his own labour and seed, and the lessor did not supply him with that seed. Thus, this has nothing to do with ribā (usury).

An analogous case is that of someone hiring people to extract gold or silver ore, or buried treasure, from the earth in return for dirhams or dinars. This is not like exchanging dirhams for dirhams. Likewise, someone may hire a worker to till the land, sow it, and irrigate it, paying him with grain from his own supplies, while the work for which he has hired him includes sowing grain on his behalf. This is analogous to the case under discussion.

Rāfi‘, the narrator of the hadith, explained that the mukhābara forbidden by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was a sharecropping arrangement stipulating that the landowner receive the crop from a particular plot. Some scholars, however, classified all sharecropping as mukhābara, as did Abū Hanīfa.

Others, such as al-Shāfi‘ī, held that sharecropping on bare land constitutes mukhābara.

Others held that sharecropping in which the cultivator supplies the seed constitutes mukhābara.

Still others held that leasing land in return for produce of the same kind as that grown on it is a form of

---

## Page 14925

mukhābara, as Mālik held.

The correct position is that the prohibited mukhābara is what Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj explained it to be. Al-Layth ibn Sa‘d likewise said:

“What the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade is something that anyone with insight into the lawful and unlawful would recognize as forbidden upon examining it.”

This is the position of the majority of jurists of the hadith tradition, such as Ahmad, Ishāq, Ibn al-Mundhir, Ibn Khuzayma, and others. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited practices that fall within what Allah prohibited in His Book. Allah prohibited usury and gambling in His Book, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited sales involving uncertainty, because they are a form of gambling. The same applies to selling fruit before it shows signs of ripening and to the sale known as habal al-habala. He also prohibited exchanging gold for gold and silver for silver except in equal amounts, along with other transactions that fall within usury.

Some scholars thus came to think that certain practices were covered by the general prohibition or its underlying general rationale, although they were not. Some, for example, included leasing orchards for a full year or several years to someone who waters and tends them until they bear fruit. They thought this fell under selling fruit before it shows signs of ripening, and therefore prohibited it.

Yet this is actually a lease, like leasing land. Just as he forbade selling grain before it hardens, while permitting land to be leased to someone who cultivates it until it yields a crop, so too he forbade selling fruit before it shows signs of ripening, but did not forbid leasing orchards to someone who tends them until they bear fruit. The fruit is produced through his own labour and belongs to him. The seller of fruit

---

## Page 14926

and grain must water it until it is fully mature, unlike the lessor, who is not responsible for watering the fruit and crops belonging to the lessee. Rather, watering them is the responsibility of the lessee who has undertaken the lease. Umar ibn al-Khattāb leased out the orchard of Usayd ibn al-Hudayr for three years and collected its rent in advance, using it to discharge an outstanding debt. There are many comparable examples in this area.

He was asked:

Is sharecropping valid or not? And what if the sharecropper neglects half a feddan, and the landowner swears, invoking a triple divorce, that he will take compensation for it from the good crop?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

Sharecropping in return for a third, a quarter, or another undivided share of the crop is permissible according to the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the practice of the Rightly Guided Caliphs and other Companions and Successors. This is also the position of the jurists known for their thorough scholarship.

If the worker has been negligent, causing some of the intended yield to be lost, and the owner takes an equivalent amount from another plot and allocates it to him, without committing any injustice, then he has not broken his oath, and no breach of oath is held against him. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14927

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man handed over his land to another man to cultivate, with the crop to be divided equally between them and the seed supplied by the cultivator, not the landowner. Is this permissible as a partnership between them, or is it not permissible?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. This is permissible according to the more correct of the two scholarly views. It accords with the established practice of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his Rightly Guided Caliphs, and his other Companions.

It is authentically reported that he made an agreement with the people of Khaybar for half its yield of crops and fruit, on condition that they cultivate it at their own expense. This was an arrangement for sharing the produce equally, implemented by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the seed supplied by the cultivator rather than the landowner.

His Companions likewise followed this practice after him, including the families of Abū Bakr and Alī ibn Abī Tālib, as well as Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqās and Abdullāh ibn Masʿūd.

Those who opposed this relied on two weak grounds:

First, they assumed that sharecropping was like a contract of hire, whereas it does not fall within the category of

---

## Page 14928

contracts of hire; for the object of a hire contract is the worker’s labour, and the work must be specified. Rather, the two enter into a partnership: one contributes the use of his land, and the other his labour and the use of his cattle, like other partners.

As for the sharecropping practice prohibited by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, an authentic report explains that they used to stipulate that the landowner would receive the crop from a particular plot. That is why he prohibited it.

Those who stipulate that the seed must come from the owner have likened this arrangement to a profit-sharing partnership, in which one party must provide the capital and the other the labour. They assumed that the seed must come from the landowner, regarding both the land and the seed as capital. This is mistaken, for in such contracts the capital returns to its owner, just as capital returns in a profit-sharing partnership, land in sharecropping, and land and trees in an orchard-tending partnership.

When the worker sows the seed and consumes it in the process, without receiving an equivalent amount back, the seed is treated like benefits for which no equivalent is returned. The view of anyone who stipulates that the seed must come from the owner, yet that he is not to recover it, is utterly unsound. If it were capital, he would necessarily be entitled to recover its equivalent, just as that person maintains concerning a profit-sharing partnership.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man owns cultivated land as well as other land, and someone came to cultivate it for him on an equal-sharing basis, with the seed and all other expenses associated with the crop, so that when the harvesters take

---

## Page 14929

anything, the landowner takes an equal amount, as well as half the straw. Is this permissible or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Sharecropping in return for half of what the land produces is permissible, whether the seed comes from the landowner or the worker. This is the correct view, supported by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the practice of his rightly guided caliphs. For:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made an agreement with the people of Khaybar for half of its yield of fruit and crops, on condition that they cultivate it at their own expense.”

This is the position of most of the Companions and their successors. The permissibility of sharecropping on bare land is the position of al-Thawrī, Ibn Abī Laylā, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Abū Yūsuf, Muhammad, the discerning scholars among al-Shāfiʿī’s followers who are versed in hadith, some of Mālik’s followers, and others.

Likewise, according to the sounder of the two views in the school of Ahmad and others, the seed may come from the worker, as in the Prophet’s arrangement with the people of Khaybar, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Comparing this to the capital in a profit-sharing partnership is unsound, for the seed does not return to the person who sows it as the owner’s capital returns to him.

The sharecropping practice prohibited by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was that they entered into agreements stipulating that the owner receive a specified benefit from the land, and this

---

## Page 14930

is invalid by consensus, just as it would be to stipulate a fixed sum of dirhams in a profit-sharing partnership, or the profit from one particular class of goods.

Orchard-tending partnerships, sharecropping, and profit-sharing partnerships are not forms of hire in which the work and remuneration must be specified, for in hire contracts the work itself is the object. Rather, these arrangements are forms of partnership: one party contributes his labor and the other the use of his property, and both share in the gains and losses.

The households of Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, and Ibn Masʿūd all practiced sharecropping. This has been the practice of Muslims from the time of their Prophet until today. It was more widespread among them than leasing land for dirhams and dinars, for it is further removed from injustice and deception, and closer to the justice upon which transactions are founded.

As for the cost of the harvesters, it falls upon whoever the two parties stipulate should bear it. If they stipulate that both should bear the cost, it falls upon both; if they stipulate that one of them should bear it, it falls upon that party. If no stipulation is made, there is disagreement. They may divide both the grain and the straw between them. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who leased land in return for a share of its crop and took possession of it, but did not cultivate it: is the owner entitled to charge him the customary rent for comparable land?

---

## Page 14931

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The validity of this arrangement is disputed. The apparent position of our school is that it is valid, whether it is called a lease or sharecropping. Ahmad upheld its validity in most of his recorded statements and called it a lease. Abū al-Khattāb and others said that it is sharecropping in which the cultivator supplies the seed. Al-Qādī and others, however, upheld its validity while declaring sharecropping in which the cultivator supplies the seed invalid.

If the arrangement is valid, compensation is due according to its validly stipulated terms. Here, however, the agreed consideration is not a debt owed independently of the yield. The average yield is therefore assessed, and the stipulated share of it becomes due. If we classify the arrangement as sharecropping and uphold its validity, compensation should be assessed in the same way, because the substance of the arrangement is the same.

If, however, we declare it invalid and classify it as a lease, there are two views concerning what is due. The first is the customary rent for comparable land; this is the apparent position of our fellow scholars and others. The second is the customary share, and this is the sound conclusion.

Some have answered that this is an invalid lease and that taking possession therefore makes the customary rent for comparable land payable.

---

## Page 14932

He was also asked—may Allah be pleased with him:

What if one party provides land and the other seed, and so forth?

He replied:

Likewise, if two parties enter into an arrangement whereby one provides land and the other seed or cattle, or one provides water and the other grain and grapes, there are two opinions, corresponding to two reported positions from Ahmad. The stronger view is that this is permissible.

The same applies if someone hires another to grind flour for him in return for a third or a quarter of it; to bake a loaf for him in return for a third or a quarter of it; to make clothes for him in return for a third or a quarter of them; to irrigate his crops in return for a third or a quarter of them; or to harvest his fruit in return for a third or a quarter of it. These and similar arrangements are permissible according to the apparent position of Ahmad’s school and the views of other scholars.

Likewise, if someone provides another with his water to irrigate the latter’s cotton or other crops, in return for a quarter or a third of the produce, this too is permissible, whether the water is supplied by this party. This is a form of partnership, not a hire contract, and is comparable to orchard sharecropping and crop sharecropping.

The correct position, upheld by the jurists of hadith, is that crop sharecropping is permissible whether the seed comes from the landowner, the cultivator, or both, and whether it is

---

## Page 14933

bare land or land planted with trees. Likewise, orchard sharecropping is permissible for all kinds of trees. Those who prohibit this suppose that it is a hire contract with an unknown payment. That is not the case; rather, it is a partnership, like a capital-and-labour partnership. Such a partnership accords with legal analogy rather than departing from it, for it belongs to the category of partnerships, not hire contracts, as has been discussed at length elsewhere.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him—about someone who entered into a quarter-share cropping arrangement with another man.

The arrangement was as follows: one person owned the land, another supplied the cattle and seed, and the cultivator supplied the labour. The landowner was to receive half, and the other two the remaining half, with the cultivator receiving a quarter. What if some seed remained in the ground without sprouting, then sprouted in the second year without any further work?

He replied:

If this growth comes from the land and the jointly owned grain, there are two opinions:

The first is that it belongs exclusively to the landowner. The second is that it is divided among them in proportion to the benefit contributed by the land and the grain. This is the sounder of the two opinions.

---

## Page 14934

He was asked:

A man gave his land to someone under a tree-planting partnership for a specified share, on condition that he develop it. The man planted part of the land, but the plantings on the land fell into neglect. Is it permissible to uproot the trees, or not? May the judge compel them to uproot them, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If they fail to fulfil the conditions imposed upon them, the landowner is entitled to terminate the agreement. If the cultivator terminates it, or if the agreement is invalid, the landowner may acquire the planter’s share by paying its value, unless the two agree to uproot it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man planted trees on land with its owner’s permission. The owner then died, leaving heirs, who established the land as an endowment for specified beneficiaries. The beneficiaries and the owner of the trees disputed the rent. What obligation does the landowner bear?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the trees were planted with the owner’s permission

---

## Page 14935

under a loan for use or a lease, and its term has expired or the arrangement was open-ended, the owner of the trees must pay the fair rent. The land is assessed as bare land, without trees, and then assessed with those trees; the amount arrived at is the fair rent. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A soldier received a grant from the sultan consisting of the land-tax revenues from a parcel of land. That land had previously been granted to another soldier, who died—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—after sowing it using his own seed and oxen. The sultan’s revenue official ruled that the new grantee should receive half the crop and the deceased’s heirs the other half, after first taking one-twentieth of the entire crop. He was then to give the heirs of the deceased cultivator one-quarter of his own half, because the sultan reserves one-quarter of the land-tax revenues for the deceased’s heirs, while the grantee receives three-quarters. Is this division permissible, and may the land tax be collected in this manner? If not, what should the ruling be according to the noble Sharia?

Furthermore, the revenue officials ordered him to take from the deceased’s heirs the seed needed for this land in the coming year, as a loan documented by a deed for the purpose of cultivating the granted land, and to repay it to them over two years. Is that permissible or not?

He replied:

This grant is not merely a grant of the land-tax revenues, as the questioner supposed. Rather, it is a grant of the right to derive income from the land. Grants are of two kinds:

A grant conveying ownership

---

## Page 14936

as when uncultivated land is granted to someone who will bring it into cultivation and acquire ownership of it.

And a grant of the right to derive income: this assigns the usufruct of the land to someone who may cultivate it himself, lease it out, or enter into a sharecropping agreement over it, as he wishes.

The grant in question belongs to this second category. These grantees were not merely assigned tax payable on land held by someone, such as the land tax prescribed by the Sharia which the Commander of the Faithful, ʿUmar, imposed on territories conquered by force, or the rents owed by those who lease property belonging to the public treasury. Anyone assigned such revenues has been granted tax income. These grantees, however, were assigned the usufruct itself.

Once this distinction is understood, if a grant terminates while the arrangement is still in progress—whether because the grantee dies or for some other reason—and is reassigned to someone else, the usufruct accruing thereafter belongs to the second grantee, not the first. Thus, if the first grantee had leased out the granted land and his grant subsequently terminated, that lease would also terminate. This is comparable to the termination of a lease granted by the first generation of an endowment’s beneficiaries when entitlement to the endowment passes to the second generation, according to the sounder of the two juristic views.

Accordingly, if the grant was transferred halfway through the term, the second grantee would be entitled to half the usufruct. If it concerned its past yield, he would be entitled to the income from the usufruct. If the revenue officials awarded the second grantee three-quarters of the usufruct due under the grant, and the first one-quarter, because the second had performed three-quarters for a hundred and thereby become entitled to the grant,

---

## Page 14937

for example, by serving for three-quarters of the term over which the usufruct is enjoyed, then they have acted fairly. Now, when the first grantee cultivated the land with his own labour, seed, and oxen, and part of its usufruct became someone else’s entitlement, he became a cultivator on another’s land. Yet he is not a usurper whose crop may lawfully be destroyed. Rather, his crop enjoys legal protection, like a tenant’s—and with even greater reason.

Here the jurists hold three views:

The first is that the crop belongs to the cultivator, who must pay the fair rental value of the usufruct belonging to the second grantee.

The second is that the crop belongs to the landholder, who must reimburse the first grantee for what he spent on his crop. These two views are well known.

When someone cultivates another’s land without permission, does the crop belong to the cultivator, or does it belong to the landholder, who takes it and reimburses the cultivator’s expenses? In the Sunan collections, Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever cultivates people’s land without their permission has no share of the crop, and he bears its expenses.”

There are two views on this, and the issue is well known. The second is the position of Ahmad and others, while the first is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and others.

The cultivator in the case under discussion is not a usurper. Nevertheless, his situation is comparable to that of someone deemed to have cultivated another’s land without permission. He is like someone who trades with wealth he believes to be his own, only to discover that it belongs to someone else. In this matter there is a “third view,” namely the one adopted by the revenue officials.

---

## Page 14938

and it is the ruling that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb gave in a comparable case. It is the soundest of the views. Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī had accumulated funds belonging to the Muslims which he intended to send to ʿUmar. When ʿUmar’s two sons passed by him, he said:

“I cannot give you anything, but I have funds that I wish to send to him. Take them, trade with them, and pay him an equivalent amount. You will thus have benefited, while the funds will have reached him, with you both guaranteeing their repayment.”

They bought merchandise with the funds. When they came to ʿUmar, he asked, “Did he grant everyone in the ʿushr the same terms he granted you two?”

“No,” they replied.

“Then place all the profit in the public treasury,” he said. ʿAbd Allāh remained silent, but ʿUbayd Allāh asked him, “Tell me: had this money been lost, would we not have been liable for it?”

“Yes,” he replied.

“Then how can the profit belong to the Muslims while the liability rests with us?”

ʿUmar paused. The Companions then said to him, “Treat it as a profit-sharing partnership between the two of them and the Muslims: half the profit belongs to them, and half to the Muslims.” ʿUmar acted accordingly.

This is the best of the views disputed by the jurists concerning trading with money entrusted for safekeeping or other funds belonging to someone else. There are four views on this within the school of Ahmad and among other jurists. Does the profit belong to the public treasury, on the grounds that it is … (1) the capital? Or does the profit belong to the trader, because he acquired ownership by undertaking personal liability for the purchase of the goods? Should the two give the profit in charity because it is tainted, or divide it between themselves as in a profit-sharing partnership?

This fourth view is the one ʿUmar acted upon, and it was relied upon by those jurists who invoked it in support of

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 14939

the permissibility of profit-sharing partnerships. The case of sharecropping is similar: a man cultivates land believing it to be his own, but it subsequently emerges that all or part of it belongs to someone else. The crop is therefore divided between them as in a sharecropping arrangement. Sharecropping, in its unqualified form, entails an equal division: each party receives half the crop. Accordingly, the first man is assigned half the crop as the cultivator, while the other half is allocated in return for the granted usufruct. Since the first man is entitled to a quarter of that usufruct, he receives half the crop plus a quarter of the remaining half, on the basis explained above. The second receives three-quarters of that remaining half. This is the fairest opinion concerning such a case.

Indeed, the second grantee actually has a choice: he may demand rent at the going rate from the person who cultivated his land, or he may treat the arrangement as sharecropping, just as he could choose at the outset. As for the view that he may take the crop while reimbursing the first man’s expenditure, that goes further still.

This answer also entails that, in sharecropping, the seed may be supplied by the cultivator. This is undoubtedly the correct view, even though some jurists call it mukhābarah. For it is established in an authentic report:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made an agreement with the people of Khaybar for half the fruit and crops produced by the land, on condition that they cultivate it at their own expense.”

The Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, likewise permitted this, as they themselves practised sharecropping, including the households of Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, and ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, and others.

---

## Page 14940

The mukhābarah prohibited by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was specifically their practice of stipulating that the landowner receive the crop from a particular plot. This is what was prohibited, as explicitly explained in the authentic hadith.

As for the seed stock supplied for the land, it is not simply a loan, as some people suppose. An unrestricted loan becomes the borrower’s property, which he may dispose of as he wishes. Whoever receives this seed stock, however, is required to sow it in the land and may not use it otherwise. It has thus been provided to sustain the land, so that everyone who uses the land, whether grantee or cultivator, may benefit [from it] (1), since the land cannot be properly maintained without it. It is comparable to a water cistern on the land from which people benefit. Hence the saying: “Whoever takes over with seed stock must leave an equivalent stock when he departs.” Likewise, if the cistern is full of water when you arrive, fill it when you leave.

The fact is that the ruler may require those contracting for the land to leave seed stock on it, for this serves the public interest. If the first has left seed stock there and the second needs it, and the responsible authority considers it appropriate to allot the first a proportionate payment for it in accordance with the public interest, that is permissible.

If the established custom is that whoever takes over with seed stock leaves an equivalent stock when he departs, and whoever supplies seed stock of his own receives it back in full at a later date, then the ruler’s grant of the land was subject to this

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I have not found it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Textual Corruptions).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila (the Comprehensive Encyclopedia).

---

## Page 14941

condition, and that is permissible. For he acquired ownership of the crop only through the grant, while the first holder passed on to his heirs whatever he had become entitled to before his death. As for the half-tithe mentioned, its basis has not been stated, so no ruling can be given on it.

The ruler’s allocation of land grants is comparable to his distribution of the Muslim treasury. The ruler’s distribution of public funds, such as fay’ revenues, is not like dividing property among specific co-owners. Property jointly owned by specific individuals, such as an inheritance, is divided among them category by category if it admits division. Otherwise, it is sold and its proceeds divided, according to most jurists, including Mālik, Ahmad and Abū Hanīfah.

The shares are balanced by quantity when the assets are homogeneous, as with goods measured by volume or weight. When they differ, as with portions of land, the shares are balanced by valuation. Countable assets, such as camels, cattle and sheep, may also be divided according to the correct view, with the shares equitably apportioned. As for houses that differ from one another, there is disagreement. Neither of the two co-owners may take a particular category exclusively for himself.

With fay’ revenues, however, the ruler may allocate one category to one group and another category to another. Indeed, according to the correct view, this also applies to spoils of war. If the ruler gives camels to one group and sheep to another, that is permissible.

May the ruler give some recipients of the spoils a larger share because of their greater contribution? Scholars hold two views, the sounder of which permits this. It is authentically reported of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that “he awarded an additional quarter on the outward expedition, after the fifth had been set aside, and an additional third on the return expedition, after the fifth had been set aside.” It is also authentically reported that he awarded additional shares to Salamah ibn al-Akwa‘ and others.

---

## Page 14942

As for fay’ revenues, entitlement to them is determined by a person’s service to the Muslims and also by need. Fighting men have the strongest claim to them, but whether these revenues are reserved exclusively for them is a matter on which there are two views. When they are distributed among fighting men, they must be distributed justly, just as justice is required of every judge and everyone entrusted with distribution. However, if the person distributing or adjudicating is not just, this does not invalidate all his judgments and allocations, according to the correct view upheld by the early generations. Such wholesale invalidation would throw people’s affairs into disorder. Authentic hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanding obedience to those in authority despite their injustice make it clear that when they command what is right, they must be obeyed, even if they are unjust.

Thus, if such a person renders a just judgment and makes a fair distribution, these are acts of justice in which obedience to those in authority is obligatory. If an unjust person distributes an inheritance among its rightful heirs according to the Book of Allah, this is just by the consensus of the Muslims. If he rightfully distributes spoils of war among those entitled to them, this too is just by the consensus of the Muslims. If he rules in favour of a claimant on the basis of sound, unopposed evidence, this is just. A judgment involves commanding, prohibiting and permitting, so he must be obeyed in it.

This applies when the distribution is just. If, however, the distribution involves injustice—for example, giving some people more than they are entitled to and others less than they are entitled to—then this is the preferential treatment mentioned by

---

## Page 14943

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when he said:

“A Muslim must listen and obey in hardship and ease, willingly and unwillingly, and even when others are favoured over him, unless he is commanded to disobey Allah.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ubādah ibn al-Sāmit is reported to have said:

“We pledged allegiance to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to listen and obey in hardship and ease, willingly and unwillingly, and even when others are favoured over us; not to contest authority with those who hold it; and to speak—or stand up for—the truth wherever we may be, fearing no one’s blame for Allah’s sake.”

It is well known that such preferential treatment has persisted throughout Islamic history among rulers and those involved in these affairs. The only exceptions are the Rightly Guided Caliphs and those who followed their example. That being so, when a recipient is given his due or less than his due, he is entitled to it by virtue of that distributor’s allocation. Likewise, if someone distributes an inheritance and gives some of the heirs their rightful shares, they are entitled to those shares by virtue of his allocation. Similarly, if he rules that someone is to receive what is rightfully his, that person may take it on the strength of this judgment.

No one may say, “He acquired it merely by taking possession of it, just as if there had been neither a judge nor a distributor.” For if this claim were accepted, it would invalidate the judgments and grants made by all rulers other than the caliphs. The obligation to obey those in authority would then lapse, since there would be no difference between a judgment or allocation being made and none being made at all.

The unsoundness of this view, both rationally and religiously, is apparent to anyone of understanding. Opening this door would lead to harm greater than the injustice of the wrongdoer. Then everyone would suppose

---

## Page 14944

that what he takes equals his rightful share. Each person considers only his own entitlement, not the entitlements of others, without knowing the total amount of the shared assets or whether he should be allotted this much of their value or less. A person may neither judge his own case nor testify on his own behalf; how, then, can he allocate his own share?

Everyone knows that it is preferable for co-owners to submit to an independent distributor, and for litigants to submit to an independent judge—even if unjust or ignorant—rather than for every litigant to judge his own case and every co-owner to allocate his own share. The harm in the latter arrangement is greater than in the former. Islamic law came to secure and enhance benefits, and to eliminate and reduce harms. It gives precedence to the greater of two goods by forgoing the lesser. This is among the benefits of appointing those in authority. Were matters as the ignorant person supposes, the existence of a ruler would be no different from his absence. No rational person would say this, let alone a Muslim.

Indeed, wise people have said:

“Sixty years under an unjust ruler are better than a single night without a ruler.”

How fine are the words of Abd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak:

“Were it not for rulers, our roads would not be safe … and our weakest would fall prey to our strongest.”

The underlying principle of this issue has been discussed fully elsewhere. Here we have merely drawn attention to enough of it to make the intent of the answer clear. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14945

He was asked:

A village formed part of a man’s land grant. It was taken from him and then granted to two others, after its farmers had sown its lands using produce belonging to the original grantee. One of the two new grantees then requested that his share of the growing crop be partitioned off. Is this permissible, and is such a partition valid? Must the estate remain undivided until the yield is divided, with everyone entitled to a share receiving his due from the entire yield? Or may it be partitioned before the crop ripens?

He answered:

If the other partners’ shares are not diminished, whether in the land or in the crop, they must agree to the request for a partition that causes them no harm. If, however, this would cause harm by diminishing their shares through partition, harm is not to be removed by inflicting harm. Rather, if what is received in exchange for the property to be partitioned can be divided without harm, that should be done.

---

## Page 14946

He was asked:

May the holder of a land grant take a specified portion of the crop? If he agrees to share the crop on the basis of an undivided proportion, and it is known that they have favoured him, may he take more than that, or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Sharecropping in return for a proportional share of the yield is permissible, whether that share is less or more than half.

The four imams and others like them make no distinction between sharecropping for half, a third, two-thirds, or similar proportional shares, such as three-fifths or two-fifths. The permissibility of sharecropping is established by the authentic Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the agreement of the Companions. It is more equitable than tasjīl (registration). If he stipulates that he is to receive half the crop, but they take more than half, he may recover from them an amount equal to the excess.

---

## Page 14947

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had some unlawfully acquired dirhams. He gave them to his father and received lawful dirhams from him in exchange. With these, he purchased something yielding a benefit—whether offspring from camels and sheep or crops from a plot of land—and made use of it. Is this unlawful or not?

He answered:

Whenever someone exchanges unlawful wealth for an equivalent amount, the substitute has the same ruling as what it replaces. If, through his own efforts, he generates an increase, whether profits, earnings or otherwise, the scholars differ concerning it. The most equitable view is that the increase should be apportioned according to the respective contributions of the capital and the worker, as in a profit-sharing partnership. This is what Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—did regarding the money from the public treasury with which his children traded. The same applies to every increase arising from two contributing sources, when the original asset is sold.

He also answered:

The most equitable view in this matter and similar cases is that the crop produced through the contributions of the land, seed, worker and cattle should be apportioned among these contributing sources. The share attributable to what is unlawful should be disbursed to whoever must receive it, and the share attributable to what is lawful should go to whoever is entitled to it, as with all other proceeds arising from jointly contributing sources.

---

## Page 14948

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man holds a land grant from the ruler and has arranged for a farmer to cultivate it in return for half the crop. Is it permissible to have their agreement witnessed? Some accredited witnesses have refused to witness it. If he also stipulates that the farmer provide chickens, pieces of cloth, or other such goods, with the farmer’s consent, is this permissible or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The scholars hold two views concerning entrusting land—whether privately owned, held as a grant, or otherwise—to someone who cultivates it in return for half the crop. However, the unquestionably correct view is that this is permissible. This was the consensus of the Companions: the households of Abū Bakr, Umar and Alī, Abdullah ibn Mas‘ūd, Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās, and others. It has also been the practice of the Muslims since the time of their Prophet.

The Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not prohibit this. Rather, he prohibited stipulating that the landholder receive the crop from a particular plot. Indeed, he contracted with the people of Khaybar for half of the fruit and crops it produced. An authentic hadith establishes that he stipulated that they cultivate it at their own expense. Thus, the correct view is that this arrangement is permissible even when the cultivator supplies the seed; indeed,

---

## Page 14949

this arrangement has a stronger claim to permissibility than leasing out the land. Both are disputed, but leasing involves greater uncertainty: the lessor receives his rent, while the lessee does not know whether he will obtain what he seeks. By contrast, under a half-share arrangement, both parties share in the gains and losses. If Allah causes a crop to grow, both benefit; if no crop grows, both bear the loss. The benefit contributed by one party’s land corresponds to that contributed by the other’s seed, as in a profit-sharing partnership.

In a half-share arrangement, it is not permissible to stipulate that the cultivator provide any specified item, whether chickens or anything else. Witnessing such an arrangement, however, is permissible even if the witness considers it impermissible, since he recognizes it as a disputed matter, and the witness merely testifies to what occurred. This is especially so since the discerning scholars among the followers of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī hold it permissible, as do the jurists of the scholars of hadith.

He was asked:

A holder of a land grant collects his grain from the farmers when the crop is divided. Some of the grain is clean, while some is mixed with impurities. He mixes it together until sowing time, then distributes it among them during that period. What is the ruling on this?

He answered:

If some farmers’ wheat is better than that of others, he may not mix it together. If the wheat is of equal quality and he needs to mix it, there is no harm in doing so.

---

## Page 14950

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A soldier had some uncultivated land. A farmer said to him, “I will cultivate this land for you, with two-thirds for me and one-third for you,” on condition that the farmer pay the soldier a specified amount for that one-third. This condition was stipulated. The soldier then gave the farmer one wasq-measure of flaxseed to sow on that land. The soldier died before the crop matured, whereupon the farmer took possession of the entire crop and withheld the specified amount from the heirs. Is he entitled to do this, given that the condition was not put in writing?

He answered:

Whatever the soldier is entitled to receive, whether a fixed payment, a share of the crop, or anything else, passes to his heirs, regardless of whether the condition was put in writing. Whenever an upright witness, or one whose uprightness has been attested, gives testimony and the claimant supplements that testimony with an oath, judgment is given in the claimant’s favour.

---

## Page 14951

He was asked:

A man is neither a farmer nor accustomed to cultivating land. Is it permissible for anyone to enter into a sharecropping arrangement with him without his consent, or not?

He answered:

No one may compel him to undertake farming that is not obligatory upon him, for that is oppression. Allah Most High says, as His Messenger reported from Him:

“My servants, I have forbidden oppression for Myself and have made it forbidden among you, so do not oppress one another.”

Indeed, compelling such a man is permissible neither under the pure Sharia nor under established governmental practice.

He was asked:

What is the ruling concerning someone who cultivates jointly owned land without his co-owners’ permission or informing them?

He answered:

If the established custom is that whoever cultivates the land receives a specified share and the landowner receives a share, then he must divide the crop grown on the equivalent of his co-owners’ shares between himself and them in the customary manner. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14952

He was asked:

What is the ruling concerning someone who enters into a sharecropping agreement with one of the co-owners of undivided land, for an area corresponding to that co-owner’s share, when the others refuse to cultivate it?

He answered:

If some co-owners refuse to contribute to the expenses required for cultivation, another co-owner may cultivate an area corresponding to his own share and retain exclusively what he grows on that area. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning land jointly owned by two people: one asked the other to cultivate it with him. The other gave permission but then went away, so the first cultivated less than the area corresponding to his share. The first then demanded his payment. What is the ruling?

He answered:

If one co-owner asks the other to cultivate the land with him or agree with him on an allocation of its use, and the other refuses, the first may cultivate an area corresponding to his share and owes his co-owner no rent for doing so. This is because the latter has neglected his obligation, whereas the first

---

## Page 14953

is taking what is rightfully his. This is analogous to a property with a building on it jointly owned by two people: one of them lives there when the former refuses to fulfil his obligations.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman gave someone a sum of dirhams to farm in partnership. He stated that he had planted crops. Later, he paid her forty, saying that this was profit and that her capital remained intact. Then he paid her fifty dirhams, saying, “This is part of your money.” One hundred dirhams remained, apart from the profit. When she demanded them from him, he said, “The forty is included in the hundred; only sixty remain due to you.” Is she to take this amount and receive no profit?

He answered:

If she entrusted the money to him under a profit-sharing investment agreement, and he paid her an amount, saying, “This is from the profit,” she is entitled thereafter to demand her capital. His claim that the additional amount was part of the capital is not accepted. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14954

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A village was endowed for two beneficiaries, with their shares held jointly and undivided. The administrator acting for one beneficiary supplied its farmers with a specified quantity of wheat and other seed for cultivation. The farmers sowed it on the jointly owned land, with nothing contributed on behalf of the other beneficiary. Those representing the other beneficiary now demand a share of the crop produced from the seed that the administrator supplied to the farmers. Are they entitled to this? Is the administrator’s statement to be accepted concerning what he supplied and his claim that it belonged exclusively to one beneficiary? If the yield belongs exclusively to one beneficiary, may anyone contest their entitlement to it?

He answered:

Those representing the other beneficiary are not entitled to share with the owners of the seed as they would have done had they also sown seed. However, if the farmers cannot sow separately because the land is held in undivided ownership and the co-owners refuse to partition it or cooperate, then the entire crop belongs to the owner of the seed, provided that he cultivates an area corresponding to his undivided share.

If he treats the cultivation of the non-participating party’s share as a sharecropping arrangement—with the seed supplied by its owners, the land by the other party, and the labour by the cultivator—the crop is divided among them as it would be had they entered into a partnership for this purpose, according to the prevailing custom in such cases. For the cultivator is not a usurper; rather, he is authorized by custom to cultivate the land.

---

## Page 14955

He was asked:

A man entered into a partnership to cultivate a plot of land, but negligently delayed preparing it beyond the proper time, resulting in a loss of half. Is his partner entitled to compensation for the shortfall caused by his negligence?

He answered:

If a partner has been negligent with his partner’s property—for example, by sowing seed outside its proper season, or in land that does not meet the description they agreed upon, or something similar—the negligent partner is liable. At a minimum, he must restore the equivalent of the original capital. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A landowner had grain in his land from the previous year, known as zarīʿ. He engaged a labourer to water it on condition that one-third of the crop be shared between them. What is the ruling on this arrangement?

He answered:

This is a valid arrangement, and the labourer is entitled to the share stipulated for him, provided that the purpose is to obtain the crop through his labour, whether the work required is little or much. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14956

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling concerning someone who has carried out cultivation work on land but has derived no benefit from it?

He answered:

He is entitled to the value of that work after rescission, pending a ruling on whether the arrangement is binding or not. His case is unlike that of a worker engaged to tend an orchard for a share of its fruit, because there is no common basis for drawing an analogy between them.

The distinction is that the subject of the orchard-tending contract is the fruit, which does not yet exist, rather than the work. If he withdraws from the contract before its subject comes into existence, he is entitled to nothing from it. The jurists of our school have explicitly stated that, once the fruit has appeared, he remains entitled to his share of it and is obliged to complete the work.

In a partnership, however, the subjects of the contract are the capital and the work. The capital must already exist. As for the work, if any portion of it has been performed, payment for it is due even if the contract is invalid; upon rescission, a person hired to perform work is entitled to wages for his labour.

---

## Page 14957

He was also asked:

A man farms with a cow, using his own earnings, on the sultan’s land or land held by a grantee. He pays the tithe on both his own share and the grantee’s share. Is it permissible for him to steal something without the grantee’s knowledge, or not?

He answered:

If the farmer is a sharecropper—for example, working for a third, a quarter, or half of the crop—he is required to pay the tithe only on his own share. As for the grantee’s share, its tithe is payable by the grantee.

Anyone who says that the farmer must pay the entire tithe while the grantee remains entitled to his share of the crop has contradicted the consensus of the Muslims.

However, scholars hold two views concerning sharecropping:

The first is that it is invalid, that the entire crop belongs to the owner of the seed, who must pay the entire tithe, and that the landowner is entitled to the value of the land. Thus, any land grantee who holds that the farmer must pay the entire tithe must, to be consistent with his position, give him the entire crop and demand from him the value of the land.

The second view—which is the correct one, in accordance with the established Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah

---

## Page 14958

, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Sunnah of his Rightly Guided Caliphs, and which reflects established practice—is that sharecropping is valid. Accordingly, the grantee is entitled to his share and must pay its zakat, while the farmer is entitled to his share and must pay its zakat. If they require the farmer to pay the tithe owed by the soldier, then he is to pay it on the soldier’s behalf from the soldier’s own property, as is evident. This is a clear entitlement about which the scholars do not disagree; it is not a concealed entitlement that the soldier could deny. Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Hind:

“Take what reasonably suffices for you and your child.”

The obligation to provide maintenance for one’s wife and child is a manifest right that Abū Sufyān could not deny.

This is comparable to his saying:

“Return what has been entrusted to you to the one who entrusted you with it, and do not betray someone who has betrayed you.”

In another narration:

“We have neighbours who take every single thing of ours they can find. If we gain access to anything of theirs, may we take it?”

He replied: “Return what has been entrusted to you to the one who entrusted you with it, and do not betray someone who has betrayed you.”

For the entitlement here is concealed and is not immune from injustice. Thus, taking something without a manifest entitlement constitutes a betrayal. And Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 14959

Chapter on Leasing

Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leased another man land containing fruit-bearing trees and open ground for a specified rent and a specified term. The open ground alone is not worth the full rent; rather, part of the rent corresponds to the open ground and part is in exchange for the fruit. They drew up a document stipulating a lease of the land and a fruit-sharing arrangement for tending the fruit-bearing trees. Is this valid or not?

If it is valid, does it include fruit-bearing walnut trees and everything else that bears fruit, on the grounds that they are fruit-bearing? May the lessor include some and exclude others, even though they are fruit-bearing, or not? If an agreement to tend trees in return for a share of their fruit is a matter of widespread practical concern, and one judge considers it permissible, may another judge invalidate it or not?

He replied:

Leasing orchards containing both land and trees for a number of years is valid. This is the position adopted by Ibn ‘Aqīl and others. It is established that the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, leased out an orchard belonging to Usayd ibn al-Hudayr for three years after his death and used the proceeds to pay off his debt. There are many such instances, which this fatwa does not allow room to set out in detail.

---

## Page 14960

Accordingly, these fixed-payment arrangements for the winter orchards of Damascus, which contain both land and trees, are valid. Even if the written document stipulates a lease of the land and a fruit-sharing arrangement for tending the trees, what the parties intended and agreed upon is the fixed-payment arrangement mentioned above. What counts in contracts is the terms agreed upon by the contracting parties, and their intentions are taken into account. The transaction involving the sale of fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent, which the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade, is the sale of the fruit alone, as vineyard produce is sold in Damascus, with the seller remaining responsible for the cultivation and work. These fixed-payment arrangements, by contrast, resemble leases.

He was asked:

Someone leased out open land amounting to four shares of the orchard farm and the circular reed bed. Is it permissible for him to include the reed bed in the lease of the open land corresponding to his aforementioned share?

He replied:

It is permissible to lease land where reeds grow so that the tenant may plant reeds there. Likewise, it is permissible to lease a reed bed so that the tenant may tend and water it. The ground containing its roots is comparable to land that someone waters so that herbage will grow for him without sowing seed.

---

## Page 14961

He was asked:

A man had a lease registered for land that he would plant with flax in the first year and broad beans in the second. The lessor sought an additional payment because he had planted it with flax. What must the tenant pay?

He replied:

If he leased it on condition that he plant a particular kind of grain, he may not plant anything more harmful to the land. If he does plant something more harmful, the lessor is entitled to demand compensation from him. But if he leased it to plant whatever he wished, he may do so, and no additional payment is due from him for planting whatever he wishes. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man leased the land of an orchard and entered into a fruit-sharing arrangement with its owner for tending the trees. The owner then cut down some of the fruit-bearing trees. Is he permitted to cut them down before the lease expires? Must he pay the tenant the value of their fruit?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he cuts them down, the payment due is reduced in proportion to the reduction in the benefit to which the tenant is entitled. Although this arrangement is worded as a lease of

---

## Page 14962

the land and a fruit-sharing arrangement for tending the trees, the payment is in fact intended to cover both. The tenant agreed to pay only in order to obtain the fruit of the trees as well as cultivate the land. The scholars have differed over the validity of this contract. Whether it is considered valid or invalid, whatever trees are lost, the corresponding portion of the payment is no longer due, whether the owner cut them down or they were lost otherwise. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leased the land of an orchard from Mashārif al-Ajnās for a specified period, then died, leaving children. The rent was payable in instalments of twenty dirhams each year. The deceased tenant’s children have been asked to pay the entire rent immediately. Can they be required to pay the full rent, or should it be collected from them in its annual instalments?

He replied:

In these circumstances, his children are not required to pay the entire rent immediately. However, if the owners of the land do not trust them to discharge this obligation, they may require them to provide someone who will guarantee payment of the rent as its instalments fall due.

This is clear according to the view that a deferred debt does not become due upon the debtor’s death. As for the view that it does become due upon his death … (1), the same applies here, according to the

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 14963

sounder of the scholars’ two views. For the heir who inherits the right to use the property must pay the rent for the use he enjoys. Thus, if the deceased owed debts, the heir could not reserve that use for himself while treating the rent as a claim competing with those of the creditors, on the grounds that it is a debt owed by the deceased, like the purchase price owed under an effective sale.

This is comparable to the right of use passing to a purchaser or the recipient of a gift, as when someone sells the land, gives it away, or leaves it to be inherited. From the time of transfer, responsibility for the land falls upon the purchaser, the recipient of the gift, or the child, according to the sounder of the scholars’ two views. This is also the established practice among Muslims: they require the purchaser and the heir to pay the land rent in instalments, rather than demanding the entire rent from the seller or the deceased’s estate.

This is because entitlement to rent is not finally established until the corresponding use has been enjoyed. If that use becomes unavailable before it is enjoyed, the rent ceases to be due, by agreement. Hence, the position of Abū Hanīfah and others is that ownership of the rent is acquired not through the contract, but as the use is enjoyed, and the right to demand payment arises only progressively. For this reason, he held that the lease terminates upon death.

Although al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad held that ownership of the rent is acquired through the contract, and that the right to demand it arises when the property itself is handed over, there is no dispute that liability for it arises only upon enjoyment of the use. Nor is there any dispute that it ceases to be due if the use becomes unavailable before it is enjoyed. Likewise, there is no dispute that, when payment is deferred, it cannot be demanded until the agreed due date.

Thus, if the heir provides a guarantor, yet payment that was not due until a later date is brought forward while his enjoyment of the use to which he is entitled remains deferred, this is an injustice to him, contrary to the justice that is

---

## Page 14964

the foundation of transactions involving an exchange. If the heir does not agree to become liable for the rent, and the lessor says, “I will not make the use available to you so that you may enjoy his entitlement to it,” then requiring the heir to pay the rent immediately from the estate, while enjoyment of the use remains deferred, plainly entails injustice to him.

If the leased property is an endowment or the like, its administrator has no right to collect all the rent in advance. Even stipulating this would not make it permissible. For if he does not own the future benefits and is entitled to their rent only as they arise in the future, collecting it in advance without any need for repairs amounts to taking something to which the endowment’s beneficiary is not yet entitled.

He also answered:

Payment of the rent in advance is not required according to the sounder of the scholars’ two views, especially if the rented property is an endowment. Collecting rent in advance for an endowment is permissible only for repairs and similar needs, because successive generations of beneficiaries are entitled to its benefits. Each group is entitled to the rent for the benefits arising during its own time. If they receive payment in advance for future use, they will have taken compensation for something in the endowment to which they are not entitled, and this is impermissible.

However, if those entitled to the property ask the tenant’s heirs to provide a guarantor for the rent, they have that right. The sum remains a liability of the heirs, backed by a knowledgeable guarantor for the endowment’s beneficiaries in respect of its occupant. Moreover, even if the property were not an endowment, the debt would not fall due upon the debtor’s death. The same applies according to those who hold that debts do fall due upon death, on the more evident of their two views, since they distinguish leases from other transactions, just as they distinguish land held under a ground-rent arrangement when it is sold or

---

## Page 14965

inherited, the ground rent is payable by the purchaser or heir, and they have no right to collect it from the seller or the deceased’s estate, according to the more evident of their two views.

He was asked:

A man rented an orchard for ten years and received the rental sum. He then died after five years of the term had elapsed, leaving five years remaining on the lease. He had heirs, who were evicted a year after his death. Is the owner permitted to terminate the orphans’ lease, or not?

He answered:

According to the vast majority of scholars, the lessor has no right to terminate the lease merely because the tenant has died. Some, however, hold that the rent owed by the tenant becomes immediately due upon his death and is collected from his estate; if he leaves no estate, the lessor may terminate the lease.

Others hold that the rent does not become immediately due if the heirs secure its payment with collateral or a guarantor. Rather, they must pay it to him as the deceased used to do. This is the more evident of the two views. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14966

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Some people live in a village near the mountain in the Fayyūm region, where half is seen in some years. This year, a three-year lease of the local land was drawn up for the village elders before the previous lease had expired and before the crops had been cleared from the land. Is this lease valid?

He answered:

If they were wrongfully compelled to enter into the lease, it is neither valid nor binding, without any disagreement among the imams. If, however, they leased it voluntarily or were rightfully compelled to do so, while the land was still leased to others, this is called a lease commencing at a future date. This has been the practice of Muslims in most times and places, for, as with a contract of sale, there is nothing in it that would invalidate the lease. It makes no difference whether enjoyment of the benefit begins at the time of the contract or not.

The fact that the tenant does not take possession immediately after concluding the contract does not invalidate it. Taking possession follows what the contract requires and entails. If it requires possession immediately afterward, possession must be taken immediately afterward; if it requires possession to be deferred, possession must be taken when the contract requires it, since what is taken into possession

---

## Page 14967

under the contract is not something the Lawgiver has required to be delivered in a particular manner; rather, this is governed by what the two parties have stipulated in the contract. Accordingly, if someone sells date palms that have not been pollinated, their fruit belongs to the seller according to Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Imam Ahmad, as indicated by the Sunnah. The seller may enter the property to attend to his fruit, even though this is inconsistent with complete possession.

Likewise, if someone sells a married slave woman, the right to marital relations remains with her husband and is not included in what the purchaser takes possession of for himself, by agreement of the four imams. The same applies to leased property according to most scholars. For this reason, groups of these scholars consider it valid to continue enjoying an asset’s benefits in cases of sale, gift, endowment, manumission and the like, as indicated by the hadith … (1). This is the position of Mālik and Ahmad.

Thus, when possession of leased property is handed over, it remains the lessor’s responsibility with respect to its benefit, even while it yields income through the tenant’s use. If it is destroyed by a natural calamity, the lessor bears the loss, by agreement of the Muslims. Mālik, Ahmad and others hold the same view concerning the sale of fruit when a calamity strikes it.

In short, no contract is forbidden except one prohibited by a scriptural text, scholarly consensus, or an analogy corresponding in meaning to what a text or consensus establishes. None of these grounds exists in a lease commencing at a future date. If someone leases land containing crops belonging to another person, those crops remain there for their owner at the prevailing rent, just as they would remain had the land not been leased. Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Omitted in the original.

---

## Page 14968

He was asked:

A man rented a shop, then someone else came and offered a higher rent for the shops and was given preference over him. May the lease of the tenant who rented the single shop be terminated, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If he rented the premises from the owner, his agent or his guardian, no one may accept a higher offer over his or evict him before his lease expires, even if there is no written agreement or witnesses. Indeed, if the lessor says, “Go and have a lease drawn up in your name,” and the tenant has the lessor’s agreement to the lease witnessed, and the lessor allows him to occupy the premises, this constitutes a binding lease. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man offered a higher rent than some people for a house so that he could live in it. Does he incur sin by doing so? Must he be subjected to discretionary punishment for this?

He replied:

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is not lawful for a Muslim to make a competing offer against his brother’s offer, or to propose marriage over his brother’s proposal.”

Thus, if

---

## Page 14969

the landlord had already settled on letting the premises to someone, no one else may offer a higher rent. How much more so when that person is already living there and continuing to occupy the premises! Whoever does this deserves discretionary punishment. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man rented a house next to a bad neighbour. The tenant went to the landlord and said, “I am not willing to have him as my neighbour. Either move him elsewhere or return my rent.”

The landlord replied, “I will move him today.” The tenant then swore an oath invoking triple divorce that if the neighbour did not move that day, he would not live in the house. The tenant did not move out of the house. He then requested the rent, but it was not given to him.

He replied:

If the matter is as described, something like this constitutes a defect in the property. If the tenant was unaware of it when the contract was concluded, he is entitled to terminate the lease, and no rent is due from him from the time of termination. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14970

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man owns a property whose fair rent is five dirhams. He gives the tenants money to help them financially, and they pay a higher rent. Is this permissible or not?

He replied:

If he lends someone ten dirhams on condition that he rent his shop from him for more than the fair rent, this is impermissible by Muslim consensus. Indeed, even if this were arranged between them without a stipulation, it would be invalid and forbidden according to most scholars.

(As authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he said:

“It is not lawful to combine a loan with a sale, to impose two conditions in a sale, to make a profit from something for which one has not assumed liability, or to sell what one does not have.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “This is an authentic hadith.”) (*)

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus forbade selling to someone while also lending to him, because the borrower would favour the lender in the sale on account of the loan. How much more objectionable, then, if he stipulates, along with the loan, that the borrower rent from him and favour him, when he has nothing! If the debtor is in hardship, he must be given time until he is able to pay.

Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains of interest, if you are believers.”

“If you do not, then be warned of war from Allah and His Messenger. But if you repent, you may retain your principal, neither wronging nor being wronged.”

“If the debtor is in hardship, then grant him time until he is able to pay; and for you to

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 225):
> It appears that the word “hadith” was omitted before “sahīh” (authentic), so that the phrase should read, “as established in the authentic hadith.” The customary expression “as established in al-Sahīh” refers to reports found in either of the two Sahīh collections, those of al-Bukhārī and Muslim, whereas this hadith appears in neither. After quoting the hadith, the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—mentioned that al-Tirmidhī recorded it. Alternatively, by “al-Sahīh,” the Shaykh may have intended the usage of some scholars who also apply this designation to the Sunan collections, provided the hadith in question is not defective. Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 14971

remit the debt as charity is better for you, if you only knew.”

“And fear a Day when you will be returned to Allah. Then every soul will be paid in full for what it has earned, and none will be wronged.”

He was asked:

A soldier has an allotment, and someone compelled him to lease it to him. He leased it under duress for two hundred dirhams, then showed that it was worth four thousand dirhams. Is this lease valid or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he compelled him to enter into the lease without right, it is invalid. If he deceived him, the soldier has the right to rescind the lease. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man collected money for someone at a rate of six dirhams per thousand, whereas the customary rate is twelve dirhams. He incurred expenses in collecting it and was misled by the stipulated terms. What is his position?

He replied:

If the hirer deceived and misled the person he hired into accepting less than the customary rate, with a disparity greater than people ordinarily accept, the latter has the right to demand the customary remuneration.

---

## Page 14972

He was asked:

A man leased a property to another man for a fixed term. Towards the end of that term, someone else offered a higher rent, so he leased it to him. The first tenant objected, saying, “This property is under lease to me.” Is he entitled to object?

He replied:

If he leased it for the period following the first tenant’s lease, the first tenant has no right to object. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man owns shops occupied by people who have no lease from the owner. Others have now offered several times more for the shops than the current occupants. May the owner lease the shops to them and accept the higher rent?

The Answer

He replied:

If they are unjust usurpers who have occupied the premises without the owner’s permission, evicting such people does not require an offer of higher rent. Rather, they must leave even before any higher rent is offered, and the owner may evict them before such an offer. Nor

---

## Page 14973

is it lawful for the owner to demand a stipulated rent from them; they owe only the fair rental value.

If the lessor is the trustee of an endowment or the guardian of an orphan, allowing them to remain when he could evict them constitutes injustice and transgression on his part. This impugns his integrity and his authority to act in that capacity.

If, however, they occupy the premises according to the customary practice of tenants—for example, someone approaches the owner and says, “Lease me such-and-such premises for such-and-such an amount,” and the owner replies, “Go and have your agreement witnessed”—then the tenant, but not the lessor, has witnesses attest to his undertaking, and the premises are handed over to him. When the occupant wishes to leave, the owner does not allow him to do so. This constitutes a valid lease under Islamic law.

Whoever says, “This is not a valid lease under Islamic law; the occupant may not leave without the owner’s permission, yet the owner may evict him whenever he wishes,” has contradicted the consensus of the Muslims. If the lease is valid under Islamic law, it is binding on both parties; if it is invalid, it is invalid for both parties. Whoever makes it binding on the tenant but terminable at will by the lessor has contradicted the consensus of the Muslims.

If the lessor is the trustee of an endowment or an orphan’s property, hands the premises over to the occupant, instructs him to have a lease drawn up recording his obligations, and demands both the lease document and the stipulated rent, yet says, “I have not granted him a valid lease under Islamic law,” this impugns his integrity and his authority to act in that capacity. For the jurists hold two views concerning a valid lease under Islamic law:

---

## Page 14974

The first is that a lease or hiring contract is concluded through whatever people customarily regard as such. Thus, if someone hands his food to a cook who cooks for payment, or his clothes to a washer who washes for payment, or entrusts work to a weaver, tailor, or similar craftsman who customarily works for payment, they are entitled to fair remuneration. Likewise, if someone enters a bathhouse, boards a boat, or rides an animal, as is customary with mounts and conveyances made available for hire, the provider is entitled to the fair customary charge.

How much more so, then, if someone says, “Lease it to me for such-and-such an amount,” and the other replies, “Go and draw up a lease,” whereupon he draws it up and the premises are handed over to him? According to these jurists, this is a valid lease under Islamic law. This is the view of most jurists, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, Imam Ahmad, and others.

The second view is that a particular form is indispensable, just as a similar requirement has been stipulated for sales. This is the position of those among al-Shāfiʿī’s followers who hold this view. Anyone who believes this must not impose a rental obligation except in this manner. If someone believes that rent can be validly stipulated only in this manner, yet leases the premises in the customary way, hands them over, and demands the stipulated rent, then, when an increase is in question, claims that no lease exists, his claim is not to be accepted. This is injustice: if he adheres to a legal school, he must adhere to it both when it serves his interests and when it works against them.
