As for holding the lease to be valid when it serves his interests, but invalid when it works against them, this is neither acceptable nor permissible, by the consensus of the Muslims. Whoever persists in such conduct is unjust by the agreement of the Muslims. Indeed, he is a sinful person, disqualified from giving testimony or exercising such authority.

---

## Page 14975

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man was renting a nearby plot of land. While he was away travelling, someone bought the house adjoining the land he was renting, built it up, and incorporated it into his own house. What must be done?

He replied:

(*) He has no right to seize the rented land along with other property or incorporate it into his house. By doing so, he is an unjust usurper. The tenant may either terminate the lease on this account, in which case his obligation to pay rent lapses, or continue the lease and demand payment from the usurper for his use of the land.

He may also choose whether to leave the building on the land or have it taken down, if this falls within the terms of his lease. If it does not fall within those terms, he may not act with respect to the land without the owner’s permission. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd writes (p. 226):
> The beginning of the answer is missing; it appears to have read “laysa lahu” (he has no right). And Allah Most High knows best.

## Page 14976

He was asked:

A man appointed another as his agent to rent property for him, let property on his behalf, sell on his behalf, and purchase for him. The agent rented a share in a village for his principal for a specified term under a valid, binding lease. He then agreed to cancel the lease during its term, although the principal had not authorized him to agree to its cancellation. Is this cancellation by mutual agreement valid? Does the lease remain valid as originally concluded, with its benefits belonging to the principal? Is the lessor entitled to the rent in these circumstances?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the tenant cannot collect the rent to which he is entitled, he may cancel the lease, just as when a purchaser cannot recover the price, if he requests cancellation in these circumstances. As for the tenant’s leasing to the agent, he had done what was required of him. This is not one of those permissible cancellations by mutual agreement that require the principal’s permission. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14977

He was asked:

A group holds a land grant containing unused land. They authorized someone to let it, and he leased it for thirty years without consulting the grant-holders about this thirty-year term. Is this lease valid or not?

He replied:

This lease is valid only if the grant-holders authorized it, expressly or implicitly. Mere general permission to let the land—which customarily means a lease of one or two years, or thereabouts—cannot be understood as permission to lease it for such a lengthy term. The lease is therefore not valid on the basis of that permission alone.

He was asked:

A man holds a land grant, as attested by his official grant document. He leased certain parts of it to someone to cultivate and benefit from for a specified term. The grant he held was then transferred to someone else. Is the original lease valid? If it is valid, is it binding upon the second grant-holder, or is it to be terminated? May the grant-holder prevent those leasing the land from benefiting from it?

---

## Page 14978

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, the original lease is valid. However, the second grant-holder may uphold it if he wishes. Indeed, the land became his from the moment it was granted to him, so he may lease it to that tenant or decline to do so. If the tenant has crops growing on it, he is to allow them to remain at the prevailing rent until they mature. If the tenant has neither tangible property nor any benefit in the land, he is entitled to nothing.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man held a land grant. Someone approached him and asked to lease the agricultural land, and he leased it to that person without inspecting it; he also inquired about it. The tenant had told the other man, “Unless you lease out your land, it will lie fallow.” Fearing that the land would lie fallow, the soldier leased it without inspecting it. Then someone else from the locality came and informed the soldier, “The tenant has leased your land for less than its rental value, for the partners’ land is registered at a higher rent than this.” May the soldier cancel the written lease and lease the land to someone else for a year at its proper rental value, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the tenant deceived the lessor—for example, by giving him information about the land that would diminish its rental value when the facts were otherwise—the lessor may cancel the lease.

---

## Page 14979

The same applies if he told him that no one there wished to lease it when there were, in fact, prospective tenants, and in similar cases. Allah knows best.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A soldier leased agricultural land from an emir, who subsequently ceased to hold the land grant. The tenant chose to terminate the lease, but the newly appointed emir came and demanded tahdīr from him. Is he obliged to comply?

He replied:

When the land grant passes to another person, the lease terminates from the moment of its transfer, for the right to use the land accruing thereafter had belonged to neither the first nor the second. The grant-holder may lease it out or decline to do so, and the tenant may lease it from him or decline to do so. Neither may compel the other to enter into a lease, nor may either demand tahdīr from the other.

---

## Page 14980

He was asked:

A man leased some land, after which an unjust levy was imposed on the locality, and he was asked to contribute towards it. Is the tenant obliged to pay anything or not?

He replied:

Unjust levies are not binding on either party. However, if they are imposed on the crops, they are collected from the owner of the crops; if they are imposed on the property, they are collected from the property, unless the tenant has been made responsible for them by stipulation. Thus, if the stipulated terms did not include such a levy among the tenant’s obligations, and it was imposed on the property rather than the crops, it is collected from the landowner. If it was imposed on the crops, it is collected from the tenant. If it was imposed without specifying either, the customary practice in such cases is followed.

He was asked—may Allah be pleased with him:

An emir took over a locality that was leased to its headman. Some of the land was planted with cane, and the cane remained covered by the tenant’s lease from before the emir assumed possession of the land grant until he relinquished it. So, if

---

## Page 14981

the land planted with cane remained covered by the tenant’s lease from before the emir assumed possession until he relinquished it, does the lease cease to have legal effect, or does it remain in force?

He replied:

A lease of the land granted by its land-grant holder is valid, and he may lease it to someone who will cultivate cane or other crops. Likewise, his tenant may sublet it to someone else under the terms of his own lease.

If that land-grant holder dies or his grant is reassigned, the new holder is not bound by the former holder’s lease. However, he may not uproot the tenant’s crops and cane without compensation. Rather, he has the option of allowing the tenant’s crops and cane to remain at a newly agreed rent, whether equal to, lower than, or higher than the original rent, as the two parties mutually agree. He may not, however, compel the tenant to pay more than the fair rental value.

If the owner of the cane and crops leases the land, the lease is valid, since he is able to benefit from it. If someone else leases it, that too is permissible according to the correct view. That person then takes the lessor’s place: he may allow the crops and cane to remain in return for the fair rental value, or he may lease the land to their owner with his consent.

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14982

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man holds a qīrāt share in some land and leases it to someone for 160 irdabbs—80 irdabbs less than others charge—before irrigation has reached it. Is the lease valid before irrigation reaches the land? May he demand its rental value?

He replied:

If these lands are ordinarily irrigated, leasing them before they have been irrigated is valid according to the jurists generally. The disagreement is confined to the school of al-Shāfi'ī, whose apparent position permits leasing such land, as do the other imams. The unqualified wording concerning the contract found in some of his books has been explained by the leading scholars of his school—may Allah be pleased with them.

Land in Egypt has always been leased before irrigation reached it, throughout the times of the early generations and the imams. None of them objected on account of the delay in irrigation.

If he demands an increase, he is entitled only to the stipulated rent. If, however, he was deceived, that is another matter, which the questioner should clarify so that it can be answered.

---

## Page 14983

He was also asked:

A man leased out land forming part of his land grant for a specified term. The lessee took possession of the land, and the lessor received part of the rent. The lessee then recovered the rent he had paid to the lessor and terminated the lease. He subsequently stated that he had ploughed part of the land and demanded that the lessor pay for the ploughing. Is the lessor entitled to an amount equivalent to the cost of ploughing merely on the lessee’s statement, or not? May the lessor rescind the lease without a valid basis in Islamic law?

He replied:

If the lessee rescinded the lease after taking possession of the land, then, provided that they mutually agreed to cancel the lease or that he rescinded it on valid grounds, he must pay rent proportionate to the duration of his possession of the land, and he is entitled to the customary value of his ploughing.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning the trustee of an endowment or an orphan’s property: may he hand over premises belonging to the endowment or the orphan’s property for someone to occupy without a lease valid under Islamic law? And if someone calls witnesses to attest to his declaration that he has leased specified premises from the endowment’s managing agent for a specified term

---

## Page 14984

at a specified rent, paid the rent to the managing agent, took possession of the premises from them and occupied them for a time, and they then demanded the rent already paid, may the trustee make this lease binding on the tenant but not binding on himself and his deputies? May he invoke it to prevent the tenant from leaving when he wishes to leave and demand the rent specified in it, while accepting any higher offer from someone who outbids him? If this is impermissible and the trustee persists in it, does that compromise his integrity and his standing as trustee? Must he lease the endowment property or the orphan’s property under a valid lease, or not?

He replied:

He may not hand over endowment property, an orphan’s property, or any other property that he administers by virtue of his trusteeship except under a lease valid under Islamic law. He may not hand it over to the tenant under an invalid lease. Likewise, an agent acting for his principal may not hand over property he has been commissioned to lease except under a lease valid under Islamic law.

The trustee may not make the lease binding on the tenant but revocable by the lessor, for this contradicts the consensus of the Muslims. Rather, if he holds that leases, sales and similar transactions are valid when concluded through customary means—as the majority hold—he may hand over the property through an arrangement customarily recognized as a lease. If, however, he holds that sales, leases and similar transactions are valid only through a verbal formula, he must not hand over the property unless he has concluded the lease verbally. Likewise, he must not hand over anything he has sold from an orphan’s property or other property unless he has concluded a sale valid under Islamic law. Thus, whoever considers a sale concluded by mutual exchange without a verbal formula permissible may hand over the property on the basis of such a sale. This is the view

---

## Page 14985

held by the majority of the imams and followed in Muslim practice from the time of their Prophet to the present day.

Whoever holds that a sale is not valid without a verbal formula from both parties may not, while holding that belief, hand over an orphan’s property except under a valid contract, such as a lease or a sale. In leases, sales and similar contracts—which the majority permit without a verbal formula, whereas some scholars permit them only with one—everyone must act consistently with the view he holds, whether it works to his advantage or against him. No one may adopt one view to determine his rights and the other to determine his obligations.

An example is someone who holds that, when he is a neighbor, he is entitled to a neighbor’s right of preemption, but when he is the purchaser, he need not honor that right. Another is someone who holds that, when he is one of the maternal half-siblings in the shared-inheritance case known as al-Himāriyya, the full siblings are excluded, but when he is one of the full siblings, he is entitled to share with the maternal half-siblings. Likewise, someone may hold that, when he is the plaintiff, judgment should be given in his favor on the basis of an oath referred back to him, and when he is the claimant, judgment should be given in his favor on the basis of one witness and an oath. There are many similar examples.

By agreement of the Muslims, no one may adopt such an approach to a disputed question. This amounts to permitting himself what he forbids someone in the same position, and forbidding that person what he permits himself; imposing obligations on another person in the same position that he does not impose on himself; and claiming rights for himself against others that he does not recognize for someone in the same position. It is necessarily known in the religion of Islam—indeed, in every religion—that this is impermissible. Whoever believes it permissible is a disbeliever. Indeed, whoever holds to the validity of

---

## Page 14986

a sale concluded by exchange without a verbal formula, and similar leases that customary practice recognizes as sales and leases, holds that such a contract is valid whether concluded by himself or by others. Whoever considers it invalid considers it invalid whether concluded by himself or by others.

Thus, if the trustee acting as lessor holds either of these two views, he must abide by it, whether it works to his advantage or against him. If he considers this contract invalid, he may neither hand over the leased property, demand the stipulated rent, nor [prevent] (1) the tenants from leaving. He would be like someone who hands property over to a usurper: he would be liable for whatever perishes while in the possession of the person who has taken control, just as if he had handed over his own property under an invalid contract that he himself considers invalid.

If, however, he considers this contract valid, he may hand over the property and demand the stipulated rent. He may not accept a higher offer over that of the tenant, nor evict him before the lease expires without a ground recognized by Islamic law that warrants rescission.

Whenever the trustee insists on treating the contract as invalid with respect to the tenant, valid with respect to himself, and nonbinding with respect to the tenant, he is unjust and oppressive. This impugns his fitness to serve as trustee and his uprightness. He must lease whatever he lets under a valid lease. By agreement of the Muslims, he may not enter into a lease that he knows to be invalid. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I have not found it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Textual Corruptions).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for the Shāmila Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 14987

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man responsible for a share of an endowment who owed someone a debt. He leased the estate to that creditor for three years, offsetting the rent against the debt he owed him, this being the requirement of the legal school followed by the endowment’s founder. He owed another man a debt as well. This second creditor had him confined in the governing authorities’ prison for a time, until imprisonment had broken him, and swore that he would not release him until he leased him the share. Because of the imprisonment and threats he suffered, he leased him the aforementioned share. The creditor was already leasing another share, and took possession from the beginning of the term. This lease was for five years, while the amount of the debt was the same.

Should effect be given to the first lease, which accords with the founder’s stipulation and provides a higher rent, or to the second, which was obtained through coercion and compulsion, is below the proper rental value, and contravenes the founder’s stipulation? If he has already collected revenue from it, is there recourse against the first tenant or not? Once the first tenant’s term has expired, who has the authority to lease it?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he had leased it under a valid lease, then any further lease he granted for that same period, or part of it, before the existing lease expired would be invalid, whether the lessor entered into it voluntarily or was coerced. This latter tenant would be acting wrongfully by taking possession of the property and drawing revenue from it. The first tenant would have the choice between rescinding

---

## Page 14988

the lease, with his liability for rent ceasing from the time of rescission, and remaining liable under it, paying the rent, and demanding compensation from this wrongdoer for the use of the property.

He was asked:

About a house endowed for the benefit of a minor and an adult man. The founder’s father leased it forty years ago under coercion and compulsion from an influential man. Is the father’s lease valid with respect to his adult son? The son saw that his father was being coerced and held under guard. Wishing to free him from the wrongdoer who had forced him to grant the lease, the son formally declared before witnesses that he ratified it. Is this witnessed declaration valid? And is a forty-year lease valid?

He replied:

If someone is wrongfully coerced into granting a lease or wrongfully coerced into ratifying it, it is not valid. By the consensus of the Muslims, someone subjected to wrongful coercion is not bound by his sale, lease, or ratification. As for leasing an endowment for this length of time, scholars disagree, as is the case within the schools of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī.

---

## Page 14989

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About orphans who own a share in a property, which their guardian leased to the partnership for three years at less than the fair rental value. What is the ruling?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the guardian leases it for less than the fair rental value, he is liable for the loss he has caused the orphan. The lease is not binding on the orphan after he attains sound judgment. Rather, according to one of the two scholarly opinions, it is invalid and automatically dissolved; according to the other, the orphan may rescind it.

If the lessee did not know that the guardian’s action was unlawful, he may hold the guardian liable for any liability he had not undertaken himself. If he knew, however, ultimate liability rests with him. Indeed, even if the guardian leased it at the fair rental value for a term during which he knows the orphan will come of age, most scholars allow the orphan to rescind the lease. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14990

He was asked:

About a man who rented a house, after which a bathhouse was built beside it. Its water breeds mosquitoes, and his wife miscarried because of the smell of its smoke. May he rescind the lease?

He replied:

If the tenant did not know that operating this bathhouse would cause harm that ordinarily diminishes the value of the property’s use, he may rescind the lease. His statement that he did not know is accepted if supported by his oath. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a land grant leased under a registered agreement at three ardabbs and three dirhams per feddan, with cultivation advances to be supplied by the grantee and the oxen supplied by the tenants. Is this permissible or not?

He replied:

If the lessor leases it at the stipulated rental charge whether or not the farmer borrows, and does not seek that charge on account of the advance, this is permissible, since the loan has brought him no benefit. Some scholars, however, have disliked this and considered it a loan that brings a benefit, since the advance enables the farmer to lease the land. But this is a benefit

---

## Page 14991

to both parties. If he does not increase the rent on account of the advance, he has done well. It makes no difference whether it is called a lease or a registered agreement; both are the same.

He was asked:

About a man who hired a worker to tend an orchard, but the worker stopped working until part of the orchard was ruined. Is he entitled to his wages? Is he liable for compensation or not?

He replied:

There is no doubt that if he fails to perform the work stipulated in his agreement, he is not entitled to his wages. If he performs part of it, he is paid in proportion to the work he has done. If any property is lost or damaged through his negligence, he is liable for that loss or damage. Negligence means failing, without an excuse, to perform what one is obliged to do.

He was asked:

Regarding a riding animal: Which is better—to carry people free of charge, or to charge a fare and give it in charity?

He replied:

If they are poor, it is better to waive their fare. If they are wealthy and there is someone in need, it is better to collect the fare for the benefit of that person.

---

## Page 14992

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About someone who leased land belonging to the public treasury to certain individuals, each lease specifying a particular area at a specified rent in dirhams. Plantings were established on the land, but the land held by the tenants included an additional area beyond that recorded in their leases. Is it permissible to survey the land and recover the excess for the public treasury?

He replied:

Whatever they have planted beyond the area to which their leases entitle them is subject to the customary rent. Whenever they use the excess land, they must pay the customary rent, by agreement of the Muslims.

If they have not used it, may the landowner uproot the plantings upon reimbursing what they have spent? The scholars hold two well-known opinions on this.

If he chooses to leave the plantings in place and demand the customary rent, he is entitled to do so by agreement.

---

## Page 14993

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About someone who rented premises from their administrators for a specified term at a specified rent. If he wished to be released from the lease, they would not release him before its term expired. May they accept a higher offer from someone else before his lease expires?

He replied:

If the lease is valid, it is binding on both parties by agreement of the Muslims. In these circumstances, the landlord may not evict the tenant because someone has offered a higher rent. Nor may he accept a higher offer over the tenant’s, by agreement of the imams.

If the lease is invalid, the endowment’s trustee may not allow the tenant to take possession of the premises under such a lease. Nor may he prevent him from leaving if he wishes, or demand the rent specified in the contract. The trustee’s entering into such an arrangement would call into question both his integrity and his trusteeship. By agreement of the imams, he must lease the premises only under a contract valid according to Islamic law. Likewise, by agreement of the imams, once he has leased them under such a contract, he must not accept a higher offer over the tenant’s or evict him on that account.

As for the person who offered more than the tenant, if he made a higher offer after the landlord had become favourably disposed

---

## Page 14994

to leasing it to him, he would have bid over his brother’s bid. If he offered more after the contract had been concluded, while rescission was still possible, he would be like someone who undercuts his brother’s sale. Both are forbidden by the explicit words of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and this is the position of the four imams. How much more serious, then, is offering more when a valid lease is already in force! Such a bidder is disobedient, sinful and unjust, and deserves discretionary punishment. Whoever helps him in this has helped him in sin and transgression.

The tenant’s having his own commitment witnessed, without the landlord doing likewise, makes no difference here, for the contract does not require witnesses; it is valid without them. The trustee’s saying to him, “Have your commitment witnessed,” together with the tenant’s doing so, constitutes a lease valid according to Islamic law. Indeed, once the trustee has told him, “Have your commitment witnessed,” no one may offer more than he has.

The trustee must not lease the premises until he considers it most likely that no one will offer more. He must publicize their availability among prospective tenants who customarily rent such premises. Once he has done so, he has leased them at the going rate, and this is a lease valid according to Islamic law.

If, however, he favours one of his friends, someone who has done him a favour, or anyone else by leasing the premises below the going rate, he is acting unjustly and is liable for the shortfall in the rent due to the endowment’s beneficiaries.

If property rental rates change after a valid lease has been concluded, he has no right to rescind it on that account, for such fluctuations cannot be precisely controlled and fall outside what one can be required to account for. The value of the benefit derived from the property may vary over time rather than remain uniform. Thus, its value in winter

---

## Page 14995

may exceed its value in summer, or vice versa. Someone who rents it for a year accepts paying more during certain parts of the rental period in view of its higher value at other times. No one may therefore outbid him because the rental value of those premises has risen. If the lease were to terminate during part of that period, the amount would be apportioned according to the respective rental values, not merely the lengths of time involved.

Thus, one asks: “What is its rental value during the summer?” The rent is then apportioned according to its value at each time, with each period assigned a share of the rent proportionate to its rental value.

The trustee must act in the endowment’s best interests when leasing the premises, whether yearly, monthly or daily. If its interests are best served by leasing them day by day, so that at the end of each day the tenant may vacate and the landlord may require him to do so, he should adopt that arrangement. If its interests are best served by a monthly lease, so that at the beginning of each month the tenant may vacate and the landlord may require him to do so, he should adopt that arrangement. But if a yearly lease serves its interests, he has fulfilled his duty and may not evict the tenant before the lease expires merely to obtain a higher rent.

As for the distinction drawn by some later jurists among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Imam Ahmad between an increase amounting to one-third and one amounting to less, this is an innovated opinion with no basis in the teachings of any of the imams—neither al-Shāfiʿī nor Ahmad nor anyone else—whether concerning endowments or other property.

---

## Page 14996

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man rented a shop from the administrators of endowments for a specified term at a specified rent. He took possession of the shop and used it, and they collected the rent due to them. Had he sought release from the lease, they would not have released him until the term expired. May they accept a higher offer from someone else before his lease expires, or not?

He replied:

They may not accept a higher offer in these circumstances, whether the property is an endowment, belongs to an orphan, or is otherwise owned.

Anyone who considers it permissible to accept a higher offer while refusing to allow the tenant to withdraw when he wishes has contravened the consensus of the Muslims. If the lease is invalid or impermissible, both the landlord and the tenant may withdraw from it. If it is valid and binding, neither may terminate it merely for a higher rent or the like, without grounds warranting termination. To treat it as revocable on the landlord’s side but binding on the tenant’s side is contrary to the consensus of the Muslims.

Moreover, if the trustee claims that he did not lease these premises, or that he leased them under an invalid contract, this would call his administration and integrity into question, for it amounts to an admission on his part that he is handing over

---

## Page 14997

the endowed property to someone to whom he may not lawfully hand it over, and allowing him to use it without a stipulated rent. There is no dispute that the trustee has no right to do this. Moreover, this amounts to an admission that the tenant is not liable for the stipulated rent, but only for the fair rental value. The fair rental value is often less than the stipulated rent, so this constitutes an admission that he is liable for the loss he has caused the endowment’s beneficiaries. If the trustee claimed that the lease was invalid while the tenant maintained that it was valid, the statement of the party asserting its validity would prevail, since contracts between Muslims are presumed valid. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was also asked:

A man works as a weigher using a qabbān (weighing balance), and takes his fee from those for whom he weighs goods. Is this permissible for him? Is the fee lawful or unlawful?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Weighing with an accurate qabbān balance is like weighing with any other scales. If the weigher uses these accurate instruments fairly, his weighing is permissible. If the instrument is faulty and the weigher gives short weight, he is among the wrongdoers and transgressors.

If he weighs fairly and takes his fee from the person responsible for having the goods weighed, that is permissible.

---

## Page 14998

He was also asked:

A man stamps cloth, and another man lodges with him. If the man claims that the rent comes from a source other than his earnings, is it permissible to accept it?

He replied:

If he has another lawful source of income, states that he is paying the rent from it, and is thought likely to be telling the truth, [it is permissible] (1) to accept it. If he is not thought likely to be lying, he may be believed in this matter, provided he is not known to be lying.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Is a cupper’s fee unlawful? Does food he prepares with his hands become ritually impure? Did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, pay the cupper his fee? What reports prohibit it?

Has it been reported in a hadith that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Healing for my community lies in three things: a verse from the Book of Allah, a lick of honey, or a cupping treatment administered by a cupper”?

How, then, did he prohibit this, yet prescribe it here as a treatment and describe it as a cure?

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I could not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-fatāwā min al-saqt wa-l-tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Scribal Errors).
> Osāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 14999

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. As for his hand, if there is no impurity on it, it is like the hands of other Muslims. Contact with blood does not affect its purity once he washes it, just as contact with filth while cleaning himself after relieving himself does not affect its purity if he washes it afterward.

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, underwent cupping and paid the cupper his fee.”

Had it been unlawful gain, he would not have paid it to him. Also in the two Sahīh collections, Anas was asked about a cupper’s earnings and said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, underwent cupping. Abū Tībah performed it, and he ordered that he be given two sāʿ measures of food. He also spoke to his masters, who reduced the dues they required of him.”

There is no doubt that, according to the overwhelming majority of scholars, a cupper who performs cupping is entitled to his fee, although there is a weak opinion to the contrary. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted him to use these earnings to feed his water-drawing camel and his slaves, as related in the hadith of Muhsan:

“His father asked the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for permission concerning income from cupping, but he refused to grant him permission. He persisted in asking until he said: ‘Use it to feed your slaves and your water-drawing camel.’”

Abū Hātim Ibn Hibbān narrated it in his Sahīh, as did others. Most scholars cited this as evidence that such earnings are not unlawful; for a free person, they are merely disliked, not prohibited. They argued that, had these earnings been unlawful, he would not have instructed him to use them to feed his slaves, since they too are bound by religious obligations.

---

## Page 15000

and it is inconceivable that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would permit him to feed his slaves anything unlawful.

Others said that such earnings are indeed unlawful, citing what Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj—may Allah be pleased with them both—that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A cupper’s earnings are foul, the price of a dog is foul, and a prostitute’s fee is foul.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Abī Juhayfah said:

“I saw my father buy a slave who practised cupping. He ordered that his cupping instruments be broken, and they were broken. I asked him about this, and he said: ‘The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade the price of blood.’”

Those scholars said: Describing these earnings as foul implies that they are forbidden, just as a prostitute’s fee and a soothsayer’s fee are forbidden.

Those who held the first view replied: It is established that he said:

“Whoever eats from these two foul plants must not come near our mosque.”

He called them foul because of their offensive smell, yet they are not forbidden. He also said:

“Let none of you pray while holding back the two foul things”—that is, urine and feces.

Thus, describing these earnings as foul is due to the earner’s contact with impurity, not because they are forbidden. The evidence for this is that he paid the cupper his fee and permitted him to use it to feed slaves and animals. By contrast, neither a prostitute nor a soothsayer is entitled to such fees, nor may they be used to feed slaves or animals.

In any case, someone who needs these earnings is not in the same position as someone who can do without them. As the early generations said:

“Earnings that carry some indignity are better than begging from people.”

For this reason, when the scholars differed over accepting payment for teaching the Quran and similar activities:

---

## Page 15001

there were three views within the school of Imam Ahmad and among other scholars. The most balanced is that it is permissible for someone in need. Ahmad said: “Payment for teaching is better than grants from the ruler, and grants from the ruler are better than gifts from one’s brethren.”

All the principles of the Sharia rest on this foundation: a distinction is made between someone in need and someone who is not, regarding prohibitions just as regarding commands. Thus, prohibited things become permissible in cases of necessity, especially if the alternative would be to beg from people, for begging is more strictly prohibited.

For this reason, the scholars said that obligations must be fulfilled even if they can only be fulfilled through doubtful means. As Abū Tālib and Abū Hāmid related, a man asked Imam Ahmad: “A son of mine has died owing debts, and others owe him debts that I am reluctant to collect.”

Imam Ahmad said to him: “Will you leave your son bound by his unpaid debt?”

His point was that repaying a debt is obligatory, and one is commanded to set aside one’s misgivings in order to fulfil an obligation.

Likewise, the scholars agreed that a judge and others in comparable positions should receive a stipend when in need, though they differed over receiving one when there is no need. The basis for this is in the Book of Allah, in His words concerning an orphan’s guardian:

“Whoever is wealthy should refrain, and whoever is poor may take a reasonable amount.”

The same applies to comparable cases, for the Sharia is founded on securing and perfecting benefits, and eliminating and reducing harms. True scrupulousness means choosing the greater of two goods at the expense of the lesser, and averting the greater of two evils even if the lesser results.

Many hadiths have been reported concerning cupping. In the two Sahīh collections, it is reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 15002

that he said:

“Healing for my community lies in three things: a drink of honey, an incision with a cupping lancet, or cauterization with fire. But I do not like to undergo cauterization.”

Treatment by cupping is permissible according to the mass-transmitted Sunnah and the agreement of the scholars.

He was asked:

A widow left on her own has a small amount of jewellery which she hires out, supporting herself with the rent. Is this lawful or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. This is permissible according to Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfi'ī and other scholars. Mālik, Ahmad, Mālik’s followers and many of Ahmad’s followers considered it disliked. This, however, means that it is discouraged, not forbidden. This applies when the rent is of the same kind as the jewellery; if it is of a different kind, there is no objection.

Thus, if this woman hires out her jewellery and supports herself with the rent because she is in need, she should not be forbidden from doing so. However, she must pay zakat on it according to most scholars, including Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and Imam Ahmad.

This applies if she hires it out to a woman who adorns herself for her husband or master, or to someone who wears it to a permissible gathering, such as a wedding that one may lawfully attend. But if she hires it out to a woman who adorns herself with it for men who are not her mahrams, this is not permissible.

---

## Page 15003

But if she hires it out to a woman who adorns herself with it in order to commit sexual immorality, this is too grave a matter even to ask about.

Allah Most High says:

“Cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression.”

It is not permissible to help anyone commit sexual immorality or any other sin, whether by providing jewellery, clothing, accommodation, a riding animal, or anything else, whether for hire or otherwise. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Candle sellers hire out candles, weighing them before handing them over, then weighing them again upon their return and charging for the loss in weight. Is this disliked? If a candle breaks, is the person who hired it liable or not?

He answered:

If someone gives candles to another person to burn, saying, “For every ūqiyya (a unit of weight) consumed, the charge is such-and-such,” this is permissible. It does not fall under lease contracts or binding sales, since a binding sale requires the goods being sold to be known. Rather, it is a revocable exchange, not a binding one.

This is like saying, “Live in this house for one dirham a day,” without specifying a term. This is permissible according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. Thus, this case involving tangible property is analogous to that case involving the use of property. It constitutes permission to consume the property

---

## Page 15004

for beneficial use in return for consideration, as when someone says, “Throw your goods into the sea, and I will pay their value.” This is undoubtedly permissible, because the person undertaking payment benefits from lightening the load, just as he benefits by undertaking payment here. Thus, burning candles for a charge is permissible provided their consumption is known. However, they must be burned for a permissible purpose, not a prohibited one.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A zarkashī artisan was commissioned to make a cloth. When he had finished, its owners permitted him to wash it. However, the artisan’s slave woman mishandled it while polishing the goldwork, leaving cuts in the cloth. Must he compensate them for the cloth?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If the slave woman damaged the cloth, the claim for compensation attaches to her person. Her owner must either pay compensation for the damage or surrender her so that compensation can be recovered from her value.

Whether the damage was caused by her, her master, or someone else, the person responsible is not liable for what the owners spent on the cloth. Its value is assessed without these cuts and again after they have occurred, and those responsible must compensate for the reduction in value. If they mutually agree that the artisan should take the cloth and pay them its market value before it was cut, that is permissible. He is not obliged to give them a new cloth better than it.

---

## Page 15005

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About leasing buffaloes: someone leases them for one year on unrestricted terms, intending to obtain their milk, and uses them for that purpose. The lease is left unrestricted on the understanding that he will make use of them, though the milk is his aim. Is it also permissible to lease sheep for their milk? And is it permissible to entrust them to someone who will tend them in return for their wool and milk, or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. There is a well-known disagreement on this issue among the early generations and the scholars. The same applies to purchasing a specified quantity of milk over a period, to be received in instalments from these livestock. Prohibition is the well-known position in the schools of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

These scholars said:

This is the sale of something neither seen nor described; indeed, it is the sale of something nonexistent that has not yet come into being. A lease concerns benefits, not tangible property, whereas these are tangible things.

These scholars also said:

Hiring a wet nurse to breastfeed is contrary to legal analogy but was permitted because of need. They disagreed about this hiring arrangement.

One view is:

The subject of the contract is her service, while breastfeeding is incidental to it. This is the view of Ibn ʿAqīl and others.

Another view is:

Rather,

---

## Page 15006

the subject of the contract is what the contract is intended to secure, namely the milk. This is the view of Judge Abū Yaʿlā and others.

As for permitting this in principle, that is the position of Mālik and others. These scholars may describe hiring a wet nurse to breastfeed as subordinate to the milk, since the wet nurse is subordinate to milk that has not yet come into existence. This rests on regarding it as a contract concerning tangible property, and contracts concerning tangible property fall under the category of sales. The disagreement here is terminological: this arrangement falls within the broad meaning of sale, which encompasses tangible property and benefits, whether already in existence or not. It does not fall within the narrower meaning of sale, which is confined to existing tangible property.

They likewise disagreed over advance-payment contracts: are they a form of sale? There are two views. Can an advance-payment contract be concluded using the word “sale”? There are two positions on this within the school of Ahmad and those of others. Some who did not classify it as a sale even said that an advance-payment contract providing for immediate delivery is permissible when concluded using the word “sale”, but not the word “salam”. The correct view is that contracts are judged by their substance, not merely their wording.

The correct position is that the lease asked about is permissible. The scriptural evidence establishing the permissibility of compensated transactions, together with the analogies drawn from it, encompasses this lease, and no evidence rules it out.

The assertion that hiring a wet nurse is contrary to legal analogy is unsound. In Allah’s Book, no hiring arrangement is explicitly prescribed as part of our law other than this one, as Allah Most High says:

“If they breastfeed for you, then give them

---

## Page 15007

their wages.” And He says:

“The father is responsible for their provision and clothing according to what is reasonable.”

The Sunnah and the consensus of the Muslim community establish the permissibility of this arrangement. It would be contrary to legal analogy only if an analogy drawn from another scriptural text conflicted with it. Yet nothing in the other scriptural texts or the analogies drawn from them contradicts it.

The assertion that hiring and leasing concern only benefits, not tangible property, is not a statement of Allah, His Messenger, the Companions, or the leading scholars. It is merely a statement made by a group of people.

We therefore say to them: We do not concede that hiring and leasing can concern benefits alone. Rather, they may concern something that is continually produced and replenished while the underlying property remains, such as the water of a well and similar things, whether what is obtained is tangible property or a benefit. Likewise, property placed in an endowment may yield something that is renewed and produced successively, whether that yield is a benefit or tangible property, such as dates, milk, or spring water. The same applies to a gratuitous loan for use, where benefit is obtained from something continually produced and replenished.

Thus, one speaks of lending a mount to be ridden, granting the use of a date palm, or lending a she-camel for its milk. If someone lends another a she-camel to drink its milk and then return it, or grants him the use of a date palm to eat its fruit and then return it, this is like lending him a mount to ride and then return. The same applies to hiring a woman, a bird, a she-camel, a cow, or a ewe, for someone to drink

---

## Page 15008

its milk for a specified period; this is like hiring a mount to ride for a specified period. If the usual yield changes, this constitutes a change in the benefit obtained, entitling the lessee either to rescind the contract or to compensation. The same applies to leasing someone an orchard to use for one or two years, as ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb did when he leased out the orchard of Usayd ibn al-Hudayr for three years, received the payment, and used it to discharge a debt Usayd owed.

If the lessee himself tends these animals, the arrangement is a lease, and there is even stronger reason to permit it than the hiring of a wet nurse. If, however, the livestock’s owner feeds, waters, and shelters them, while the person seeking their milk is concerned only with the milk and has hired them to suckle his young livestock, this is like hiring a wet nurse. If he is to collect the milk himself, this resembles hiring a wet nurse to provide milk generally, rather than to nurse a particular child. This may be called a sale or a lease; the dispute is merely terminological.

If it is said that this is the sale of something that does not yet exist, the reply is: Yes, but no principle of Islamic law prohibits every sale of something not yet in existence. Rather, something that does not yet exist may be sold when there is a need for its sale and it is known through customary experience, just as fruit may be sold once it begins to show signs of ripeness. This is valid according to the majority of scholars, as the Sunnah indicates, even though the portions that develop later do not yet exist but are nevertheless included in the contract. Likewise,

---

## Page 15009

the sale of cucumber crops and the like is permissible according to this view. Allah knows best. All praise is due to Allah.

He was asked:

A sick man asked someone to treat him and cover his expenses, and he did so. Is the person who paid entitled to demand reimbursement from the sick man?

He replied:

If he covered the expenses expecting reimbursement, whether expressly stipulated or understood by custom, he is entitled to demand reimbursement. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A blind man was made party to a written lease. Is his lease valid?

He replied:

A blind person’s renting and purchasing are valid according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Imam Ahmad according to the well-known position reported from him. The item purchased or rented must be described to him. If he finds that it does not match the description, he has the right to rescind the contract.

---

## Page 15010

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man does not have enough to meet his needs and leads prayers for payment. Is this permissible or not?

He replied:

Hiring someone to lead prayers is not permissible according to the well-known position in the schools of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Imam Ahmad.

Another view permits it. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, one view reported from Imam Ahmad, and an opinion within Mālik’s school. The same disagreement applies to giving the call to prayer. However, the well-known position in Mālik’s school permits hiring someone to give the call to prayer and to lead prayers in conjunction with that duty, but not to lead prayers alone.

A third view within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere concerning payment for these duties and similar activities, such as teaching, is that it is permissible when there is a need, but not otherwise. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15011

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man died, having stipulated in his will that someone be paid in dirhams to pray on his behalf. What is the ruling?

He replied:

No one may perform an obligatory prayer on another’s behalf, whether for payment or otherwise, by agreement of the imams. Indeed, they agree that it is not permissible to hire someone even to perform voluntary prayers on one’s behalf, whether during one’s lifetime or after one’s death. How much less permissible, then, is hiring someone to perform obligatory prayers on one’s behalf!

The scholars differed only over whether a deceased person benefits when someone performs voluntary prayers without payment and dedicates their reward to him. There are two scholarly views on this. Likewise, if the deceased had vowed to pray but died before fulfilling his vow, may those vowed prayers be performed on his behalf? There are two views, both reported from Imam Ahmad.

However, the dirhams he bequeathed should be given in charity on his behalf, specifically to those who pray, so that the deceased receives a reward. He will also have a share in the reward of every prayer they perform with the help of his charity, without their own reward being diminished in the least. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever provides a fasting person with food to break his fast will have a reward like his.”

And he said:

“Whoever equips a warrior has himself taken part in the campaign.”

---

## Page 15012

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A scholar was approached by someone wishing to read to him some hadiths of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and other religious sciences, but he refused to teach them except for payment. He was told:

“The reports describing how the early generations and the imams of guidance taught knowledge solely for the sake of Allah, the Most Generous, are too well known to escape any reasonable person. Such conduct is inappropriate.”

He replied:

“Am I to teach without payment? That is forbidden for me!”

Is his statement correct or false? Is this an ignorant statement for which he may be excused? Is he permitted to accept payment for teaching beneficial knowledge, or is that disliked for him?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Teaching the Quran and religious knowledge without payment is the finest of deeds and the most beloved to Allah. This is a self-evident teaching of Islam, not something unknown to anyone raised in Muslim lands. The Companions, the Followers, the generation after them, and other scholars renowned throughout the Muslim community for their knowledge of the Quran, hadith, and jurisprudence taught without payment. None of them taught for payment at all. For the scholars are the heirs of the prophets, and the prophets left neither dinars nor dirhams

---

## Page 15013

but only knowledge; whoever acquires it has acquired an abundant share. The prophets—may Allah Most High be pleased with them all—taught knowledge without payment. As Noah, peace be upon him, said:

“I ask you no payment for it. My reward rests only with the Lord of all worlds.”

Hūd, Sālih, Shuʿayb, Lot, and others said the same. Likewise, the Seal of the Messengers said:

“Say, ‘I ask you no payment for it, nor am I one of the pretentious.’”

And he said:

“Say, ‘I ask you no payment for it—only that whoever wills should take a path to his Lord.’”

The scholars have never disputed that teaching the Quran, hadith, jurisprudence, and other such subjects without payment is a righteous deed, let alone that it is permissible. Indeed, it is a collective obligation. For teaching the knowledge he expounded is a collective obligation, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“Convey from me, even if only a single verse.”

And he said:

“Let those present convey it to those absent.”

The scholars differed only over whether it is permissible to hire someone to teach the Quran, hadith, and jurisprudence. There are two well-known positions, both reported from Ahmad. The first—which is the position of Abū Hanīfah and others—is that hiring someone for this is not permissible. The second—which is the position of al-Shāfiʿī—is that it is permissible. There is also a third position within Ahmad’s school: that it is permissible when there is need, but not when one is financially self-sufficient, as Allah Most High said concerning an orphan’s guardian:

“Whoever is wealthy should refrain from taking anything, and whoever

---

## Page 15014

is poor may take a reasonable amount.”

These teachers may be paid from the Muslims’ public funds for teaching, just as imams, muezzins, and judges are paid. This is permissible when there is need. Whether one may receive such a stipend while financially self-sufficient is a matter on which the scholars hold two views. No Muslim has ever said that performing these duties without payment is impermissible.

Anyone who says that it is impermissible must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is to be put to death. However, he may mean that he is poor and that teaching without payment would prevent him from earning a living for his dependants. Providing for his dependants is an individual obligation upon him, and he may not abandon an individual obligation for something that is not individually obligatory. If this is what he means, and he also believes that teaching for payment is permissible either when there is need or without qualification, then his statement rests on an interpretation. By agreement of the imams, he is neither deemed an unbeliever nor a transgressor on that account; rather, he is either correct or mistaken.

The reasoning of the scholars who prohibit hiring someone to provide this service is that these duties—teaching the Quran, hadith, and jurisprudence, leading prayers, and giving the call to prayer—may only be performed by someone qualified to perform acts of devotion. An unbeliever may not perform them; only a Muslim may do so. This differs from services that both Muslims and unbelievers can perform, such as building, sewing, weaving, and similar work. When an act is performed for payment, it ceases to be worship of Allah, for it becomes something owed in exchange for compensation and is performed for the sake of that compensation. When an act is performed for compensation, it is no longer

---

## Page 15015

an act of worship, but is like a craft practised for payment.

Those who prohibit hiring people to perform these duties argue that they may only be performed as worship of Allah, just as prayer, fasting, and Quran recitation may only be performed as worship of Allah; hiring someone to perform them removes that character.

Those who permit this argue that it provides a benefit to the person hiring, so payment may be taken for it, as for other benefits. They further argue that if these duties are not acts of worship in such circumstances, they are not performed as worship; they may therefore be performed either as worship or otherwise, because of the benefit they provide.

Those who distinguish between someone in need and someone who is not—and this is closer to the correct view—argue that a person in need who earns a living through these duties can intend to perform them for Allah, taking payment to help him worship. Earning a living to support one’s dependants is also obligatory, so he thereby fulfils his obligations. This differs from someone who is financially self-sufficient: he has no need to earn a living, and thus no need prompting him to perform these duties for other than Allah. Rather, since Allah has provided him with sufficient means and this is a communal obligation, he is among those required to fulfil it. If it cannot be fulfilled except through him, it becomes an individual obligation upon him. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15016

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

If someone rents a house for his own enjoyment, may he let it out?

He replied:

If someone hires a service for a prohibited act, such as singing, fornication, false testimony, or killing someone whose life is protected, that hiring is prohibited.

Likewise, he may not hire out his services to perform something already obligatory upon him—for example, giving testimony concerning a right, issuing a fatwa on a question, adjudicating a dispute, or undertaking jihad—when that duty has become an individual obligation upon him. Taking payment for this is impermissible.

If the hiring is for an act particular to those engaged in devotional duties—such as teaching the Quran or religious knowledge, leading prayer, giving the call to prayer, performing Hajj on another’s behalf, or undertaking jihad that is not individually obligatory—then scholars differ concerning it.

If the hiring is for work such as tailoring, carpentry, or construction, it is permissible by agreement.

---

## Page 15017

He—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section: Hiring Someone to Provide a Prohibited Service

Hiring someone to provide a prohibited service—such as illicit sexual intercourse, sodomy, singing, transporting wine, or the like—is invalid. Yet if the hirer receives that service and withholds the worker’s payment, this too constitutes treachery and injustice.

I have discussed hiring someone to transport wine comprehensively in my book al-Sirāt al-Mustaqīm (The Straight Path). There I explained that the correct position is the one expressly stated by Ahmad: the worker should be awarded his payment, although it is not wholesome earnings for him; taking it is either discouraged or prohibited. This ruling, however, concerns work whose general category is permissible, such as transporting goods, unlike illicit sexual intercourse.

There is no doubt that a prostitute’s earnings are foul, as is a soothsayer’s fee. The judge orders the punishment of the hirer who has received the prohibited service, and the punishment imposed on him thus takes the place of the fee.

As for his responsibility before Allah, should he give the worker that payment, even though receiving it is unlawful on account of Allah’s right? This, then, has a compensable value. If he is not obliged to pay it, this would enable him to satisfy his desire: he commits the prohibited act and is excused, and Allah will not punish him in the Hereafter except

---

## Page 15018

for committing the prohibited act, not for treachery and injustice. This discussion is connected to the rulings governing other invalid contracts and what is received under them.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man commissioned a book to be copied and gilded, paid the fee, received the book, bound it, and took it away for forty days. He then brought it back to the craftsman who had copied and gilded it and said, “Give me back the fee you received from me, for I am only an intermediary.” May he compel the craftsman to take it back and refund the fee he paid?

He replied:

If he hired him to perform work for which hiring is permissible, and paid him his fee upon completion of the work, the craftsman is not obliged to refund it. Indeed, if the intermediary did not name his principal in the hiring contract, he is undoubtedly liable for the fee.

If he did name his principal, is he still liable for the fee? There are two opinions, both reported from Imam Ahmad. Even if he had not paid the fee, the worker would have been entitled to demand it from him. What, then, if he has already paid it? Moreover, unless he paid the fee from his principal’s funds, the agent is entitled to recover from the principal the fee he paid on his behalf. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15019

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A beggar approached a man in the guise of a flute-player and played his flute. The man gave him something. Someone present told the recipient that this gift was unlawful for him under these circumstances, since flute-playing had been the means of obtaining it. The giver replied, “I gave it to him only because he was poor. Besides, even if I had given it to him for his flute-playing, that would have been permissible, for some have permitted listening to the flute.”

He cited as evidence the report that “the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, passed a shepherd while accompanied by Ibn ‘Abbās or someone else. The shepherd was playing a flute, so the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, blocked his ears with his fingers and kept asking his companion, ‘Can you hear the sound of the flute?’ He continued in this manner until his companion told him that he could no longer hear it, whereupon he uncovered his ears.”

He argued, “Had listening to the flute been forbidden, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would have instructed his companion to block his ears as he himself had done, or would have forbidden the shepherd to play. This proves that it is permissible for those who are not prophets.” Is this report authentic? And is this argument consistent with the Sunnah or not?

He replied:

Attributing this report to Ibn ‘Abbās is baseless. However, Abū Dāwūd narrated in his Sunan:

“He was with Ibn ‘Umar, who passed a shepherd carrying a pipe

---

## Page 15020

so he kept asking, ‘Can you hear it, Nāfi‘?’ When he told him that he could no longer hear it, he removed his fingers from his ears and told him that he had been with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who had done the same.” After narrating this hadith, Abū Dāwūd said, “This is a rejected hadith.” Abū Bakr al-Khallāl also narrated it through several chains that corroborate one another. Even if it is authentic, it provides no evidence for those who permit the flute, especially since the position of the four imams is that the flute is forbidden. Among the adherents of the four schools, only the later Khurāsānī scholars of the Shāfi‘ī school disputed this, mentioning two juristic views concerning it. The Iraqi scholars, who were more knowledgeable about his school, unequivocally declared it forbidden, as did the other schools. In any event, the permissive view is a weak one within his school.

Al-Shāfi‘ī said:

“Singing is reprehensible and resembles falsehood. Whoever indulges in it excessively is a foolish person whose testimony is rejected.”

He also said:

“I left behind in Baghdad a practice introduced by heretics, which they call ‘taghbīr’, by which they turn people away from the Quran.”

According to the four imams, it is impermissible to acquire musical instruments or to pay for their use. Thus, even if this hadith is authentic, it provides no evidence that the flute is permissible. Rather, it more readily supports its prohibition, for several reasons:

First: What is forbidden is deliberate listening, not merely hearing. A man might hear words of disbelief, lies, backbiting, singing, or flute-playing without intending to; rather, he might simply be passing

---

## Page 15021

along a road and hear such things; he incurs no sin thereby, by the agreement of the Muslims. But if he sits and deliberately listens without rejecting it in his heart, with his tongue, or with his hand, he is sinful, by the agreement of the Muslims. As Allah Most High says:

“When you see those who engage in mockery of Our revelations, turn away from them until they turn to another subject. And if Satan makes you forget, then once you remember, do not sit with the wrongdoing people. Those who fear Allah are in no way accountable for them, but should remind them, so that they may fear Him.”

Allah Most High also says:

“He has already revealed to you in the Book that when you hear Allah’s revelations being denied and mocked, you must not sit with them until they turn to another subject; otherwise, you would be like them.”

Thus, He places the person who sits and listens without rejecting it on the same footing as the perpetrator. Hence the saying: “The listener is a partner in the backbiter’s sin.” A report states:

“Whoever witnesses a sin and detests it is like one who was absent from it, and whoever is absent from it but approves of it is like one who witnessed it.”

If someone witnesses it because of a need or under compulsion, he must reject it in his heart, for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand. If he cannot, then with his tongue; and if he cannot, then with his heart—and that is the weakest level of faith.”

If a man were passing by and heard the Quran without deliberately listening to it, he would not be rewarded for that. Rather, he is rewarded for intentional listening, as Allah Most High says:

“When the Quran is recited, listen to it attentively and remain silent, so that you may receive mercy.”

And He said to Moses:

“So listen to what is revealed.”

---

## Page 15022

Once it is understood that commands, prohibitions, promises of reward, and threats of punishment concern deliberate listening, not merely hearing, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and Ibn ‘Umar were simply passing by, not deliberately listening. Likewise, Ibn ‘Umar, when accompanying the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and Nāfi‘, when accompanying Ibn ‘Umar, were merely hearing, not deliberately listening. Neither was therefore required to cover his ears.

The second point: The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, covered his ears only as an added precaution, so that he would not hear anything at all. This shows that avoiding hearing it is better than hearing it, even though merely hearing it entails no sin. Had the sound been permissible, he would not have covered his ears to avoid hearing something permissible. Rather, his covering his ears so as not to hear it—even though merely hearing it was not forbidden—indicates that refraining from deliberate listening is all the more appropriate. Thus, this provides stronger evidence for prohibiting deliberate listening than for permitting it.

The third point: Even assuming that deliberate listening is impermissible, if he and his companion had both covered their ears, they would not have known when the sound had ceased, so that the one being followed could uncover his ears.

The fourth point: It is not known whether the companion had reached puberty or was still a child below that age. Children are allowed forms of play that adults are not.

The fifth point: A shepherd’s pipe does not stir the emotions like the flute fashioned by someone other than a shepherd. Even if permission to hear it were assumed, this would not necessarily entail permission for what has been described and its accompanying sounds, which affect the soul as potent wine does.

---

## Page 15023

The sixth point: Ibn al-Mundhir reported the scholars’ agreement on the prohibition of hiring people to sing or wail. He said:

“All the scholars whose views we have preserved agree that hiring female wailers and singers is invalid. Al-Sha‘bī, al-Nakha‘ī, and Mālik disapproved of it. Abū Thawr, al-Nu‘mān, Ya‘qūb, and Muhammad said: ‘Hiring anyone for any kind of singing or wailing is impermissible.’ This is also our position.”

Given that he reported the consensus of the scholars whose views he had preserved on the invalidity of hiring female wailers and singers—even though singing is permissible for women at weddings and on occasions of rejoicing, while for men it is either forbidden or disliked, though some scholars have allowed it—what, then, of the flute? None of the scholars permitted it for either men or women, whether at weddings or elsewhere. Those who permit it are not among the imams who are followed and renowned for their authority in religion.

Thus, the statement, “If one paid him for his flute-playing, that would be permissible,” is false and contrary to the positions of the imams of the Muslims, even if flute-playing were a permissible form of idle amusement. How much more so when it is a prohibited form of idle amusement! This becomes clear from the seventh point: not everything that is permissible to do may permissibly be done for payment.

Do you not see that, in the well-known hadith, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“No prize may be awarded except in camel racing, horse racing, or archery.”

He thus prohibited awarding prizes in competitions other than these

---

## Page 15024

three activities. Yet wrestling may be permissible, as when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, wrestled Rukānah ibn ‘Abd Yazīd. Footracing is also permissible: the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, raced ‘Ā’ishah and permitted Salamah ibn al-Akwa‘ to race during the expedition of al-Ghābah and Dhū Qarad. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Every amusement a man engages in is vain, except shooting with his bow, training his horse, and playing with his wife, for these are worthwhile pursuits.”

Whoever takes money for such vain amusement takes it wrongfully. Nevertheless, this amusement may be permitted, just as children are permitted to play, and just as:

“Two young girls of the Ansār were singing during the days of Eid in ‘Ā’ishah’s house, while the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, neither listened to them nor forbade them. When Abū Bakr said, ‘Satan’s flute in the house of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him?’ the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said, ‘Leave them, Abū Bakr, for every people has a festival, and this is our festival.’”

He thereby showed that those for whom play is appropriate are permitted to play during festivals, even though men do not engage in such activities. But money must not be spent on vain amusement.

It is thus clear, on all these grounds, that anyone citing this hadith as evidence for the permissibility of that activity and of paying a fee for it would be mistaken even if the hadith were authentic. How much more so, then, given the defects it contains!

---

## Page 15025

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

When someone leases out land or properties such as houses, shops, inns, and the like, a binding lease binds both parties. It cannot bind one party while leaving the other free to terminate it. Rather, according to many scholars, it must either bind both parties or remain non-binding on both.

For example, if someone rents a property for one dirham per day without fixing an end date, this lease is non-binding according to one of the two scholarly opinions. For each day he occupies it, he becomes liable for that day’s rent. He may stay the following day, but the landlord may prevent him from doing so. The same applies if the rent is set at so much per month or per year without the two parties fixing an end date.

As for a lease that is binding on both parties, if the tenant cannot

---

## Page 15026

leave before the term expires, the landlord may not evict him before its expiry, whether because he receives a higher offer during the term or for any other reason. This applies whether the property is an endowment or unrestricted private property, and whether it belongs to an orphan or anyone else. This is the position of the four imams and other leading Muslim scholars. None of the imams held that an unqualified lease could bind the tenant without binding the landlord, whether it concerns an endowment, an orphan’s property, or anything else. Even if a later scholar departs from this and reports disagreement over some aspect of it, the imams’ agreement predates him.

Allah Most High has commanded the fulfilment of contracts and covenants. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“On the Day of Resurrection, a banner will be raised by the backside of every betrayer, proportionate to his betrayal.”

He also said:

“Whoever possesses four traits is a complete hypocrite, and whoever possesses one of them has a trait of hypocrisy until he abandons it: when he speaks, he lies; when entrusted, he betrays the trust; when he makes a covenant, he breaks it; and when he disputes, he transgresses all bounds.”

If the administrator tells the prospective tenant, “I will draw up a lease for you; take up residence,” he has thereby leased the property to him. If he has not leased it to him, he is not permitted to hand the property over to him, for he would then have handed over the endowment and the orphan’s property in an impermissible manner, thereby committing a wrong and incurring liability.

If the occupant had not rented the property, he would be entitled to leave whenever he wished, and he would be a usurper. He would not owe the stipulated rent, but rather the fair rental value of the use he had enjoyed, according to one of the two scholarly opinions. According to those who hold that no compensation is owed for the use of usurped property, no compensation would be due

---

## Page 15027

from him.

At most, it might be said that he took possession of it under an invalid lease. Were that so, he would be entitled to leave whenever he wished; indeed, he would be obliged to return the property to the landlord, as with anything received under an invalid contract. Moreover, according to one of the two scholarly opinions, he would owe the stipulated rent or the fair rental value. According to the other, he would owe whichever of the two is less.

Thus, a higher offer may not be accepted, whether the property is an endowment, an orphan’s property, or anything else, unless the lease is nonbinding. This is the case when the tenant is free to leave and hand the property back to them whenever he wishes—what people commonly call “vacating and locking up.” If he is free to vacate and lock up, the landlord is likewise free to evict him and lease the property to someone else, even if no higher offer has been made. He must act according to what he judges to be in the best interests.

---

## Page 15028

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Is it permissible to lease orchards and land containing date palms or other trees before their fruit has begun to ripen, for one or two years, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

There are three opinions on this issue.

The first is that this is impermissible under all circumstances, on the grounds that it falls within the Messenger of Allah’s prohibition—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—against selling fruit before it has begun to ripen. It is therefore impermissible, just as it is outside such a lease: for example, purchasing the fruit alone after it has appeared but before it has begun to ripen, with the seller bearing the expense of watering and tending it until it is fully ripe.

This is the well-known position in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, and it is transmitted as an explicit ruling of Ahmad. Abū Hanīfah’s position on this is even more restrictive.

The proponents of this opinion disagree over whether it is permissible to circumvent this prohibition by leasing out the land and entering into a tree-tending contract stipulating a very small share of the fruit. They hold two opinions. Ahmad’s explicit ruling is that this is impermissible. Al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā stated in his book Ibtāl al-hiyal (Invalidating Legal Stratagems):

---

## Page 15029

that it is permissible. This is the recognized position among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī.

Yet this stratagem is unworkable even on the principles of those who uphold the validity of legal stratagems. It is invalid on several grounds:

One is that, among the many properties concerned, some are endowments or belong to an orphan or someone else whose assets are administered under legal guardianship. Entering into a tree-tending contract for such property in return for a very small share of its fruit is impermissible, and making either contract conditional upon the other is invalid.

Another is that the harm underlying the prohibition against selling fruit before it has begun to ripen—such as its involving uncertainty akin to gambling, and its leading to disputes and enmity, among the very harms for which gambling was prohibited—is present in these transactions to an even greater extent than in the simple sale of fruit before it has begun to ripen.
