It is well known that the party undertaking such an arrangement pays his money only in return for the benefit he will derive from the land and trees, especially where the benefit from the trees predominates, as in gardens and orchards mostly occupied by trees or containing little open ground. Here, if Allah withholds the fruit and the party liable for the rent is required to pay it in full, the resulting wrongful consumption of wealth, disputes, and evil are plain to see.

Another is that renting for a thousand dirhams land whose rental value is one hundred dirhams is the conduct of financially irresponsible people who deserve to be placed under legal interdiction. The same applies to a tree-tending contract for a share

---

## Page 15030

of one thousandth for their owner—another act of financial irresponsibility warranting legal interdiction. Whoever does this must be placed under legal interdiction by the authorities, rather than having the contract enforced and declared valid.

If it is argued, “He may grant a concession under this contract because of the concession he receives from the other party under the other contract,” the answer is: “If he is entitled to that reciprocal concession, then one of the two contracts must be a condition of the other. If he is not entitled to it, then he is granting a concession under this contract without a corresponding concession from the other party under that contract.”

Such an arrangement is beneficial only when both parties take possession of what they are to receive. If a man grants someone a concession on one commodity, and the other grants him a concession on another, and both take possession, it might be said that the intended purpose is thereby achieved, whether in a case like this or in a case like this. Yet the fruit may or may not materialize, while the other party is entitled to demand the full rent even if no fruit materializes. This is not financially sound conduct; rather, it is the conduct of financially irresponsible people who deserve to be placed under legal interdiction.

This is especially so if the person administering the property has no authority to make gratuitous concessions, such as an administrator of an endowment or an orphan’s property. He would say to the tenant, “I am obliged to demand the full rent from you, whether or not the fruit materializes.” Would a financially responsible person enter into such an arrangement, paying a thousand dirhams for the use of land whose rental value is a hundred dirhams, hoping that the fruit will be delivered to him and come into his possession, while remaining liable for the rent whether or not the fruit materializes? If he did so, would this be anything other than engaging in the very thing the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited?

---

## Page 15031

For in the two Sahīh collections, Ibn ‘Umar reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“forbade selling fruit before its soundness became apparent, forbidding both seller and buyer.”

If this tenant pays his money to obtain the fruit, then, according to their own position, he is effectively the purchaser whom the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited from doing so. How, then, can he pay his money for such a transaction? There is abundant evidence that transactions of this kind are invalid, which we have discussed elsewhere. A jurist is not someone who takes a practice that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited to avert harm to people, only to turn instead to something even more harmful. Such a person is like someone seeking refuge from scorching ground in a fire.

This can be understood from the principle of invalidating legal stratagems: many of them entail greater corruption and harm than openly committing the prohibited act. As Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī said:

“They seek to deceive Allah as though they were deceiving children. It would be less objectionable to me if they did it straightforwardly.”

For this reason, tahlīl marriage—a marriage intended to make a divorced woman lawful again for her former husband—entails greater corruption than temporary marriage. A man entering a temporary marriage intends to be married for a certain period, whereas a man entering a tahlīl marriage has no such purpose. Every harm underlying the prohibition of temporary marriage is therefore present in tahlīl marriage, with further harms besides. This is why people’s hearts recoil more strongly from tahlīl marriage than from temporary marriage. Temporary marriage was permitted in the early days of Islam

---

## Page 15032

and the early Muslims disputed whether it remained lawful. Marriage for the purpose of tahlīl, however, was never permitted, nor did the early Muslims dispute its prohibition. Whoever denounces the Shia for permitting temporary marriage while himself permitting tahlīl marriage gives them an opening to attack the Sunnah, just as Christians have used such permission for tahlīl marriage to attack Islam. They have even said:

“Their prophet told these people: ‘If one of you divorces his wife, she will not be lawful for him until she commits fornication.’”

This is because tahlīl marriage is fornication, as the Companions themselves called it.

The second view on the original question is that, if the use of the land is the principal object and the trees are incidental, the land may be leased with the trees included incidentally. This is Mālik’s position, and he sets the incidental portion at one-third. Someone holding this view permits the sale of fruit before its soundness becomes apparent when it is included incidentally within another transaction. Similarly, in a purchase made after pollination, the purchaser may stipulate that the fruit be included, as is established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the two Sahīh collections. Here, the purchaser has bought the fruit before its soundness becomes apparent, but only as an adjunct to the principal property. This is permissible by scholarly consensus. Thus, he draws an analogy between what is included incidentally in a lease and what is included incidentally in a sale.

The third view is that the land and trees may be leased together, even if the trees predominate. This is the view of Ibn ‘Aqīl, and it is what is reported from the Commander of

---

## Page 15033

the Faithful, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb. He leased out the orchard of Usayd ibn Hudayr for three years, received the rent, and used it to settle Usayd’s debt. This was related by Harb al-Kirmānī, a companion of Imam Ahmad, in his well-known collection of questions answered by Ahmad. It was also related by Abū Zur‘a al-Dimashqī and others, and is well known as an action of ‘Umar. Trees predominate in the orchards of Medina.

One of the Zāhirī jurists of the Maghreb mentioned this report from ‘Umar in his writings and claimed that it contradicted scholarly consensus. This claim has no substance; indeed, a claim of consensus permitting it would be more plausible. ‘Umar did this in the Prophet’s city, in the presence of the Emigrants and the Helpers. This was a matter likely to become widely known, yet no objection to it has been reported from anyone. They would object to lesser matters, even when ‘Umar himself did them, just as ‘Imrān ibn Husayn and others objected to his stance on combining ‘umrah with Hajj. This case is comparable to ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān’s ruling that the wife of ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf should inherit from him, although he had irrevocably divorced her during his final illness, and to similar cases. What ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb did was correct.

When a jurist reflects on the foundational principles of the Sharia, it becomes clear to him that property of this kind does not fall within the scope of what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited. This becomes evident in several ways:

First, it is well known that land can be leased, and that the grain growing on it can be sold before it hardens. Yet when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling grain before it hardens, this did not constitute a prohibition on leasing land

---

## Page 15034

even though the tenant’s objective is the grain. For it is the tenant who works the land until he obtains the grain, unlike the purchaser, who buys only the grain, while the seller remains responsible for tending it until it is ready. Likewise, the Prophet’s prohibition on selling grapes before they turn dark does not prohibit someone from taking trees under a lease, tending them and watering them until they bear fruit. Rather, the prohibition concerns someone who purchases only the grapes, while the seller remains responsible for tending them until they fully ripen, as happens when people purchase grapes from what are known as vineyards. For this reason, these people do not sell them until their ripeness becomes apparent, unlike the practice of leasing the trees.

Second:

Sharecropping on land is comparable to tending trees in return for a share of their fruit. Both are permissible according to the hadith jurists, such as Imam Ahmad and others, including Ibn Khuzayma and Ibn al-Mundhir. Sharecropping is also permissible according to Ibn Abī Laylā, Abū Yūsuf, Muhammad, al-Layth ibn Sa‘d and other imams. Its permissibility is established by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the consensus of his Companions after him.

Those who prohibited it thought that it fell under leasing and would therefore constitute a lease for an unspecified consideration, which is impermissible. Abū Hanīfa applied his analogy consistently and did not permit it under any circumstances. Al-Shāfi‘ī, however, made an exception where there was a need, as when open land is included incidentally with the trees in an agreement to tend them for a share of their fruit. Mālik did likewise, though, in accordance with his principles, account is taken of whether the amount of such land is small or large. These jurists also regarded profit-sharing investment as an exception to analogy, supposing that it fell under

---

## Page 15035

hiring for an unspecified consideration, and that it was permitted out of necessity, since the owner of the money cannot lease it out.

The correct understanding is that these transactions are partnerships. Sharecropping is a partnership: one party contributes the use of his labour, and the other the use of his property. Whatever profit Allah apportions is shared between them, as between partners in an ‘inān partnership. Thus, the work itself is neither the object of the contract nor specified as it is in a contract of hire. If this were a contract of hire, the work would have to be specified.

To describe it as a reward-based contract would be closer, since the work in such a contract need not be specified. Moreover, it is a revocable, non-binding contract. Yet it is not a reward-based contract either, because in such a contract the two parties seek different kinds of things: one seeks the return of his runaway slave or the construction of his wall, while the other seeks the stipulated reward. In agreements to tend trees for a share of their fruit, sharecropping agreements, and profit-sharing investments, however, both parties seek the same kind of thing: profit. They share alike in gain and loss: if one receives something, so does the other; and if one receives nothing, neither does the other.

For this reason, what is stipulated for either party must be an undivided share of the profit, of the same kind as that stipulated for the other; it cannot be a specified fixed amount. This establishes that these transactions are partnerships, like an ‘inān partnership, in which the two parties share in the profit. If a fixed sum were stipulated, whether from the profit or otherwise, that would not be permissible. This is the form of sharecropping that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited, as related

---

## Page 15036

in detail in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, on the authority of Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj: they used to lease out land, stipulating that the landowner would receive the crops from a particular plot, such as those growing along large waterways and irrigation channels. Sometimes the crops in one area would survive while those in another would not.

For this reason, al-Layth ibn Sa‘d said:
“The form of sharecropping that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited is something that anyone knowledgeable about the lawful and unlawful would, upon examining it, recognize as impermissible.”

This reflects the juristic understanding of al-Layth, of whom al-Shāfi‘ī said:
“Al-Layth had a deeper understanding of jurisprudence than Mālik.”

For he explained that what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited accords with analogical reasoning from established principles, since it involves stipulating a particular portion of the profit for one of the two partners. A partnership requires fairness between the partners in the gains they receive and the losses they bear. If it departs from this, it becomes an unlawful injustice. What a difference there is between someone who regards what the Sunnah teaches as consistent with established principles and someone who regards it as contrary to them!

Anyone who gives the matter due consideration will recognize that the Sunnah’s prohibition of this form of sharecropping, its account of the agreement with the people of Khaybar for half of the fruit and crops produced there without this stipulation, and the Companions’ practice of profit-sharing investment all accord with analogical reasoning. These transactions belong to the category of partnerships, not contracts of hire.

That being so, we say: it is well known that if someone contracts with another to tend trees in return for a share of their fruit, this is like entering into a sharecropping agreement with him

---

## Page 15037

on land for a share of its crops, or entrusting him with money for a share of the profit. The fruit is thus treated as growth and gain produced by one party’s labour and the other’s property. In the sale of fruit prohibited by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, however, the buyer does no work at all to produce it; rather, all the work falls to the seller. Thus, leasing land and trees to obtain fruit and crops is like leasing land to obtain crops.

The third point:

Fruit is treated like benefits and yields in endowments, loans for use, and similar arrangements. Trees may therefore be endowed so that the beneficiaries of the endowment benefit from their fruit, just as land may be endowed so that they benefit from its yield. It is also permissible to grant someone the fruit of trees, just as it is permissible to lend a mount for riding, lend a house for use, or lend a milk-producing animal for its milk.

All these arrangements involve gratuitously granting the growth and benefit of property. Giving someone a property to live in is like giving him an animal to ride, trees whose fruit he may harvest, land to cultivate, or a she-camel or ewe whose milk he may drink. These benefits may be the subject of gratuitous contracts, whether the underlying asset is rendered inalienable, as in an endowment, or not. They may also be the subject of partnership contracts; likewise, they may be the subject of contracts involving an exchange for consideration.

If it is objected:

“This entails making tangible assets the subject of a lease, whereas a lease is a contract for benefits, not tangible assets. Hiring a wet nurse is permitted only as an exception to analogical reasoning.”

The reply is:

There are two answers.

---

## Page 15038

The first answer:

Leasing land and trees is not a contract for a tangible asset. Rather, it is comparable to leasing land for cultivation. The tangible produce is what the tenant seeks, since he leases the land only to obtain crops; nevertheless, the contract concerns benefits, which are analogous to these tangible yields.

The second answer:

We do not concede that hiring a wet nurse is contrary to analogical reasoning. How could this be said when the only hiring expressly mentioned in the Quran as part of our law is the hiring of a wet nurse, in the words of Allah Most High:

“If they breastfeed for you, give them their payment.”

Those who considered this contrary to analogical reasoning did so only because they mistakenly assumed that a lease could concern benefits alone. That is not the case. Rather, a lease may concern anything that can be obtained while its underlying source remains intact, whether tangible produce or a benefit.

Since a wet nurse’s milk can be obtained while its source remains intact, as can the water accumulated in a well, these may be the subject of a lease just as benefits may. Allah brings these tangible yields into existence successively while their source remains intact, just as He brings benefits into existence successively while their source remains intact. Hence, these underlying assets may be endowed with these yields—both tangible produce and benefits—reserved.

If it is objected:

“This would entail permitting the leasing of an animal in order to drink its milk.”

The reply is:

This question, too, is disputed among the jurists. Crop-sharing can only be justified by evidence

---

## Page 15039

grounded in Islamic law—a scriptural text, consensus, analogical reasoning, or the like. As for disputed cases raised as objections, we give a general answer:

If the evidence we have cited establishes the validity of this lease, that evidence must be applied consistently and acted upon. If it does not establish its validity, this constitutes no flaw in our argument.

As for the evidence said to invalidate this lease, if it can be reconciled with the evidence already mentioned, there is no contradiction. Otherwise, the evidence we have cited carries greater weight, for prohibiting benefits rests only on the same kind of grounds invoked in the disputed case here.

If it is objected:

“Ibn ʿAqīl permitted leasing land and trees together because of need, following Mālik’s approach. However, Mālik stipulated that the trees be few, whereas Ibn ʿAqīl imposed no such restriction. There is a need to lease open land containing trees, and leasing the land separately from the trees is impossible or difficult because of the harm this entails. He therefore permitted the trees to be included in the lease, just as al-Shāfiʿī permitted land to be included incidentally with trees in contracts to tend trees for a share of their fruit.

“Part of Ibn ʿAqīl’s argument is that this amounts, at most, to permitting fruit to be sold before its ripeness becomes apparent, as an adjunct to something else, because of need. Scriptural evidence and consensus permit this when someone sells trees bearing fruit that has already appeared, with the purchaser stipulating that the fruit be included. The purchaser has thus bought both trees and fruit before the fruit’s ripeness becomes apparent. Yet what you have stated implies that permitting this accords with analogical reasoning, and that it is permissible without such need, even when the trees are leased on their own.”

---

## Page 15040

The reply is:

This provides further confirmation, for this question may be approached on two grounds. The first is to concede that the basic principle entails prohibition, but that need makes it permissible, as in comparable cases.

The second is to reject this premise and say: “We do not concede that the basic principle entails prohibition. Rather, the evidence does not cover a case such as this, either in its wording or in its underlying meaning.”

As for its wording, this person has not sold fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent. Had he sold it, he would have borne the costs of delivery, just as he would also bear those costs if he sold it after its ripeness became apparent.

The Muslims have agreed upon what the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—did in imposing kharāj (land tax) on the Sawād and other lands conquered by force. This holds whether the position taken is that this is obligatory on land conquered by force, or that the land should be made communal property, as Mālik held—which is also one reported view of Ahmad—or that it must be divided among those who took part in the conquest, as al-Shāfi‘ī held—which is also one reported view of the Imam—or that the ruler may choose between these two alternatives, as held by Abū Hanīfah, al-Thawrī, Abū ‘Ubayd, and others. This last position is also the apparent position of Imam Ahmad’s school.

Al-Shāfi‘ī maintains that ‘Umar obtained the willing consent of those who took part in the conquest before making the land communal property and imposing kharāj upon it. Thus, the Muslims have agreed in general that imposing kharāj on land conquered by force is permissible, provided that it entails no injustice to those who took part in the conquest.

---

## Page 15041

Moreover, according to most of them, kharāj is rent for the land, and ‘Umar did not fix the duration of the lease because of the general benefit it served. Kharāj is a levy on both land bearing trees and treeless land. He imposed a specified amount on each jarīb (a unit of land area) of date palms, and a specified amount on each jarīb of vines. This is precisely a lease of land together with its trees. If permitting this accords with legal analogy, that establishes our point. If, however, it is permitted because of a compelling need, then people own orchards containing dwellings that command substantial rents. If they entrust these properties to someone to cultivate under fruit-sharing and crop-sharing agreements, the benefit of the dwellings on them is lost, as happens in the countryside around Damascus and similar places.

Furthermore, the property may be an endowment, belong to an orphan, or the like. How, then, can it be permissible to forgo the benefit of the dwellings built in those gardens? The dwelling may provide most of the benefit, with the trees and land being merely ancillary. They therefore need to lease those dwellings, yet cannot lease them separately from the use of the land and trees.

For if the cultivator is someone other than the resident, both suffer harm: there is harm from the construction of the dwellings, and the resident and his dependants remain barred from benefiting from the fruit and crops, despite their being right there with them. They are also inconvenienced by the cultivator entering their home. The cultivator, too, cannot remain confident that his fruit and crops are safe; rather, he fears for them during his absence. Not every resident is trustworthy, and even if he is trustworthy, his guests, children, and womenfolk cannot be relied upon. All this is well known.

Thus, if the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade muzābanah, which is the sale of

---

## Page 15042

fresh dates for dried dates, because this involves exchanging an item subject to the rules of ribā for another of the same kind without measuring their quantities—and the prohibitions concerning ribā are stricter than those concerning gambling—yet he permitted ‘arāyā sales on the basis of estimated yields because of need, and instructed a man to sell a tree he owned on another person’s property, or give it to him, because the property owner suffered harm from the man entering his property. When he did neither, the Prophet ordered the tree to be uprooted. Thus he required a transfer for compensation to remove the harm suffered by the property owner, just as he established a co-owner’s right to acquire a share at its sale price to avert the harm arising from co-ownership and partition. How much more so, then, when the harm is as described!

It is well known that the Sharia came to secure and perfect benefits, and to eliminate and reduce harms. We have been commanded to give precedence to the greater of two benefits by forgoing the lesser, and to avert the greater of two evils by tolerating the lesser. The harm in this situation is greater than the harm we previously mentioned as possibly affecting one of the parties to the exchange. Here, the harm is considerable and certain; there, if any harm occurs, it is slight and doubtful.

Moreover, fruit-sharing and crop-sharing agreements depend on the cultivator’s trustworthiness, which may often be difficult to find. People therefore need leasing arrangements, under which payment is a binding debt. For this reason, many people in many places and times turn from crop-sharing to leasing.

It is well known that the Sharia imposes obligations according to what is possible, and likewise stipulates conditions for acts of worship and contracts according to what is possible. Thus, despite a ruler’s sinfulness, legal effect may still be given to such of his acts of governance and distribution

---

## Page 15043

and adjudication as are permissible, although a ruler must be upright whenever this is possible without causing a greater harm. The same applies to prayer leaders when it is only possible to pray behind a sinful person.

Thus, if land can only be handed over to a sinful person, and entrusting him with it would lead to harm, it becomes necessary to lease it to him. This is one ground for permitting leasing. Moreover, there may be no one available to take it under a sharing arrangement, such as fruit-sharing or crop-sharing. Unless it is leased out, it will lie unused and its owners will suffer harm, even if they are poor. The only harm involved here is the possibility that the fruit yield may fall below its usual level, leaving an element of risk.

This degree of risk can be offset by providing the lessee with compensation. Likewise, in a lawful lease, if the benefit is lost, the corresponding rent is no longer due. The same applies, according to the correct view, if the benefit is diminished: the rent is reduced in proportion to that diminution. It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) ordered that payment be remitted for produce struck by calamities, and said:

“If you sell something to your brother and it is struck by a calamity, it is not lawful for you to take any of your brother’s money. On what grounds would any of you take his brother’s money without right?”

This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad and others. This is because, although the fruit has been taken into possession, possession is not yet complete in a way that enables its recipient to harvest it. Similarly, when a lessee takes possession of the leased property, this does not constitute the complete possession that enables him to obtain its entire benefit. Thus, if the benefit is lost before

---

## Page 15044

he is able to obtain it, the rent ceases to be due. Likewise, if the fruit perishes before it can be harvested, its price ceases to be due. Thus, suppose a calamity prevents the leased orchard from yielding its usual benefit—for example, rainfall or river water diminishes, reducing the benefit below its customary level. The property covered by the contract must remain in a condition that allows its intended benefit to be obtained. If it ceases to be in that condition, the lessee is entitled either to rescind the contract or to receive compensation. This is not a case of remission on account of a calamity affecting what is unobtainable, as with purchased fruit; rather, it concerns the destruction or loss of the benefit intended under the contract. In this respect, the lessee of an orchard is exactly like the lessee of land.

He takes possession only of the underlying property, which he himself tends until the crop grows firm and the fruit begins to ripen, just as the worker does under fruit-sharing and crop-sharing arrangements. If it is permissible to describe this person as a purchaser of the fruit, then the lessee should likewise be described as a purchaser of the crop, and the worker in fruit-sharing, crop-sharing, and profit-sharing investment partnerships as a purchaser of the resulting growth.

Since this does not fall within what is termed a sale, it cannot fall within the literal scope of general wording concerning sales. Nor can it be included by analogy or through the general scope of that wording’s meaning, for the distinction between the two is abundantly clear. Classifying this lease with land leases—since the two are treated alike in fruit-sharing, crop-sharing, profit-sharing investment partnerships, endowments, and other matters in which the ruling on one applies to the other—is more appropriate than classifying it with sales, as explained above.

---

## Page 15045

Anyone who considers this matter soundly and carefully will see that it belongs to the category of leases and leasing arrangements known as damān. Ordinary people call it damān, while the early generations called it qibālah; it does not belong to the category of sales.

The rules governing sales do not apply here, such as the seller’s responsibility for the costs of delivering what is due in full. All the Prophet’s prohibitions—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—concerning the sale of things not yet in existence concern a different situation. These include his prohibition of the sales known as malāqīh, madāmīn, and habal al-habalah: selling what is in the loins of breeding males, what is in the wombs of females, and the offspring of offspring. They also include his prohibition of selling future years’ produce, known as mu‘āwamah, and similar transactions. In these cases, the purchaser buys the particular things that have not yet been created, while the seller tends the animals or trees from which they are to come. The seller breeds the animals and cultivates the trees, then delivers the resulting offspring and fruit to the purchaser. This is what people used to do in the pre-Islamic era.

This follows the majority’s interpretation of “habal al-habalah” as the sale of the offspring of offspring; its invalidation would then be due to uncertainty about the due date. These sales prohibited by the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—are forms of gambling, which is maysir, and constitute the wrongful consumption of wealth. The owners of these animals and trees can postpone the sale until Allah creates whatever fruit and offspring He creates. They engage in such transactions only to take risks and gamble on chance.

Commercial dealings involving the lease of an orchard to someone who tends it, however, are like leasing land to someone who cultivates it, or granting a ground lease to someone who builds and plants on it, and similar arrangements.

---

## Page 15046

The scholars agree that when the benefit contracted for in a lease is lost before the lessee can make use of it, no rent is due for that benefit. An example is hiring an animal that dies before one can use it. Likewise, if an item sold perishes before the purchaser can take possession of it—for example, someone buys a qafīz (a measure of grain) from a heap, and the heap perishes before that quantity is separated out and taken into possession—the seller bears the loss, without dispute. They disagree, however, about its perishing after the purchaser has been enabled to take possession but before actually doing so, as when someone purchases a defective item and is enabled to take possession of it. There are two well-known opinions on this.

The first is that the seller is not liable. This is the position of Mālik and the well-known position reported from Ahmad, based on Ibn ʿUmar’s statement:

“The established Sunnah is that grain sold after it has been gathered belongs to the purchaser.”

The second is that the seller is liable. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī, although Abū Hanīfah excepts real property. Nevertheless, he holds that placing the property at the purchaser’s disposal constitutes taking possession, as does Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him.

His position thus comes close to that of Mālik and Ahmad: the seller bears the loss when the property perishes, on the basis of the authentic report that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity strikes it, it is not lawful for you to take any of your brother’s wealth. How can any of you take his brother’s wealth without right?”

According to al-Shāfiʿī’s well-known position, however, the purchaser bears the loss if it perishes

---

## Page 15047

after taking possession of it. As for Abū Hanīfah, his position is that leaving the fruit on the trees is neither entailed by the contract nor permissible to stipulate.

Those who hold the first view say that taking possession of this fruit is comparable to taking possession of the benefit under a lease: it is not complete possession that transfers liability for loss, because the recipient has not yet been able to obtain what the contract is intended to provide.

This consistently applies their principle that what matters is the ability to obtain what the contract is intended to provide. Hence, they say that if the purchaser neglects to gather the fruit after it has fully ripened, and it consequently perishes, he bears the loss, just as he would if he neglected to take possession of a specifically identified item until it perished. This distinction has a clear and operative rationale: if the seller has not neglected any of his obligations, and the negligence is solely the purchaser’s, it is more fitting to place liability for loss upon the negligent party than upon someone who has fulfilled his obligations without negligence.

For this reason, they agree on the corresponding ruling concerning leases: if the lessee neglects to make use of the benefits until they are lost, he bears the loss. If they are lost without negligence on his part, the lessor bears the loss. Likewise, if a calamity prevents the lessee from cultivating the land, no rent is due from him. If, however, the crop grows and then a natural calamity strikes before it can be harvested, there is disagreement.

Those who distinguish this case from that of fruit and leased benefits argue that the fruit itself is the subject of the contract, as is the benefit in a lease. Here, however, the crop is not the subject of the contract; rather, it is the benefit, which the lessee has already received in full.

Those who treat the two cases alike argue that the purpose of the lease is the crop, so if

---

## Page 15048

a natural calamity prevents him from obtaining what the lease is intended to provide, then the intended object of the contract has been lost before he could take possession of it. Although the lessor did not receive payment for a crop, he did receive payment for the benefit that enables the lessee to obtain a crop. Thus, if a natural calamity destroys the crop before it can be harvested, the benefit contracted for has not been secured; rather, it has been lost before it could be enjoyed. There is no difference between the land becoming unusable at the beginning of the lease term or at its end, if the lessee has been unable to derive any benefit from it. Clearly, if a natural calamity causes the crop to be lost altogether, such that the land cannot be put to use under those conditions, it makes no difference whether the calamity strikes early or late. The question of “leasing orchards for a fixed payment” rests on this principle. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is it permissible to lease out orchards before their fruit ripens?

He replied:

As for leasing out one’s garden or orchard containing date palms, grapevines, and other trees, for a specified payment, to someone who tends the trees and cultivates the land: some scholars have prohibited this, considering it to fall under the Prophet’s prohibition—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—of selling fruit before its soundness becomes apparent.

---

## Page 15049

Some of these scholars, however, permit this when the open land is the principal object of the lease and the trees are incidental, as is reported from Mālik. Others among them permit a stratagem whereby the land is leased and a separate contract is concluded for tending the trees in return for a share of their yield. However, if either contract is made conditional upon the other, the arrangement is invalid. If neither is made conditional upon the other, the orchard’s owner may require the lessee to pay the rent for the land without the fruit-sharing contract. Yet the lessee’s chief objective is the fruit, which forms a substantial part of what he seeks. Moreover, the property may be an endowment or belong to an orphan, in which case preferential concessions in its fruit-sharing contract are impermissible.

Although Judge Abū Ya‘lā mentioned this stratagem in his book *Ibtāl al-Hiyal* (The Invalidation of Legal Stratagems), following others, Ahmad’s expressly stated ruling is that it is invalid. Elsewhere, we have demonstrated with numerous proofs the invalidity of stratagems whose outward form contradicts their underlying reality and whose purpose is to do what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden—such as stratagems to circumvent the prohibition of usury, extinguish the right of pre-emption, and so forth.

Other scholars permit leasing the land and trees without this restriction, even when the trees are an intended object of the lease, as Ibn ‘Aqīl has mentioned. This is the sounder view, and it rests on two grounds.

The first is that when land and trees are combined, they may both be leased together, since ordinarily they cannot be separated.

---

## Page 15050

The second ground is that this arrangement does not fall within the Prophet’s prohibition, peace and blessings be upon him, for the landowner has not sold his fruit without any payment at all. The two arrangements differ in several respects:

First, leasing the land is permissible, whereas purchasing the crop before its grain has hardened, on condition that it remain standing, is not permissible. The same distinction therefore applies to trees.

Second, the seller is responsible for watering and whatever else the fruit requires until it is fully ripe; none of this is incumbent upon the purchaser. By contrast, the orchard contractor or lessee undertakes the watering and work until the fruit and crops are produced. Purchasing fruit is purchasing the grapes and fresh dates themselves, with the seller responsible for completing the work until they ripen. This differs from the case of someone entrusted with an orchard who tends it himself.

Third, if someone hands over an orchard to another person to tend in return for half its fruit and crops, this constitutes a fruit-sharing and crop-sharing arrangement. The worker thus becomes entitled to half the fruit and crops through his labour; this is not a purchase of grain and fruit.

Fourth, if someone lends his land to another person to cultivate, or gives him his tree to benefit from its yield and then return it to him, this constitutes a loan for use, not a gift of the property itself.

Fifth, the fruit of trees forms part of an endowment’s yield, like the benefit derived from land and the milk of

---

## Page 15051

a wet nurse. Hiring a wet nurse is permissible according to the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Since milk is produced gradually, it may validly be the subject of a hiring contract, just as the use of property may, even though milk is a tangible substance. Thus, an owner may lease livestock for their milk. Leasing an orchard to someone who obtains its yield through his own labour belongs to this category, not to that of a purchase.

If it is objected, “This involves uncertainty,” the answer is: “It is like the uncertainty involved in leasing land.” Someone who leases land to cultivate it seeks its crops, which may or may not materialize. It is established that, after the death of Usayd ibn Hudayr, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb leased out his orchard for three years, collected the rent, and used it to discharge his debt. None of the Companions objected to this.

Furthermore, when the Muslims conquered land by force, ʿUmar handed it over to them, including its date palms and grapevines, for cultivation in return for the land tax. Most scholars regard this as a lease.

He was asked about orchard leases: when the lessees heard that the forsaken enemy was approaching, they entered the city, whose gates were then shut. They no longer had any access to the orchards, and their crops and produce were plundered. Were they entitled to a reduction in the rent on account of this loss?

---

## Page 15052

He replied:

Widespread fear that prevents the use of property is classed among heaven-sent calamities. If crops are destroyed by such a calamity, should the rent be reduced to account for the loss, just as the price of fruit is reduced? The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) expressly prescribed this in the authentic hadith narrated by Muslim:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity strikes it, it is not lawful for you to take any of your brother’s money. On what grounds would any of you take his brother’s money without right?”

Scholars hold two opinions on whether a reduction should be granted when crops are destroyed before the lessee has had the opportunity to harvest them. The opinion more consistent with the explicit texts and established principles is that it should be granted. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Is leasing out a land grant valid or not?

He replied:

Leasing out a land grant is valid. We know of no Muslim scholar whose opinions are relied upon in issuing fatwas who has declared it invalid. Nor do we know of any fair-minded scholar who has declared it invalid, except that we have heard reports of some people relating differing opinions on the matter: one permitting it, another prohibiting it, and a third permitting it for one year only.

Muslims have continually leased out such grants, and no one has issued a fatwa prohibiting this except some people of the present age, because of a misconception that occurred to them: they believed that the recipient of a land grant was in the same position as

---

## Page 15053

a borrower. They overlooked the fact that holders of land grants are entitled to the benefits, rather than merely being allowed to enjoy them, just as successive generations of beneficiaries have rights under an endowment—even though a lease may terminate upon the beneficiary’s death, according to those who hold this view. The ruler is a distributor, not a benefactor, and his apportionment of rights of use is like his apportionment of property. They also overlooked the fact that the ruler who made the grant authorized its recipient to derive income from it and lease it out. Even if someone lending property authorizes the borrower to lease it out, the lease is valid by agreement. How much more so, then, in the case of a land grant! Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leases an undivided half share in an orchard that has not been partitioned. Its walls had fallen into disrepair, so the lessee and the owner of the other half agreed to rebuild them. They divided the walls between themselves so that each would rebuild his assigned portion. The lessee rebuilt his portion, but the other man refused, until most of the fruit was stolen. He also refused to irrigate, until most of the fruit was ruined.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, if he failed to carry out what they had agreed upon, and some of the fruit was lost because of that neglect, he must compensate his partner for whatever was lost from his share. If, however, he refuses from the outset to join in rebuilding and irrigation, he is compelled to do so according to the more correct of the scholars’ two opinions. According to the other opinion, he is not compelled, but the other party may undertake the rebuilding and irrigation.

---

## Page 15054

And the party who does not undertake rebuilding and irrigation is barred from benefiting from what his property yields. Anyone who persists in neglecting an obligation thereby compromises his uprightness.

He was asked:

Concerning the leasing of endowed property: May it be leased for several years? And may each year be leased separately? If the lessee cuts down trees on the endowed property, must he compensate for their value or not? If someone purchases the endowed property for less than its value, what must he do?

He replied:

If the endowment serves a public purpose, it may be leased as its best interests require. According to most scholars, no fixed limit is set on the number of years. The lessee must pay compensation for whatever he cuts down. The beneficiary of the endowment may not sell the endowed property; rather, he must return the purchase price to the buyer, and the property remains an endowment as before.

---

## Page 15055

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An emir took over a land grant and found a farmer renting the land. The farmer remained its tenant until the grant expired, after which it passed to someone else. The new grantee found that the farmer had left some of the rented land fallow. He demanded from the former grantee the revenue due on the fallow land, claiming that the lease drawn up for the farmer was invalid because part of the land was occupied. Is the lease invalid or valid? Is the tenant liable for the fallow land or not?

He replied:

The second grantee has no right to demand payment from the former grantee for the land left fallow, just as he has no right to demand payment from him for the land cultivated. His claim lies against the tenant to whom the land was leased and handed over, whether he cultivated it or not. The grantee, however, has a choice: he may demand the rent accepted by the first grantee, or he may demand the fair rental value of the use made available to the tenant.

It is permissible to lease land to someone who will grow crops and cane on it. The second grantee, however, may either uphold the lease or decline to uphold it. Even supposing that the first grantee had leased it to the tenant under an invalid lease and handed over the land before the second grantee received his grant, the tenant would owe compensation for all the land to the grantee

---

## Page 15056

who holds the second grant and is entitled to the use of the land, whether the tenant cultivated it or not. For whatever entails liability upon taking possession under a valid contract likewise entails liability upon taking possession under an invalid contract. Similarly, someone who takes possession of a purchased item under an invalid contract is liable for it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Some soldiers had land which they leased to farmers for a specified amount of produce, a specified sum in dirhams, and siyāfa, allowing them to cultivate it or otherwise benefit from it for a full year. Before the year ended, however, the soldiers wrongfully interfered with the farmers’ sheep and forcibly exacted a sum of dirhams for grazing before the lease expired. Is what the soldiers obtain lawful or unlawful?

He replied:

If their lease contains permissible conditions—for example, if the tenant stipulates that he may benefit from everything on the land, including freely available pasture, crop stubble, and other such things—then this is a binding condition that must be honoured.

The same applies if this is not expressly mentioned in the lease, but the lease is unrestricted and such use is customary. An unrestricted lease is understood to cover the customary use. If that use includes these benefits, then the unrestricted lease includes them. Thus, whatever is covered by the wording of the lease or by established custom belongs to the tenant.

---

## Page 15057

But if the custom is that an unrestricted lease does not include freely available pasture, then such pasture is not included in it. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leased some land on another man’s behalf, in accordance with Islamic law, for a specified period. The man on whose behalf it was leased subsequently died. When arranging the lease for this period, the agent had paid the lessor a year’s rent in advance through his agent. The landowner then brought a claim against the heir of the man on whose behalf the land had been leased, and was asked to establish the authority of the agent who had arranged the lease. Must the claimant prove that agency after having received a year’s rent from him, given that it was he, rather than the agent, who benefited from the leased land?

He replied:

If the person alleged to have had the land leased on his behalf benefited from it, he is liable to compensate for the use he enjoyed, whether or not the land was leased. If, in these circumstances, he does not acknowledge that he enjoyed its use under a lease or with the owner’s permission, then he is a usurper who deserves discretionary punishment of a kind that deters him and other transgressors like him from wronging people and denying their rights.

All this applies when neither the facts mentioned nor any facts left unmentioned indicate the existence of a lease. Even if the cultivator claimed that he cultivated the land only under a gratuitous loan, and the other party said:

---

## Page 15058

the landowner: “No, it was under a lease”—then the landowner’s account is accepted, as expressly stated by the imams Mālik, Ahmad, al-Shāfiʿī, and others.

Al-Shāfiʿī held one view concerning a dispute over a riding animal, where the rider says, “You lent it to me,” and the owner says, “Rather, I hired it out to you.” In this case, he held that the rider’s account is accepted. Some scholars of his school treated the two cases alike. The school’s established position in both cases is that the owner’s account is accepted.

Others distinguished between them, saying that people customarily lend riding animals freely, unlike land. Thus, according to one report, Mālik held that the owner’s account is accepted unless he is someone who would not ordinarily hire out animals. Abū Hanīfa likewise held that, in the case of a riding animal, the rider’s account is accepted. This is also one view within Imam Ahmad’s school.

In short, the correct position concerning land, held by the majority of scholars, is that the owner’s account is accepted. He is therefore entitled to demand rent in this case. But may he demand the rent he claims, the fair rental value, or the lesser of the two? There are three views on this within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere.

---

## Page 15059

He was also asked:

A farmer ploughed some land but did not sow it; someone else then sowed it. Is the first farmer entitled to wages and a share of the crop, or not?

He answered:

If the land is cultivated under a crop-sharing arrangement, with one share for the landowner and another for the farmer, the farmer’s share is divided between the ploughman and the sower in proportion to the work and money each contributed. Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man rented a plot of land and a specified water well from three people for an agreed rent, and a person cultivated the land. He then sold a half-share in the aforementioned person to one of the lessors, retaining ownership of the other half. He paid the other two lessors the rent corresponding to their respective shares, but paid the purchaser none of the rent due to him. On his deathbed, he declared before witnesses that all the rent due to the purchaser remained an outstanding debt upon him, with its legal status unchanged. He left nothing but

---

## Page 15060

a half-share in that person, while still owing the aforementioned rent and his wife’s dowry. Is the claimant entitled to receive payment on the same footing as his partners, or to share proportionately with them, his entitlement being assessed in light of the claims of others? Please give us your ruling.

He answered:

The rent owed by the tenant to one of the lessors remains an outstanding debt, even if the tenant did not acknowledge that it remained unpaid. His acknowledgment therefore reinforces this. However, his creditors may require the lessor to swear that he had not discharged the tenant from this rental obligation through payment, waiver, or any other means. Yet, from the time it passed into someone’s possession, his partners are entitled to demand their share of the rent from him from that time onward. This rent constitutes a debt for which the claimant shares proportionately with the other creditors.

He was also asked:

A man was granted a feddan of clayey land, entered it in the endowment register, and cultivated it. The soldier then died, and someone else was appointed in his place and prevented the man from continuing. The man obtained a decree from the sultan permitting him to continue as before, but the successor prevented him, although he had already sown the land. Is he entitled to the rent for the land, or to the crop?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If the grantor gave it to him from his own land grant, and it was transferred from the land-grant register to the endowment register, whose lands are not assigned as grants, and he ratified that arrangement,

---

## Page 15061

then the second grantee has no right to take it away.

If, however, the first grantee gave it to him gratuitously out of his land grant, and the second grantee has the right either to make such a gift or not, then the matter rests with the second grantee. The crop belongs to whoever sowed it, while the landholder is entitled to the customary rent from the time the land was granted to him until its use comes to an end. Before it was granted to him, however, the right to its use belonged to the first grantee, who made the gift, not to the second. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A cowherd took the cattle out to water them at a watering place customarily used for that purpose. When he had finished watering them, one of the cows was stricken with an illness from Allah Most High and fell into the water. People tried to get it to stand, but it could not. They dragged it onto dry land so that it might stand, but it still could not. It had not been struck or otherwise harmed. Its owner’s agent and a group of people came and saw what had happened to it. They considered it best to slaughter it, so they did so. Must the cowherd pay its value?

He answered:

The cowherd owes nothing if he was neither negligent nor guilty of wrongdoing. Indeed, if matters were as they described, those who slaughtered it likewise owe nothing, for they acted properly: slaughtering it was better than leaving it to die. A shepherd did something similar during the lifetime of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), and no objection was raised by

---

## Page 15062

the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), nor did he state that the shepherd was liable for compensation. This is analogous to Moses’ companion making a hole in the boat so that its owners could benefit from it once patched up, for that was better for them than losing it altogether. Likewise, if a man sees his fellow Muslim’s property facing destruction in similar circumstances and salvages it as best he can, he will be rewarded for doing so, even if its value is diminished. Something diminished is better than something destroyed. How much more so when he has been entrusted with its care, as a herdsman or the like?

He was also asked:

A man tends camels or sheep, and one of the animals falls ill or suffers some mishap, bringing it to the point of death. He then slaughters the animal in the prescribed manner to spare its owner the loss. The person doing this may also be someone other than a herdsman. Is he liable for compensation or not?

He answered:

If the animal is on the point of death, the herdsman should slaughter it in the prescribed manner, taking care to do so properly, for that is better than letting it die on its own. He is not liable for compensation for doing so. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15063

He was also asked:

A shepherd took charge of some sheep and entrusted them to his twelve-year-old boy. The boy took them out to graze, and two sheep went missing. Is the hired boy liable for compensation, or is the original shepherd?

He answered:

Liability rests with the person who entrusted them to the boy without their owners’ permission.

He was also asked:

Concerning orchards in Damascus taken under contract: when the victorious army defeated the enemy, arrived in Damascus, and encamped in the orchards, it let its animals graze on their crops and produce, which were consequently destroyed. Are those who took the orchards under contract entitled to a reduction in the amount due on account of this loss?

He answered:

Destruction caused by an army from which compensation cannot be recovered is treated as a natural calamity, like locusts. If crops are destroyed by a natural calamity before the other party has had the opportunity to harvest them, should the loss be deducted from the amount due, as it is for fruit that has been purchased? The scholars hold two views on this. The sounder of the two—and the more consistent with the Book, the Sunnah, and justice—is that the loss should be deducted.

---

## Page 15064

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him—about someone who says:

“I undertake to pay such-and-such an amount for it, even if, for example, locusts devour it.”

He answered:

This condition is invalid, for it involves undue uncertainty and gambling. If he has taken it under contract for a payment subject to this condition, that payment would be less than the fair equivalent payable without the condition. According to the well-known view, a distinction must then be made according to whether the contract is valid or invalid. If the contract is invalid, whatever was received under it, or its value, must be returned. If it is valid, the portion of the stipulated payment corresponding to the surviving produce is increased proportionately by the difference between the value with the condition and the value without it.

For example, if the stipulated payment was one thousand and one third of the fruit remained, the share corresponding to it would be one third of what remained of the thousand. The value of the whole subject to the condition is then considered, taking nine hundred . . . (1) one thousand two hundred; one third of it is then added to the stipulated payment and to its share. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 15065

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him—about someone who leased land, but it did not receive its usual rainfall and the crops perished: “Is the rent to be reduced on account of the calamity?”

He answered:

If someone leases land for cultivation and the usual rainfall does not come, he has the right to rescind the lease, by scholarly consensus. Indeed, if the land becomes unusable, the lease terminates without rescission, according to the stronger view. If, however, the benefit is diminished, the rent is reduced in proportion to that diminution. Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others expressly stated this.

Thus, one asks: “What would the rent for the land be with the usual supply of water?” The answer is: “One thousand dirhams.” Then one asks: “What would its rent be with this degree of rainfall deficiency?” The answer is: “Five hundred dirhams.” Half the stipulated rent is therefore deducted for the tenant, because part of the benefit to which he was contractually entitled was lost before he could obtain it. This is comparable to part of a purchased item being destroyed before the buyer could take possession of it.

Likewise, if locusts, fire, or another calamity strikes the land and destroys some of the crops, the rent is reduced in proportion to the reduction in benefit. As for the crops that are destroyed, their owner bears the loss; no compensation is owed to him by the owner of the

---

## Page 15066

land, by scholarly consensus. When some scholars observed this consensus, they assumed that the scholars also agreed that the stipulated rent should not be reduced in proportion to the diminution in benefit. They failed to distinguish between the benefit, for which the lessor remains liable until the lease expires, and the crops themselves, for which he bears no liability.

The scholars agree that if the benefit to which the tenant is contractually entitled diminishes, he has the right to rescind the lease. This applies, for example, when someone leases a mill, bathhouse, or orchard with a known water supply, and that supply falls substantially below its customary level. This differs from a calamity affecting fruit sold on the tree, concerning which there is a well-known disagreement. If someone purchases fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent and a calamity strikes it, the seller bears the loss according to the schools of Mālik and Ahmad.

This is also the position of al-Shāfiʿī, which he made conditional upon the authenticity of the report. The report is indeed authentically recorded in Sahīh Muslim, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity strikes it, it is not lawful for you to take any of your brother’s money. On what grounds would any of you take his brother’s money without right?”

Mālik stipulated that the loss must be substantial, exceeding one-third. This is also one opinion reported from Ahmad. The apparent position of Ahmad’s school, however, is that the price is reduced for losses both small and large.

The issue does not arise according to Abū Hanīfah’s position, since he does not distinguish between the period before ripeness becomes apparent and the period after it. Rather, he requires immediate picking under the contract in all cases. If the buyer stipulates that the fruit be left on the tree, the transaction is not permissible, even after its ripeness has become apparent. The other three distinguish between the period before ripeness becomes apparent and the period

---

## Page 15067

after it, as established by authentic hadiths. As for taking orchards under contract for one or more years, so that the contracting party may obtain their yield through his own irrigation and labour, in an arrangement resembling a lease, there is disagreement. Likewise, if one kind of fruit, such as mulberries, begins to ripen, may the fruit of the entire orchard be sold? There is disagreement concerning this too. The stronger view is that both arrangements are permissible, as has been discussed at length elsewhere.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leases land for cultivation, but a calamity strikes his crop and destroys it. Does the rule concerning calamity losses apply, or not?

He replied:

When someone leases land for cultivation and a calamity strikes his crop, this concerns “the question of reducing the price for calamity losses affecting fruit.” If someone purchases fruit whose ripeness has become apparent, and a calamity destroys it before it is fully ripe, the seller bears the loss according to the jurists of Medina, such as Mālik and others, and the jurists of hadith, such as Ahmad and others. This is also a conditional position of al-Shāfi'ī, who made his ruling dependent upon the authenticity of the hadith.

The hadith is indeed authentically recorded in Sahīh Muslim, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

---

## Page 15068

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity strikes it, it is not lawful for you to take any of your brother’s money. On what grounds would any of you take his brother’s money without right?”

Reasoning by analogy also supports this view: the goods sold perished before the purchaser could take possession of them. This resembles the loss of the benefits of a leased property before the tenant can make use of them.

If it is objected, “This fruit perished after possession,” the response is that taking possession of fruit that has not fully ripened is comparable to taking possession of the benefits of a lease. The purpose is to harvest the fruit once it is fully ripe. Thus, if the purchaser stipulates taking possession of it after it has fully ripened, he bears the risk of its loss.

The jurists have differed over whether he may sell it before harvesting it. There are two opinions, both reported from Ahmad:

The first is that this is not permissible, because it amounts to selling purchased goods before taking possession of them. Had he taken possession of the fruit, he would bear the risk of its loss.

The second—and this is the correct view—is that selling it is permissible. He has taken possession in a manner that permits him to dispose of it, although not in a manner that transfers the risk of loss to him. This resembles taking possession of leased property: once the tenant takes possession of it, he may dispose of its benefits, even though the lessor bears the loss if the leased property perishes.

However, the jurists have differed over whether he may sublet the property for more than he rented it for. There are three opinions, all reported from Ahmad.

---

## Page 15069

One view permits it, as al-Shāfiʿī holds.

Another prohibits it, as Abū Hanīfah and his two companions hold, because it constitutes profit from something whose risk he does not bear, since he does not bear the risk of loss of the benefits.

A third view permits it if he has made improvements to the property, but not otherwise. The first view is the most correct, because taking possession makes him bear the risk of losing those benefits: if he fails to make use of them, their loss is borne by him, not by the lessor. This is comparable to fruit perishing after it has begun to ripen and could have been harvested. However, if the leased property itself perishes, the lessor bears the loss of its benefits, because the tenant was unable to make use of them. A distinction must therefore be drawn between loss occurring before and after the tenant has had the opportunity to use them.

Section

As for someone who leases land for cultivation and a calamity strikes it: if the crop perishes after the tenant has had the opportunity to gather it—for example, a thief steals it from the threshing floor, or the tenant delays harvesting it beyond its proper time until it perishes—then the tenant must pay the rent.

If, however, the calamity prevents cultivation, no rent is due from him, and there is no disagreement on this point. But if the crop has sprouted and the calamity prevents it from reaching full maturity, such as

---

## Page 15070

fire, wind, hail, or anything else that destroys it and would likewise have destroyed any other crop grown there.

There are two views on this:

The stronger view is that the loss is borne by the lessor, because this calamity has destroyed the benefit intended by the contract. The intended benefit is that which enables the crop to remain growing until it can be harvested. If something happens to the land that entirely prevents this benefit, the purpose of the contract is frustrated before the tenant has had the opportunity to obtain it. The same applies if the land is saline and the crop consequently perishes, or if it lies beside a sea or river and the water ruins the land before the crop fully matures, and in similar cases.

In all these cases, the loss is borne by the lessor, and the tenant owes no rent for what has become unusable. This is comparable to the hired animal dying, or the water supply failing so that none of the benefit intended by the contract can be obtained, and other such cases.

This is not like the tenant’s property being stolen from the house or destroyed there by fire, for the benefit intended by the contract remains unchanged. Both he and others can still make use of the house by protecting it against thieves or fire. A comparable case is the loss of the goods for whose transport he hired an animal: he still owes the hire charge. This differs from a calamity that entirely prevents both him and others from making use of the property, for that is equivalent to the hired animal dying or the leased house burning down. And analogous to the theft of

---

## Page 15071

his belongings from the house is a thief stealing his crop. But if an army sweeps through and destroys the crop, this counts as a calamity beyond one’s control, for the army cannot be made to pay compensation, nor can one guard against it. A comparable case is an army sweeping through and expelling people from the homes in which they live.

Shaykh al-Islām Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, with no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and grant him abundant peace.

Section

“Remission of liability for losses caused by calamities” in sales, guarantees, and leases is a matter of pressing practical need. This falls under “the principle governing the destruction of the intended subject matter of a contract before one is able to take possession of it.”

---

## Page 15072

Allah Most High says in His Book:

“O you who believe, do not consume one another’s wealth unjustly, but only through trade by mutual consent.”

He also says:

“Do not consume one another’s wealth unjustly, nor offer it to the judges so that you may knowingly consume a portion of other people’s wealth sinfully.”

And, in His condemnation of the Children of Israel, He says:

“For their breaking of their covenant”—up to His words—“and their taking of usury, though they had been forbidden it, and their consuming people’s wealth unjustly.”

Whoever consumes people’s wealth unjustly takes one of the two things exchanged without delivering the other. The purpose of financial covenants and contracts is for both parties to take possession: each contracting party asks the other to deliver what was agreed upon. Hence Allah Most High says:

“And fear Allah, in whose name you appeal to one another.”

That is, you enter into covenants and contracts with one another. This is what contracts require and entail, because each contracting party, through the contract, undertakes to provide what the other has requested of him. Contracts therefore impose obligations to deliver possession, and taking possession is what is requested, intended, and sought.

Contracts are thus completed when both parties take possession. Indeed, if two unbelievers embrace Islam, or submit their dispute to our judgment, after both have taken possession under contracts they regard as valid, we do not interfere with those transactions, since the contracts have been concluded through the fulfilment of their requirements.

For this reason, exchanging one deferred obligation for another is forbidden: it creates a contract and imposes obligations without securing the intended result for either party individually or for both together. This is also why Allah has forbidden gambling, which includes sales involving uncertainty. Such uncertainty includes things of which one may be able to take possession

---

## Page 15073

or may not, such as animals that have strayed, since the purpose of the contract—taking possession—is beyond one’s control. For this reason, scholars have differed over “selling a debt owed by another person.” Two positions are reported from Ahmad on this issue, although the well-known position among his followers is that it is prohibited.

This same reasoning is given for prohibiting the sale of fruit before signs of ripening appear. Thus, both Sahīh collections report from Anas ibn Mālik:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade selling fruit until it began to ripen. He was asked, ‘What does beginning to ripen mean?’ He replied, ‘Until it turns red.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Consider: if Allah withholds the fruit, on what grounds does any one of you take his brother’s wealth?’”

Another wording states:

“He forbade selling fruit until signs of ripening appeared, and selling dates until they began to ripen. He was asked, ‘What does beginning to ripen mean?’ He replied, ‘Turning red or yellow.’”

Another wording states:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade selling fruit until it began to ripen. I asked Anas, ‘What is meant by its ripening?’ He said, ‘It turns red or yellow. Consider: if Allah withholds the fruit, on what grounds do you deem your brother’s wealth lawful for yourself?’”

These are al-Bukhārī’s wordings. Muslim reports:

“He forbade selling the fruit of date palms until it began to ripen.”

He also reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If Allah does not cause them to bear fruit, on what grounds does any one of you deem his brother’s wealth lawful for himself?”

Abū Masʿūd al-Dimashqī said: “Mālik and al-Darāwardī treated Anas’s words, ‘Consider: if Allah withholds the fruit,’ as part of the hadith of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They interpolated these words into it, and this is regarded as an error.”

---

## Page 15074

There is, however, reason to question what Abū Masʿūd has said. This principle is agreed upon among Muslims, without dispute. It is among the rulings on which all peoples and faiths must broadly agree, for it rests upon the justice and equity by which the heavens and earth stand, and for which Allah sent down the Scriptures and sent the messengers. As He, Most High, says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance, so that people may uphold justice.”

This is because transactions involving an exchange, such as sales and leases, rest upon balance and fairness between the two parties. Neither gives what he gives except to obtain what he seeks. Each therefore both receives and gives, seeking something from the other while being asked for something in return.

If the subject matter sought through the contract perishes before possession can be taken—for example, if leased property perishes before possession can be taken, or goods sold by measure or weight perish before being separately measured or weighed and delivered, or in similar cases—there is no obligation to pay the rent or purchase price.

If the loss occurs in circumstances in which no one can be held liable—that is, through an act of God—the contract becomes void. The purchase price must then be returned to the buyer if it has already been received from him; otherwise, he is released from the obligation to pay it.

If, however, the loss occurs in circumstances in which someone can be held liable—that is, a person who can be required to compensate for it destroys the property—the buyer may rescind the contract because the property perished before he could take possession. Alternatively, he may uphold the contract, since he can seek compensation from the person who destroyed it. If

---

## Page 15075

he rescinds the contract, the seller is entitled to seek compensation from the person who destroyed the property, while the buyer may reclaim the purchase price from the seller if the latter has received it. If he does not rescind it, he remains liable for the purchase price and may seek compensation from the person who destroyed the property. However, that person is liable only for the compensation due for its destruction, whereas the buyer may claim only the stipulated price due under the contract.

For this reason, the jurists of our school and others have stated:

The property may be destroyed by the seller, the buyer, or a third party, or it may perish through an act of God. If the buyer destroys it, his destruction of it is equivalent to taking possession, and his obligation to pay the consideration becomes binding. If it perishes through an act of God, the contract becomes void. If a third party destroys it, the buyer has the choice. If the seller destroys it, the more widely held of the two views treats this like destruction by a third party; the second treats it like loss through an act of God. This principle is firmly established in all contracts of exchange: if the subject matter of the contract perishes before possession can be taken, with no one liable for the loss, the contract becomes void. If someone is liable, there is a choice whether to uphold or rescind the contract.

The same applies to all other circumstances in which the purpose of the contract cannot be fulfilled, though hope of fulfilment remains. Examples include someone wrongfully seizing the property sold or leased; the seller becoming insolvent with respect to the purchase price; a wife being unable to obtain the maintenance, marital intimacy, or allotted time with her husband to which she is entitled; or a husband being unable to obtain the marital intimacy and similar rights to which he is entitled.

This principle is not contradicted by the death of either spouse, for that marks the completion and endpoint of the contract. Nor is it contradicted by divorce before consummation, because the very establishment of the bond between the spouses is one of the contract’s two objectives. Hence this bond establishes the marriage prohibitions arising from affinity, except in the case of a stepdaughter.

---

## Page 15076

Section

The basis in the Sunnah for the rule that a contract is dissolved when property sold or leased perishes before possession can be taken is the report narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity befalls it, it is not lawful for you to take anything from him. On what grounds would you take your brother’s wealth without right?”

Another version states:

“The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) ordered that payment be waived for fruit lost to calamities.”

In this authentic hadith, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) made clear that if someone sells fruit and a calamity befalls it, he may not lawfully take any payment from the buyer. He then explained the reason and underlying rationale, saying:

“On what grounds would you take your brother’s wealth without right?”

This points to Allah’s prohibition in His Book against consuming wealth wrongfully. When the property sold perishes before possession can be taken, taking any of the purchase price amounts to taking the buyer’s wealth without right—indeed, wrongfully. Allah has forbidden consuming wealth wrongfully because it is an injustice contrary to the justice upon which the heavens and the earth stand. This hadith is a foundation for this subject.

---

## Page 15077

Although the scholars have disagreed over the ruling conveyed by this hadith—as we shall explain—while agreeing that the loss of the property sold before possession can be taken invalidates the contract and makes taking the price unlawful, I know of no authentic, explicit hadith from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), other than this one, establishing the rule:

“If the property sold perishes before possession can be taken, the contract is invalidated.”
