There are numerous parallels to this. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) may make an explicit statement establishing a general principle, yet that text remains unknown to some scholars. They consequently agree with others on certain rulings derived from that principle, while disputing cases concerning which the text has not reached them. For example, they agree on capital-and-labour profit-sharing partnerships, yet disagree over contracts for tending fruit trees or cultivating crops in return for a share of the yield. Both of the latter are established by explicit texts, whereas profit-sharing partnerships have no explicit text establishing them, only the practice of the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them).

For this reason, jurists versed in hadith would establish a principle on the basis of a text and derive subsidiary rulings from it, rather than dispute the explicitly established principle while agreeing on cases for which there is no text. The result is that the ruling upon which there is consensus becomes prominent, because of the reverence that agreement commands in people’s hearts and because no one may oppose it. Some people, however, have hesitated over the ruling established by an explicit text, even though it may have a stronger basis than the ruling upon which they agree. Even if its basis is unknown to some scholars, this does not detract

---

## Page 15078

from its actual strength, such that those to whom its basis becomes clear may affirm it with certainty. Remitting the price of produce lost to calamities falls into this category. It is established by an explicit text, by longstanding practice from which no Companion or Successor is known to have dissented, by clear analogy, and by established principles. Indeed, careful consideration reveals that no scholar truly opposes this hadith.

This is because accepting it has been the position of the scholars of Medina, both earlier and later, and their practice has followed it from the time of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) until the time of Mālik and others. It is well known as the position of their scholars, such as al-Qāsim ibn Muhammad, Yahyā ibn Saʿīd the judge, Mālik, and his followers. It is also the position of the jurists versed in hadith, such as Imam Ahmad and his followers, Abū ʿUbayd, and al-Shāfiʿī in his earlier position.

As for his later position, he made accepting it conditional upon its authenticity being established, because he was unaware that it was authentic. He said (may Allah be pleased with him):

“It has not been established to my satisfaction that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) ordered the remission of the price of produce lost to calamities. Were it established, I would not depart from it. Were I to hold that such losses warrant remission, I would apply it to losses both small and large.”

He thus explained that he refrained from affirming it categorically only because he was unaware of its authenticity. He made accepting it conditional upon its being established, saying:

“Were it established, I would not depart from it.”

The hadith is established as authentic among hadith scholars. None of them has impugned it; rather, they have declared it authentic and transmitted it in the collections of authentic hadith and the Sunan collections. It was narrated by Muslim, Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah, and Imam Ahmad. Thus, the obligation to accept it becomes clear

---

## Page 15079

on al-Shāfiʿī’s own principles. As for Abū Hanīfah, no genuine disagreement with him on this principle can actually arise, because one of his principles is that he makes no distinction between fruit before and after it begins to ripen. In his view, an unqualified sale contract requires the fruit to be picked immediately. Even after it begins to ripen, a sale stipulating that it remain on the trees would, in his view, be invalid. This rests on his view that the contract requires an immediate exchange of possession, which may not be deferred, since such a stipulation conflicts with what the contract entails. Thus, in his view, if the fruit perishes after the sale and after the buyer has been given unrestricted access to it, it has perished after picking it became obligatory—just as, according to other scholars, it might perish after becoming fully ripe.

He applies his principle consistently to leasing as well. In his view, the lessee acquires the benefits only by receiving them progressively; they are not acquired merely through the contract and taking possession of the leased property. This is why he holds that the lease is annulled by death and other causes.

It is well known that the hadiths from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) distinguishing between fruit before and after it begins to ripen are mass-transmitted. This distinction is upheld by the vast majority of scholars, for the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prohibited selling fruit until it begins to ripen. This is established in the collections of authentic hadith through the reports of Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās, Jābir, Anas, and Abū Hurayrah. Had Abū Hanīfah held that fruit could be sold after it begins to ripen and left on the trees until fully ripe, the disagreement with him would have become apparent.

Those who dispute remitting the price of produce lost to calamities do not dispute that, if the item sold

---

## Page 15080

perishes before the buyer is able to take possession, the loss is borne by the seller. Indeed, al-Shāfiʿī takes the strictest position on this, holding that if any item sold perishes before possession is taken, the loss is borne by the seller. He also extends this rule to transactions other than sales. Abū Hanīfah applies it to all movable property. Mālik and Ahmad, who uphold remitting the price of produce lost to calamities, distinguish between property of which possession could have been taken, such as an item physically present, and property of which possession could not have been taken. This is based on al-Bukhārī’s report through al-Zuhrī, from Sālim, from Ibn ʿUmar, who said:

“The established Sunnah is that any grain sold after it has been gathered is the buyer’s property.”

The disagreement, rather, concerns whether fruit that perishes before becoming fully ripe has perished before the buyer was able to take possession. They say that it has perished after possession was taken, because possession was achieved by giving the buyer unrestricted access to it. By agreement, this constitutes taking possession of real estate and whatever is attached to it. Moreover, the buyer may dispose of it by sale or otherwise, and the permissibility of doing so indicates that possession has been taken, since disposing of an item sold before taking possession of it is not permissible. This, then, is the underlying basis of their position.

They have also invoked the apparent meaning of certain hadiths in support of their position, such as the report narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh from Abū Saʿīd, who said:

“A man in the time of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) suffered a loss involving fruit he had purchased, leaving him heavily in debt. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, ‘Give him charity.’ The people gave him charity, but it was not enough to pay off his debt. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) then said to his creditors, ‘Take what

---

## Page 15081

you find; you are entitled to nothing more.’” They also cite the report in the two Sahīh collections:

“A woman came to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and said, ‘My son bought some fruit from so-and-so, but a calamity destroyed it. He asked the seller for a remission, but the seller swore not to grant it.’ The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, ‘He has sworn not to do good.’”

Neither hadith, however, supports their position.

As for the first, its wording is inconclusive. It relates that a man bought some fruit and became heavily indebted. It is possible that prices were low and his debt grew for that reason. It is also possible that all or some of the fruit perished after becoming fully ripe, or after being taken to the drying floor, the house, or the market. It is also possible that this occurred before the Prophet prohibited selling fruit before it showed signs of ripeness.

Even supposing that this report contradicted that ruling, it would be abrogated, because it retains the original ruling, whereas the other report changes it and establishes a new Sunnah. Were the latter then superseded, the ruling would have changed twice.

As for the second hadith, it contains nothing beyond the Prophet’s statement (peace and blessings be upon him):

“He has sworn not to do good.”

Doing good may be obligatory or recommended. He did not issue a ruling against the seller because the opposing party had not brought a formal claim, nor had evidence or an admission been presented. Moreover, the fruit may have perished after becoming fully ripe.

Some have objected to the hadith concerning losses caused by calamities, arguing that it refers to selling fruit before it shows signs of ripeness, as in the hadith of Anas. This is incorrect for several reasons.

---

## Page 15082

First, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity strikes it…”

An unqualified reference to a sale can only mean a valid sale.

Second, he spoke of selling fruit without qualification; he did not say, “before it shows signs of ripeness.” There is therefore no basis for restricting his statement to fruit sold before it shows signs of ripeness.

Third, he made the ruling conditional upon a calamity occurring, whereas no payment is due under any circumstances for fruit sold before it shows signs of ripeness.

(Fourth, property taken into possession under an invalid contract entails liability for its loss. Thus, if fruit still on the trees were considered to have been taken into possession, the buyer would necessarily bear liability for it under an invalid contract. This argument requires that the hadith of Anas be cited as evidence for remitting the price of fruit lost to calamities under a valid sale, just as it is remitted under an invalid sale. For whatever entails liability under a valid contract also entails liability under an invalid one, and whatever does not entail liability under a valid contract does not entail liability under an invalid one.

As for their claim that the fruit perished after possession had been taken, we dispute this. Rather, we say that it perished before possession had been fully completed—indeed, before the buyer was even able to take possession. The seller remains responsible for completing its cultivation by watering it; were he to neglect this, he would be negligent. Even assuming that the seller had done everything within his power to place the fruit at the buyer’s disposal, the buyer is only required to take possession of it in the customary, usual manner. Thus, delivery has occurred, but receipt has not been completed. This is

---

## Page 15083

one of the two aspects of the transfer of possession. The buyer could do no more than this. The buyer is only required to take possession of the property purchased in the customary, usual manner entailed by the contract, whether possession follows immediately upon the contract or is delayed, and whether it occurs all at once or gradually.

We apply this principle consistently to all contracts. Taking possession need not follow immediately upon the contract; rather, it must occur as the contract requires, according to its wording and customary practice. Thus, according to the correct view, it is permissible to reserve some of the use of the property sold for a specified period, even if this delays taking possession. Likewise, it is permissible to sell property that is leased out, or to sell trees while reserving their fruit for the seller, even though this delays the completion of possession. It is also permissible to conclude a lease for a period that does not begin immediately after the contract.

The underlying reason is that taking possession is an obligation arising from the contract. What is required in this regard is therefore what the contracting parties have made binding, according to their intention as expressed through their wording and customary practice. For this reason, we have said that if they stipulate immediate picking, this is permissible, provided it entails no harm prohibited by Islamic law. For Muslims are bound by their stipulations, except a stipulation that makes lawful what is forbidden or forbids what is lawful. If they leave the matter unspecified, customary practice is to delay picking and harvesting until the produce has fully ripened.

As for their argument that taking possession consists in placing the property at the buyer’s disposal, what constitutes taking possession is determined by people’s customary practice, since neither language nor Islamic law provides a fixed definition of it. Taking possession of fruit on the trees

---

## Page 15084

necessarily requires tending it and keeping it continually at the buyer’s disposal until it has fully ripened, unlike taking possession of the trees alone. Each thing must be placed at the recipient’s disposal in the manner appropriate to it. The benefits of leased property provide evidence for this.

As for their argument based on the permissibility of disposing of it by sale, two positions are reported from Ahmad on this issue:

The first is that it may not be sold so long as the seller remains liable for its loss, because this would constitute selling something before taking possession of it, which is impermissible. On this view, the ruling claimed for the original case is not conceded.

The second position is that disposing of it is permissible. According to this position, it is comparable to the benefits acquired through a lease: if those benefits are lost before they are received, the lessor bears the loss by agreement. Nevertheless, they may be disposed of before possession is taken. This is because, in both cases, delivery has been effected to the extent possible, permitting disposal on account of the recipient’s ability to exercise control. Yet liability for loss has not passed to the recipient, because delivery has not been fully completed in the manner that enables the buyer or lessee to receive what is due in full.

Accordingly, in our view, permission to dispose of something and liability for its loss do not necessarily go together. Disposal may be permissible without liability, as in the present case. Liability may also arise without permission to dispose of the property, as where possession is taken in a legally defective manner—for example, when someone buys a qafīz (a measure of grain) from a heap but takes possession of the entire heap. The same applies to a heap before it is moved, according to one of the two reported positions, which al-Khiraqī preferred. Both permission and liability may arise together, and both may be absent.

---

## Page 15085

In our school, there are also two reported positions on whether leased property may be sublet for more than its original rent, because this involves making a profit from something for which one bears no liability.

A third reported position permits the higher rent if the lessee has made improvements to the property, so that the additional rent is in return for those improvements. Thus, the two reported positions concerning the resale of purchased fruit parallel the two concerning the subletting of leased property.

It would also be a tenable view to say that, in the case of fruit, only resale above the purchase price is prohibited, corresponding to the reported position prohibiting an increase in rent.

This discussion clarifies the rationale underlying the issue: the fruit has perished before the buyer could take the possession intended by the contract, so the seller bears the loss, just as the lessor bears the loss of benefits before the lessee can receive them. Placing the property at the buyer’s disposal is not an end in itself; its purpose is to enable the buyer to take possession of what was sold. Fruit still on the trees is neither securely kept nor in the buyer’s possession; hence, stealing it does not incur the penalty of amputation. Nor is the purpose of the contract for the fruit to remain on the trees; rather, it is for it to be harvested and picked. The seller must therefore provide what enables the buyer to pick and water it.

The growth that develops after the sale is included in it, even though it did not yet exist, just as benefits are included in a lease even though they do not yet exist. How, then, can something nonexistent be regarded as having been taken into definitive possession such that liability for its loss passes to the recipient?

---

## Page 15086

Section

The following rulings follow from this principle.

A calamity means a natural hazard for which no one can be held liable, such as wind, cold, heat, rain, frost, lightning, and the like—just as when such hazards destroy property other than the produce sold here.

If a person who can be held liable destroys the fruit, or someone wrongfully seizes it, our jurists, including al-Qādī and others, state that this is treated like the destruction of sold property before the buyer is able to take possession of it. The buyer may choose either to uphold the sale or to rescind it, as explained above.

If it is destroyed by people who cannot be held liable, such as armies that plunder it or thieves who lay it waste, they have derived two views:

The first is that this does not constitute a calamity, because it results from human action.

The second—which accords with the principles of the school—is that it does constitute a calamity. This is also Mālik’s position. We have taken a similar position regarding the benefits due under a lease, because the determining consideration is whether anyone can be held liable. Thus, if the destruction is caused by armies of unbelievers or people at war with the Muslims, it is treated like a natural hazard. Although armies and thieves act wrongfully, if they cannot be held liable, they are effectively like the cold.

If the calamity damages the produce without destroying it, this is treated like a defect

---

## Page 15087

arising before the buyer is able to take possession. This is treated like a pre-existing defect, entitling him to a remedy or to compensation for diminished value, where the latter is recognized.

Since this is treated like the destruction of sold property before the buyer is able to take possession of it, no distinction is made between minor and major losses caused by a calamity, according to the better-known of the two reported positions. This is also the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Abū ʿUbaydah, and other jurists of hadith, because both the hadith and its underlying rationale are general in scope.

The second reported position sets the qualifying loss at one third or more, as Mālik holds. Some loss of fruit is ordinarily inevitable, so a threshold must be set for a calamity. This is fixed at one third, just as one third is the limit applied to bequests, vows, certain cases involving wounds, and other matters, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“One third—and one third is much.”

According to the first reported position, the distinction is determined by customary practice. Whatever fruit ordinarily falls or is eaten by birds or other creatures is implicitly allowed for in the contract; a calamity is a loss beyond that. If the loss exceeds what is customary, the entire loss is deducted from the price. Likewise, if it exceeds one third and we adopt that threshold, the entire loss is deducted.

Is this third measured by value or by quantity? There are two views, both of which are also held within Mālik’s school.

---

## Page 15088

Section

According to the jurists of our school, losses caused by calamities are deducted from the price of fruit from all kinds of trees. This is also Mālik’s position.

Ahmad is reported to have said: “The ruling on calamities applies only to date palms.” Al-Qādī interpreted this as excluding field crops and vegetables. It is also possible that Ahmad meant that the term “calamities” in the hadith refers to date palms, while the ruling extends to other trees by analogy rather than by the explicit text, since the trees of Medina were date palms.

As for calamity losses affecting purchased field crops, there are two views, mentioned by al-Qādī and others.

The first view is that no deduction is made for calamity losses affecting them. Al-Qādī said: “This is the more fitting view, because they are sold only once they have fully matured and harvest time has arrived. Fruit, by contrast, may be sold as soon as it begins to ripen, with a long interval before harvest.”

Al-Qādī understood Ahmad’s statement, “The ruling on calamities applies only to date palms,” in accordance with this view—meaning the statement he made while in Baghdad. When asked about calamity losses affecting field crops, he replied: “The ruling on calamities applies only to date palms.”

Likewise, Mālik’s position is that no deduction is made for calamity losses affecting fruit once it has dried. Field crops are similarly excluded, because they are sold only once dry. This is also the position of those who do not allow deductions for calamity losses affecting fruit, such as Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī in his conditional later position.

The second view is that deductions for calamity losses apply to field crops just as they do to fruit. This is the view categorically affirmed by

---

## Page 15089

several jurists of our school, including Abū Muhammad. They mention no disagreement on this issue and make no distinction between field crops and fruit, because the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him,

“forbade selling grapes until they turn dark and grain until it hardens.”

Selling grapes after they turn dark is thus like selling grain after it hardens. Between the hardening of the grain and its readiness for harvest, there is an interval during which a calamity may strike it.

Some jurists of our school said that plants which bear repeated crops—such as snake cucumbers, cucumbers, and similar vegetables and greens—are treated like trees, and their produce like tree fruit, in this respect. This is because the plants themselves may validly be sold, whether small or large, bearing produce or not.

Section

This applies when the fruit perishes before it has fully ripened and reached harvest time. If the purchaser leaves it until harvest time and it then perishes, the same ruling applies according to the jurists of our school. It is reported from Mālik that the loss falls upon the purchaser. Al-Shāfiʿī has two positions on the matter. The issue is that the seller has no remaining obligation of delivery, while the purchaser has committed no negligence, whether specific or general, since leaving the fruit until this time is an entitlement arising from the contract. The jurists of our school therefore take into account the purchaser’s inability to take full possession and his lack of negligence, whereas the opposing view takes into account the seller’s having delivered the fruit and made it available to the purchaser.

But if the purchaser leaves it until the time for removing it has passed and it has fully ripened, and it then perishes:

---

## Page 15090

the jurists of our school hold three views:

The first is that the loss is likewise borne by the seller, because the purchaser has not taken full possession. This is the position categorically adopted by al-Qādī in al-Mujarrad, by Ibn Aqīl, and by most jurists of our school. It is also the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. However, in al-Mujarrad, al-Qādī explained it with reference to the case in which the purchaser had no excuse, rather than one in which illness or some other impediment prevented him. Others, however, adopted the third view: that the ability to remove and harvest the fruit is not taken into account.

Ibn Aqīl said:

“This is what our school’s position entails.”

It is indeed as he said, for this fruit is comparable to the benefit contracted for in a lease. If an impediment particular to the lessee, such as illness or the like, prevents him from enjoying that benefit, he is not relieved of the rent. A general impediment, by contrast, relieves him of the rent corresponding to the benefit it has deprived him of.

Section

This applies when someone purchases the fruit or field crop. If, however, he purchases the trees themselves after the fruit has appeared, or before pollination, and stipulates that the fruit be included, no compensation for calamity-related loss is due, according to the jurists of our school, Mālik, and others.

Al-Khiraqī therefore excluded this case by saying:

“If someone purchases the fruit without the trees, and it is destroyed by a natural calamity, he has recourse against the seller for the loss.”

This is because, in the case under discussion, full possession has been achieved by taking possession of the trees themselves. For this reason, there is no obligation

---

## Page 15091

for the seller to water the trees or bear any maintenance costs whatsoever. What has been sold is real property, possession of which is taken by granting the purchaser unrestricted access; the fruit is included implicitly and as an accessory. Thus, if it may be sold before ripening, it may be sold here as an accessory. Were it sold in its own right, however, it could not be sold before ripening.

Section

This discussion concerns the outright sale of fruit and field crops.

As for damān and qabāla contracts—where the land and trees are leased together for a single payment to someone who tends them and receives their fruit and crops—there are three views concerning this contract.

The first is that it is invalid. This view is expressly reported from Ahmad and is also the position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfi‘ī, on the grounds that, in this arrangement, it is ancillary to fruit before it begins to ripen.

The second is that it is permissible when the land is the principal object of the contract and the trees are ancillary to it, as when there are only a few trees. This is Mālik’s position.

The third is that it is permissible without qualification. This was stated by a group of jurists from our school and others, including Ibn ‘Aqīl. This is the correct view, because leasing land is permissible, and this cannot be done without including the trees in the contract. Including them as an accessory is therefore permissible because of the need, even if this entails selling fruit before it begins to ripen, provided it is sold together with the trees themselves. Moreover, this is not a sale

---

## Page 15092

of fruit, for the lessee here is the one who waters the trees and cultivates the land. His position with regard to the trees is therefore like that of a tenant with regard to the land. The purchaser of fruit, by contrast, is like the purchaser of a standing crop; the two arrangements cannot therefore be treated alike.

Moreover, after the death of Usayd ibn al-Hudayr, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, leased out his orchard for three years, collected the rent, and used it to discharge Usayd’s debt. This was related by Harb al-Kirmānī in his Masā’il and by Abū Zur‘a al-Dimashqī in his History, with an authentic chain of transmission.

Furthermore, with the agreement of the Companions, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb imposed kharāj (land tax) on land containing date palms and grapevines, assessing a portion for the land and another for the trees. Most of those who dispute our position on this question regard this as a lease, yet it is a damān contract covering both land and trees. I have discussed this question at length in Legal Maxims.

Our concern here, however, is “relief for losses caused by calamities.” If we hold that this contract is invalid, how, then, is the transaction to be conducted?

One answer is that the owner leases out the land and enters into a crop-sharing agreement for tending the trees on it. This is the view of a group of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers and others, and it is also the view of Judge Abū Ya‘lā in his Refutation of Legal Stratagems. Ahmad, however, expressly declared this stratagem invalid. This is the correct view, as we have demonstrated its invalidity on numerous grounds in the Book of the Refutation of Legal Stratagems.

One of these grounds is that if either contract is made conditional upon the other, the arrangement is invalid. If, on the other hand, he concludes them as two separate contracts, he may not grant such preferential terms with respect to property entrusted to his administration, such as an endowment

---

## Page 15093

and an orphan’s property or similar assets, nor with the property of his absent principal or anyone in a similar position.

Another ground is that the substantial payment made by the party undertaking the damān is clearly not for the use of the land, which may not be worth even a tenth of that payment; rather, it is for the fruit. Likewise, the owner knows that he has not stipulated any fruit for himself. He does not claim that negligible, worthless share, but has assigned all the fruit to the party undertaking the damān.

In short, either this contract is valid in the form commonly practised among people, or it is not valid under any circumstances. The latter position, however, entails grave harm that the Sharia cannot countenance, so the former must be accepted. As for this stratagem, its invalidity is apparent upon even the slightest reflection.

Accordingly, if a calamity causes losses under this damān arrangement, and we hold that the contract is invalid, then the purchaser has bought fruit before it showed signs of ripening, and the seller has allowed him free access to it, but it has perished before fully ripening or has never appeared at all.

As explained earlier, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling fruit before it showed signs of ripening precisely for the reason expressed in his words:

“What if Allah withholds the fruit?”

Or he said:

“Tell me, if Allah does not bring forth the fruit, on what grounds does any of you take his brother’s wealth without right?”

If a calamity strikes the fruit, preventing it from fully ripening and destroying it, then Allah has indeed withheld the fruit. He must therefore not take his brother’s wealth without right.

Anyone who holds that the purchaser becomes liable for the fruit upon taking possession under a valid contract must also

---

## Page 15094

hold that he becomes liable for it upon taking possession under an invalid contract. If it perishes in this case, the loss is his to bear, because goods received under an invalid contract are the purchaser’s liability. Yet they must assess that liability at its value when it perished. It may perish when it first appears, while its value is small, or after it has begun to ripen.

This presents a particularly compelling objection to their position: if he purchases the fruit after it has begun to ripen, with the right to leave it on the trees, many of its constituent parts and qualities have not yet come into existence. If it then perishes through a calamity, and we do not relieve him of the resulting loss, he must be liable only for what he has taken possession of, not for what has yet to come into existence and of which he has not taken possession. Its value when the calamity struck must therefore be assessed and compared proportionately with its value when it first showed signs of ripening. He is then liable for the corresponding proportion of the price. This is like someone who has taken possession of only part of the goods purchased, or received only part of the benefit due under a lease: he is liable for what he has received, not for what he has yet to receive. But to make him bear liability for nonexistent parts and qualities that have not yet been created is contrary to the principles of Islam. It is manifest injustice, with no justification.

Anyone who holds this position must also hold that if someone purchases fruit before it shows signs of ripening and takes possession of its trees, but none of the fruit comes into existence because a blight prevents the blossoms from emerging, he remains liable to the seller for the entire price. This is contrary to the revealed text and scholarly consensus.

He must also hold that if the fruit begins to ripen under an invalid contract and then perishes through a natural calamity, the purchaser is liable for all the fruit, just as, in his view, he would be under a valid contract. For whatever becomes his liability upon taking possession under one of the two contracts

---

## Page 15095

also becomes his liability upon taking possession under the other, except that here he is liable for the stipulated price, whereas there he is liable for its equivalent value. This is a powerful argument from which there is no escape: if he treats those parts that have not yet been created as having been taken into possession, he must hold the purchaser liable under an invalid contract. If he treats them as not having been taken into possession, he must hold that the purchaser is not liable under a valid contract. The former is unquestionably false, contrary to the revealed text and scholarly consensus.

Those scholars of Kūfa who say that the contract covers only what already exists, which is what has been taken into possession, avoid this contradiction. Their position, however, necessarily contradicts the widely transmitted texts, the practice of Muslims past and present, established principles, and the justice by which heaven and earth are upheld, as explained in its proper place. This amounts to conclusive proof that relief for losses caused by calamities must be granted under both valid and invalid contracts; the case for granting it under an invalid contract is even stronger.

If, however, we regard the guarantee arrangement as valid, we hold that relief for losses caused by calamities applies to it, just as it applies to a purchase—indeed, with even greater reason. But someone who accepts this legal stratagem and regards the contract as valid may say: “You are a cultivator under a fruit-sharing contract, and no relief for losses caused by calamities applies to such contracts.” The issue thus turns on whether such relief applies to fruit-sharing contracts.

---

## Page 15096

Section

As for losses caused by calamities in leases, we say:

The imams do not dispute that if the use contracted for under a lease becomes unavailable before the lessee has had the opportunity to enjoy it, the rent ceases to be due. They have not disagreed over this as they have over the destruction of fruit that has been sold. In the latter case, they may say, “Possession was taken by granting access to it.” But benefits that have not yet come into existence have certainly not been taken into possession.

Accordingly, scholarly consensus has been reported that if a leased asset perishes before possession is taken of it, the lease is annulled. The same applies if it perishes immediately after possession, before there has been any opportunity to use it—apart from an anomalous dissent attributed to Abū Thawr. For the subject matter of the contract has perished before possession was taken of it; the case resembles the destruction of a sold item after possession, treating possession of the asset as possession of its benefits.

It may be said that this is the logical implication of the position of those who reject relief for losses caused by calamities. But they say:

“The subject matter of the contract here consists of benefits that have not yet come into existence and have not been taken into possession. Possession of them occurs only through enjoying them or having the opportunity to do so. Possession of the asset is treated as possession of its benefits only for the purposes of transferring ownership, establishing entitlement, and permitting disposal. Thus, if the asset perishes, it has perished before there has been any opportunity to enjoy its benefits, and the lease is annulled.”

Yet this commits them to the same conclusion regarding fruit, with respect to those parts of it that have not yet come into existence.

---

## Page 15097

The trees bear the same relation to the fruit as the leased asset does to its benefits, and in both cases it is impossible to obtain what the contract was intended to provide. Abū Thawr thus consistently applied an unsound analogy, just as the majority consistently applied the sound analogy in granting relief for losses caused by calamities and annulling leases.

If the asset perishes during the lease term, the lease is annulled for the remaining period, but not for the period already elapsed. There is an anomalous dissenting view that it is also annulled retrospectively. If some of the leased assets become unusable, the corresponding share of the rent ceases to be due, just as when some of the items sold perish—for example, when some of the hired animals die or some of the leased houses collapse.

The loss of use can occur in two ways:

First, the asset itself perishes, as when a hired slave or animal dies.

Second, the asset loses its usefulness because something happens to it that prevents its use, as when a house collapses or farmland is flooded or loses its water supply. If no benefit remains, it is exactly like an asset that has perished; no scholar distinguishes between the two.

If some of its intended benefit is lost while some remains—for example, the land can be cultivated without water, but with a diminished crop, or the water recedes from flooded land in a manner that prevents some cultivation or crop growth—the lessee has the right to rescind the lease. This is comparable to a defect in an item sold; it does not automatically invalidate the lease. There are two opinions within the school as to whether he may retain the lease with compensation. If its use is lost for part of the term, he owes rent in proportion to the benefit he has derived

---

## Page 15098

from it, as al-Khiraqī stated. If an irresistible event prevents the lessee from enjoying the use contracted for, he owes rent in proportion to the period during which he benefited.

If some use remains, but it is not the use intended by the contract—for example, land leased for cultivation loses its water supply but can still be used for storing firewood or pitching a tent; a collapsed house can still accommodate a tent; or flooded land can still be used for fishing—does the lease become void, or does this constitute a diminution that entitles the lessee to rescind it?

There are two views:

The first is that the lease becomes void. This is the view of most scholars, including Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and al-Shāfi'ī in the case of a collapsed building. Since the remaining use was not what the contract intended to provide, its existence is equivalent to its absence.

The second is that the lessee is entitled to rescind the lease. This is al-Shāfi'ī’s explicit position in the case of a loss of water supply. Al-Qādī and Ibn 'Aqīl also adopted it in some of their discussions, while other scholars of the school adopted the first view.

---

## Page 15099

Section

(With this established, if someone leases land for cultivation, its water supply may be cut off or it may be flooded before sowing. [Its water supply may be cut off or it may be flooded] or the crop may be struck by a calamity after sowing and before harvest. What is the ruling in these cases?) (*).

The explicit position reported from Ahmad, the scholars of the school, and others concerning the loss of water supply is that its loss after sowing is treated like its loss before sowing: if some benefit has been obtained despite this, a proportionate amount of rent is due; if all benefit is lost, no rent is due.

Ahmad ibn al-Qāsim said:

“I asked Abū ‘Abd Allāh about a man who leased land to cultivate it, but its water supply was cut off before the end of the term.

“He replied: ‘His rent is reduced to the extent that he was unable to benefit from the land, or in proportion to the interruption of its water supply.’”

He thus expressly stated that the loss of water supply after sowing requires a reduction in rent proportionate to the loss of benefit. This is the position of the scholars of our school, without any disagreement that I know of.

Al-Qādī and others stated that if someone leases land for cultivation and sows it, but it is then struck by disastrous flooding from a source other than irrigation, so that the crop does not sprout, he remains liable for the rent. He mentioned that Ahmad had expressly ruled to this effect, and that if the land were flooded at sowing time so that the lessee could not cultivate it,

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (225) said:
> “The text between square brackets appears to be repeated.”

---

## Page 15100

he would owe no rent, since delivery of the leased benefit was impossible. The author of al-Tafrī‘ likewise reported Mālik’s position on both cases.

Al-Qādī thus distinguishes between the two cases in keeping with the two differing explicit rulings. He distinguishes the loss of the water supply from flooding and other calamities on the grounds that the loss of water entails the loss of the very benefit contracted for: the subject of the contract is land supplied with water. The loss of its customary water supply is therefore tantamount to failure to provide the stipulated benefit, as when a hired animal dies. Rent is due only so long as the stipulated benefit continues to be provided. Flooding and other calamities that ruin the crop, however, destroy property belonging to the lessee himself. This is comparable to someone renting a house and having a garment of his destroyed in it.

The essential distinction is that, when the water supply is cut off, the benefit is no longer available; when the crop is destroyed, the benefit remains available, but something has occurred that destroys the lessee’s property. This is comparable to its being destroyed after harvest.

A group of scholars of our school, including Shaykh Abū Muhammad, treat the loss of water and flooding that prevents sowing or harms the crop alike with regard to the lease. If this renders the benefit unavailable, the rent ceases to be due. If benefit remains possible, but with hardship arising from its diminished extent—for example, if flooding prevents some cultivation or impairs the crop—the lessee acquires the right to rescind the lease. But if no harm results—as when floodwater recedes shortly afterward without preventing sowing or harming the crop, or when the land’s water supply is cut off but the lessor brings water to it from elsewhere, or the interruption occurs at a time when water is not needed—he would not

---

## Page 15101

have the right to rescind the lease. On this approach, Ahmad’s ruling concerning interruption of the water supply is extended to the flooding of crops, and his ruling concerning the flooding of crops is extended to interruption of the water supply, since the underlying consideration is the same in both cases. Where a crop is flooded, if flooding before sowing prevents sowing, flooding afterward prevents the crop from growing, just as interruption of the water supply prevents it from growing. The intended subject matter of the contract is the ability to benefit from the land until harvest; sowing the seed does not constitute the whole of what is contracted for. If that alone were the subject matter of the contract, it would follow that, if the water supply were interrupted afterward, the lessee would have no right to rescind and no reduction in rent would be due. Yet they have not maintained this, nor can it legitimately be maintained. We know with certainty that the benefit the lessee seeks and contracts for is the ability to make use of the land’s soil, air, water, and sunlight until his crop reaches full maturity. Whenever the benefit of the soil, water, air, or sunlight is lost, the crop does not grow, and the lessee does not obtain the benefit intended by the contract. This is comparable to renting a house to live in and then being unable to reside there for some reason, such as a ruined wall, interruption of the water supply, a collapsed roof, or the like.

There is no disagreement within the Muslim community that the loss of the contracted benefit through a natural calamity entails the cancellation or reduction of rent, or rescission of the lease, even when the lessee has had no hand in it—as when a hired animal dies, a house collapses, or rainfall ceases. The same therefore applies to flooding

---

## Page 15102

and other calamities that prevent full benefit from the crops.

This is made clear by the fact that the intended subject matter of the contract is not merely the lessee’s act of ploughing the land and sowing the seed, such that one could say: “Once he has been able to do that, he has been able to obtain the entire benefit, even if something subsequently ruins the crop and prevents him from benefiting from it.” This is disproved by the case of the water supply being interrupted afterward. Moreover, what is contracted for is the benefit of the land itself, and his benefiting from it is not the same as his own labour. His labour is not itself a benefit to him, nor does he derive benefit from it in itself; rather, it is a burden, toil, and exertion that consumes his energies and wealth. This differs from living in a house or riding an animal: living and riding are themselves ways of benefiting, through which the leased property has benefited him.

Ploughing the land, however, entails toil and exertion, while sowing the seed entails an expenditure of wealth. He does this only in the hope of enjoying the benefit Allah creates in the land by causing plants to grow, as the Most High says:

“Exalted is He who created all pairs—from what the earth produces, from themselves, and from what they do not know.”

He also says:

“With it He causes crops, olives, date palms, and grapes to grow for you.”

And He says:

“And We caused grain to grow in it, and grapes and green fodder, and olives and date palms.”

No one may object that causing the land to produce vegetation lies within the power of neither the lessee nor the lessor, whereas the subject matter of the contract must lie within their power; for this is

---

## Page 15103

contrary to the consensus of Muslims—indeed, of all rational people. The intended subject matter of a lease need not be produced by either party to it. Rather, it may be provided by another living creature or an inanimate object, even though neither party is capable of producing that benefit.

For example, someone may hire out a slave or an animal, whose service is performed through its own voluntary actions. Likewise, someone may let a house as a dwelling: the benefit derived from it depends on Allah keeping it in its existing form, not on any action of the lessor. Similarly, water falling from the sky and springing forth from the earth is included in the subject matter of the contract, although neither party has power over it. The same applies when someone hires out movable property, such as weapons, books, clothing, tools of a trade, or anything else: the benefit it provides is not produced by the lessor. There are many comparable examples.

Likewise, the benefit that Allah creates in the land, enabling crops to grow through its soil, water, air, and sunshine, is the intended subject matter of the contract, even though most of it lies beyond human power. If this subject matter is lost, the contract becomes void. If part of it is lost, the case is like that of a loss of benefit from any other leased property. Indeed, there are stronger grounds here for voiding the lease or reducing the rent than in cases of calamities affecting fruit crops.

Those followers of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfi‘ī who disputed the ruling in the latter case argued that the fruit had perished after possession had been taken of it, making it comparable to fruit that perished after being harvested or after

---

## Page 15104

the time for harvesting it. Here, however, the imams agree that possession of the benefit—in the sense that makes the lessee liable for its loss—is acquired only progressively. Hence, they agree that if the leased property perishes, or its benefit becomes wholly or partially unavailable during the lease term, the rent is waived in whole or in part, or the lessee acquires the right to rescind the contract.

The confusion arose only because some supposed that the benefit intended by the contract was tilling the land and sowing it. They imagined that the subsequent destruction of the crop by flooding or some other cause was comparable to the destruction of the farmer’s crop after harvesting, or of a garment belonging to him in a rented house. Anyone who reflects will recognize this as an obvious oversight. Thus, everyone of sound natural disposition rejects this view, even those with no practical familiarity with jurisprudence, such as farmers, people with only a smattering of legal learning, and the like. They know that the subject matter of the contract is the lessee’s enjoyment of the benefit of the leased property, not merely his labour and expenditure, which are means to obtaining that benefit.

Such labour is comparable to saddling, bridling, and leading a hired horse: these are means to enjoying the benefit of riding, not themselves the intended subject matter of the contract, even though they are included within it. Likewise, securing loads, tying ropes, and similar acts are means to obtaining the benefit of carrying goods on the animal; they are included in the subject matter of the contract only incidentally. The intended subject matter is, rather, the animal’s actual carrying of loads and riders.

Although carrying is the benefit supplied by the animal, while saddling and securing the load are acts performed by the lessee, the same applies here: ploughing and sowing, although performed by the lessee, are included in the lease only incidentally, because they are means to obtaining the benefit that constitutes the intended subject matter of the contract, namely

---

## Page 15105

the benefit the land provides through the water, air, and sunshine Allah creates in it. Whoever supposes that his own activity alone is the subject matter of the contract has plainly erred, beyond any doubt. His error arises because his activity is perceptible to the senses, involving movement, whereas the benefit of the land is apprehended by the intellect, since the land does not move. When the mind perceives the observable movement, it mistakenly imagines that this is the subject matter of the contract.

This error is refuted by all other forms of leasing. The subject matter of the contract is the benefit of whatever is hired—whether inanimate, such as land, houses, and clothing, or animate, such as people and animals—not the activity of the person hiring it. His activity is merely a means of obtaining the benefit. Sometimes the benefit is obtained simultaneously with that activity, as with riding and wearing clothes; sometimes it is obtained afterwards, as with building, planting trees, and sowing crops. The subject matter of the contract is the benefit the land provides for buildings, trees, and crops, not merely the labour of the builder, planter, or farmer, which already belongs to him. How could something already his be what he pays rent to obtain? He pays rent only for the benefit he receives from the leased property, not for his own labour, which already belongs to him. For it is impossible to purchase what is already his with his own property. Whoever understands this will be certain of what we have stated and will retain no doubt about it, Allah willing.

And if the subject matter of the contract is the benefit of the property itself, from the beginning of the term to its end,

---

## Page 15106

then whenever this benefit is diminished—through a reduction or cessation of the water supply, excess water and flooding, or the occurrence of locusts, cold, heat, snow, or similar conditions that depart from the ordinary and prevent the customary benefit—the benefit to which he is entitled under the contract is thereby obstructed. He must therefore be entitled either to rescind the lease or to have the rent reduced in proportion to the benefit lost, just as when the water supply ceases. There is no distinction between the cessation of the water supply, its excess, and other impediments that would justify different rulings.

Section

Having established this, we have already cited the explicit statements of Ahmad, al-Khiraqī, and others that the rent payable is proportionate to the benefit actually obtained.

This takes two forms:

The first is that the benefit is obtained during only part of the lease term or from only part of the leased property. In this case, the rent is reduced accordingly, and only the proportion corresponding to the benefit obtained is payable. The rent is apportioned according to the value of the respective locations and periods, for these may be equal or may differ: some parts of the land may be better than others, and rent during some seasons of the year may be higher than during others. Our jurists and others have expressly stated this.

The second is a diminution of the benefit within one and the same place and period;

---

## Page 15107

as when rainfall falls below its customary level, or flooding diminishes the crop, and similar cases. Here our jurists hold two views:

(The first is that he is entitled only to rescind the lease.

The second—which follows from the explicit rulings and the analogical principles of the school—is that he may choose between rescission and compensation for the diminution, as in a sale. Indeed, the grounds for this are even stronger in a lease: in a sale, he can return the item and demand repayment of the price, whereas here he cannot return the entire benefit, since he can return it only in an altered state.

If it were said here that he is entitled only to claim compensation—just as, according to one of the two transmitted opinions, we hold that a defect arising in the purchased item while in the buyer’s possession prevents its return for a pre-existing defect and instead entitles him to compensation—that would be more defensible and more consistent with analogy than the view that, upon finding fault with the benefit, he is entitled only to rescind, without claiming compensation.

This is an exceedingly weak view, far removed from the principles of the Sharia and the rules of the school, and contrary to the explicit statements of Ahmad and the leading scholars among his followers, even if al-Qādī expresses it in al-Mujarrad and Ibn ‘Aqīl or others follow him in it. Al-Qādī, may Allah be pleased with him, composed al-Mujarrad early on, after writing Sharh al-Madhhab and before bringing al-Ta‘līq and al-Jāmi‘ al-Kabīr to their finished form. He takes the cases that other scholars formulated and answered according to their own principles, and answers them on the basis of what Ahmad explicitly stated

---

## Page 15108

and his followers have stated, and what he understands Ahmad’s principles to entail. Yet in questions with numerous branches and ramifications, the jurist deriving subsidiary rulings may sometimes overlook the relevant principles and explicit rulings in some of those branches.

Accordingly, if harm—such as severe cold, flooding, damaging winds, locusts, frost, mice, or the like—diminishes the intended, customary benefit to which the contract entitles the lessee, the same procedure applies as when assessing compensation for a defect in a purchased item. The land’s value without the affliction and its value with it are assessed; the diminution is calculated as a proportion of its full value, and the agreed rent is reduced by that proportion. For example, if its rental value without the affliction is one thousand, but with it is eight hundred, the affliction has reduced its value by one fifth, so one fifth of the agreed rent is deducted.

The same applies when a calamity affects fruit: one assesses how much the calamity has diminished its value—by a third, a quarter, or a fifth—and reduces the price payable by the corresponding proportion. Likewise, if the fruit deteriorates and becomes defective, one assesses how much that defect has diminished its value and reduces the price proportionately.

Some people may imagine that the loss caused by a calamity affecting crops on leased land is to be borne by the landowner, or that the landowner must remit part of the crop, by analogy with a calamity affecting fruit or crops that have been sold. This is mistaken. Through the contract, the purchaser of fruit or crops acquires ownership of the fruit or crops themselves. Thus, if they are destroyed before it becomes possible to

---

## Page 15109

take possession of them, their loss is borne by the seller as owner. The lessee, however, acquires through the contract only the right to use the land. The crop itself is his own newly produced property, arising from property he already owns; he did not acquire it through the lease. What he acquired through the lease was the benefit of the land that enables the crop to grow until it reaches full maturity.

A distinction must therefore be drawn between a calamity affecting purchased crops or fruit and one affecting the benefit of leased land under cultivation. This is a treacherous point, where judgment falters and understanding goes astray. Many judges, valuers, those administering calamity remissions, property owners and lessees have erred here. Some even suppose that a calamity affecting the lease of cultivated land is equivalent to one affecting purchased crops. Conversely, some students of jurisprudence suppose that, when cultivated land suffers an affliction preventing the crop from reaching maturity, the benefit has not diminished and none of it has been lost. Anyone who reflects on the matter will recognize that both views are mistaken.

Land leased for planting trees or constructing buildings is analogous to land leased for cultivation. The lessor does not guarantee the value of the trees or buildings if they are destroyed. But suppose an affliction prevents the full enjoyment of the benefit to which the contract entitles the lessee: for example, an enemy takes control and prevents the use of the trees and buildings; locusts or another affliction damage the planted trees; or wind demolishes the buildings, or something similar occurs. In such cases, the benefit to which the contract entitles the lessee has diminished, just as it does in the case of cultivated land.

Since many people mistakenly suppose that a lessee is granted a remission for calamity damage affecting

---

## Page 15110

the crops, buildings and planted trees themselves, as a buyer is, the scholars have rejected that view. This appears to be the meaning of Ahmad’s explicit ruling as transmitted by our scholars, such as al-Qādī and Abū Muhammad. They stated—the wording is Abū Muhammad’s:

“If someone leases land and cultivates it, and the crop is destroyed, the lessor bears no liability. Ahmad explicitly stated this, and we know of no disagreement concerning it. This is because the contract concerns the benefits of the land, which have not been destroyed; what has been destroyed is merely the lessee’s property on it. It is therefore like a house that someone leases to bleach garments in, and the garments are destroyed there.”

This statement entails that the lessor bears no liability for any part of the lessee’s crop, unlike the seller’s liability for the buyer’s crop. Hence, he discussed this in the chapter on calamity damage to tangible property, explaining that what was destroyed was the lessee’s own property, not the benefit. This is sound insofar as it denies liability for the crop itself. This is particularly clear when the crop is destroyed after reaching full maturity.

We have already explained that the very benefit covered by the contract may be diminished or lost through an affliction affecting the crop. The rent is therefore reduced in proportion to the diminution in benefit. Thus, the matter on which the Shaykh reported no disagreement was liability for damage to the property itself. He did not discuss liability for diminished benefit here, although he did discuss it in the Book of Leasing.

This is a point liable to confusion, and most scholars’ statements on it lack sufficient distinction. The correct position becomes clear through the analysis we have presented. And Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

---

## Page 15111

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leased land as a resting place at midday and at night, and also for cultivation if possible, intending to make the customary use that someone in his position would make of such land. Water subsequently reached part of the land but not the rest. Is the lease valid on these terms? Must the lessee pay the full charge for the land despite not benefiting from part of it? Is the lessee’s word to be accepted concerning the benefit he has obtained?

Also, a man leases land, a house, a shop or some other property from the administrator of an endowment or the guardian of an orphan. A more advantageous offer, involving a higher payment, subsequently becomes available for the orphan or the endowment. May the administrator or guardian annul the lease and accept the higher offer?

He replied:

Leasing land suitable for cultivation is permissible, whether irrigation has already reached it or not, provided that it is land which irrigation customarily reaches. Likewise, land that customarily receives water may be leased before rain falls upon it.

This is the position of the leading Muslim scholars, such as Mālik, Abū Hanīfah and Imam Ahmad. It is also al-Shāfiʿī’s position, according to the correct view within his school.

---

## Page 15112

But some of his followers misunderstood his position and failed to distinguish between land that receives water most of the time and land that receives it only rarely, such as land watered outside the usual seasons.

Once such land has been validly leased, the rent becomes payable if all of it is irrigated and the customary crops can be grown. If none of it is irrigated, the lessee owes no rent. If only part of it is irrigated, rent is payable in proportion to the irrigated area. Whoever holds the lessee bound by the lease and demands rent when the land has not been irrigated has contravened the consensus of the Muslims.

Accordingly, there is neither any need nor any benefit in saying, “I have leased it to you as a resting place at midday and at night.” This wording was used only by those who thought that land could not be leased before it was irrigated. Their practice of leasing it as a resting place at midday and at night is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims, for two reasons:

First: This land is unsuitable as a resting place at midday and at night. Livestock rest at night and at midday only on land where they would ordinarily stay, such as land near their grazing grounds and drinking water. Land with no water, crops or habitation is unsuitable for such use. Leasing a property for a benefit it cannot provide is invalid.

Second: Even if this benefit is available, it has no monetary value in

---

## Page 15113

land of this kind; indeed, the entire open countryside shares with this land the possibility of serving as a resting place at midday and at night. A benefit that customarily has no monetary value is like a tangible object that has no monetary value. By agreement, the former cannot validly be the subject of a lease, nor the latter of a sale. Examples include benefiting from shade or light at a distance. However, leasing land to make the customary use that someone in the lessee’s position would make of comparable land is permissible.

As for the wording, “He leased it as a resting place at midday and at night, and also for cultivation if possible, to make the customary use that someone in his position would make of comparable property,” the lease is valid. However, the words “as a resting place at midday and at night” are superfluous and serve no purpose. If the land cannot be used, no rent is due. If only part of it can be used, rent is due in proportion to that part. If the two parties dispute whether it can be used, the matter should be referred to others, for people know whether or not the land has been irrigated.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leased land and expressly declared in the lease that he had inspected it, although he had not inspected it before leasing it. The lessor described it as land that was irrigated every year. The lessor did not hand it over to the lessees and expressly stated that it provided a resting place at midday and at night. It then became apparent that approximately a quarter of it was unirrigated. Is this lease valid if the lessees have not inspected the land? And

---

## Page 15114

are they obliged to pay only for the portion of the aforementioned land that was irrigated? Or must they also pay for the portion that remained unirrigated, from which they derived no benefit and which they had not inspected?

He replied:

If the lessee has neither seen the land nor had it described to him, the lease is invalid according to the majority of scholars. Those who consider it valid grant the lessees the right to rescind it upon inspection. If it was described as being irrigated every year but was not irrigated, they may rescind the lease when the land proves contrary to the description and the condition stipulated for them.

Even if he leased it to them without qualification, and part of it was irrigated while another part was not, they would owe no rent for the unirrigated portion—even if the lease stated that it provided a resting place at midday and at night. Scholars regard leasing such unirrigated land for rest at midday and at night as invalid, because unirrigated land offers no such benefit: it is like any other open countryside with neither crops nor water. Such a benefit has no monetary value, nor would any value be assigned to it even if it existed—so how much more so when it is absent? A lease is valid only when it concerns an intended benefit. If something provides no benefit, or its benefit has no monetary value, the transaction is invalid. Likewise, it is invalid to lease something that provides no benefit for the purpose for which it was leased and whose supposed benefit has no monetary value.

This holds according to the view of those who uphold legal stratagems and do not invalidate them. The matter is clear to such a person, for he knows that the intended purpose of the contract is solely to benefit from cultivation; professing any other purpose is a lie and a deception.

Leasing land that is usually irrigated before it has been irrigated is permissible according to the imams. As for land that is irrigated only occasionally, there is disagreement.

---

## Page 15115

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leased land without knowing its area, to use for resting at midday and at night, grazing, and cultivation over a period of seven years. The land flooded and became submerged, leaving him unable to benefit from it. When it flooded, he sought cancellation of the lease by mutual agreement. The lease still had time to run when the flooding occurred, and the remaining benefit was lost. Must he pay rent for the year in which it flooded and became submerged? And may his request for cancellation be granted?

He replied:

Leasing a specified parcel of land is permissible even if its area is unknown, just as selling it or any other specified property is permissible even if its quantity is unknown. Selling a specified property as a whole, without measuring it, is permissible according to the Sunnah and scholarly consensus. It is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted the sale of a jointly owned share in land, a dwelling, or an orchard, and the sale of fruit on the trees once its soundness had become apparent. He also approved their practice of selling food in bulk without measuring it.

If the land can no longer be used because of flooding or any other cause, no rent is due for the use that has been lost, by agreement of the Muslims.

---

## Page 15116

He was also asked:

A man leased a village, and its land was inundated because a river burst its banks and they were unable to contain the water. Is their rent reduced in proportion to the land flooded, or not? If a judge rules that he must pay the rent in full, should that ruling be enforced, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. He may terminate the lease, and he may have the rent reduced in proportion to the loss of use. Whoever rules that the contract remains binding and that the rent must be paid in full has ruled contrary to scholarly consensus, and his ruling must not be enforced.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

If a house becomes partly unusable, is the rent reduced accordingly?

He replied:

Yes, his rent is reduced in proportion to the loss of the use to which the contract entitles him.

---

## Page 15117

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man leased an orchard for two years. It contained some open ground, but most of it was occupied by trees. The land was described as open ground, while the trees were covered by a fruit-sharing agreement for a one-thousandth share. They used this agreement as a legal stratagem to sell the fruit before its sale became permissible. Locusts then destroyed most of the fruit. Is the lessee entitled to a reduction for what the locusts destroyed?

He replied:

In these transactions concerning orchards, known as damān (guarantee contracts), it makes no difference whether they take place before the fruit appears and before it shows signs of ripeness, after both stages, or between them. Nor does it matter whether they are called guarantee contracts or, as a legal stratagem, fruit-sharing and lease contracts. If the fruit is destroyed by locusts or similar calamities beyond the parties’ control, such as plundering by armies and the like, the lessee-purchaser must be granted a reduction to account for the calamity. The amount he owes must be reduced in proportion to the loss of what he was to receive in exchange, whether the contract is invalid or valid.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, expressly ruled on both cases in authentic hadiths narrated by Anas and Jābir. This is the position of the great majority of scholars regarding a valid contract. How much more, then, does it apply to a contract that is invalid, whose validity is disputed, or that is made to appear valid through a legal stratagem? Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15118

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Some people owe the owners of a village sums of money and seed grain. They sowed all the seed grain in that village, but a cold spell destroyed the crop after it had begun to flourish. Are these farmers required to repay all the seed grain they received, or not?

He replied:

If the landowners provided the seed grain to be sown on the land under a valid or invalid farming agreement, the farmers bear no liability, provided they used it as instructed—even if the arrangement was termed an invalid loan. Its intended substance is a sharecropping agreement. And if the landowner sows seed there, he is entitled to recover it.

If, however, it was an unrestricted loan for which the borrower was personally liable, free to use it in various ways, it remains a debt owed by the borrower, even if his crop and money are lost.

---

## Page 15119

He was asked:

A man leased endowed properties, but demand for occupying them declined, and he had this documented in a report by experts. May the administrator reduce his rent if he considers this to be in the endowment’s interests? If he grants a reduction, may he recover the amount from the tenant once the lease expires? May those entitled to the endowment’s income object to the administrator’s action on this account?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If someone leases premises whose benefit lies in hiring them out to the public—such as a bathhouse, an inn, a commercial complex, or the like—and their customary benefit diminishes, then the tenant’s rent is to be reduced in proportion to that loss of benefit. This may occur, for example, when the people living nearby move away and customers become fewer because of fear, devastation, relocation by someone in authority, or similar circumstances. The reduction applies whether the administrator and the endowment’s beneficiaries approve or object. The amount remitted may not subsequently be recovered from the tenant, provided the reduction was no greater than the loss of customary benefit. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15120

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man leased another man’s land grant, comprising a share of one and a half qīrāts in the district, under a lawful lease allowing the tenant to cultivate it however he wished within lawful bounds. The lease did not describe it as a place for overnight or midday rest. Some of what was in the district was stolen, and the tenant derived no benefit from it. Must the tenant pay rent for the portion he could not use, or not?

He replied:

For whatever portion of the land irrigation has not reached, a corresponding amount of rent is remitted, by agreement of the scholars—even if the lease specifies “a place for midday and overnight rest” or leaves this unspecified. If none of the land is irrigated, no rent at all is due, by agreement of the scholars—even if the lease specifies “a place for midday and overnight rest.” And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15121

Chapter on Loans for Use

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man borrowed a horse from another man to ride to Bāb al-Nasr. The borrower stipulated that he would ride it no farther than Bāb al-Nasr and return immediately. Instead, he rode it to Birkat al-Hujjāj and did not return until after the ‘Asr prayer. The horse suffered an injury, and its owner sold it for half its value. Must the borrower pay half the loss in value?

He replied:

Yes. If he exceeded the use permitted by its owner, he is a wrongdoer and is liable for any damage caused by his transgression. He is therefore liable for whatever reduction in the horse’s value resulted from this wrongdoing, by agreement of the imams.

---

## Page 15122

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man lent a jointly owned horse without his co-owner’s permission. The horse died while in the borrower’s possession. Who is liable for the co-owner’s share?

He replied:

If he lent his co-owner’s share without permission and the horse died, the co-owner may demand the value of his share from the lender who acted wrongfully. He may also demand it from the borrower. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A woman borrowed a pair of earrings, which she subsequently lost. Must she pay their value?

He replied:

If she was negligent in safeguarding them, she must pay compensation, by agreement of the scholars. If she was not negligent, there is a well-known disagreement among them. According to the school of Abū Hanīfa, she is not liable; according to the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, she is liable. According to Mālik, if their loss occurred through a known cause, she is not liable; but if she claims that they were lost through a hidden cause, her claim is not accepted. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15123

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man was travelling and reached a village, where another man insisted that he stay with him, so he spent the night at his house. He asked his host for a mount, but when he reached the inn, it died. What is the ruling?

He replied:

The scholars hold two views on this matter:

The first is that he is not liable if the animal died without negligence or wrongdoing on his part. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and some followers of Imam Ahmad.

The second is that he is liable. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

Someone borrowed an item from a man, who lent it to him without any doubt that he was ʿUmar, being certain that he was that very person. When the lender asked for the item back, the person denied having borrowed it. The lender then swore, on pain of a threefold divorce, that this was indeed the borrower. It subsequently turned out that he had been mistaken, and the borrowed item was brought back. Does divorce take effect in these circumstances, or not?

---

## Page 15124

He replied:

If the circumstances are as described, and he believed that what he swore was true, divorce does not take effect, even if he later discovers that he was mistaken. And Allah knows best. All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Two men were with an emir, who said to one of them, “Ask to borrow your companion’s sword.” He complied, and the emir took it, but it was lost while in his possession. Should the claim be made against the emir or against the messenger who borrowed it?

He replied:

If the messenger neither lied nor exceeded his authority, he bears no liability. Rather, the borrower is liable if he was negligent or transgressed, by agreement of the scholars. Otherwise, the scholars differ over his liability. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15125

Chapter on Unlawful Seizure

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man unlawfully seized another man’s crop and harvested it. Is it permissible for the poor to gather the fallen remnants?

He replied:

Yes, gleaning is permissible, just as it would be if the owner had harvested the crop. Likewise, it is permissible to graze livestock on the grass growing on unlawfully seized land. Imam Ahmad explicitly stated this ruling concerning the latter issue. This is because whatever grass and gleanings may lawfully be taken remain lawful whether or not unlawful seizure has occurred, and the owner’s rights do not preclude taking them.

He was also asked:

A man owns land that has been in his possession for thirty years. Another man came and cut down his crop on it, then cultivated the land the following year. What is he obliged to do?

He replied:

No one has the right to take unlawful possession of it. Rather, its owner is entitled to demand

---

## Page 15126

payment of the customary rent from the person who cultivated his land. He may also take the crop, if it is still standing, and reimburse the cultivator for his expenses. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man stole a measure of grain and sowed it, but does not know its owner. Is the entire crop lawful for him?

He replied:

He must undoubtedly give the equivalent of the seed in charity. As for the additional yield, there is disagreement concerning it. The most equitable opinion is to treat this as a sharecropping arrangement: he takes his own share and gives the seed owner’s share in charity on his behalf. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:
