A man unlawfully seized an item of property and sold it to someone who knew that it had been unlawfully seized. Its owner then came and recovered it from the purchaser. Is the purchaser entitled to recover the price he paid from the usurper who sold it to him, despite having known that it had been unlawfully seized? Or is the purchaser entitled to recover nothing from the usurper, so that the money he paid him is lost without compensation? What is the ruling in this matter?

---

## Page 15127

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. Rather, the purchaser is entitled to recover from the usurper the price he received from him, whether or not the purchaser knew that the property had been unlawfully seized. The usurper received the price without right, even though the purchaser paid it willingly.

If the two had willingly traded something unlawful to sell, such as wine or a pig, the item sold would have to be returned, the wine or pig destroyed, and the price refunded to the purchaser. How much more so when he has sold him someone else’s property? On what grounds could the price remain in the usurper’s possession? He has no right to it; it belongs to the purchaser. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked:

About a man who planted date stones on someone else’s land.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he plants a date palm that he owns on another person’s land—his son’s—his son’s heirs have no right to the palm. Rather, the respective rights belong to him and the landowners: the palm belongs to him, and he must pay the landowners rent for the land if they allow it to remain there. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15128

He was asked:

About a man who acquired a camel, which then gave birth to a calf. Is there any concession regarding its offspring in the four schools of law?

He replied:

An animal’s offspring belongs to its owner and is not lawful for the usurper. However, if the offspring resulted from the efforts of the person who took possession, some scholars hold that the increase should be shared between the owner and the person who did the work, as in a profit-sharing partnership and similar arrangements. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a man who lawfully owns livestock and has them covered by a stud unlawfully in his possession. Is there any doubt concerning the lawfulness of their offspring?

He replied:

If he has his livestock covered by someone else’s stud, the offspring belongs to him. However, if he wrongfully uses the stud for breeding in a way that harms it, he must compensate its owner for any diminution in its value. If he does not know its owner, he must give the equivalent of that diminution in charity. But if his use does not harm the stud, no payment is due for its use, for:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade charging stud fees.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15129

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who purchased an animal with money that was partly lawful and partly unlawful: what is the ruling of Islamic law?

The answer

He replied:

If he purchased it with money that was partly his own and partly usurped, half of the animal belongs to him, but he has no right to the other half. Rather, he must return that half to its rightful owner if possible; otherwise, he must give it in charity on the owner’s behalf. Any increase arising from it follows the same ruling as the original property: half belongs to him and half to the other party. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a slave woman who requests a zarkash for herself in her mistress’s name, then requests a ring in her mistress’s name. Her mistress denies this, while the slave woman admits it.

He replied:

If she made the request in her mistress’s name without her mistress having authorized her, then the slave woman wrongfully took possession of the item without any right to it. If it is lost or destroyed while in her possession, the claim for compensation attaches to the slave woman’s person. Her mistress has the choice either to redeem her by paying the value of what she took, or to surrender her to be sold, with the compensation taken from the proceeds of her sale. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15130

He—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

About property seized in the plundering that takes place among the Bedouin, including livestock that have reproduced, money that has yielded a profit, or trees that have borne fruit: do the offspring and profits belong to the usurper because he tends the livestock, trades with the money, and waters the trees? Or do they belong to the owner from whom the property was usurped? Is zakat payable on the property held by these Bedouin or not? If the usurper repents but the owner is unknown, what must he do? Should he give all of it in charity, or only part? Is repentance from unlawful sexual intercourse, theft, and similar sins valid?

He was also asked about some members of the Ahmadiyyah and others who attend gatherings of singing and instrumental music, handle snakes, and enter fire without being burned. If they are not given any zakat, they become angry and direct their will against the person who withholds it from them, saying: “This is for your camels; this is for your sheep; for such-and-such…” When some of the camels and sheep die, they say: “This is the effect of our thoughts.” Is it permissible to give these people zakat out of fear of them, or for any other reason?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

As for usurped property that the usurper puts to use until it yields an increase, the scholars hold differing views: does the increase belong to the owner

---

## Page 15131

alone? Or should they give it in charity? Or should it be shared between them, as it would be if the property were used under a profit-sharing investment partnership, an orchard-tending agreement for a share of the yield, or a sharecropping agreement, or if livestock were entrusted to someone to tend in return for a share of their milk and offspring? Or should the worker receive the customary wage for such work, if that is their established practice?

Such was the course followed by Umar ibn al-Khattāb when Abū Mūsā al-Ash'arī lent Umar’s two sons 200,000 dirhams from the public revenues, singling them out from among all the Muslims. Umar ibn al-Khattāb considered this impermissible favouritism towards them. The money had yielded a considerable profit, bringing the total to 800,000 dirhams. He therefore ordered them to pay both the principal and its profit into the public treasury, ruling that they were entitled to none of the profit because they had received the money without right.

His son Abdullāh said to him: “This is not lawful for you. Had the money suffered a loss or been destroyed, we would have been liable for it. Why, then, do you hold us liable but deny us the profit?” Umar withheld judgment.

One of the Companions then said to him: “Let us treat it as a profit-sharing investment partnership between them and the Muslims: the two sons receive half the profit, and the Muslims receive the other half.” Umar acted accordingly.

This is one of the precedents on which the jurists have relied concerning profit-sharing investment partnerships. It was the ruling on which Umar ibn al-Khattāb ultimately settled, and the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, agreed with him. This is justice: the increase resulted from one party’s capital and the other’s labour, so neither is entitled to all the profit. Nor are they obliged to give the increase in charity, for the right belongs to them alone; no one else has a claim to it. Rather, the profit is divided between them as though they had entered into a profit-sharing investment partnership.

---

## Page 15132

The same applies to someone who tends another person’s livestock, orchard, or land until it yields crops, milk, or offspring. Some scholars, however, do not permit such work in return for a share of the yield; they permit only hiring for a wage.

The more correct of the two scholarly opinions is that orchard-tending agreements for a share of the yield and sharecropping agreements are permissible, whether the seed comes from the owner, the worker, or both. Thus:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made an agreement with the people of Khaybar for half of the fruit and crops it produced, on condition that they cultivate it at their own expense.”

Al-Bukhārī narrated this in his Sahīh. Likewise, Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, such as Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqās, Abdullāh ibn Masʿūd, and others, would entrust land to someone to cultivate, with the cultivator providing his own seed and the crop being shared between them. Most households among the Emigrants and the Helpers engaged in sharecropping.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade the form of sharecropping known as mukhābara that they used to practise: they would stipulate that the landowner receive the crop from a particular plot. This is established in the two Sahīh collections. This arrangement, which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade, is prohibited by scholarly consensus, just as it would be prohibited to stipulate in a profit-sharing investment partnership that one party receive a fixed sum of dirhams.

Justice requires that they share whatever increase Allah provides, each receiving an undivided proportional share. They thus share in both gain and loss. If nothing is produced, one loses the benefit of his property, and the other loses the benefit of his labour.

---

## Page 15133

Section

As for the property held by these Bedouins who plunder one another, if no specific owner can be identified, the person holding it must pay its zakat. If it belongs to him, its zakat is his responsibility. If it does not belong to him and its owner is unknown, he must give it all in charity. Thus, giving an amount equivalent to its zakat in charity is better than giving none of it. In either case, paying an amount equivalent to the zakat is better than not doing so.

When they plunder one another, if the plundering occurs between two identifiable groups, the amount each group has taken from the other is assessed. If the amounts are equal, their claims are settled against one another, and each group is left in possession of what it holds, even if the particular person from whom it was plundered cannot be identified.

This is like a case in which they fight in the manner of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance, each side killing members of the other, and one side destroying some of the other’s property. Reciprocal redress must then be effected between the two groups: lives are set against lives, and property against property. If a balance remains due to either group from the other, it is to claim that balance.

---

## Page 15134

And this is indicated by the words of Allah Most High:

“Prescribed for you is retribution for those killed: the free for the free, the slave for the slave, and the female for the female.”

More than one of the early Muslims said: This verse was revealed concerning two Arab tribes that had fought one another. Allah Most High commanded that their slain be set against one another: a free person from one tribe against a free person from the other, a slave against a slave, and a female against a female.

Then He said:

“But if anyone receives any pardon from his brother, then the claim should be pursued fairly and payment made to him graciously.”

That is, if either party is owed a balance by the other, the latter should pay it to them fairly, and the other party should seek it from them with kindness. Pursuing the claim means demanding payment, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Delay in payment by someone able to pay is injustice. If any of you is referred for payment to a solvent person, let him accept the referral.”

This is because, among groups with the strength to resist, whose members assist one another in fighting, liability rests, according to the majority of scholars, both upon those who directly engage in fighting, seizure, and destruction, and upon the supporters who assist them. Hence, according to the majority’s position, both the supporting accomplice and the direct perpetrator among highway robbers are subject to execution. Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, executed the highwaymen’s lookout—the man who watched the road for them.

Thus, those who cooperate in wrongdoing and aggression must face penalties, including liability for compensation and other sanctions. For this reason, the generality of jurists have said that, when two groups fight out of factional loyalty and rivalry for leadership, each group is liable for the losses it inflicts on the other in respect of

---

## Page 15135

lives and property. Thus, they imposed liability upon the group as a whole, even when the individual responsible for the loss was unknown. If the amount plundered is unknown, so that neither what one side plundered from the other nor how much the other side plundered from them can be determined, the amounts are presumed equal. This is like someone whose wealth contains a mixture of lawful and unlawful assets, without his knowing which is greater: he relinquishes half his wealth, and the remaining half is lawful for him. Umar ibn al-Khattāb did this with the officials responsible for public funds: he divided their wealth in half, taking half the wealth of his officials in Syria, Egypt, and Iraq. He considered that some of the Muslims’ wealth had become mixed with theirs, but he did not know precisely which assets belonged to whom, or how much either side had taken from the other. Indeed, an individual might possess less than his entitlement or more. In such a case, each person is allowed to retain what is in his possession if he repents of cooperating in wrongdoing and aggression. For what cannot be ascertained is treated as nonexistent, and the obligation concerning it is waived. He must pay zakah on that wealth as its owner would.

If, however, he knows that his wealth includes both lawful assets that he owns and unlawful assets whose owners are unknown, and he knows the amount, he must divide the wealth in proportion to its lawful and unlawful portions. He takes the lawful portion, while giving the unlawful portion in charity on behalf of its owners. This is what someone does when he holds property whose owners are unknown, whether wrongfully seized property, borrowed items, or deposits entrusted for safekeeping. For the majority of scholars, such as Mālik

---

## Page 15136

and Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad ibn Hanbal, among others, maintain that he should give it in charity. This is what is reported from the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning such cases. If he does not know the respective amounts of lawful and unlawful wealth, he divides the wealth into two halves, keeping one half for himself and returning the other half to its owners if he knows them; otherwise, he gives it in charity.

Whatever he gives in charity is to be spent for the benefit of the Muslims: it may be given to those eligible for zakah, used to entertain guests and assist pilgrims, or spent on jihad and other good works that Allah and His Messenger love, just as with all other property whose owners are unknown. The same applies to someone who repents of acquiring unlawful wealth while still holding such wealth without knowing its owner.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A governor seized twenty thousand dirhams belonging to someone, and his liability was established before a judge. They know that all his assets are unlawful, having been plundered from other people. May they take some of this wealth as compensation for what he took from them, given that they know all his wealth is unlawful?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If everything in his possession was taken from

---

## Page 15137

people unjustly—for example, by taking from thieves and highway robbers some of the wealth they steal from people; by setting out to wrong certain people, who then pay him to desist; by exempting certain people from the demands imposed on their peers so that they will pay him a bribe; by ruling unjustly, or ruling justly only in return for a bribe; by seizing people’s property through fabricated accusations against them; or by denying justice to those killed in return for bribes from their killers—then these funds and others like them rightfully belong to their owners.

This is like a thief who steals property and mixes it together: doing so does not make it unlawful for its owners. Rather, they divide the property among themselves in proportion to their respective claims. Even if a particular owner’s property cannot be identified because the thief sold it or the like, what was received in exchange takes its place.

Concerning someone who earns profits by trading or otherwise using these funds, one view holds that the profits belong to the owners of the funds. Another holds that they belong to him if he incurred personal liability for the purchase. Another holds that both he and the owners should give the profits in charity because they are ill-gotten gains. Yet another holds that the profits should be divided between him and the owners of the funds, as in a profit-sharing partnership. This is what ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb did concerning the money that Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī lent to ʿUmar’s two sons, to the exclusion of the rest of the army. This is the most equitable of these views.

Accordingly, the owners of the funds divide whatever they find in proportion to their respective claims, since it is either their actual property or payment of the debts established as his personal liability. Indeed, the correct view is that their rights attach to both: his personal liability and the funds themselves.

But if he does not know how much he seized unlawfully or the identities of all the creditors, then whoever among them takes from these

---

## Page 15138

funds an amount equal to his claim is not to be judged to have taken something unlawful—especially if the holder has traded with the funds in his possession, for the claimant is then entitled to more than the amount of his original claim. There is, however, a risk that the funds in the holder’s possession may not suffice to meet the claims of everyone entitled to them. Yet those whose identities are unknown are treated as though they did not exist. Thus, if a person takes only what is due to him and does not wrong the other known creditors, we do not judge what he has taken to be unlawful. But if other creditors subsequently appear who are entitled to a share of the holder’s property, they may demand their rightful shares. Recovery is then made from each person who took possession of the property in proportion to the amount he took. And Allah knows best.

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

I was asked about people whose sheep or other property had been taken from them and then returned, in whole or in part, when one person’s property could no longer be distinguished from another’s.

I answered:

If the precise amount of each person’s property is known, whatever is available is divided among them in proportion to that amount. If only the number is known, it is divided in proportion to the number. This is because when two people’s property becomes mixed together, it is divided between them, even if each receives, in exchange for his own property, something that belonged to the other: the mixing has made them co-owners. This is particularly so according to our principle that a partnership may validly be established by contract even while the two parties’ assets remain distinct. The inability to distinguish ownership of sheep and similar property, however, has the same effect as the commingling of liquids.

---

## Page 15139

On this basis, a partnership involving similar animals or garments should be valid, just as one whose capital consists of dirhams—if we recognize the validity of partnerships funded with non-monetary assets. When people become co-owners because their property has become mixed or indistinguishable, it is divided in proportion to their respective holdings. If everything belonging to them is returned, the matter is clear. If only part is returned, that part belongs to the jointly owned property, just as when some commingled dirhams are returned.

There remains the question of livestock: is there only one view—that it must be divided in kind when some of the owners request this—or do the two views concerning jointly owned livestock apply? The more appropriate conclusion is that the same disagreement applies. For if one person owned ten head and the other twenty, one is entitled to a third of whatever is recovered and the other to two-thirds.

The difficulty in this case, however, concerns valuation. If the value of each person’s property is known, the matter is clear. But if only the number is known, even though one person’s sheep may be better than the other’s, then, when it is impossible to establish whether one person’s sheep are superior to the other’s, they must be treated equally, since necessity compels equal treatment.

Accordingly, whether one person’s sheep became mixed with the other’s deliberately or by mistake, the combined property is divided in proportion to their numbers if no superiority in value is known. If such superiority is known but its extent is not, the amount established with certainty is recognized, while any doubtful excess is disregarded, since the presumption is that it does not exist.

---

## Page 15140

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

May a person tear another’s garment just as the latter tears his?

He replied:

Regarding retaliation in kind for the destruction of property—for example, when someone tears another’s garment and the latter tears an equivalent garment belonging to him, or demolishes another’s house and the latter demolishes his, and similar cases—the scholars hold two views, both reported from Ahmad.

The first is that this is not lawful, because it constitutes destruction and because properties and garments are not equivalent to one another.

The second is that it is lawful, because lives and limbs are more precious than property. If taking a life or destroying a limb is permissible as retaliation to secure full redress for the wronged person, then this applies all the more to property. Hence, we may destroy the property of those at war with us if they destroy ours, as in cutting down fruit-bearing trees, even if doing so without necessity is held to be prohibited. This matter is disputed.

If someone destroys another’s garments, animals, real estate, or similar property, must he compensate him for their value, or provide replacements of the same kind with due regard to their value? The scholars hold two well-known views on this. These are also two positions within

---

## Page 15141

the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. Al-Shāfi‘ī explicitly stated that if someone demolishes another’s house, he must rebuild it as it was, thus requiring compensation in kind. A similar position concerning animals has also been reported from him. Likewise, according to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, a man deceived into believing a slave woman was free must compensate for his children with equivalent slaves. Ahmad also explicitly stated that whoever borrows an animal must return its equivalent.

The account of David and Solomon falls under this heading. David, peace be upon him, awarded the owners of the field in which the people’s sheep had grazed at night compensation based on its value, giving them the livestock in lieu of that sum. Solomon, peace be upon him, ordered the livestock owners to restore the field to its former condition, while the field owners were to benefit from the livestock in compensation for the benefit they had lost from their field.

Al-Zuhrī gave ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz a ruling to this effect when some members of the Umayyad family wrongfully uprooted the trees of an orchard belonging to him. When asked what was owed for this, he replied:

“He must replant it as it was.”

He was told:

“Rabī‘ah and Abū al-Zinād have said that its value must be paid.”

Al-Zuhrī then spoke about them in terms whose substance was that they had contradicted the Sunnah.

There is no doubt that compensating for property with something of the same kind, while taking its value into account, is closer to justice than compensating for it with something of a different kind—namely, dirhams and dinars—while taking its value into account. Value is considered in both cases, but sameness of kind is preserved in only one. People’s purposes undoubtedly depend on the kind of property itself. Otherwise, what use are dirhams to someone who wants a book, a horse, or an orchard?

If it is said, “He can buy its equivalent with them,” the answer is: “The wrongdoer who deprived him of his property should be the one required to provide him with the equivalent of what he deprived him of, or a counterpart to the property he damaged.”

---

## Page 15142

He was asked:

About merchants who were seized by robbers, who then returned some of their property to them: may anyone who recognizes any of his property take it, or should it be shared equitably in proportion to the amounts stolen from each, and so forth?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Whoever finds his actual property has the greater right to it. As for those whose property is no longer to be found, they should divide whatever compensation the robbers pay them in proportion to the value of their property, not according to the number of people. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About soldiers who camped somewhere overnight. Some people came and stole some of their belongings. They pursued the thief, and one of them struck him with a sword. He was then carried to the commander of the troops and subsequently died.

The answer

He replied:

If this was the means of recovering what the thief had taken, the man who struck him bears no liability.

Ibn ‘Umar related that a thief entered his house, whereupon he rose

---

## Page 15143

to confront him with a sword. Had they not held him back, he would have struck him with the sword.

In the two Sahīh collections:

“Whoever is killed defending his property is a martyr.”

He was asked:

About the wrongfully seized property he had presented to the ruler, and so forth.

He replied:

As for the wrongfully seized property he presented to the ruler, and whatever he gave him, he should give an equivalent amount in charity on its owner’s behalf if he does not know who the owner is. The same applies to whatever he gave the emir as a gift or for which he compensated him.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who grinds grain in mills belonging to the ruler, which he leases despite knowing that some of them have been wrongfully seized; and about a man who works on the ruler’s crops: is his share of the crop lawful, and are the first man’s earnings from the mill lawful?

He replied:

As for the ruler’s lands and mills that are not known to have been wrongfully seized, a person may work on a sharecropping basis in return for a share of the crop. He may also lease them or work in them for wages

---

## Page 15144

while bearing liability. If, however, he knows that they have been wrongfully seized but no specific owner is known, there is disagreement concerning them. The stronger view is that working in them is permissible if the worker takes only the wages for his work, for he will then have wronged no one in any way. In any event, putting them to use is better than leaving them idle. This applies if they can be restored to their owners; otherwise, they are to be devoted to the public interests of the Muslims. An unknown owner is treated as nonexistent. But if the land is known to belong to a specific owner from whom it was taken unjustly, it must not be worked without his permission or that of his guardian or agent. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About people required to collect levies from the townspeople: if they apportion the demands equally among the people, while themselves being subjected to this injustice, do they incur any sin?

He replied:

Rather, these levies demanded of people, whether rightfully or wrongfully, must be apportioned justly. It is unlawful to spare some people their share and place it upon others. Whoever undertakes this task intending to uphold justice, alleviate oppression as far as possible, and assist the weak so that they are not repeatedly wronged, without intending to assist the oppressor, is like one who fights in Allah’s cause—provided that he earnestly pursues justice and seeks the countenance of Allah.

---

## Page 15145

The shaykh and imam, the learned and erudite scholar, Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-‘Abbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī—may Allah sanctify his soul and illuminate his grave through His grace and generosity—also said:

Praise be to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help and ask His forgiveness. We seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant them abundant peace.

Section on “Collective Unjust Exactions”

These are demands made upon people jointly, such as the inhabitants of a village or town, when payments are demanded from them on their property or per head. Examples include levies imposed by the ruler upon them all, assessed according to their number, the number of their animals or trees, or the extent of their wealth. Thus, they may be charged more than the zakat required by Islamic law, or more than the land tax required by Islamic law, or they may be charged newly introduced levies on categories other than those prescribed by Islamic law

---

## Page 15146

such as the levies imposed upon buyers and sellers of food, clothing, animals, fruit and other goods. These are collected when sales take place, sometimes from sellers and sometimes from buyers. Some of these levies, admittedly, are said to have been imposed on the interpretation that people are obliged to support jihad with their wealth and that jihad requires such funds—as mentioned by the author of Ghiyāth al-Umam (Relief of the Nations) and others—despite the injustice that has entered into this practice and for which scholars recognize no justification.

Other examples include the levies that some kings collect periodically from the inhabitants of their lands, claiming that these are contributions to support their purposes. Likewise, governors sometimes demand payments on an irregular basis: they may be arriving with an army and collecting supplies for their troops, or collecting for some occasion, such as the ruler’s arrival, the birth of a child to him, or something similar. Alternatively, goods may be forced upon people and sold to them for more than their value; these are called “al-hatā’it.”

Another example concerns caravans travelling for pilgrimage, trade or other purposes. Payments are demanded from them according to the number of people or animals, or the amount of their wealth; alternatively, a sum is demanded from them collectively without specifying a basis of assessment. This applies whether the person demanding payment holds authority in a town or village—like those who station themselves at bridges and city gates and collect what they collect—or whether those taking payment are highway robbers, such as the Bedouins, Kurds and Turks who exact tolls from travellers and will not let them pass until they give them what they demand.

Those compelled to pay these sums must observe justice regarding what

---

## Page 15147

is demanded of them, and none of them may wrong another in apportioning what is demanded. Rather, they must observe justice regarding what is taken from them without right, just as they must observe justice regarding what is rightfully taken from them. For these levies, imposed upon them on account of their persons and property, are no different from other levies as far as they themselves are concerned. The distinction concerns only the collection itself: it may be rightful or wrongful. As for those required to pay, these are levies imposed upon them on account of their persons and property, so none of them may wrong another in this matter.

Rather, everyone owes justice to everyone else in all circumstances, and no injustice whatsoever is permissible under any circumstances. Indeed, Allah Most High has obliged believers to act justly even towards disbelievers, saying:

“Stand firm for Allah as witnesses in justice, and do not let the hatred of a people lead you to be unjust. Be just; that is nearer to righteousness.”

The believers opposed the disbelievers by Allah’s command, so He Most High said [by way of clarification] (1): Do not let your hatred of the disbelievers lead you to treat them unjustly. Rather, treat them justly, for that is nearer to righteousness.

Accordingly, none of those who share this liability may do anything that wrongs another. He may either pay his own share, thereby acting justly, or pay more than his share, thereby helping the others with what is taken from them and acting benevolently. He may not refuse to pay his share of that sum in such a way that it is collected from the others instead, thus compounding the injustice inflicted upon them. For if the money will inevitably be collected, and he evades payment through his influence

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I did not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā against Omissions and Scribal Errors).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for the Shamela Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 15148

or bribery or some other means, he has wronged those from whom his own share is collected. This is not the same as protecting oneself from injustice without wronging anyone else, which is permissible—for example, refusing to pay one’s share when it will then be collected neither from oneself nor from anyone else.

This is the case with governmental levies imposed on villages. For example, ten thousand dirhams may be imposed upon them, and someone with influence through a position of command, a position as a shaykh, bribery, or some other means asks that nothing be collected from him, although the collectors are bound to collect the entire sum. If he does this, his share is collected from all the others who share the liability: he refuses to pay what falls to him, and it is collected from the others instead. This is an injustice on his part towards them, for he has only protected himself from injustice by wronging them, and this is impermissible.

He cannot say, “I did not wrong them; rather, the one who collected both shares from them wronged them.” The reply is as follows:

First: The collector may have been ordered by his superior to collect that sum, so he cannot waive one person’s share without collecting it from another instead. Thus, instructing him not to collect one’s share amounts to instructing him to commit injustice.

Second: Even supposing that he is the highest authority issuing the orders, he must treat them justly in what he demands from them. Although the demand itself is unjust, he must apportion this injustice fairly among them and not commit a further injustice in doing so, thereby compounding the injustice. For if one person’s share is one hundred and he is required to pay two hundred, he has suffered a compounded injustice, unlike when

---

## Page 15149

he collects each person’s share from him. Moreover, people accept being treated equally in what they are denied and in what is unjustly taken from them, but they do not accept some being singled out for gifts or exemptions. Thus, Islamic law permits a sick person to bequeath a third of his wealth to a non-heir, but not to single out an heir for anything from that third in addition to his rightful share, even though he may give the entire third to a non-heir.

The same applies to gifts to one’s children: a person is commanded to treat them equally, whether giving or withholding, and not to single out some for gifts without a reason warranting it, as indicated by the hadith of al-Nu‘mān ibn Bashīr and other reports.

Third: If he asks the oppressor not to collect anything from him, knowing that he will impose his share on someone else, he has asked him to do something that he knows will involve wronging another person. A person may not ask someone else to do something that will involve wronging another, even if he has not instructed him to commit injustice. This is like appointing someone to a position of authority and instructing him not to commit injustice while knowing that he will do so: he may not appoint him. The same applies to appointing an agent and instructing him not to commit injustice while knowing that he will do so. Likewise, a person may not ask someone to repay a debt owed to him from his lawfully acquired wealth, while knowing that he will repay it only from what he has wrongfully taken from others. Similarly, the person in question has asked to be spared injustice while knowing that he will be spared only by someone else being wronged. He may not ask for this.

(Fourth: This leads to the weak, who have no one to support them, having the entire sum collected from them, while the powerful pay nothing towards the levies on property, even though their holdings are greater. This entails such corruption and evil

---

## Page 15150

as none but Allah Most High knows, as is indeed borne out in practice.

(Fifth:
If Muslims need to collect money to repel their enemy, those who are able must contribute, even if the unbelievers take that money without right. It is therefore all the more incumbent upon them to contribute jointly towards what oppressors take from Muslims.

Section

Accordingly, if one of those sharing this liability absents himself or refuses to pay, so that nothing is collected from him and his share is taken from someone else, he must reimburse whoever paid on his behalf for the amount of his share, according to the stronger of the two scholarly views. This is like discharging any other obligation he owes. He must be compelled to do so and may be punished to compel payment, just as he may be punished to compel him to discharge his other obligations.

This is comparable to a zakat collector demanding more than the amount due from one of two partners and taking it on the basis of an interpretation: the partner from whom it was taken may recover the other partner’s proportionate share from him. If it was taken without such an interpretation, there are two scholarly views; the stronger is that he may recover it in this case as well.

The same applies to the administrator of an endowment, an orphan’s guardian, the manager of a profit-sharing partnership, a partner, an agent, and anyone else who administers another person’s property by virtue of guardianship or agency, when payment is demanded from him for the levies falling on that property. Examples include government levies collected from him on real estate, or levies collected from merchants on roads and in villages on the assets they carry. They may pay these from the property itself; indeed, they must do so if they fear that failure to pay will result in an even greater amount being taken. And if it should happen

---

## Page 15151

that the property is unavailable and they borrow on its account to pay what is demanded, or use their own funds to pay on behalf of the property of their principal or the person under their guardianship, they are entitled to recover the corresponding amount from that property. This has been the practice of Muslims in every age and land. Whoever rejects this view adopts a position whose harmful consequences are known only to the Lord of all servants, for the levies unjustly imposed on property are exceedingly numerous.

If the sums that someone entrusted with another’s property pays on its behalf towards these levies, forcibly and wrongfully exacted from him, were charged against him when paid from his own funds, much of the wealth of trustworthy custodians would be lost. They would consequently have to refrain from undertaking such responsibilities, lest they lose their wealth. These responsibilities would then fall to treacherous, wicked people who do not fear Allah. Rather, they take whatever wealth they can and claim that less was collected or more was disbursed, as is well known of many entrusted with government funds. Yet some of what these people do may be based on an interpretation, unlike an agent, a partner, the manager of a profit-sharing partnership, an orphan’s guardian, the administrator of an endowment, and others like them.

That being so, if someone entrusted with property jointly owned by himself and his partner is credited with the amounts taken from him for these levies, then he has an even stronger claim to be credited with what zakat collectors take in the name of zakat, even if they take more than is due without any interpretive justification—especially since this

---

## Page 15152

is a frequent, if not predominant, occurrence in these times. Zakat collectors take far more than is due in zakat on livestock, and likewise on trade goods. They take it from whoever holds the property, whether its owner, an agent, a partner, the manager of a profit-sharing partnership, or anyone else. If those entrusted with property were not credited with what is wrongfully taken from them, the resulting harm would be beyond anyone’s reckoning except the Lord of all servants.

Moreover, making such a payment may be obligatory to protect the property, since, if he did not pay it, the oppressors would take even more. It is well known that when someone entrusted with another’s property cannot avert a greater injustice except by paying part of what is demanded, he must do so, for safeguarding the property is obligatory. If this can only be accomplished by such a payment, then the payment becomes obligatory: whatever is indispensable to fulfilling an obligation is itself obligatory.

Furthermore, the opposing party concedes that if they compelled the person entrusted with the property to take property belonging to someone else, he would bear no liability. They also concede that if an unjust collector took more than was due from jointly owned property, the person entrusted with it would bear no liability. Their uncertainty arises only when the collector compels someone to pay on another’s behalf by whatever means, and he pays on that person’s behalf using funds borrowed on his account or someone’s money, intending to recover the amount from him.

They should therefore be asked:

What difference is there between compelling him to pay on that person’s behalf from his own funds and compelling him to pay from the absent person’s funds? Clearly, obliging him to pay on behalf of someone who is absent or refuses to pay causes him greater harm than making the payment from that absent or refusing person’s own property. Paying what is demanded of the absent person is easier for him than paying it from his own funds. Thus, if he is excused for what he

---

## Page 15153

pays from the absent person’s property because he is compelled to make the payment, then he has even greater reason to be excused when compelled to pay on that person’s behalf.

If the opposing party argues, “That is because, in that case, the payment comes from the compelled payer’s own property, so he is the one wronged,” they should be told:

Rather, both are wronged: the payer is wronged by being made to pay on the other’s behalf, and the other is wronged by the demand for his property. How, then, can the entire burden be placed on the payer, when the primary purpose is to demand money from the person on whose behalf he pays? Actions are judged only by intentions. The unjust claimant intends to take that person’s property, not the payer’s; he merely demands that the payer pay on the other’s behalf.

Moreover, the person compelled to pay on behalf of someone absent is an outright victim of injustice affecting both his person and his property, whereas the absent person is wronged in respect of his property. How, then, can the payer’s property be made a shield for the other’s property against the injustice of the oppressor who compelled him? Or how can someone with little wealth have many times his share taken from him, while nothing is taken from someone with great wealth?

At most, this may be likened to the wrongful seizure of undivided jointly owned property. If the usurper takes one of the two partners’ shares from a jointly owned asset, what he takes is reckoned as coming from that partner’s property, according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. This is the apparent position of the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, among others, because he intended only to take the property of one of the two partners.

---

## Page 15154

If one of two sons acknowledges a third brother, while the other denies his claim, the acknowledging son must give the acknowledged brother whatever exceeds his own rightful share—namely, one-sixth of the estate—according to the schools of Mālik and Ahmad ibn Hanbal. This is also the apparent position of al-Shāfi‘ī’s school and the view of the majority of the early generations. They regarded what the denying brother wrongfully seized as belonging exclusively to the acknowledged brother, because he did not intend to take any part of the acknowledging brother’s rightful share.

Abū Hanīfah, however, held concerning the wrongful seizure of undivided jointly owned property that whatever the usurper takes comes from both partners’ shares, considering the outward act of taking possession without regard to intention.

Likewise, he held concerning the denying brother that whatever he wrongfully seized comes from both brothers’ shares. Accordingly, the acknowledging brother gives the acknowledged brother half of what he holds—namely, one-quarter of the estate—while the half wrongfully seized by the denying brother is reckoned as coming from both of them. This is also one opinion within the schools of Ahmad and al-Shāfi‘ī. The majority’s view is correct because intention must be taken into account. Likewise, in the case under discussion, the oppressor took payment only on behalf of the person from whom it was demanded; he did not intend to take the payer’s property.

If it is objected:

“What if the oppressor makes a mistake—for example, highway robbers intend to take one person’s property but take someone else’s, mistaking him for the first? Must the first person compensate the person they mistook for him for the property taken?”

The answer is:

Cases of mistake involve distinctions that this is not the place to discuss. Nevertheless, the difference between the two situations is clear: they are not alike. The mistaken oppressor who took this person’s property did not take it on someone else’s behalf. Rather, he thought it was Zayd’s property, but it turned out to belong to ‘Amr. He intended to take Zayd’s property but took ‘Amr’s, just as someone who sought to kill a person whose life is inviolable

---

## Page 15155

but killed another person whose life was inviolable, mistaking him for the first. This differs from someone who specifically targets Zayd’s property, or intends to take from partners an amount to be apportioned fairly among them, and takes from some on behalf of others. Such a person has made no mistake; rather, he has done what he intended: he meant to take a particular person’s property and demanded it from someone holding that person’s property—a partner, agent, or the like—to pay on his behalf. Likewise, if they demand money from one partner for shared affairs, to be collected from all the partners, they have made no mistake in their understanding. If, then, they intended to take from just one person—or rather, intended to apportion the payment fairly between him and his partners—but could only take from his partner, how can that partner be wronged twice?

A comparable case is when the administrator of the public treasury needs to pay an oppressor to ward off his harm from the Muslims—as with payments to those whose hearts are to be reconciled, to ward off their harm, or payments to disbelievers if such a need arises, and we seek refuge in Allah from that. If the treasury contains nothing, and he borrows money from people and pays it over, would any reasonable person say that the loss of this money must be borne by those from whom it was taken, with no right to reimbursement from the public treasury, merely because the money received was their actual property rather than the treasury’s?

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions would make their payments sometimes from the funds themselves and sometimes from money they borrowed. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would borrow against zakat and fay’ revenues and spend the borrowed money on purposes prescribed by Islamic law, including gifts to those being won over

---

## Page 15156

and others. Among the recipients were some for whom taking it was unlawful. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would say:

“I give one of them a gift, and he leaves carrying it under his arm as fire.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, why, then, do you give to them?” He replied, “They insist on asking me, and Allah will not allow me to be miserly.”

No reasonable person would say that this money should be lost to the person from whom it was borrowed. Rather, it is treated just as though it had come directly from zakat and fay’ funds, because the giver was permitted to give, even though the recipient was not permitted to take. This applies even when he gives of his own free will; how much more, then, to someone compelled to pay, for whom payment is permissible or obligatory?

Nor may it be objected, “Here, the ruler borrowed people’s money from them.” The answer is that he borrowed it only to pay that oppressor who sought to seize the Muslims’ wealth. He thus paid the borrowed money on their behalf to avert harm from them, and he must repay it from their shared funds: zakat and fay’ revenues. Nor may it be said, “It is not lawful for him to spend their funds, since what that oppressor took belonged only to some of them.” Rather, paying this small amount to safeguard their lives and property is obligatory.

When payment is obligatory to avert a greater harm, the position of Mālik, the well-known position reported from Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and that of other scholars is that anyone who discharges another person’s obligation is entitled to reimbursement from him, provided he did not do so gratuitously, even if he discharged it without that person’s permission. An example is someone who settles another person’s debt without his permission, whether he had guaranteed the debt without his permission and then paid it without his permission, or paid it on his behalf without having guaranteed it.

---

## Page 15157

And likewise, someone who ransoms a captive without his permission is entitled to recover the ransom from him. The same applies to someone who pays maintenance owed by another—for example, by supporting that person’s son or wife, or feeding his livestock—especially when the person paying has a right over the livestock, as a pledgee or lessee, or has been entrusted with them as a depositary. Other examples include someone who returns a runaway slave, and one of two partners who pays for the upkeep of their jointly owned livestock.

This principle is supported by the words of Allah Most High:

“If they breastfeed for you, then give them their payment.”

He commanded that they be paid simply for breastfeeding, without stipulating a contract for their services or the father’s permission for them to breastfeed for payment. Rather, since the father is obliged to provide for his child’s nursing, a woman who nurses the child becomes entitled to payment simply by doing so.

Regarding a divorced mother, this is the position of most jurists: they hold that she becomes entitled to payment simply by breastfeeding. Abū Hanīfa also holds this view concerning the mother, although he does not hold that someone who pays another person’s debt is entitled to reimbursement. His two disciples disagreed with him.

One who distinguishes between the two cases argues: “A mother has a greater right than anyone else to breastfeed her son. Even if she asks to breastfeed for payment, she takes precedence over a woman who offers to do so gratuitously.”

The response is: “Likewise, someone who has a right over another person’s livestock, such as a lessee or pledgee, is entitled to demand that the owner provide for their upkeep. Such a person has an even stronger claim than the mother has to breastfeed.”

Furthermore, being entitled to payment under a contract of exchange does not necessarily entail being entitled to it without a contract, unless the father is obliged to provide for the nursing. And if he only

---

## Page 15158

pays it because it is incumbent upon him, then the same applies to all his obligations: he must reimburse whoever fulfils them on his behalf and thereby does him a kindness. This applies when the payer acts voluntarily; how much more so when he is compelled to pay what is due from him? For if he does not give a powerful oppressor what he demands, the oppressor will inflict great harm upon him, either through physical punishment or by taking an even greater amount. In that case, he must pay what will avert the greater of the two harms by accepting the lesser. If someone else pays on his behalf without being compelled, that person is entitled to recover what he paid from him. How much more so if he is compelled to pay on his behalf!

Furthermore, when the demand is made of all the partners, it has already been explained that none of them may refuse to pay his share in a way that results in greater oppression of the others. Payment then becomes obligatory upon all the partners: each must pay the share that falls to him when the amount demanded is divided fairly among them.

Anyone who pays another’s share without being compelled is entitled to recover it from him. He has done him a kindness by paying on his behalf and dealing directly with the oppressors in his place. The person who deals with them directly suffers both personal and financial harm, whereas the other generally suffers financial harm alone. If he pays on the other’s behalf so that the latter need not appear in person, he has thereby done him a kindness. The latter must therefore reimburse him, just as one repays a lender who has done one a kindness; for a loan is recompensed with repayment and praise.

Anyone who remains absent and does not pay, until those present pay on his behalf, must reimburse them for the amount they paid for him. He is to be compelled to do so and punished if he refuses to pay. It is lawful for those who paid on his behalf to take an equivalent amount from his property, just as

---

## Page 15159

a lender takes from the borrower the equivalent of what he lent him. If someone collects this amount from the person on whose behalf payment was made and delivers it to the payer, it is lawful to accept it, whether payment was enforced by the original oppressor or someone else. Accordingly, the payer may bring a claim for what he paid on the other’s behalf before just judges. They must order that person to reimburse him, just as they would order him to repay a loan. There is nothing dubious about accepting reimbursement for one’s money.

It should not be said, “He has taken people’s money,” for he has merely recovered from them what he paid on their behalf, repayment of the money he lent them, and reimbursement for what they were obliged to pay. As explained above, no partner may refuse to pay his share when he knows that it will be taken from the other partners. Since he has no right to refuse, payment is obligatory upon him. Whoever voluntarily pays on his behalf, intending to recover the amount, is entitled to recover it, because he has done him a kindness by paying for him. How much more so if he was compelled to pay on his behalf!

Even if payment were not obligatory upon the person concerned, but another man were compelled to pay on his behalf, that man would be entitled to recover it from him, for it was because of him that the other was coerced and his money taken. This is like someone subjected to a monetary exaction whose relatives, neighbours, friends, or partners are compelled to pay on his behalf and seek reimbursement from him. They are entitled to reimbursement, for their money was taken only because of him and to avert the demand from him.

For the person taking money from him either does so because he believes him to have acted unjustly—as when rulers exact money from some of their deputies, saying: “They have taken more money than

---

## Page 15160

was exacted from them”—or because the person possesses considerable wealth, and the claimant demands from him what he says is the share payable on his wealth.

His relatives, neighbours, friends, and others whose money was taken because of his wealth or his actions were wronged only on his account. Their money was taken because of his wealth and to preserve it. The claimant seeks his wealth, not theirs; his supposed justification and his intent concern that man’s wealth, not theirs. How, then, can their money be lost without compensation, through no fault of their own, while this man’s wealth remains protected, when it was on his account that demands were made upon them?

If those who pay on behalf of others were not entitled to reimbursement, considerable harm would befall lives and property. Lives and property may be exposed to harm and ruin that can be averted only by making a payment on someone’s behalf. If those making such payments knew that they would not be entitled to recover what they paid unless the person concerned gave permission, they would not pay. Yet he might not give permission because he is absent, imprisoned, or otherwise prevented; or because he wrongs himself and persists in conduct harmful to himself and his property, out of foolishness and wrongdoing that the Lawgiver has forbidden him.

It is well known that people are subject to the commands of Allah and His Messenger. No one may therefore inflict upon himself or his property any harm that Allah has forbidden. Whoever averts such grave harm from him by a lesser harm has done him a kindness. It is inherent in the nature of all people that whoever fails to repay kindness with kindness is unjust and transgresses. Whatever the Muslims consider injustice is indeed injustice. As Ibn Masʿūd, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “Whatever the Muslims deem good is, in the sight of

---

## Page 15161

Allah, good; and whatever they deem bad is, in the sight of Allah, bad.”

The underlying principle here is that purposes and intentions must be taken into account in people’s dealings. This principle has been established and explained at length in the book Bayān al-Dalīl ‘alā Butlān al-Tahlīl.

Consider what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning Ibn al-Lutbiyya, the official who accepted gifts. When the Prophet appointed him to collect zakat, gifts were given to him. Upon his return, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called him to account for what he had received and disbursed—a procedure that officials of the financial administration call auditing. This is like a person calling his agent or business partner to account for his receipts and expenditure, which officials of the financial administration call collections and disbursements. Ibn al-Lutbiyya said, “This is for you, and this was given to me as a gift.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“What is the matter with a man whom we appoint to perform some duty entrusted to us by Allah, who then says, ‘This is for you, and this was given to me as a gift’? Why did he not sit in his father’s and mother’s house and see whether he would be given gifts or not? By Him in whose hand is my soul, any man whom we appoint to a duty who misappropriates anything from it will come on the Day of Resurrection carrying it on his neck—whether a camel grunting, a cow lowing, or a sheep bleating.”

He then raised his hands towards heaven and said, “Take heed! Have I conveyed the message?”—or words to that effect, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The hadith’s authenticity is agreed upon.

Since those who gave him money and gifts did so only because of his office, those gifts were counted among the funds rightfully belonging to the recipients of zakat, because it was on account of

---

## Page 15162

their funds that it was received, and it was not reserved for the collector who received it. Likewise, whatever is received on account of someone’s assets must be accounted for as part of those assets, for it is incidental to them. Just as whatever is given on their account constitutes a gain and an increase belonging to them, rather than to the recipient, so whatever is taken on their account constitutes a charge and a reduction borne by those assets, rather than personally by the one who paid it.

Likewise, anyone who saves another person’s property from loss by making a payment on his behalf is entitled to reimbursement from him. This includes someone who saves property from brigands, an oppressive army, or an oppressive official, and can only secure its release by making such a payment. He is entitled to recover that payment, having done the owner a kindness—even if he was neither entrusted with the property nor compelled to make the payment on the owner’s behalf. He has nevertheless done him a kindness, and is there any reward for good other than good?

If he saves ten thousand dirhams by paying one thousand on the owner’s behalf, he has acted benevolently. When the owner gives him the thousand, he has merely repaid his loan; his work and effort in saving the property remain an act of kindness for which he has not been recompensed. This is the more correct of the two scholarly opinions. Whoever treats him in such a case as having made a gratuitous donation and awards him nothing has uttered something reprehensible and false, and has repaid kindness with mistreatment.

Whoever says, “This is the revealed law with which Allah sent His Messenger,” has spoken untruthfully about Allah. Rather, it is the opinion of some scholars, while others have disagreed with them. Attributing such opinions to the revealed law causes many people to think ill of it, turn away from it, and disparage its adherents. For some scholars have expressed a view based on their own judgment

---

## Page 15163

and others have disagreed with him, while he has no law revealed by Allah to support his view. Indeed, the evidence of the revealed law may indicate the opposite of what he says. It may happen that someone who judges according to that view compounds the injustice through his own ignorance and wrongdoing. It may also happen that all those given to injustice and evil compound the evil and attribute all this injustice to the law of the Messenger whom Allah declared free of injustice and sent with justice, wisdom, and mercy, making pure justice, free of all injustice, his law. Thus, justice and his law are inseparable.

Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to return trusts to their rightful owners and, when you judge between people, to judge with justice.”

And He Most High says:

“So if they come to you, judge between them or turn away from them. If you turn away from them, they will not harm you at all. But if you judge, judge between them with justice. Indeed, Allah loves those who act justly.”

And He Most High says:

“So judge between them according to what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires, turning away from the truth that has come to you.”

What was revealed to him and justice are therefore inseparable: there is never any injustice in what Allah revealed to him. Indeed, He Most High says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance so that people might uphold justice. And We sent down iron, in which there is great might and benefits for people, so that Allah may know who supports Him and His messengers unseen. Indeed, Allah is All-Strong, Almighty.”

Allah knows best. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah’s blessings and peace be upon our master Muhammad, his family, and his Companions. Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.

---

## Page 15164

The shaykh—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

About a man who holds administrative offices and iqtāʿ grants, which are subject to the customary levies imposed by the ruler. He wishes to eliminate all injustice and strives to do so to the best of his ability. He knows that if he relinquishes these positions and someone else receives the grants and is appointed in his place, none of the injustice will be removed; rather, it may even increase. He can reduce the levies imposed within his grant by remitting half of them. The other half, however, is allocated to expenditures for which he cannot cancel the levies, since he would then be required to provide replacement funds. He is unable to do that and cannot avert these demands.

Is it permissible for someone in this situation to retain his office and grant, given his known intentions, his efforts, and the injustice he has removed to the best of his ability? Or must he relinquish this office and grant, even though, if he withdraws, the injustice will not disappear but will remain and increase? Is he therefore permitted to retain his office and grant as described? Does he incur any sin by doing so, or not? If he incurs no sin, can he nevertheless be held accountable for it, or not?

Which course is better for him: to remain while striving to remove and reduce injustice, or to withdraw while the injustice remains and increases? If the subjects prefer him to remain in charge because of the benefit he brings them and the injustice he has removed, is it preferable

---

## Page 15165

for him to comply with the subjects’ wishes? Or should he withdraw, although the subjects dislike this, knowing that the injustice will remain and increase if he withdraws?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

Yes. If he strives to uphold justice and remove injustice to the best of his ability, and his administration is better and more beneficial for the Muslims than that of someone else, and his control of the grant is better than someone else’s, as described, then he may retain his office and grant without incurring any sin. Indeed, retaining them is better than relinquishing them if he would not devote himself to something better upon relinquishing them. It may even be obligatory for him to remain if no one else capable of fulfilling this responsibility undertakes it.

Establishing justice and removing injustice, to the extent possible, is a collective obligation. Each person must do whatever he can if no one else takes his place in doing so. In these circumstances, he is not required to remove injustice that he is unable to remove. Nor is he held responsible for levies imposed by rulers that he cannot abolish.

If the rulers and their deputies demand sums that can only be paid by retaining some of those levies, and if withholding payment would lead them to transfer those grants and that office to someone who would maintain or increase the injustice rather than reduce it, then collecting those levies and paying the proceeds to them is better for the Muslims than retaining all the levies. Whoever is turned away from these practices toward justice and beneficence is closer to them than others, while anyone touched by any of this is further removed from justice and beneficence

---

## Page 15166

than others. The holder of the grant who does this good removes as much injustice from the Muslims as he can and wards off the evildoer’s harm by collecting part of what he demands from them. As regards what he cannot abolish, he is doing good to the Muslims, not wronging them. He is rewarded and incurs no sin for what he collects under the circumstances described. He is not liable to repay what he has collected, nor does he incur any sin in this world or the Hereafter, provided that he strives for justice and beneficence to the best of his ability.

This is comparable to an orphan’s guardian, the administrator of a charitable endowment, the managing partner in a profit-sharing investment, a business partner, or anyone else who manages another’s affairs through guardianship or agency. If he cannot secure their interests without paying some of their wealth to a powerful oppressor, he is doing good by doing so, not wrong. Examples include what such people pay to collectors of oppressive tolls and others along the roads, in connection with the ashwāl and the assets entrusted to them, as well as the prescribed levies they pay on real estate and on goods bought and sold. Anyone managing affairs for himself or another in these times, in these lands and others like them, must inevitably pay these levies. If this meant that no one could lawfully manage another’s affairs, people would suffer ruin and their interests would be lost.

If people heeded someone who forbids this in order to prevent a little injustice, the injustice and harm they suffered would multiply. His case is like that of people travelling along a road who are confronted by highway robbers: unless they appease them with some of their wealth, the robbers will seize all their wealth and kill them.

Thus, whoever says to that caravan:

“It is not lawful for you to give these men any of the wealth

---

## Page 15167

that you are carrying for others,” seeks thereby to safeguard the small amount he forbids them to pay. Yet if they followed his advice, they would lose both that small amount and the rest, and would themselves be plundered besides. No sensible person would recommend this, much less would divine laws prescribe it. For Allah Most High sent the messengers to secure and enhance benefits, and to prevent and reduce harms as far as possible.

Thus, consider an officeholder who holds a grant and, while retaining his office and grant, uses the levies collected and remitted to the person with whom he is affiliated to ward off far greater injustice and evil from the Muslims. If he cannot avert that harm except by these means, and his withdrawal would allow someone else to take over who would maintain the injustice without reducing it at all, then he is rewarded for doing this. He incurs neither sin nor liability, in this world or the Hereafter.

His case is like that of an orphan’s guardian or the administrator of a charitable endowment who cannot safeguard the interests entrusted to him without paying the unjust levies remitted to the authorities. If his withdrawal would allow someone unjust, who deliberately seeks to oppress, to take over, then his holding that position is lawful, and he incurs no sin for what he pays. Indeed, assuming that responsibility may become obligatory for him.

The same applies to a soldier who holds a grant and reduces the levies imposed on his lands but cannot eliminate them altogether, because he is required to provide horses, weapons, and expenditure that he cannot meet without collecting some of those levies. Despite this, he benefits the Muslims in

---

## Page 15168

jihad. If he is told, “It is not lawful for you to take any of this; rather, relinquish this grant,” and he relinquishes it, only for someone intent on injustice who brings no benefit to the Muslims to take it over, then the person who told him this is mistaken and ignorant of the realities of the religion. Rather, it is better for the Muslims that cavalrymen among the Turks and Arabs who are better than others, more beneficial to the Muslims, and closer to justice should retain their grants while reducing injustice as far as possible, than that those grants should pass to people who are less beneficial and more unjust.

Whoever among all these grant holders strives to uphold justice and do good as far as possible will be rewarded by Allah for the good he does. Allah will not punish him for what is beyond his ability, nor hold him accountable for what he collects and disburses, if there is no alternative [and] (1) abandoning it would bring about a greater evil. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I could not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Protecting the Collected Fatwas from Omissions and Scribal Errors).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 15169

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

A man’s property was taken unjustly and without right, his honour was violated, or he suffered bodily harm. He did not seek retribution in this world, knowing that what is with Allah is better and more lasting. Would pardoning his oppressor cause him to forfeit his reward with Allah, or diminish it? Or would it do neither, leaving his reward intact, full and undiminished? Which is preferable: demanding redress from this oppressor and exacting retribution from him on the Day of Resurrection, with Allah punishing him, or pardoning him and accepting that Allah Most High will recompense him instead?

He replied:

Pardoning the oppressor, even in the slightest degree, neither forfeits nor diminishes the wronged person’s reward with Allah. Rather, pardoning the oppressor makes Allah Most High the one who recompenses him. If he does not pardon, his claim remains against the oppressor, and he may exact retribution commensurate with the wrong he suffered. But if he pardons and brings about reconciliation, his reward is with Allah. The reward he receives from Allah is better and more lasting.

Allah Most High says:

“The recompense for an evil deed is an evil deed like it. But whoever pardons and brings about reconciliation—his reward is with Allah. Indeed, He does not love wrongdoers.”

He thus states that the recompense for an evil deed is its equivalent, without transgression. This is just retribution for wrongs involving life and limb, property, honour, and the like.

Then He says:

---

## Page 15170

“But whoever pardons and brings about reconciliation—his reward is with Allah.”

It is related that when Imam Ahmad was wronged during his well-known ordeal, he did not leave until he had absolved those who had wronged him. He said:

“I recalled a report related from Mubārak ibn Fadāla, from al-Hasan, who said: ‘On the Day of Resurrection, a caller will proclaim: Let those whose reward is assured with Allah rise! None will rise except those who pardoned and brought about reconciliation.’”

Allah Most High says:

“If you retaliate, then retaliate only to the extent that you have suffered. But if you are patient, that is certainly better for those who are patient.”

He, glorified and exalted be He, permits them, if they punish the wrongdoer, to punish him only to the extent that he has punished them. Then He says:

“But if you are patient, that is certainly better for those who are patient.”

It is thus clear that patiently refraining from punishing him in kind is better than punishing him. How, then, could it forfeit or diminish one’s reward? Allah Most High has said:

“And wounds are subject to equal retribution. But whoever forgoes it as charity, it will be an expiation for him.”

He has thus made the charitable relinquishment of the retribution due from the wrongdoer—that is, pardoning him rather than exacting retribution—an expiation for the one who pardons. Exacting retribution, however, is not an expiation for him. It is therefore clear that pardoning is better for him than exacting retribution.

This is because the afflictions that befall a person expiate sins. The servant is rewarded for bearing them patiently, and his rank is raised through his contentment with the afflictions Allah decrees for him.

Allah Most High says:

“No affliction befalls except by Allah’s permission. And whoever believes in Allah—He will guide his heart.”

One of the early generations said: “This is a man whom an affliction befalls: he knows that it is from Allah, so he is content and submits.” And in the two Sahīh collections, it is reported from the Prophet

---

## Page 15171

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that he said:

“No illness, fatigue, anxiety, sorrow, distress or harm befalls a believer—not even the prick of a thorn—without Allah expiating some of his sins thereby.”

It is related in al-Musnad:

“When Allah Most High’s words, ‘Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it,’ were revealed, Abū Bakr said: ‘O Messenger of Allah, a back-breaking revelation has come down! Which of us has not done evil?’ He replied: ‘O Abū Bakr, do you not grow weary? Do you not grieve? Does hardship not afflict you? That is how you are recompensed.’”

It also contains the report:

“Afflictions remove sins from the person who suffers them, just as a standing tree sheds its leaves.”

There is abundant evidence that afflictions expiate sins. If a person bears them patiently, he is rewarded for his patience. Reward and recompense are only for the deed—namely, patience. The affliction itself, however, is an act of Allah, not an act of the servant. It is part of Allah’s recompense to the servant for his sin and His expiation of that sin through it.

It is related in al-Musnad:

“They visited Abū ‘Ubayda ibn al-Jarrāh while he was ill and mentioned that he would be rewarded for his illness. He said: ‘I receive no reward—not even this much. Rather, afflictions remove sins.’”

Abū ‘Ubayda—may Allah be pleased with him—thus explained to them that illness itself does not earn a person reward; rather, it expiates his sins. The word ‘reward’ is often understood to mean the forgiveness of sins, so there is reward in this

---

## Page 15172

sense.

Some people say that this must involve compensation, reward, and gracious favor. A person may receive reward without doing any deed himself, as when righteous deeds are performed on his behalf.

As for patiently bearing afflictions, there is great reward in this. Allah Most High says:

“Give good tidings to the patient, who, when an affliction befalls them, say, ‘Indeed, we belong to Allah, and indeed, to Him we will return.’ Upon them are blessings from their Lord and mercy, and it is they who are rightly guided.”

If someone is wronged by being wounded or the like, and he forgoes redress as an act of charity, the wound is an affliction through which his sins are expiated. He is rewarded for his patience and for his kindness to the wrongdoer in pardoning him. Doing good may take the form of bringing benefit or warding off harm; this is why Allah calls it charity.

Allah Most High also says:
