“Hasten to forgiveness from your Lord and a Garden as wide as the heavens and the earth, prepared for the righteous—those who spend in ease and hardship, restrain their anger, and pardon people. And Allah loves those who do good.”

He thus states that He loves those who do good and those who pardon people. This makes clear that pardoning others is a form of doing good.

Doing good is the opposite of doing evil. It means performing a good deed, whether its benefit is confined to the doer or extends to others. This includes His words:

“Whoever comes with a good deed will have ten times its like, and whoever comes with an evil deed will be recompensed only with its like.”

The person who restrains his anger and pardons people has done good both to himself and to others, for he has performed a good deed toward himself and toward others. Whoever does good to others does good to himself.

As it is related that one of the early generations said:

“I have done good to no one, nor have I done harm to anyone

---

## Page 15173

, but have only done good to myself and harm to myself.”

Allah Most High says:

“If you do good, you do good for yourselves; and if you do evil, it is against yourselves.”

He also says:

“Whoever does righteousness, it is for his own benefit; and whoever does evil, it is to his own detriment.”

If doing good to others were not also doing good to the benefactor himself, bringing benefit back to him, he would be committing a sin or doing harm. For an action that brings its doer no benefit either offers no benefit at all or, if it harms its doer, is worse than futility.

Pardoning a wrongdoer is one of the two forms of charity—that is, kindness and doing good to people. Zakat encompasses all of this. Allah, glorified is He, repeatedly commands prayer and zakat, which is charity. It is established in authentic reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through more than one chain of transmission, that he said:

“Every act of kindness is charity.”

This takes two forms: bringing someone benefit and warding off harm from him. If a wronged person has the right to exact punishment for the injustice he has suffered, and feels inclined to do so, but restrains himself and spares the wrongdoer the harm he is inclined to inflict, this is an act of kindness toward him and charity bestowed upon him. Allah Most High rewards those who give charity and does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste. How, then, could the reward of the one who pardons be forfeited?

This applies generally to all the rights a person has over others. Thus, when

---

## Page 15174

Allah mentions people’s rights in His Book and speaks of justice concerning them, He also encourages graciousness in these matters, for He, glorified is He, commands justice and graciousness. As Allah Most High says:

“If the debtor is in hardship, grant him time until a time of ease. But to remit the debt as charity is better for you, if you only knew.”

Thus, He makes giving charity to an insolvent debtor by cancelling his debt better for the giver than merely allowing him more time.

Allah Most High also says:

“Whoever kills a believer by mistake must free a believing slave and pay blood money to the deceased’s family, unless they waive it as charity.”

He thus calls waiving the blood money charity.

Allah Most High also says:

“If you divorce them before you have consummated the marriage, but after you have stipulated a dower for them, then half of what you stipulated is due, unless they waive it or the one in whose hand is the marriage bond waives it. And to waive is nearer to righteousness.”

Thus, He makes waiving the half of the dower owed by the husband upon divorce before consummation nearer to righteousness than collecting it. The woman’s waiver means relinquishing half the dower, by the consensus of the Muslim community.

As for the waiver of the one in whose hand is the marriage bond, one view is that this means the husband’s waiver, whereby he gives the woman her full dower. On this interpretation, this waiver is of the same kind as the other: this one consists in giving the whole amount, while the other consists in relinquishing the whole amount. Those who held this view were led to it by their judgment that no one other than the woman has the authority to waive what is rightfully owed to her, just as no one else has the authority to cancel any other debts owed to her.

Another view is that the one in whose hand is the marriage bond is the woman’s guardian who has independent authority to conclude the marriage contract without seeking her permission:

---

## Page 15175

such as a father in the case of his young virgin daughter, or a master in the case of his slave woman. On this interpretation, both acts of waiver are of the same kind. This is why He did not say, “unless the women waive it or they [the husbands] waive it,” since the verse addresses the husbands directly.

Allah Most High relates that Luqmān said to his son:

“Enjoin what is right, forbid what is wrong, and patiently endure whatever befalls you. That is a matter requiring firm resolve.”

Allah Most High also says:

“No blame attaches to those who defend themselves after being wronged. Blame attaches only to those who wrong people and transgress on earth without right. They will have a painful punishment. But whoever is patient and forgives—that is indeed a matter requiring firm resolve.”

There, in Luqmān’s words, He mentioned patience in adversity, saying:

“That is a matter requiring firm resolve.”

Here, He mentioned patience and forgiveness, saying:

“That is indeed a matter requiring firm resolve.”

He mentioned this after saying:

“No blame attaches to those who defend themselves after being wronged. Blame attaches only to those who wrong people and transgress on earth without right.”

Thus, glorified is He, He mentioned three categories of people in relation to wrongdoing inflicted without the victim’s consent: the just, the wrongdoer, and the gracious.

The just person is one who defends himself after being wronged. His recompense is that no blame attaches to him: he is not praised for doing so, but neither is he censured.

He mentioned the wrongdoer in His words:

“Blame attaches only to those who wrong people and transgress on earth without right.”

Such people are liable to punishment and retribution. And He mentioned those who act graciously

---

## Page 15176

saying:

“But whoever is patient and forgives—that is indeed a matter requiring firm resolve.”

The Quran expresses comprehensive meanings in few words.

Similarly, near the end of Sūrat al-Baqarah, He mentions three categories of people in transactions undertaken by the parties’ own choice: the gracious, the unjust, and the just. The gracious person gives charity; the unjust person takes usury; and the just person sells. Thus, He sets out the rulings governing charity, usury, sales, and debt transactions.

Anyone who imagines that forgiving forfeits or diminishes his right is mistaken, ignorant, and misguided; rather, forgiveness makes his reward greater. Likewise, anyone who imagines that forgiving brings him humiliation while granting the wrongdoer honor and dominance over him is mistaken. As is established in the Sahīh and elsewhere, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There are three things I would certainly swear to: Allah only increases a servant in honor through his forgiving others; charity does not diminish wealth; and no one humbles himself before Allah but Allah raises him.”

Thus, the truthful and trustworthy Prophet made clear that Allah only increases a servant in honor through his forgiving others, that charity does not diminish wealth, and that no one humbles himself before Allah but Allah raises him. This refutes the assumptions of those who follow conjecture and their own desires: that forgiveness humiliates them, charity diminishes their wealth, and humility lowers their standing.

In the two Sahīh collections, ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, said:

“No blow was ever struck by

---

## Page 15177

the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, against a servant of his, a woman, an animal, or anything whatsoever, except when fighting in Allah’s cause. Nor did he ever avenge himself for any wrong done to him, unless Allah’s sacred limits were violated. When those limits were violated, nothing could withstand his anger until he had exacted retribution for Allah’s sake.”

The character of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was the Quran—the most perfect character. Among his qualities was that he never avenged himself; yet when Allah’s sacred limits were violated, nothing could withstand his anger until he had exacted retribution for Allah’s sake. Thus, he would forgo his own rights while upholding his Lord’s rights in full.

People fall into four categories in this regard:

Some stand up both for themselves and for their Lord. Such a person has both religious commitment and a temper. Others stand up neither for themselves nor for their Lord. Such a person is ignorant and weak in religious commitment. Others avenge themselves but not their Lord’s rights; these are the worst category. The person of complete virtue, however, stands up for Allah’s rights while forgoing his own.

As Anas ibn Mālik said:

“I served the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for ten years, and he never once said ‘Ugh!’ to me. He never asked me about anything I had done, ‘Why did you do it?’ Nor did he ask me about anything I had not done, ‘Why did you not do it?’ If someone in his household reproached me for something, he would say, ‘Leave him be. Had something been decreed, it would have happened.’”

This concerns his forgiveness in matters relating to his own rights.

---

## Page 15178

As for Allah’s prescribed punishments, however:

“When Usāmah ibn Zayd—his beloved, the son of his beloved, who was dearer to him than Anas and held in greater esteem—interceded with him on behalf of a noblewoman who had stolen, asking him to spare her hand from amputation, he became angry and said, ‘Usāmah, are you interceding concerning one of Allah’s prescribed punishments? What destroyed those before you was that, when a noble person among them stole, they let him go, but when a lowly person stole, they carried out the prescribed punishment on him. By Him in whose hand is Muhammad’s soul, if Fātimah, Muhammad’s daughter, stole, I would cut off her hand.’”

Thus, he became angry with Usāmah when he interceded concerning a punishment prescribed by Allah, yet pardoned Anas in matters concerning his own rights. Likewise:

“When Usāmah told him that he had killed a man after the man had said, ‘There is no god but Allah,’ he said, ‘Did you kill him after he had said, “There is no god but Allah”?’ He kept repeating this until I said, ‘If only he would stop!’”

There are very many hadiths and reports concerning the merit of pardoning a wrongdoer and the greater reward this brings. This is knowledge firmly rooted in human nature.

Allah Most High said to His Prophet:

“Accept what is freely given, enjoin what is right, and turn away from the ignorant.”

He thus commanded him to accept what came readily from people’s character, meaning such conduct as could be accepted. Ibn al-Zubayr said:

“Allah commanded His Prophet to accept what came readily from people’s character.”

This is like His saying:

“They ask you what they should spend. Say, ‘Whatever is surplus’”—

that is, of their wealth. This is part of accepting what is freely given; he is also to enjoin what is right and turn away from the ignorant. This verse encompasses all noble qualities of character. For, in a person’s dealings with others, they may act toward him otherwise than

---

## Page 15179

he would like, or do what he dislikes. He is therefore commanded to accept whatever pleasing conduct they willingly offer, without demanding more from them. If they treat him in a way he dislikes, he should turn away from them. For his part, he should enjoin them to do what is right. This is a broad subject.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man is owed a debt by another who denies it, or has something wrongfully taken from him. He then gains access to property belonging to that person of the same kind as his own. May he take from it an amount equivalent to what he is owed?

He answered:

As for someone who has a claim against another, whether to specific property or to a debt, may he take that property or its equivalent without the other person’s permission?

There are two cases:

The first is where the basis of entitlement is evident and requires no proof, such as a wife’s right to maintenance from her husband, a child’s right to maintenance from his father, or a guest’s right to hospitality from his host. In such cases, he may undoubtedly take what he is entitled to without the permission of the person who owes it. Thus, it is established in the two Sahīh collections that Hind bint ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah said:

“O Messenger of Allah, Abū Sufyān is a stingy man, and he does not give me enough maintenance for myself and my children.”

He replied:

“Take what is reasonably sufficient for you and your children.”

He thus permitted her to take a reasonable amount for her maintenance without the permission of the person responsible for providing it.

---

## Page 15180

Likewise, someone who knows that his property was wrongfully taken in a manifest manner known to others may recover the property itself or its equivalent from the usurper’s property. The same applies if he has a debt claim before the judge, but the debtor delays payment, so he takes an equivalent amount from the debtor’s property, and to similar cases.

The second case is where the basis of entitlement is not evident—for example, where the other person denies the debt or denies having wrongfully taken the property, and the claimant has no evidence.

There are two views concerning this. The first is that he may not take anything; this is the position of Mālik and Ahmad. The second is that he may take what he is owed; this is the position of al-Shāfiʿī. As for Abū Hanīfah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—he permits taking property of the same kind as that which is owed, because this constitutes recovery of one’s due. He does not permit taking property of a different kind, because that constitutes an exchange, which is permissible only with the debtor’s consent.

Those who permit this say that, if the debtor refuses to discharge his obligation, the exchange becomes permissible without his consent because of the need.

However, those who prohibit taking property when the entitlement is not evident cite the report in the Sunan collections from Abū Hurayrah that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Return what has been entrusted to you to the one who entrusted it to you, and do not betray the one who betrays you.”

The Musnad also records that Bashīr ibn al-Khasāsiyah said:

“O Messenger of Allah, our neighbours take anything of ours that strays or is on its own. If we gain access to something of theirs, may we take it?

---

## Page 15181

He replied, ‘No. Return what has been entrusted to you to the one who entrusted it to you, and do not betray the one who betrays you.’”

The Sunan collections also report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked, “The zakat collectors take more from us than is due. May we conceal an amount of our wealth equal to the excess they take from us?” He replied, “No.” This was narrated by Abū Dāwūd and others.

These hadiths show that, when the grounds for a wronged person’s actual entitlement are not evident, [and] (1) he takes what he is owed through a breach of trust, he is not permitted to do so, even if he intends only to take the equivalent of his due. He has nevertheless betrayed the person who entrusted him: when that person handed over his property, he took some of it without permission, although his entitlement was not evident. He has therefore committed a breach of trust.

If he says, “I am in fact entitled to what I have taken,” his claim is still not evident and known. His situation is like that of a man who marries a woman, but she denies the marriage and he has no evidence. He is not permitted to force her to have intercourse without manifest proof of the marriage. Likewise, suppose a judge ruled that a man had divorced his wife, relying on evidence he believed to be truthful but which was in fact false. The man would not be permitted to have intercourse with her on the grounds of the true state of affairs.

If it is objected, “There is no doubt that he must be prevented from doing this on the basis of the apparent facts, and that he may not do it openly before others, since they are commanded to object to it because it is outwardly forbidden. But what if the true state of affairs is known privately, between him and Allah?”

The answer is that doing this secretly entails many prohibited harms. For if he does

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I could not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Protecting Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā against Omissions and Misreadings).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 15182

so, it is liable to become apparent and widely known. Moreover, care must be taken that those whose actual circumstances differ from his do not imitate him. A person may think that his conduct will remain hidden, yet bring about many harms and also open the door to interpretive justifications.

His situation is then like that of a wronged person who can obtain redress only by committing injustice, such as someone who cannot exact retribution without transgressing: he is not permitted to exact it. This is because breach of trust is inherently forbidden, so one may not recover one’s due through it. Likewise, if someone forced him to drink wine, committed sodomy with him, or testified falsely against him, he would not be permitted to do the same in return, because such acts are inherently forbidden. Breach of trust belongs to the same category as lying.

If it is objected, “This is not a breach of trust; rather, it is the recovery of a right. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited betraying someone who has betrayed one, meaning taking from his property an amount to whose equivalent one is entitled.”

The answer is that this argument is weak for several reasons.

First, the hadith includes the question, “Some people take every last thing of ours. May we take from their property an amount equal to what they take?” He replied, “No. Return what has been entrusted to you to the one who entrusted it to you, and do not betray the one who betrays you.”

Similarly, in the hadith about zakat, the question was, “May we conceal an amount of our wealth equal to what they take from us?” He replied, “No.”

Second, he said, “Do not betray the one who betrays you.” If by betrayal he had meant taking in retaliation, he would not have distinguished between someone who had betrayed him and someone who had not; and the prohibition of such

---

## Page 15183

conduct is self-evident and requires no explanation or inquiry. Yet he said:

“Do not betray the one who betrays you.”

It is therefore clear that he meant that you must not respond to someone’s betrayal by doing to him what he did to you. If a man is entrusted with money and betrays its owner with respect to part of it, and the owner is subsequently entrusted with an equivalent amount and treats him in the same way, this is what is meant by his words:

“Do not betray the one who betrays you.”

Third, there is no doubt that this constitutes betrayal. The question is only whether it is permissible by way of retaliation. For some acts, retaliation is permissible, as in cases of killing, highway robbery, and the taking of property. For others, retaliation is not permissible, such as acts of indecency, lying, and the like. Concerning the former, Allah Most High says:

“The recompense of an evil deed is an evil like it.”

He also says:

“If you retaliate, then retaliate with the like of what you were made to suffer.”

And He says:

“Whoever transgresses against you, transgress against him in the same measure as he transgressed against you.”

Thus, He permitted punishment and retaliation in equal measure. But when he said here, “Do not betray the one who betrays you,” it became clear that this belongs to those acts for which punishment in kind is not permissible.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who owed a debt and had merchandise in his creditor’s possession. The price owed was seventy dirhams, while the merchandise was worth ninety dirhams. The debtor had died, and his assets had been secured. The creditor wished to inform the heirs of the merchandise

---

## Page 15184

but feared that they would take it without paying him what he was owed. Yet if he concealed it, he would remain burdened with the sin of neglecting it, and he feared being required to make restitution in something other than the merchandise?

He replied:

He should sell it and recover from the proceeds whatever the deceased owed him in wages and payment for goods, then deliver the remainder to those entitled to the deceased’s estate. If they require him to swear an oath, he may swear, “I hold nothing else belonging to him.” If he wishes, he may purchase merchandise like the original merchandise and swear that nothing else in his possession is due to the deceased, provided that the merchandise is equivalent to the original or better.

He was asked:

A man’s property was wrongfully seized, or repayment of a debt owed to him was unjustly delayed, and then he died. Will the right to claim redress in the Hereafter belong to him or to his heirs? Give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.

He replied:

If a person’s property was wrongfully seized, or payment owed to him was unjustly delayed, the right to claim redress in the Hereafter belongs to him.

As authentically reported, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever has wronged his brother in a matter involving blood, property, or honour should seek his pardon before a day comes when there will be neither dinars nor dirhams. If he has good deeds, some of his good deeds will be taken from him; if he has no good deeds, some of the wronged person’s sins will be taken and placed upon him.”

---

## Page 15185

Thus, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—made it clear that when a wrong concerns property, the wronged person himself will seek redress from the wrongdoer; he did not assign that claim to his heirs. This is because the heirs’ position in this world differs from his: whatever can be recovered in this world belongs to the heirs, while whatever cannot be recovered in this world will be claimed in the Hereafter by the wronged person himself. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Two buffaloes entered some people’s crops, and they hamstrung them, causing their deaths, although it may have been possible to drive them away without doing so. What are they liable for? What duties do livestock owners have to safeguard their animals, and crop owners to safeguard their crops?

He replied:

They may drive animals out of their crops only by the least harmful means necessary. If it was possible to remove the two buffaloes without hamstringing them, yet they hamstrung them, they must receive a discretionary punishment sufficient to deter them from such conduct, for unjustifiably torturing animals and violating other people’s property. They must also compensate the owner for the two animals.

Crop owners must safeguard their crops during the day, and livestock owners must safeguard their livestock at night, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—stated.

---

## Page 15186

He was asked:

Regarding unlawfully seized property, such as camels and other livestock, which increases while in the possession of the person who seized it: if he then repents, how should he dispose of the property? Is it unlawful or not?

He replied:

The fairest view on this matter is that the increase should be shared between the owner and the person tending the property, just as if the owner had entrusted it to someone to tend in return for a share of its increase. Then, if it is impossible to return the original property and the owner’s share to its owner, he should devote them to purposes benefiting the Muslims.

He was asked:

Regarding someone who unlawfully seized a sheep and then reached a mutually acceptable agreement with its owner: is it permissible to eat it?

He replied:

Yes. If he and its owner reach a mutually acceptable agreement, it is permissible to eat it.

---

## Page 15187

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A boy was holding a horse when an ostrich emerged from a stable and rushed at the horses. While the boy was holding the horse, it kicked one of two people who were sitting nearby, and he died. What liability falls upon the boy, and what liability falls upon the horse’s owner?

He replied:

If the horse kicked him with its hind leg, neither the boy nor the horse’s owner is liable. Rather, the horse remains its owner’s property. This is the position of the majority of the leading scholars, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad and others.

It is reported in the Sunan collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No liability is incurred for injuries caused by an animal’s hind leg.”

Al-Shāfiʿī held that a person riding, leading or driving the horse is liable for injuries it causes with its hind leg, just as Ahmad and others agreed with him regarding injuries caused by its foreleg.

However, if the boy holding the horse was not negligent, the scholars unanimously agree that he is not liable. An example is when the horse becomes startled and he warns those nearby, saying, “Watch out!” If he gives this warning, whichever of the two the horse kicks is himself at fault, and no one else bears liability, by agreement of the leading scholars. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15188

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A large camel was tethered in a pasture, beside a small, young male camel belonging to someone other than the owner of the large camel. The owners of both camels then went away, and the larger camel turned on the smaller one and killed it. What is the ruling?

He replied:

If the owner of the large camel was not negligent in restraining it—for example, if he had secured it with a tether sufficient to restrain it—he bears no liability. But if he was negligent by securing it with a light tether that would not restrain it, he must compensate for the damage it caused. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15189

Chapter on Pre-emption

Shaykh al-Islām Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

The imams agree that the right of pre-emption applies to real estate that admits of compulsory partition, such as a village, an orchard, and the like. They disagree, however, about property that does not admit of compulsory partition and can only be divided with resulting harm or a compensatory payment, thus requiring mutual consent. Does the right of pre-emption apply to such property? There are two views.

The first is that it does apply. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, and it was adopted by some followers of al-Shāfiʿī, such as Ibn Surayj, and a group of Ahmad’s followers, such as Abū al-Wafā ibn Aqīl. It is also al-Muhadhdhab’s report from Mālik. This is the correct view, as we shall explain, Allah willing.

The second is that the right of pre-emption does not apply to such property. This is al-Shāfiʿī’s own position and the view adopted by

---

## Page 15190

many of Ahmad’s followers.

Two arguments are advanced in support of this view.

The first is their contention that pre-emption was instituted only to avert the harm arising from the expense of partition, whereas property not subject to compulsory partition entails no such harm.

The second is that granting pre-emption over such property would harm the co-owner. If he sells his share, people will be reluctant to buy for fear of its being taken from them through pre-emption. If he requests partition, his request need not be granted. Thus, he can neither sell nor obtain partition, and so cannot escape the harm caused by his co-owner. Were we to establish pre-emption in order to avert harm from the co-owner who has not sold, this would necessarily harm the selling co-owner. Harm is not to be removed by causing harm.

The first view, however, is more correct. It is authentically reported in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever has a co-owner in land, a dwelling, or an orchard may not sell until he has informed his co-owner. The latter may choose to buy or decline. If he sells without informing him, his co-owner has the prior right to it.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not stipulate that the land, dwelling, or orchard must admit of partition. It is therefore impermissible to restrict the scope of the Messenger’s words without evidence from his own words, especially since he stated this when laying down the basis for the right of pre-emption. No authentic, explicit statement reported from him concerning pre-emption is more firmly established than this. In the two Sahīh collections, it is reported of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that “he ruled that the right of pre-emption applies to everything that has not been divided

---

## Page 15191

; once boundaries have been fixed and access routes separated, there is no right of pre-emption.” He thus excluded pre-emption only where boundaries have been established and access routes separated. This hadith is narrated from Jābir in the Sahīh.

The Sunan collections also report from him that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A neighbor has the prior right to acquire his neighbor’s property by pre-emption. He is to be waited for, even if absent, when they share a single access route.”

If he granted pre-emption on account of a shared access route, then granting it on account of co-ownership of the property itself is all the more appropriate.

Scholars have differed over a neighbor’s right of pre-emption, holding three views. The most balanced is this: if he shares in the rights attached to the property, he has the right of pre-emption; otherwise, he does not.

Moreover, it is evident that if the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, established pre-emption for property that admits of partition, then property that does not admit of partition is even more deserving of it. The harm associated with divisible property can be removed through partition, whereas the harm of co-ownership is more severe where partition is impossible.

The claim that pre-emption was established to avert partition, rather than the harm of co-ownership, is manifestly false. It is established by the revealed texts and scholarly consensus that when either co-owner requests partition of property that admits of it, his request must be granted. If the harm of co-ownership were greater, the lesser of the two harms would not be removed by incurring the greater. Allah and His Messenger have not required incurring a greater harm to avert a lesser one, for the law of

---

## Page 15192

Allah is far above anything of this kind.

As for their claim, “This entails harm to the selling co-owner,” the answer is that if he requests partition and the property itself cannot be divided, it is sold. Any co-owner who refuses to sell is compelled to do so, and the proceeds are divided between them.

This is the position of the majority of scholars, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfa, and Ahmad ibn Hanbal. Some Mālikī scholars have stated that there is scholarly consensus on this.

This is supported by the established report in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever emancipates his share in a slave and possesses enough wealth to cover the slave’s price must have the slave fairly valued, without undervaluation or overvaluation. He must then pay his co-owners their shares, and the slave becomes wholly free at his expense. Otherwise, only the share he emancipated becomes free.”

This hadith indicates that a co-owner is entitled to half the value of the whole, not the value of a half-share. For example, if the slave were sold as a whole, he would fetch a thousand dirhams, whereas a half-share in him would fetch less than five hundred dirhams. Yet the co-owner is entitled to half of the thousand.

The same applies to real estate that cannot be divided: the co-owner is entitled to half its value as a whole. Thus, if he requests this, the entire property is sold and he is given his share of the proceeds. In this way, the harm he suffers is removed. This demonstrates the consummate excellence of the revealed law and the benefits it secures for people in this life and the Hereafter. All praise belongs to Allah alone.

---

## Page 15193

He was asked:

A man jointly owned a property and needed to sell his share. Someone offered him a specified sum for it, and he sold it. He then said, “Weigh out for me the amount you offered,” but the buyer paid him less than the usual value. Is he obliged to pay that amount? Does the co-owner have a right of pre-emption?

He replied:

If he sold it for a specified price, the buyer must pay that price. If the sale was invalid and restitution is no longer possible, he must pay its fair market value. If the share is subject to pre-emption, the co-owner has the right to exercise it. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man purchased a share subject to pre-emption. Whenever the person entitled to pre-emption claimed it, the buyer presented the sale as having taken place without a legally sufficient inspection, so the judge annulled it. The buyer acknowledged that the seller bore no liability for the sum he had received, and the share was made an endowment for the buyer’s benefit—all to thwart pre-emption. Does this extinguish the right of pre-emption? And are these

---

## Page 15194

transactions valid?

He replied:

There is consensus that using legal stratagems to extinguish the right of pre-emption after it has become established is impermissible. The disagreement concerns only using such stratagems before that right becomes established but after its legal cause has arisen—that is, when the owner intends to sell a share subject to pre-emption. The correct view, however, is that it is impermissible to use stratagems to extinguish a Muslim’s right, and any transactions undertaken for the sake of such unlawful evasion are void.

As for the arrangements described—making it appear that the sale had been rescinded and the share returned to the seller, then presenting the seller as discharged from liability and the share as having been made an endowment—all of this is void. The share remains the buyer’s property, and the pre-emptor’s right to it remains established, unless he relinquishes it in a manner that extinguishes his right of pre-emption. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a share subject to pre-emption: it has been established that it was made an endowment, and that a judge ruled that the co-owner was entitled to pre-emption over it, but the co-owner has not established that he exercised that right. What is the ruling?

He replied:

The endowment is not invalidated unless it is established that the co-owner owns the endowed share subject to pre-emption, bearing in mind the scholarly disagreement concerning his acquisition of ownership. The judge’s ruling that he is entitled to pre-emption, by itself, does not invalidate the previously established endowment

---

## Page 15195

before that, just as it does not divest the buyer of ownership. Rather, the matter remains in abeyance: if the co-owner acquires the share by pre-emption, the prior disposition concerning it is invalidated, according to those who hold this view; otherwise, it remains valid.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a man who bought half a courtyard, while another man bought the other half and made his share an endowment before the first co-owner claimed pre-emption. The first co-owner then said, “I shall acquire it by pre-emption.” Is he entitled to do so?

He replied:

If the matter is as stated, he has no right of pre-emption, for the second buyer made his share an endowment, so it is not subject to pre-emption. The first buyer’s right of pre-emption has lapsed because, after learning of the sale, he delayed making his claim until the share had passed out of the buyer’s ownership through an endowment or otherwise. Thus, he has no right of pre-emption.

If, however, the buyer transferred it out of his ownership by selling it before the co-owner learned of the sale, the co-owner retains his right of pre-emption. As for an endowment or a gift, there is scholarly disagreement. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15196

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Concerning a man who co-owned a property with a witness. His co-owner then sold his share to another witness, ostensibly for a price far above its market value, while the two secretly agreed on the market price in order to defeat the right of pre-emption. Does the right of pre-emption lapse or not?

He replied:

It is unlawful to lie or resort to stratagems to nullify a Muslim’s right. If the co-owner requests it, the buyer must surrender the share subject to pre-emption at the price the two secretly agreed upon. If he denies him this, his religious integrity is called into question. The judge must uphold the right of pre-emption if he ascertains the true facts.

---

## Page 15197

Chapter on Deposits for Safekeeping

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man gave a broker some cloth to have stamped and sell. Unable to have it stamped, the broker deposited it with a trustworthy tailor with whom they customarily left goods for safekeeping. The owner of the cloth then came with another broker, and they collected it from the tailor while the broker who had deposited it was absent. The owner claimed that one piece of his cloth had gone missing while in their hands, but the broker denied this. Is the broker who had been entrusted with the goods liable for anything or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If they claim not to have received the goods entrusted for safekeeping, and the broker denies their claim, his word is accepted upon his oath, unless evidence admissible under Islamic law establishes the truth of their claim.

As for anything that has gone missing, if the broker was negligent by doing something for which he had neither express nor customary authorization, he is liable. But if it is their customary practice to leave goods with this trustworthy person, and the owners of the cloth know of this and approve of it, then the brokers bear no liability. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15198

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was also asked:

A man died, leaving two daughters and a wife, with one daughter absent. May the person overseeing the estate deposit the absent daughter’s property with someone when it is not known whether that person will safeguard it or use it for his own benefit?

If an unjust demand is made against the estate as a whole, should the cost of warding it off be borne solely by the absent daughter’s property, or by the entire estate?

If the property is entrusted to someone who might safeguard it or might use it for his own benefit, may its rightful owner pursue a claim against the person who took possession of it, or against the person who deposited it where its safety could not be assured? The overseer and the depositor have died; the property was sought among the depositor’s estate but was not found, and it is not known whether it was forcibly taken or not. Is the custodian’s liability discharged by leaving the matter as it stands, despite the possibility that he actually took possession of the property? Or should his representative from among his heirs pay that amount on his behalf from his charitable funds that were not earmarked for any particular purpose?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. This property came into his possession as a trust, so he must safeguard it as entrusted property should be safeguarded, and must not deposit it with anyone else except out of necessity. If he deposits it with someone who can reasonably be expected to safeguard it—such as a just judge, if one is available, or someone else—such that entrusting it to that person involves no negligence, then he bears no liability. But if he is negligent in depositing it, entrusting it to a dishonest person

---

## Page 15199

or someone unable to safeguard it, when he could have avoided doing so, then he has been negligent and is liable. As for a custodian who does not know that the property in his possession is a deposit held in trust, scholars within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere hold two views concerning his liability. The stronger view is that he bears no liability.

Any financial charges imposed on account of the jointly owned property, whether unjustly or otherwise, must be borne by the property as a whole, not by any particular share alone. If someone forcibly takes the deposited property, the overseer who deposited it may demand its return from him. The depositor may also pursue this claim in the custodian’s absence. As for the rightful owner, he may pursue a claim against the usurper, or against the overseer or custodian if there has been negligence on his part. Without negligence or wrongful conduct, however, he has no claim against the latter two.

If this custodian dies and the fate of the deposited property is unknown—whether it was taken from him, he took it himself, or it was lost—it constitutes a debt payable from his estate according to the great majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Ahmad. This is also the apparent purport of al-Shāfiʿī’s own statement and one of the two opinions within his school.

If it constitutes a debt owed by him, it must be repaid from his property. If he has property other than the endowment, it must be repaid from that. If he has no property other than the endowment, the status of the endowment in the case of a debtor whose debts exhaust his assets is a well-known point of disagreement among the scholars.

---

## Page 15200

Likewise, an endowment that remained in his possession until his death is invalid according to one of the two scholarly opinions. This is Mālik’s view and also one of the two opinions in the schools of Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad.

If, however, the endowment has become valid and binding and has eligible beneficiaries, but the creditor is not among those covered by its terms, the debt may not be repaid from it. Yet if the creditor is among those covered—for example, if the endowment is for the poor and the creditor is poor—then undoubtedly allocating funds to this poor man, to whom the founder owes a debt, takes precedence over allocating them to anyone else. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man was entrusted with money on condition that, if the depositor died, he would deliver it to his children. The depositor died, leaving heirs besides his children, namely two wives. He had two sons by one of them and two daughters by another woman. A man in authority claimed that the mother of the two sons was a slave woman whom he owned, and seized her and her children. Then one of the two sons died, followed by his mother.

Are the children alone entitled to all the money, or does it belong to all the heirs? If the claim of the man alleging ownership of the two sons’ mother is not substantiated, may the custodian hand over to him everything belonging to the two sons and their mother? Or may he retain their shares for the surviving son, hoping that ownership over him will be relinquished, or use the money to ransom him from slavery? If he retains the money, may he trade with it so that zakat does not consume it?

---

## Page 15201

He replied:

If this money belonged to the depositor, the custodian must deliver to each heir his rightful share, whether or not its owner had earmarked it for his children. The custodian may not single out some heirs to receive it without the consent of the others, for the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah has given everyone entitled to a right his due, so there is no bequest for an heir.”

Even if the executor explicitly designated the money for certain heirs, this would not be permissible without the others’ consent, according to the unanimous agreement of the imams.

As for the man claiming ownership of the slave mother, no judgment may be given in his favour merely on the strength of his claim, by agreement of the Muslims—especially if he acknowledges having given the slave woman to the depositor, since this constitutes an admission that he transferred ownership to him. Rather, she is an umm al-walad (a slave woman who has borne her master a child), and his children by her are free. Even supposing that she actually belonged to the claimant, if the man who had intercourse with her believed that she was his own slave woman, his children are free, according to the unanimous agreement of the imams.

The custodian must safeguard these young children’s shares. If there is a knowledgeable, just and capable judge in the town who will safeguard this money for them, he should hand it over to him. If he finds no one who will safeguard it for them, he should retain it and trade with it in a proper manner. The profit belongs to the orphan, and the custodian’s reward is with Allah.

An umm al-walad inherits nothing from her master; but if one of her sons dies, [she inherits from him] (1). And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila (the Comprehensive Encyclopedia).

---

## Page 15202

He was also asked:

A man had a camel entrusted to his care, which was stolen along with some of his own camels. He then caught up with the thief and recovered the camels, but refused to hand that camel over to the depositor until he swore that it belonged to him. He therefore swore by Allah Almighty that it belonged to him, intending only his right to hold it for safekeeping. What is the ruling?

He replied:

If he had the right to take possession of it and to exercise istīlād over it, then he has neither sworn falsely nor incurred any sin. But if he meant that he owned it in the usual sense, this is a lie. However, if he believed this permissible to ward off injustice—indirect wording offers an alternative to lying—he should seek Allah’s forgiveness for that and repent to Him. No expiation is required of him. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

About borrowing from a deposit without its owner’s permission.

He replied:

As for borrowing from the depositor’s money, if the custodian knows, with a certainty that sets his heart at ease, that the owner consents to this, there is nothing wrong with it. This

---

## Page 15203

can only be known in the case of a man whom you have come to know thoroughly and whose regard for you you understand. Examples of this have been reported of a number of people, as illustrated by what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, used to do in the homes of some of his Companions, and by his pledging allegiance on behalf of ʿUthmān, may Allah be pleased with him, while he was absent. Whenever there is doubt about this, borrowing is not permissible.

He also said:

If someone buys an item—a camel or something else—that is being held in safekeeping, then leaves it with the custodian, and the other party subsequently sells it, the second sale is invalid. If the custodian hands it over to the second buyer, its owner—the first buyer—may demand it back from the custodian who handed it over and from the buyer who received it.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Some people entrusted property to a monk in a monastery. He claimed that it had gone missing along with the other contents of the monastery. Then it emerged that he had sold what he claimed had gone missing from the monastery. Must he be held liable for the property or not? Is his word to be accepted? This monk’s monastery stands on the coast of the saltwater sea. He has a brother who is a pirate and takes refuge with him, as do other pirates.

---

## Page 15204

What, then, must the authorities do about him? Is it permissible to put him to death and destroy his monastery? The owners demanded their property from him, but he did not hand it over to them, and they have Christian witnesses who can testify to this.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If it becomes clear that the custodian’s own property was not lost, yet he claims that the deposit alone was lost, then he is liable for the deposit according to one of the two scholarly views. This is the view of Mālik and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. For ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, held Anas ibn Mālik liable for a deposit that he claimed had been lost while his own property remained intact. If, however, he claims that all the property was lost and his claim is then exposed as false, the grounds for holding him liable are stronger still.

If the depositor—the owner of the deposit—claims that he demanded it from the custodian but the latter did not hand it over, or that the custodian betrayed his trust and the deposit was not lost, then the grounds for accepting the depositor’s statement under oath are stronger and more compelling. Indeed, in such a case, the custodian deserves a severe discretionary punishment that will deter him and others like him from lying. This applies even though he is a non-Muslim living under Muslim protection.

If members of his own religion who are regarded as acceptable witnesses among them testify against him, their testimony is accepted according to one of the two scholarly views. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. The grounds for accepting their testimony against him here are stronger still. Even those who do not accept their testimony rule on the basis of the defendant’s oath in a case such as this, given the evident greater plausibility of the claimant’s account

---

## Page 15205

according to one of his two views as well. As for a non-Muslim living under Muslim protection who shelters those at war with the Muslims or assists them against the Muslims, he has broken his covenant, and his life and property become forfeit. The authorities must not leave such people, who cannot be trusted where Muslims are concerned, in a place where they are feared to pose a danger to Muslims or where Muslim children are brought to them; for he has broken his covenant, and his life and property become forfeit.

He was asked:

About a deposit entrusted in a sealed bag whose contents the custodian neither knew nor inspected. The depositor stated that it contained fifteen hundred and three tafāsīl. The deposit was lost along with some other belongings. When it was lost, its owner said, “It contained something worth seven thousand.” Must the custodian pay compensation for what the owner initially stated, or for what he stated later?

He replied:

If it was lost without negligence or wrongdoing on his part, he is not liable to compensate for it. This is all the more so if it was lost along with his own property. If he claims that the loss occurred through an evident, known cause, he is required to produce evidence, and his account is accepted.

---

## Page 15206

What do the esteemed jurists say concerning the following?

A man has kept a deposit in another man’s house, entrusted to his care, for more than ten years. Its amount has increased and decreased, and it has been kept in a chest without a lock, with the owner’s knowledge. The custodian became so gravely ill that he was at the point of death, while the owner was staying with him day and night. He repeatedly asked the owner to take back his deposit or secure it with a lock, but he did neither. The deposit alone then disappeared from its place of safekeeping—without any negligence—and the custodian did not know whether it had disappeared during his illness or while he was healthy.

In these circumstances, must the custodian compensate for it or not? May its owner compel the custodian to pay for it and subject him to coercion through the authorities, or is it forbidden for him to pursue this demand through them? If he persists, must the ruler—may Allah grant him success—restrain him and deter him from doing so, or not? Please give us your ruling; may you be rewarded, if Allah Most High wills.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the circumstances are as described, and the deposit disappeared without negligence or wrongdoing on the custodian’s part, together with his own property, then he is not liable to compensate for it, by agreement of the imams. Likewise, if it disappeared because of its owner’s negligence, as described, the custodian is not liable, whether it was lost alone or together with his own property.

---

## Page 15207

Chapter: Reclaiming Uncultivated Land

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling on building on a wide Muslim public thoroughfare if the construction does not harm passersby?

He replied:

There are two cases:

The first is building for one’s own use. This is not permissible according to the well-known position in Ahmad’s school. Some, however, have permitted it with the ruler’s authorization.

Al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā discussed this question—and I copied his account from his own handwriting—stating that it had arisen in his day and that the muftis had given conflicting answers. In discussing a case concerning a wide thoroughfare, he considered whether the ruler may authorize someone to appropriate part of it. He explained that some had ruled this permissible, while others had ruled it impermissible. Al-Qādī adopted the latter position and stated that it was Ahmad’s own expressed view.

For Ahmad said, as reported by Ibn al-Qāsim:

“Once people have used a route so that it has become a thoroughfare, no one may appropriate any part of it, however small or large.”

He was asked: “Even if it is wide, like the main streets?”

He replied: “Even if it is wide.”

He said: “This is more serious than encroaching upon a boundary

---

## Page 15208

between himself and his partner; for the latter takes from one person, whereas the former takes from the Muslim community.”

I say: Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Battah wrote a treatise concerning those who appropriate any part of a Muslim thoroughfare, citing reports from Ahmad and other early scholars. Several earlier and later scholars of Ahmad’s school have discussed this issue, including Shaykh Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī.

He said in al-Mughnī:

“No one may reclaim streets, roads, or open squares within built-up areas, whether they are wide or narrow, and whether or not doing so restricts people’s passage. These places are shared by the Muslims and serve their interests, so they resemble their mosques. Nevertheless, people may sit in their wider parts to buy and sell, provided they neither obstruct anyone nor harm passersby. People in towns throughout all ages have agreed to allow this without objection. Moreover, it is a harmless use of something open to everyone, and thus is not prohibited, just as passing through is not prohibited.

“Ahmad said concerning someone who reaches the market stalls first: ‘Leave him be; the place is his until nightfall.’ This was formerly the practice in the market of Medina. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘Minā is a camping ground for whoever arrives first.’

“He may provide himself with shade using a mat, a chest, a cloak, or the like, provided this causes no harm, since there is a need for it and it causes no harm. He may not, however, build anything—neither a raised platform nor anything else—because it restricts

---

## Page 15209

people’s passage, and passersby may trip over it at night, as may blind people by night or day. Moreover, it remains permanently in place, and its builder may therefore come to claim ownership of it. Whoever arrives first has the greater right to the place so long as he remains there.”

I say:

All of this concerns someone who builds the raised platform for his own use, as both the beginning and the end of the discussion indicate. This is why the possibility that he might consequently claim ownership of it was given as a reason. Yet the reasoning offered in this case implies that the restriction applies only where harm may reasonably be expected. If, then, the structure is assumed to stand in line with what lies to its right and left, without causing passersby any harm whatsoever, this reason no longer applies. The implication of this reasoning is that it is permissible when that reason is absent, in accordance with one of the two opinions mentioned by the judge.

In short, there are two opinions concerning the permissibility, with the ruler’s permission, of erecting a structure for the builder’s exclusive use that causes no harm whatsoever.

A comparable case is that of extending a rawshan (projecting structure) or a waterspout over a through road without causing any harm. Is this permissible with the ruler’s permission? There are two opinions within Ahmad’s school:

The first is that it is permissible, as preferred by Ibn ‘Aqīl and Abū al-Barakāt.

(The second is that it is not permissible, as preferred by several scholars. This is the well-known position reported from Ahmad, whether understood as outright prohibition or mere discouragement. Abū Bakr al-Mardhawī mentioned reports concerning this in Kitāb al-Wara‘ (The Book of Scrupulousness).

Among these is al-Mardhawī’s report that he roofed a house for Ahmad and directed its waterspout towards the road. The following morning, Ahmad said:

“Summon the carpenter for me so that he may redirect the water

---

## Page 15210

into the house.”

So I summoned him, and he redirected it. Ahmad said:

“Yahyā al-Qattān’s water used to drain into the road, but he resolved to change this and diverted it into the house.”

He also reported that Ahmad mentioned the scrupulousness of Shu‘ayb ibn Harb, who said:

“You may not coat the wall with clay, lest it encroach upon the road.”

Al-Mardhawī asked Ahmad about a man digging a well or a muharram for the upper storey in the open space in front of his house. He replied:

“No; this is the Muslims’ road.”

Al-Mardhawī said: “I said, ‘It is only a well that will be dug and its opening covered.’ He replied, ‘Is it not in the Muslims’ road?’”

Ibn al-Hakam asked him about a man who extends a privy or a column into the Muslims’ road: “Would he still be considered upright?”

He replied:

“He would not be considered upright, and his testimony would not be admissible.”

Ahmad also narrated, with his chain of transmission, that ‘Alī used to order that drain outlets and privies projecting into the Muslims’ road be cut away.

It is also reported that ‘Ā’idh ibn ‘Amr al-Muzanī said:

“I would rather have my mud poured into my own chamber than into the Muslims’ road.”

He said: “We were told that he would not even let rainwater drain from his house into the road.” He continued: “Someone saw in a dream that he was among the people of Paradise. He was asked, ‘What earned you this?’ He replied, ‘Keeping my harm away from the Muslims.’”

Those who permitted this cited the hadith concerning the waterspout of al-‘Abbās as evidence.

The second type:

Building something in a wide road for the benefit of the Muslims without harming passersby—for example, building a mosque that people need, enlarging a cramped mosque by incorporating part of a wide road into it, or taking part of the road for something that serves the mosque, such as a shop from which the mosque benefits. This type is permissible in the school of

---

## Page 15211

Ahmad, according to the school’s well-known position. The followers of Abū Hanīfa likewise stated this. But does it require the ruler’s permission? Two positions are reported from Ahmad. Some scholars of Ahmad’s school mention no disagreement over the permissibility of this type of construction, while others mention a third report prohibiting it outright.

This question is discussed in the earlier and later works of Ahmad’s school, from the time of his disciples and their disciples down to later authors such as Abū al-Barakāt, Ibn Tamīm, Ibn Hamdān, and others. Ahmad’s statements are recorded in *Jāmi‘ al-Khallāl*, *al-Shāfī* by Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, *Zād al-Musāfir*, *al-Mutarjim* by Abū Ishāq al-Jūzajānī, and other works.

Ismā‘īl ibn Sa‘īd al-Shālanjī said:

“I asked Ahmad about a wide road that the Muslims could dispense with, whereas they needed a mosque there: ‘Is it permissible to build a mosque there?’

“He replied: ‘There is no harm in it, provided it does not impair the road.’”

This *Questions of Ismā‘īl ibn Sa‘īd* is among the most distinguished collections of questions answered by Ahmad. Abū Ishāq Ibrāhīm ibn Ya‘qūb al-Jūzajānī explained it in his book *al-Mutarjim*. He served as a preacher here at the congregational mosque of Damascus. He also transmitted answers from Ahmad to legal questions, and would read aloud Ahmad’s letters addressed to him from the pulpit of the congregational mosque of Damascus.

Thus, Ahmad permitted construction here without qualification and did not stipulate the ruler’s permission. Muhammad ibn al-Hakam asked him: “Do you disapprove of praying in a mosque

---

## Page 15212

built on land taken from the road?”

He replied: “I disapprove of praying in it unless it has the ruler’s permission.” Here, then, he made the ruler’s permission a condition of permissibility.

Ismā‘īl’s questions to Ahmad came later than those of Ibn al-Hakam. Ibn al-Hakam had accompanied Ahmad early on and died about twenty years before him. Ismā‘īl, however, had followed the approach of the adherents of juristic reasoning before adopting that of the scholars of hadith. He questioned Ahmad later in his life, and also questioned Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd al-Hāshimī and other scholars of hadith.

Sulaymān was ranked alongside Ahmad, to the point that al-Shāfi‘ī said: “In Baghdad, I saw no one sounder in judgment than these two men: Ahmad ibn Hanbal and Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd al-Hāshimī.”

Those who recognize a third report on this question derived it from Ahmad’s statement, as transmitted by al-Mardhawī: “The ruling concerning these mosques that have been built in the road is that they must be demolished.”

Muhammad ibn Yahyā al-Kahhāl said:

“I asked Ahmad: ‘What if a man extends a mosque onto part of the road?’

“He replied: ‘One should not pray in it.’”

Those who do not recognize a third report say that Ahmad was referring here to mosques that narrowed the road and harmed the Muslims. There is undoubtedly no permission to build such mosques. This interpretation reconciles his recorded statements, which is preferable to treating them as contradictory.

Even more significantly, Ahmad permits replacing a mosque with another when doing so serves the public interest, as the Companions did.

Sālih ibn Ahmad said:

“I said to my father: ‘A mosque falls into ruin

---

## Page 15213

and its people move away. Do you think it should be relocated?’

He replied: ‘Yes, if the intention is to benefit the people; otherwise, no.’

He added: ‘Ibn Mas‘ūd relocated the congregational mosque from the date sellers’ quarter. If it serves a beneficial purpose, there is no harm in doing so; otherwise, it should not be done.’

I also asked my father about a man who built a mosque and then wanted to move it elsewhere. He replied: ‘If the man who built the mosque wishes to relocate it for fear of thieves, or because its location is filthy, there is no harm in doing so.’”

Ahmad said:

“Yazīd ibn Hārūn narrated to us, from al-Mas‘ūdī, from al-Qāsim, who said: ‘When ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd came to the treasury, Sa‘d ibn Mālik had already built the palace and established a mosque near the date sellers. The treasury was then broken into, and the man who had broken into it was caught. He wrote about him to ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, and ‘Umar wrote back: “Subject the man to amputation, relocate the mosque, and place the treasury on the mosque’s qiblah side, for there will always be someone praying in the mosque.” So ‘Abd Allāh relocated it and marked out this site for it.’”

Sālih said:

“My father said: ‘It is said that the treasury at the mosque of Kufa was broken into, so ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd relocated the mosque to what is now the date sellers’ quarter, at the site of the old mosque.’”

Ahmad means that the mosque built by Ibn Mas‘ūd occupied the site of the date sellers’ quarter in Ahmad’s time. This mosque was the old mosque. The mosque of Kufa was subsequently altered a third time.

Abū al-Khattāb said:

“Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked: ‘May a mosque be relocated?’

“He replied: ‘If it is cramped and cannot accommodate its congregation, there is no harm in relocating it to a more spacious site.’”

---

## Page 15214

Ahmad also permitted a mosque built at ground level to be raised and a drinking-water facility to be constructed beneath it for a beneficial purpose. If the neighbours disagreed, some saying, “We are elderly and cannot climb the stairs,” while others favoured its construction, Ahmad said: “Consider what the majority prefer.”

Some scholars of his school interpreted this as referring to the mosque’s initial construction. However, the discerning scholars of his school recognize that this interpretation is mistaken, because his explicit statements in several places clearly permit relocating a mosque.

If, then, Ahmad sanctioned what the Companions did when they converted a mosque site to another use for a beneficial purpose—even though a mosque has greater sanctity than other sites, for it is established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of Abū Hurayrah, that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The places most beloved to Allah are their mosques, and the places most hated by Allah are their marketplaces”—

then, if it is permissible, for a beneficial purpose, to turn a site possessing sanctity and shared by the Muslims into a site without that sanctity, it is all the more permissible and appropriate to turn a shared site that does not possess such sanctity, such as a wide road, into a site possessing sanctity and forming part of an existing sacred site. There is no doubt that mosques have greater sanctity than roads, while both provide a shared benefit.

---

## Page 15215

Section

Matters entrusted to the ruler are also entrusted to his deputies. In matters assigned to judges, the order of a judge acting as the ruler’s deputy has the same force as the ruler’s own order. Examples include arranging marriages for the unmarried, overseeing endowments and administering them according to their founders’ stipulations, and maintaining mosques and their endowments, wherever the ruler is permitted to undertake such matters. Whatever the ruler may lawfully administer, his deputy may likewise administer.

If a matter is open to juristic judgment and disagreement concerning it is widespread, no one may censure the ruler or his deputy, whether a judge or another official, or overturn what the ruler and his deputies have done in that matter.

This applies when the building is in the road, even if it adjoins the road, according to most scholars: Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. The same applies to a house’s forecourt. But does the forecourt belong to the house’s owner, or is its use one of the rights attached to the house?

There are two views within Ahmad’s school. The first is that it belongs to the house’s owner; this is also the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. Mālik even spoke of forecourts situated in the road that their occupants rent out, saying: “If

---

## Page 15216

they are so narrow that they cause harm to Muslims, and something is constructed in them, those responsible must be stopped and not allowed to proceed. But where the occupants’ use of a forecourt does not restrict Muslims’ passage, I see no objection to it.”

Al-Tahāwī said:
“This indicates that he regarded forecourts as belonging to their occupants, since he permitted renting them out. A sale should therefore not be invalidated by a stipulation concerning them.”

He said:
“What al-Shāfi‘ī’s statement indicates is that if the forecourt serves the house, it belongs to the house’s owner, although, in his view, it may not be sold.”

Al-Tahāwī mentioned that Abū Hanīfah’s position is that forecourts belong to the Muslim community and are not privately owned, just like the rest of the road. What al-Qādī, Ibn ‘Aqīl, and other scholars of Ahmad’s school stated is the second view: land may be privately owned, but not the road. Nevertheless, the landowner has a greater right than others to use the facilities serving his property, and thus has a greater right than others to the house’s forecourt. This is also Ahmad’s position regarding herbage growing on someone’s property: the owner has a greater right to it than others, even though he does not own it … (1) according to the majority: Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad … (2).

Thus, building in the forecourt of a mosque or a house has a stronger claim to permissibility than building in the main thoroughfare. It is established in an authentic hadith reported by ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—that Abū Bakr al-Siddīq established a mosque in the forecourt of his house. This is like the gravel-covered area that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—set aside outside the mosque of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for those wishing to converse or engage in activities from which the mosque should be protected. It was neither a mosque nor like the road; rather, … (3) a special connection with the mosque. In a space such as this

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2, 3) Blank in the original.

---

## Page 15217

building is all the more permissible. Building there is like building in recesses that lie off the main roadway, adjoining a house or mosque while also opening onto the road, and which road users do not need—unless one supposes an unusually large forecourt resembling a through-passage connected to a public thoroughfare. In all these cases, they have a greater right than others. If they wished to build there and install a gate, most scholars would permit this for the reasons already given. According to Abū Hanīfah, however, they may not do so, since this would extinguish others’ right to enter when necessary.

Most scholars say:

Others’ right there extends only to being permitted to use it when its occupants have not restricted access. Likewise, privately owned open land may be used in ways that do not harm its owners, such as praying there, resting there at midday, or stopping there as a traveller. Such uses are permissible both there and in the forecourts of houses without the owner’s permission, according to the overwhelming majority of scholars.

Scholars of al-Shāfi‘ī’s school mention two opinions concerning the use of a forecourt without the owner’s permission. Scholars of Ahmad’s school also mention a view prohibiting prayer on privately owned open land. This, however, is difficult to reconcile with Ahmad’s explicit statements and principles, for he permits eating fruit in such circumstances. How much more, then, should uses that cause the owner no harm be permissible! Moreover, according to Ahmad’s explicit ruling, it is permissible to graze livestock on herbage growing on unlawfully seized land; one may therefore enter it without its owner’s permission for the sake of the herbage, although some scholars of his school prohibited this.

---

## Page 15218

As for uses that cause no harm whatsoever, these are like taking shelter in someone’s shade or using the light of his fire; such uses require no permission. Once the owner restricts access to the land, however, its use becomes prohibited. Hence the distinction between fruit protected by neither a wall nor a watchman and fruit to which access is restricted: according to Ahmad’s school, the former may be eaten without payment in circumstances where the latter may not—either by anyone or only by those in need—even though carrying any away is not permissible.

If an owner may build in the forecourt of his property for his own benefit, then building in a mosque’s forecourt for the mosque’s benefit is all the more permissible. The forecourt of a house or mosque is not confined to the entrance side; rather, it may extend around all sides. Al-Qādī, Ibn ‘Aqīl and others said:

“If someone reclaims a piece of land, he has a greater right to its surrounding open space. If someone else wishes to dig a well at the base of his wall, he has no right to do so.”

Abū Hāmid, al-Māwardī and other scholars of al-Shāfi‘ī’s school stated likewise. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15219

Chapter on Lost Property

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man found a horse belonging to a Muslim in the possession of some Arabs and recovered it from them. The horse then became so ill that it could no longer walk. May the man who recovered it sell it on its owner’s behalf, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, this is permissible. Indeed, in these circumstances, the man who rescued it must sell it on its owner’s behalf, even if the owner has not authorized him to sell it. The imams have explicitly addressed this case and others like it. He must safeguard the proceeds. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man found a lost item in the middle of a wilderness and announced his find along the way until he reached his town. Is it lawful for him, or not?

---

## Page 15220

He replied:

He should announce the find for a year near the place where he found it. If, after a year, he has not found its owner, he may use it or give it in charity. And Allah knows best.

He was asked about scattered dirhams that a man finds.

He replied:

He should announce the find for a year. If he does not find its owner, he may spend it or give it in charity.

He was asked:

A man found a lost item and, over several days, privately informed some people about it. It has remained in his possession for several years. What is the ruling concerning it?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. It is not permissible for him to announce it in this manner. Rather, he must announce it publicly, but in general terms, by saying, “Who has lost some money?” or something similar. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15221

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Some pilgrims encountered Bedouins who had waylaid people and taken their belongings. The robbers fled, leaving behind their camels and the stolen goods. Is it lawful to take the camels belonging to the robbers and the goods they stole, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Whatever they took of the pilgrims’ property must be returned to them if possible. Such property is treated like lost property: it must be publicly announced for a year. If its owner comes forward, it is returned to him; otherwise, the person who recovered it may spend it, provided that he remains liable to reimburse its owner. If he loses hope of finding its owner, he should give it in charity, and it should be used for purposes benefiting the Muslims.

The same applies to all property whose owner is unknown: property wrongfully seized, borrowed articles, deposits entrusted for safekeeping, people’s property recovered from robbers, or belongings people have left behind. All such property is to be given in charity and used for purposes benefiting the Muslims.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

When the Tatars came, people fled before them, leaving behind animals and household goods made of copper and other materials. A Muslim took these into his keeping, and a long time passed without any owner coming forward

---

## Page 15222

or anyone asking after them, while he has been using the animals and goods. What should he do?

He replied:

He may use them, and he may give them in charity to someone who will benefit from them. Allah knows best.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A ship sank at sea and then drifted to a certain locality, its identity being known. It had been carrying jars of hot oil. The villagers worked together to haul the vessel ashore and turn it over. The oil floated on the surface of the water and continued drifting with it. The villagers then came to the sea and found the oil floating on the water. Each gathered as much as he could, with the vessel nearby. Is the oil collected in this way lawful or unlawful?

Another vessel, laden with pomegranates, sank, and its entire cargo drifted away at sea. People began gathering what they could, but no owner could be identified. Is property whose owner is unknown lawful or unlawful to take?

He replied:

Those who collected the oil from the surface of the water saved legally protected property from destruction. They are entitled to the customary remuneration for their work, while the oil belongs to its owner. As for the oil belonging to its owner, I know of no disagreement on this, apart from a little dissent. It is reported that al-Hasan said:

“It belongs to whoever salvages it.”

---

## Page 15223

As for whether customary remuneration is due to the person who salvaged it, scholars hold two views. The sounder view is that remuneration is due. This is expressly stated by Ahmad and others, for the rescuer acts voluntarily.

The proponents of this view say:

If they salvaged it for the sake of Allah Most High, their reward rests with Allah Most High. If they salvaged it in return for compensation, they are entitled to compensation. Otherwise, property would be lost, since people would not rescue it from destruction if they knew that they would gain nothing by doing so. Regarding someone who purchased Muslims’ property from the unbelievers, the Companions ruled that its owner could recover it from the purchaser upon paying the purchase price, because that was the price he had paid to rescue it.

Moreover, this property would have been destroyed had this person not recovered it. Rescuing it is a permissible act involving no disobedience. Thus, when the property is recovered through one person’s labour, while the original property belongs to another, it is to be shared between them. However, a particular individual cannot be compelled to enter into joint ownership, so customary remuneration becomes due.

Furthermore, such work is authorized by customary practice: people ordinarily seek someone to salvage such property for them in return for payment. A contract of hire may be established by customary practice, as when someone enters a bathhouse or travels aboard a ship without expressly agreeing on terms, or hands food over to a cook and a washer without expressly agreeing on terms. There are numerous similar examples.

If the property were an animal and someone rescued it from mortal danger, he would acquire ownership of it, as stated in the transmitted report. This is because an animal has inviolability in its own right, unlike goods, whose inviolability derives from that of their owner. In the former case, the rescue is undertaken to uphold the animal’s own right, and its owner may have given up hope of it while it was in mortal danger;

---

## Page 15224

unlike goods, whose owner says to the rescuer: “You had permission from the moment I left it, but the right to it remains mine. If you do not give me my due, I do not authorize you to salvage it.”

As for pomegranates whose owner is unknown, they are treated as lost property. If there is hope of finding the owner of lost property, its discovery must be announced for a year. If there is no hope of finding its owner, there are two views concerning announcing it. According to either view, however, they may eat the pomegranates or sell them and retain the proceeds, then announce their discovery afterward. Allah knows best.

He was asked—may Allah sanctify his soul—about someone who found an infant with some money and cared for him until he was two months old. Another man then came so that his wife could breastfeed him for Allah’s sake. When the child grew older, the woman claimed that he was her son and that she had raised him in his father’s care. Is her statement to be accepted? Must she reimburse the second man for what he spent on the child? And must the first man hand over what he found with his son?

He answered:

If the child’s parentage is unknown and she claims that he is her son, her statement is accepted. The money found with him is to be used for his maintenance during his stay with the person who found him. Allah knows best.

> End of Volume Thirty.
